# COPYRIGHT STATEMENT

The copyright law of the United States—<term>Title 17, United States Code<note>The official collection of federal statutes that govern copyright in the U.S.</note></term>—concerns the making of photocopies or other reproductions of copyrighted material.

Under certain conditions specified in the law, libraries and archives are authorized to furnish a photocopy or other reproduction. One of these specified conditions is that the photocopy or other reproduction is not to be "used for any purpose other than private study, scholarship, or research." If a user makes a request for, or later uses, a photocopy or reproduction for purposes in excess of <term>fair use<note>A legal doctrine allowing limited use of copyrighted material without permission for purposes such as criticism, news reporting, teaching, or research.</note></term>, that user may be liable for copyright infringement.

This institution reserves the right to refuse to accept a copy order if, in its judgement, fulfillment of the order would involve violation of the copyright law.

<meta>This page serves as the formal half-title and the beginning of the introduction. It follows the title page, continuing the scholarly presentation of Jakob Böhme’s life. The library stamp mentioned in the metadata (408866) is noted as a physical marker of the book's provenance.</meta>

# Jakob Böhme.

## His Life and His Writings, presented through the use of manuscript sources.

->A contribution to the history of modern philosophy. Prize essay by<-
->Dr. Hermann Adolph Fechner.<-

### Introduction.

It lies in the nature of the mystic—who seeks and finds God and the world within, <term>immanent</term> <note>inherent or remaining within the self rather than existing outside of it</note> in the subject, and who is conscious of reaching the highest point in this contemplativeness—that he remains distant from external, practical life. He participates in it only insofar as his circle of life is violently disturbed from without, or so that a quiet effectiveness in domestic and civic life fits as an <term>accident</term> <note>original Latin: *accidens*; in philosophical terms, a non-essential attribute or property that does not affect the essence of a thing</note> to the <term>substance</term> <note>original Latin: *substantia*; the underlying essence or fundamental reality</note> of his interiority. This occurs without a reciprocal influence of one sphere upon the other asserting itself, because to him the real, social world of man is only a dark, heavy realm of shadows—merely the image of the bright, inner, blissful spirit-world reflected in a murky river. The outward appearance of the mystic is calm, unchanging, uniform; he moves among the living like a stranger from a world of dreams, who perceives nothing directly with physical senses, but rather through the mediation of his spiritual, inward-turned eye. This eye, in the contemplation of a kingdom closed to others, [perceives] an entire, life-filled...

<note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page, continuing the description of the mystic's internal vision.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the philosophical biography of Jakob Böhme, defining the "mystic" as someone who prioritizes an inner spiritual reality over the external, social world. Fechner sets the stage by describing the inward-looking nature of his subject.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, mysticism, interiority, philosophy of the subject, spiritual vision, Hermann Adolph Fechner, 19th-century philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s exploration of the mystic’s inner life, moving from the general definition of a mystic as someone withdrawn from the world to the apparent contradictions found in radical religious movements like the Chiliasts and early communal groups.</meta>

world revels in spiritual diversity, and there he finds his truest home. For what others merely think and conceive, he is permitted to behold; and here he experiences more than others do in the colorful bustle of humanity. He acts and suffers, only his experiences are internal, occurring within the deepest, yet all the more decisive, consciousness. Now, the directions taken by practical mystics and <term>Chiliasts</term> <note>those who believe in a literal thousand-year reign of Christ on Earth, from the Greek 'chilioi' meaning a thousand</note> on one hand, and the <term>Theosophists</term> <note>thinkers seeking direct knowledge of the divine through spiritual ecstasy or intuition</note> and Gnostics on the other, seem by their very nature to be an exception to this exclusively internal essence that abstracts itself entirely from the outside world.

For that first direction expresses itself in the founding of social structures, in <term>conventicles</term> <note>secret or unofficial religious meetings</note> and communities—as with the **Brethren of the Common Life**, to whom **Thomas à Kempis** <note>original: "Thomas von Kempen"; author of 'The Imitation of Christ'</note> belonged; with the **Beguines and Beghards** <note>lay religious orders in the Middle Ages who lived in semi-monastic communities without taking formal vows</note>; and with the **Mormons** of the most recent time. The institutions of these groups often demonstrate a strong communal spirit and have implemented a form of **communism** with significant energy, a goal that has been sought in vain through other means. **Chiliasm** has even produced grand, gigantic geniuses who, with enthusiasm, challenged entire nations to a difficult struggle with their theocratically organized hosts.

The **Reformation** did not only produce men who wanted to see the externality of church forms destroyed from the ground up—even physical stone churches and religious paintings. Such a pursuit actually reveals a connection to the mystic’s pure interiority, as the exaggerated intensity of a "God-intimate" consciousness would not tolerate even the slightest desecration of its inner heaven by earthly forms, even in others. But the Reformation also produced phenomena that stood in sharp contradiction to the quiet, inward-turned nature of the mystic. Take, for example, **Thomas Münzer**, who knew how to inspire diverse masses with political tact and organize them into powerful resistance, attempting to fight for his ideas with cannons and lances; or the **Münster Prophets**, who successfully defended their strict <term>theocracy</term> <note>a system of government in which priests or religious leaders rule in the name of God</note> against external enemies for a long time through an energetic government.

On the other hand, the **Gnostics**—and in a more modern form, the **Theosophists**—made nature itself, the world of appearances,

---

<page-num>3</page-num>

and the development of humanity the core of their mystical vision. They drew **alchemy, astronomy, botany, anatomy, physiology,** and **mathematics** in their widest extent into their circle: a unified understanding of languages and human history are among their most significant views, the development of which has sown good seeds for true science outside the realm of mysticism.

But even if the Chiliasts founded empires and the monks established communistic communities, or if the Theosophists contemplated nature, this occurred in a certain contradiction with their essence as mystics. It was a confusion of the spiritual, internal experience of the mystical world with its flat imprint: the lead-heavy reality. The kingdom of the mystics lies in the spirit; their interiority should not, originally, tolerate the externality of dogmatic and hierarchical church forms. However, the energetic drive for life and a powerful imagination enticed them into the delusion that they could establish preparatory "Kingdoms of God" here on earth. Thus arose those strange constructions of **"thousand-year empires"** which carried the seed of sudden ruin within them, because their creators, through the act itself, created a contradiction with their own world, which is meant to be only spiritually creative and spiritually experienced.

Just as these people committed the error of thinking that coarse reality was capable of identity with their inner sanctuary—and thus fell into a degeneration of true mysticism (which, according to its strictest definition, lacks ethical-practical effectiveness)—so too do **Gnosticism** and **Theosophy** view nature and history. They do not look upon them with the eye of a summarizing, comprehending researcher or with scientific interest, but instead find them entirely immanent within the subject as phases of the **One Deity**. Here, only the individual’s own **God-Self** <note>original: "Gott-Ich"</note> has assumed the diversity of appearances. The Deity appears as Truth, as the underlying substance of the world of appearances existing within the mystic's interior. Insofar as the Theosophist finds his <term>immanent</term> <note>dwelling within</note> God everywhere and brings the outer world into his inner experience only as something being and becoming—rather than trying to practically enforce his spiritual facts upon harsh reality—he does not fall into the degeneration of the Chiliast and the commu[nist]...

<summary>This section explains that while mystics are typically withdrawn, some (like Thomas Münzer or the Mormons) try to force their inner spiritual visions into the physical world, often leading to conflict or collapse. True theosophy, however, views the entire natural world and human history as reflections of the "God-Self" within the individual.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, Chiliasm, Theosophy, Gnosticism, Thomas à Kempis, Brethren of the Common Life, Beguines, Beghards, Mormons, communism, Reformation, Thomas Münzer, Münster Prophets, alchemy, thousand-year reign, God-Self</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of mystics, transitioning from a general philosophical overview to the specific historical difficulties in documenting the life of Jakob Böhme. It explains why biographical details are scarce, citing the Thirty Years' War and the loss of manuscripts.</meta>

...monastic monks. Life does not disturb his inner world because his activity does not interfere with the shaping of social relations; his inner development is not closed off to spiritual influences from the outside. However, the observer sees here the same calm outward demeanor as with the pure, so to speak, "primitive" mystic, who finds God in an immediate state by abstracting himself from the world. The realms of the <term>Chiliasts</term> <note>Those believing in a future thousand-year golden age or the literal reign of Christ on Earth</note>, on the other hand, can only be judged from the perspective of their own expanded subjectivity.

Thus, the lack of information regarding the life of the <term>theosophist</term> <note>A thinker seeking direct knowledge of the divine; here referring specifically to Böhme’s school of Christian mysticism</note> ***Jakob Böhme*** becomes easily understandable. On one hand, his existence was poor in external facts because he lived quietly, withdrawn from public life, like most of his spiritual brothers; only the harsh interference of outside forces could cause him to be passively pulled into the spotlight during his lifetime. On the other hand, his followers and friends—sharing that same interest in the inner life, which views the stormy variety of the world not as material to be worked upon, but as a foreign land—did not desire to know his fate through letters, but rather the experiences of his spirit and the knowledge of his mystical sense. Even important periods of his external life were so secondary to his friends' sense of truth that they not only cared little for factual accuracy, but the stories also unconsciously transformed under their eyes and pens into embellished myths: after all, they had never questioned the master closely about his outward fate, and after his death, they valued the preservation of his writings, but not the clarification and accuracy of his history. A third coincidence has further diminished the already meager sources: in the turmoil of the **Thirty Years’ War**, few of his fellow citizens felt moved to record the civic events of their home city with precision, as political events demanded the urgent attention of chroniclers—though even in this branch, a certain scarcity during wartime cannot be denied. Finally

<column-break/>

the dispersal and persecution of ***Böhme’s*** followers toward ***Holland*** was undoubtedly the reason that the existing letters, containing incidental notes about ***Böhme’s*** life circumstances, were almost all scattered and lost. A large portion of these, which undoubtedly passed into <insert>Abraham von</insert> ***Franckenberg’s*** <note>Böhme’s friend and first biographer</note> possession along with ***Böhme’s*** other manuscripts, was inherited by ***Angelus Silesius*** <note>Born Johannes Scheffler, a famous mystic poet who later converted to Catholicism</note>, whose estate went to the **St. Matthias Monastery** in **Breslau**. There, they have partially disappeared, likely because writings of a Protestant spirit were intentionally destroyed; thus, such manuscripts that could provide information about ***Böhme*** have, in all probability, met their end¹). ***Roth von Baumgarten***, a kind friend of the theosophist, sold a quantity of his manuscripts—which he had acquired from the deceased’s family—to ***Holland***²), where they were probably all destroyed after the publication of his works. In public libraries, no trace of such correspondence remains³). This loss—as the only record of the mutual exchange within ***Böhme’s*** theosophical school, which extended quite far—is particularly felt. This is not because so little of ***Böhme’s*** external fate can be determined with certainty (which would be of lesser importance for appreciating ***Böhme*** as a theosophist), but because such records would lead us into the heart of his intellectual development. Currently, based on the few preserved letters and writings of ***Böhme***, only a one-sided view can be established. It would be clearer than it is now how much his interaction with other minds determined the visible changes in ***Böhme’s*** world of thought, and how much he, in turn, exerted an undeniable influence upon them.

Because the available material for a biography now...

¹) *Kahlert, Angelus Silesius p. 1.*
²) *On the life and writings of J. B. No. V.* <note>original Latin: "De vita et scriptis J. B."</note>
³) Professor *Geel* in *Leiden* and Head Librarian *Holtrop* in *The Hague* <note>original Dutch/German: "Gravenhaag"</note> have had the kindness to conduct searches for the author's purpose, which have unfortunately remained without result.

<summary>This page explains the scarcity of historical records regarding Jakob Böhme’s life, attributing it to his own quiet nature, his followers' focus on his spiritual rather than physical life, and the destruction of documents during the Thirty Years' War and religious purges.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, biography, Thirty Years' War, Angelus Silesius, Franckenberg, theosophy, lost manuscripts, Holland, Breslau</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the biographical challenges of documenting Jakob Böhme’s life. It transitions from discussing the general lack of external facts to a formal proposal for how to structure his biography despite these gaps, while evaluating the primary sources available to the historian.</meta>

Böhme’s life falls so completely into two separate parts that one part deals only with external fates and his outward interactions with followers, while the other—which shows Böhme’s spiritual development most directly and at the very inner hearth of its production—stands in very little verifiable connection with the rest of the information. Because of this, a separation of the present material into two sections appears justified: one will deal with his life, and the other with Böhme’s spiritual development as it emerges from his writings. This "mastery of the material over the form of representation" <note>A scholarly way of saying the available facts dictate the structure of the book, rather than a traditional chronological narrative</note> cannot easily be avoided without leaving significant gaps and making unsustainable assumptions due to the lack of a secured connection between the two. Naturally, this arrangement does not exclude the use of his instructional writings to establish external biographical and psychological facts for the first part, insofar as his character and emotional life—the outward-facing side of his spirit—offer an object for observation.

The few sources for a biography of Jakob Böhme are:

1) His writings. Whatever incidental reports about his life and character are found therein cannot be doubted; for no one who has come to know the sincere, unreserved tone of his writing style, and especially the officially recorded reports of his public appearances, would attempt to reproach him with intentional deception, hypocrisy, or a fear of freely confessing the truth. It is not at all in the nature of souls deeply immersed in themselves and secluded from the world to see their cherished inner life expressed in a form other than what they themselves have created and built with warm care. If, however, an overextended subjectivity has produced some views on facts that seem to an unbiased observer to impair reality, such self-deceptions—which do not affect the facts themselves, but only judgments and ways of looking at things, mostly appearing in a colorful light due to an excited state of mind—can be easily resolved by

<column-break/>

comparison with views expressed elsewhere. — The most fruitful for our biographical purpose are the 66 theosophical <term>Sendbriefe</term> <note>Theosophical Epistles or Letters; personal correspondence where Böhme explained his complex ideas to friends</note>, which, taken directly from life, show the man as he positioned himself toward the outside world. Most of the letters are written to friends and followers who either sought the master’s acquaintance or asked him for instruction, or to whom he shared friendly news or expressed thanks for their kindnesses¹).

---

¹) The author has only seen one leaf of genuine manuscript. It contains the "Table of Principles of the Macrocosm" (Table of the Three Principles) and is kept in the library of the Upper Lusatian Society of Sciences in Görlitz. In the year 1702, von der Hardt (1668–1748) reports in the *New Memorials of the Rudolf-August Library in Helmstedt*: "Furthermore, Jakob Böhme, Valentin Weigel, and their associates, who professed a secret religion, together with their original manuscripts—collected by the Dresden physician and chemist Hinkelmann, a former host of Böhme, and purchased for a significant sum—which consist of nearly fifty volumes and manuscript bundles, lie hidden here in their own corner." <note>original Latin: "Quin et Jacobus Boehmius... in suo hic delitescunt angulo."</note> (Fol. 27.) — J. C. von Uffenbach writes in 1709, when he was a librarian in Helmstedt: "Because von der Hardt presents them as originals, I have examined Böhme’s things particularly; however, I did not find what ***Gottfried Arnold*** <note>A famous historian of the time known for defending 'heretics'</note> praises in his *History of the Church and Heretics*—that Böhme wrote all his works beautifully at the first draft without correcting anything. For although the hand was quite clear, it was not particularly pretty and was corrected here and there. I previously saw some of Böhme’s written things in Frankfurt, which a Jew brought to me to sell; but because he only showed me a little of it and held the whole collection at too high a price—one hundred <term>Species-Ducats</term>—and I could not know if anything unpublished was among them, nor see everything, I let it go. This was much cleaner and more consistent with Arnold’s report. It may therefore be that those found here are only copies which... Hinkelmann had made. Hinkelmann’s heirs initially demanded a thousand <term>Reichsthaler</term> for them but later gave them for 300." In 1817, the manuscripts of the Helmstedt library came to Wolfenbüttel; there, the following manuscripts by Jakob Böhme and ***Franckenberg*** <note>Abraham von Franckenberg, Böhme's first biographer</note> were still found:
1) Jakob Böhme, *On the Great Mystery* (*Mysterium Magnum*). Manuscript Helmst. 226. Fol. 795. 4.
2) Jakob Böhme, various writings. Helmst. 796. 4.
3) Jakob Böhme, *On the Election of Grace* — Epistle to G. Freudenhammer and Joh. Husern — *On True Repentance* — On

<summary>The author explains the necessity of dividing Böhme's biography into external life and internal spiritual development due to the nature of the surviving records. He identifies Böhme's own "Theosophical Epistles" as the most reliable source and provides a detailed footnote regarding the history and physical condition of Böhme's original manuscripts.</summary>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the evaluation of primary sources for Jakob Böhme’s biography. Having previously established the value of Böhme’s own writings, the author now turns to external archival records and early biographical accounts, noting their scarcity and varying degrees of reliability.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

2) The documentary reports regarding Jakob Böhme's life and fate are very few in number. They first became known to a wider public through the efforts of Mr. G. Köhler and Dr. Th. Neumann in Görlitz <note>referring to the "Resources" section below</note>, whereas in earlier times none of the biographers took the trouble to utilize them. These documents include the Görlitz Church Registry, the Property Purchase Book, the Citizenship Registry, and a Memorial Book of the Görlitz Council. The information contained therein concerns Böhme's civil status, his death, burial, and finally his clashes with the clerical and secular authorities in Görlitz. The specific document that might provide information about perhaps the most important event in Böhme's life seems to rest on little more than a mere suspicion of his followers. It was believed that the *colloquium* <note>original: "Colloquium"; a formal theological discussion or examination</note> which Böhme had to undergo in Dresden before the <term>consistory</term> <note>a governing body of the church</note> was strictly official and had therefore been minuted; that the relevant records might have been kept secret is not improbable in itself, given the strict <term>orthodoxy</term> <note>adherence to traditional Lutheran doctrine</note> of the Saxon clergy. Spener <note>Philipp Jakob Spener, a prominent German theologian and founder of Pietism</note> even suspects (Schmidt, Journal of History, Vol. 7, p. 404 in the essay by Guhrauer) that they lie buried in a vault; this may be drawn from rumor just as much as the claim by Calov <note>Abraham Calovius, a leading representative of 17th-century Lutheran orthodoxy</note> and others that the meeting never took place at all. No records of that event have been found in the Royal Saxon Main State Archives, nor in those of the Church Council in Dresden.

3) Some notes from the diaries of the Görlitz mayors Bartholomäus Scultetus (also known as Scholz

---

of the Trinity — Reasons for the Threefold Life of Man. Helmstedt 1032. quarto.
4) Jakob Böhme on Christ's Testaments — Letter to G. Freudenhammer — On True Repentance — On Regeneration — Treatise of Consolation — On the Incarnation of Jesus. Helmstedt 1033. quarto.
5) The Inverted Eye of Eternity. Helmstedt 1094. octavo.
6) Abraham von Franckenberg 1639. Written by Chr. Grumbach in the Blankenburg region. 160. 9.
These are without doubt the very same manuscripts mentioned by von der Hardt. The author owes the information regarding this to the kind efforts of Dr. Bethmann, librarian in Wolfenbüttel.

<page-num>9</page-num>

von Schollenstern) (from the years 1613, July 26, July 28, and July 30) and Johann Emmerich (1624, November 17) have a similarly well-founded claim to credibility. They are printed in a piece included in the "Reports on the Life and Writings of J. Böhme" by the Dutch editors of Böhme's works. The letter of Mr. Ehrenfried Hegenicht, Patrician and Council Member in Görlitz, concerning the late J. Böhme's talent and his writings, as well as his knowledge of the foundation of nature, together with two Görlitz mayors' testimonies about the author. Görlitz, February 21, 1669. — Otherwise, the report is not very rich in facts, with the exception of some news about Böhme's manuscripts, which Hegenicht saw in Görlitz during Böhme's lifetime and which were sold to Holland much later. Hegenicht became a councilman in 1657 and died in 1680 as mayor of Görlitz; he became acquainted with Böhme's followers and his teachings in Holland and possessed copies of his works, which were scattered after his death. — The care with which Hegenicht recorded those notes from the diaries makes him completely credible in the few dates he provides, especially regarding the Dresden examination (pp. 281 ff.).

4) The most content-rich and most frequently used source is undoubtedly the biography by Abraham von Franckenberg. He wrote it down in Latin in 1637; Heinrich Brunius translated it into German. However, it is quite meager in information, which must moreover be used with great caution. The author is known for his tendency to believe everything miraculous and to use it without criticism, to turn the simplest facts into mysterious and magical riddles, and to see spirits, ghosts, and magic formulas everywhere; his writings bristle with confused and distastefully artificial formulas and signs, which the mystics of that time turned into carriers of secret powers and sciences; he did not even shrink from carrying his rubbish into Böhme's own writings, to which he devoted great care. See in the *Mysterium Magnum* <note>Böhme's massive commentary on Genesis, the "Great Mystery"</note> Chapter 44, § 69, the sign ††††, regarding which Le Blon notes in the Amsterdam edition of 1640 that it is not by Jakob Böhme, but by Abra-

<meta>catchword: Abra-</meta>

<summary>This page evaluates the historical records of Böhme's life, noting the scarcity of official documents and the unreliability of early biographies like that of Abraham von Franckenberg. It specifically addresses the mystery of the Dresden Colloquium and lists several surviving manuscript collections.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the critical analysis of early biographies of Jakob Böhme, focusing specifically on the reliability of Abraham von Franckenberg’s account. It challenges the legendary aspects of Böhme's youth, such as the shepherd story, by comparing them against geographical and historical facts.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

...ham von Franckenberg added it later, as he was a particular lover of magical figures; he, Le Blon <note>Michel Le Blon, a Swedish ambassador and friend of Böhme who helped publish his works</note>, had not found it in two of the manuscripts ¹).

Aside from such a striking example of a meager sense of historical truth—which should not be called dishonesty, but rather shows merely the intention to "improve" and supplement Jakob Böhme—the author's own statement must arouse suspicion in anyone. He admits that he is writing down the biography only from memory ²). When one considers the time of its composition (1637), one must weigh the uncertainty of oral tradition even from a primary source, as well as the fallibility of memory. Thirteen years after Böhme's death, memory could not possibly present the same sharp images, especially in a man whose mind was not without interest in Böhme’s reputation. Caught up in a belief in mystical phenomena, he must have involuntarily beautified old memories and created new embellishments to glorify his revered friend. Furthermore, it is always the fate of men whose lives are preserved in memory for many years only through oral tradition that the accounts of them take on a more legendary coloring year after year through the imagination of the storytellers.

Therefore, all stories must be banished from his biography that contradict established conditions of time and place or documentary reports. Specifically, two claims must be identified as suspicious. The poor boy Jakob is said to have tended his father's cattle. While it is not impossible that he helped with some small chores of the farm and field management after finishing his school hours in Seidenberg...

---

¹) *Report on the Life and Writings* <note>original: "Bericht de Vita et Scriptis"</note>. J. B. No. 5, 47.
²) Franckenberg's *Life* <note>original: "Vita"</note> 1: "As much as has remained in my memory until now from oral conversations with the blessed deceased from 1623 and 1624, noted briefly and simply, yet thoroughly and truthfully" and "as much as was known to me as necessary from his own mouth, and then a little more from the reports of proving friends, setting aside what may otherwise have been noted by others or forgotten and omitted by myself." Franckenberg only met Böhme in the winter of 1623, and saw him only once more later, when he visited the Schweinichen family at Hohndorf in the summer of 1624.

<column-break/>

<page-num>11</page-num>

...for his father, the entire report becomes very doubtful due to the manner of its specific details. Jakob Böhme is said to have driven his father's herd to the *Landeskrone* <note>A prominent basalt hill near Görlitz</note>. However, the Landeskrone is not, as stated in the *Biography* <note>original: "Vita"</note>, a mountain located not far from Seidenberg, but is more than four hours away from Altseidenberg. One must rightly wonder how a boy of "poor farming folk" could have driven his cattle four hours away across the intervening Neisse River, especially since the Landeskrone—ever since the destruction of the robber knight's castle on its summit—was by no means a free pasture, but was the property of the Görlitz citizenry. Thus, the report that Jakob Böhme herded cattle on the Landeskrone is certainly false; we must reject the entire report of the boy Jakob’s alleged life as a shepherd once it becomes clear that Franckenberg only includes it to lend greater probability to his wonder-stories through local details and the use of old folk legends.

Jakob Böhme had to drive his herd to the Landeskrone because there, as the legend still goes today, an enchanted, wonderful treasure lay buried. By virtue of a divine miracle, Jakob Böhme was meant to have seen this treasure in all its glory after the mountain opened up, attaining his spiritual, matter-penetrating insight <term>clairvoyance</term> <note>original: "Hellblick"</note> for the first time. In turn, he had to have experienced this miraculous phenomenon so that his reflection would not have been awakened by a gradually stirring intellect as with other mortals, but would have reached clear consciousness through a sudden miracle. In order to have an external cause for this mystical immersion, Franckenberg invents the life of a shepherd, the visit to the Landeskrone, the divine manifestation, the clear insight into the "veins of the earth," and the enchanted hoard.

The claim that a second vision occurred precisely in the year 1600 and a third in 1610 likewise deserves mistrust. If one considers alongside this that an anecdote to be mentioned later—which belongs in the series of these divine visitations—would fall approximately in the year 1590; and finally, that after finishing the *Aurora* (1613) Böhme allowed his pen to rest for seven years: then the obsession with numbers...

<summary>This page critiques the "shepherd boy" legend of Jakob Böhme’s youth, arguing that his biographer Franckenberg invented these details to provide a mystical, miraculous origin for Böhme's spiritual insights.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Abraham von Franckenberg, Landeskrone, biography, hagiography, mysticism, Seidenberg, Aurora, historical criticism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the critical examination of Jakob Böhme’s early biographers, specifically addressing inaccuracies in reported timelines and mystical claims. It shifts into an annotated bibliography of reliable primary sources concerning Böhme’s final year and death.</meta>

...of the reporter come to light, especially since Böhme did not cease his writing for seven years, but only five: it is claimed that the divine spirit visited the divine man every ten years. Just as suspicious is the claim that Böhme stood in the highest realm of joy for seven whole days; the exaggeration and its refutation are easily uncovered.

5) As reliable documents for the life of the Theosopher <note>A term for a practitioner of "theosophy," which in Böhme's context refers to a mystical philosophy seeking direct knowledge of the divine</note>, the following must still be cited:

a) "**The Pasquill and the Defamatory Cards**" <note>A 'pasquill' is a lampoon or a public satirical attack</note> by Gregorius Richter, Head Pastor <note>original: "Pastor Primarius"</note> in Görlitz, printed in March 1624 by Joh. Rhambaw in Görlitz (a copy is held in the library of the Upper Lusatian Society of Sciences) under the title:

> The Judgement of Gregorius Richter, Head Minister of the local church of Görlitz, regarding the fanatical books of the Enthusiastic Shoemaker, the titles of which are 1) *Morning Glow at Sunrise* <note>original title: "Morgenröthe im Aufgang," often called "Aurora"</note>, 2) *The Way to Christ*, 3) *Of True Repentance*, etc. <note>original: "Judicium Gregorii Richteri, Gorlicii ministri ecclesiae patriae primarii, de fanaticis Sutoris Enthusiastici Libris..."</note>

(also reprinted in Schiebler’s edition of Böhme’s works, Vol. 6). This invective and ban-decree is written in Latin and consists of three poems, the first written in iambics, the second and third in distichs <note>Pairs of verse lines</note>; the second bears the title: **The Shoemaker Antichrist** <note>original: "Sutor Antichristus"</note>, the third **A Send-off or Escort** <note>original: "Propempticum s. Concomitativum"</note>, likely written in such a learned form to crush the simple shoemaker through awe at such high wisdom. The letter dates from March 26, 1624, the year of death for both the Head Pastor and the Theosopher. — In response, an unnamed friend of Böhme’s wrote a few lines in accented verse: **The Overseer wishes a better mind for the Head Pastor who judges the judge.** <note>original: "Adjudicantem judicem Primarium, cui optat meliorem mentem Monitor"</note>

b) "**Simple Funeral Sermon** at the honorable and well-attended burial of the highly venerable, respectable, and well-learned Mr. Gregorius Richter the elder, of the Christian Church in Görlitz, held by Nicolaus Thomas, then Deacon there. Printed in Görlitz by Joh. Rhambaw. 4to <note>Quarto: a book format where sheets are folded twice to create four leaves</note>.

c) An authentic report of Böhme’s final hours is by Magister Elias Dietrich (Theodorus): "**List of several questions** which were put to Jakob Böhme, former shoemaker in Görlitz, during his illness, before his absolution and reception of the Holy Communion,

<column-break/>

<page-num>13</page-num>

along with the answers he gave thereafter." It is printed from Dietrich’s own manuscript by Brückner, *8th Contribution to the Church History of Görlitz*, p. 57 ff. (below it, it states: "In Mr. Elias’s own hand"). Kober and Funke have it only in excerpt.

6) **Detailed Report of Mr. Tobias Kober**, Doctor of Medicine in Görlitz, regarding the illness, passing, and burial of the late Theosophical author, to the Noble Lords of Schweinichen. Nov. 2, 1624. This report, composed four days after Böhme’s death, is likely the least suspicious of all non-official sources. This is partly because the testimony of a physician—due to the habit of firmly and securely observing reality—is inherently not to be mistrusted after the passing of so few days; and partly because Kober’s character does not suggest even the remotest intention of distorting the truth—despite being a friend of the deceased who might have wanted to win the favor of the Lords of Schweinichen for the surviving family—given the documentary simplicity of the presentation. —

7) "**Truthful Account of Mr. Cornelius Weißner**", Doctor of Medicine in Breslau, regarding the late J. Böhme’s gentleness, humility, and friendliness, as well as the Examination in Dresden, in the presence of his Electoral Highness and eight of the most prominent professors, to Mr. G. R. von Beyerland in Amsterdam, the son of Abraham von Beyerland who had passed away three years earlier.

The author Weißner (also Wießner) had been the tutor for the children of Balthasar Tölke (or Tilke) in Schweidnitz, and had initially been strongly prejudiced against the Theosopher by him. However, after a meeting with our mystic himself, which was mediated by a merchant from Lauban named Liborius Schneller and his brother-in-law Schröter, the opponent became a friend and follower because he, Böhme, had endured his "academic vehemence" with "love, fear of God, and friendliness." He first reports an incident from Böhme’s disputes and dealings with the Head Pastor from January 1624, "thus certainly heard from the aforementioned faithful friends" (namely those from Lauban). Since the essay [was written] only in the year...

<meta>This page continues the critical analysis of the sources used to construct Jakob Böhme’s biography, specifically questioning the reliability of miraculous anecdotes and transitioning into a list of historical chronicles from Görlitz that mention him.</meta>

dates from 1651, and nothing is heard of a closer relationship of that person to Böhme, who mentions not a word of that event in his letters (no missive from him exists between December 27, 1623, and March 13, 1624); thus, the story can only be viewed with suspicion. It likely arose through the diligent readiness of his followers, who constantly invented new little anecdotes <note>The author uses the diminutive "Histörchen" to imply these are tall tales or legends</note> to place the Master in an even more radiant light by blackening the reputation of his enemies. — The report concerning the interrogation in Dresden also lacks precision; Hegenicht is to be preferred in this matter. The former report places the Elector in attendance, while the latter, following inquiries made on-site, records the opposite. Hegenicht cites only reliable news throughout, whereas Weisner writes only *on the authority of others worthy of trust and beyond reproach* <note>original Latin: "auctoritate aliorum fide dignorum et exceptione majorum"</note>. — In their editions of Böhme's works, the Dutch editors included two additional reports on his life and writings, under numbers 5 and 6, which contain some good information but also obvious legends. Furthermore, they provide no significant new contributions.

8) The Görlitz Chronicles and Annals yield only a small harvest; they draw from the diaries of the Mayors, for example, from that of the famous Bartholomäus Scultetus, whose chronicle only reaches 1592, but who collected materials for its continuation until the year of his death (1616); or from Johannes Emmerich, who was Mayor in 1624; and presumably from a shared older source, as almost all of them report the same thing with minor variations, partly from:

a) the Latin chronicle in the possession of the private scholar Mr. Jancke in Görlitz, likely authored in its first half by the Head Pastor <note>original: "Primarius"</note> Richter himself, as the handwriting visibly changes exactly at the time Richter died. The entry therefore deserves to be included in its entirety:

> A certain shoemaker <note>original: "Sutor"</note> among us for many years played the role of a chief fanatic <note>original: "enthusiasmum primarium"; in this era, "enthusiasm" referred to a perceived state of religious delusion or self-claimed inspiration</note>, and has thus far spread and spewed forth—and indeed with impunity—wondrous blasphemies concerning God and creation, namely that He was composed of Mercury and Saltpeter <note>An alchemical reference common in Böhme’s philosophy</note>, and other such things. He even published a fanatical little book, which emerged printed from the

<column-break/>

<page-num>15</page-num>

> Görlitz press <note>original: "officina Gorlicensi"</note>, produced with reckless daring without the consent of the Magistrate or the Ministry, concerning the "Way to Christ," "Self-Denial," and "True Repentance." In this book, he recalled most of the old and condemned heresies from hell <note>original: "ab Orco"</note>. However, when he had disseminated this poison of his far and wide among us as well as in Silesia through two noblemen, and the ministers of Görlitz and Liegnitz <note>original: "Lignicenses"</note> campaigned heavily against this impostor: on March 26, he was finally ordered by our Council to depart from the City. But it is to be feared that this decree of the Council may be without execution or, as the saying goes, a bell without a clapper <note>original: "campana sine clepulo"; a metaphor for a law that is not enforced</note>. So coldly is the glory of God vindicated and defended, nor is that saying observed: "Them that honor me I will honor" (1 Samuel 2:30).

Evidently, this passage was written down shortly after the judicial decision. The author's fear that the punishment might not be carried out, as well as his anger and vehemence regarding the presumption of the layman <note>Böhme was a layman shoemaker, which angered the ordained clergy</note>, leaves little doubt as to the identity of the writer.

b) Görlitz Yearbooks, continued until 1696, page 611. L. III. 89. in the library of the Society of Sciences in Görlitz. The first author is the brother of the father of "von Kießling’s wife;" Kießling died 1599. See page 378.

c) Görlitz Annals, continued until 1717 (anonymous). Manuscript 93 a. J. 137 h.

d) Görlitz Annual History from the Origin of the City — 1710, Part 1, page 360 ff. Library of the Society, Manuscript L. III. 150. 4to. (by Mylius).

e) Zirschke, Görlitz Annual History. L. III. 79. Manuscript of the Library of the Society. 4to. Pages 418–422. Pages 456–458.

f) Baumeister's Görlitz Chronicle 1741.

g) An anonymous chronicle, in the possession of City Councilor G. Köhler in Görlitz, perhaps by Scholze, continued into the 18th century. It is the most detailed regarding Böhme.

h) A chronicle in manuscript by the Funke Brothers. Milich Library, Manuscript Vol. II. Folio 245–251, 251–258.

i) Grosser, Lusatian Remarkable Facts II. 29–37.
Additionally to be compared:

k) Lucae, Silesian Chronicle, page 510.

l) Lichtstern, Silesian Princely Chronicle, page 110.

m) Heisel, Silesian Church History, page 329.

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of historical sources and biographies concerning Jakob Böhme, assessing their reliability and the controversies they sparked among contemporary theologians. It specifically addresses the tension between local Görlitz records and the polemical writings of the orthodox Lutheran establishment.</meta>

n) Note from the manuscript *The Hessian Gospel of Silesia at Liegnitz, p. 39* <note>original title: "Evangelium hessianum Silesiae zu Liegnitz"</note>: Although many have doubted that the mentioned booklets were actually his own work, it was reported by a very old citizen from Görlitz himself that others, and particularly one of the Mayors there, had penned the wildest parts <note>original: "die tolsten gefiedert" - literally 'feathered the craziest ones', likely meaning they wrote the most extreme sections with a quill</note> and subsequently provided them to this simple man.

Due to the consistency and documentary nature of their sources, the reports in these chronicles are credible.

The research materials are divided into two groups. The first is based primarily on the biography by Franckenberg and the other aforementioned essays (Nos. 3, 4, 6, 7), which were printed in the 1715 Dutch edition of the works and can only partially claim critical value. The other group relies on mostly local sources—some of which are now lost—or on scientific investigation of the reports above. Of the first type are:

1) *Gabriel Funcke, A Short Sketch of the Life Stories of all Clergy at the Church Service in Görlitz, both before and after the Reformation, at the Main Church of St. Peter and St. Paul, Görlitz 1711.* On the occasion of the Senior Minister Elias Dietrich, the author also speaks about Böhme on pages 40–84. The clergy took great offense at Funcke, a school colleague at the Görlitz city gymnasium, for mentioning the heretical theosophist <note>Theosophy: a philosophical system seeking direct knowledge of God through internal intuition</note>, who appeared in the book like a Saul *among the prophets* <note>original Latin: "inter prophetas" — a biblical reference to an unexpected or unwelcome presence among the godly</note>. However, in response to their complaints, the accused obtained a decision from the theological faculty in Leipzig, which, because it spoke in his favor, he had printed on two octavo sheets in 1711 ¹).

2) Closely following this author is the excerpt given in Kloß’s *Remarkable Facts of the City of Seidenberg*, which has little value aside from some local notes.

---

¹) *New Lusatian Magazine* 1822, p. 52 ff. Essay by Gottfried Schulz: “In the year 1692, Master Christian Funcke, Rector (Gabriel Funcke’s father), was denounced and reviled by the mob as a 'Boehmist,' though unfairly and out of malice, because he diligently applied himself to the seriousness of Christianity.” Knauth Vol. II. Fol. 1896 § 19. L. III. 98.

<column-break/>

3) “*History of Jakob Böhme, the Shoemaker of Görlitz, born in Old Seidenberg; or: A Description of the most eminent events that occurred involving Jakob Böhme and his writings. Presented in his own words by a lover of the aforementioned Böhmean writings. Hamburg. Published by Jakob Böhme’s good friend. In the year 1698.*” A collection of passages from Böhme's writings that provide news about his external circumstances and spiritual development; though compiled with care, it occasionally casts a false light due to the arbitrary nature of the arrangement.

4) Two writings by the orthodox Chief Court Preacher Calov, who sought to tarnish Böhme's memory and persecuted his followers: “*Dissertation on the Life of Jakob Böhme. Wittenberg 1707*” and “*Against Böhme, in which it is taught what is to be held regarding the Sect of Jakob Böhme, the Görlitz shoemaker, and whether anyone devoted to the Unaltered Augsburg Confession etc. can join or remain in the same; by Doctor Abraham Calov, Primary Professor of Theology, Faculty of Theology and Senior Member of the Ecclesiastical Consistory, Pastor at Wittenberg and Superintendent General. — 2nd Edition, Leipzig, Christian Wohlfarth, Anno 1690.*” <note>The Augsburg Confession is the primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church.</note> — The latter writing is original because of its attempt to completely deny Böhme's conversation with the Consistory in Dresden. Both treatises are dictated by the most vehement religious zeal, whereby weaknesses are not avoided, even if the points in question might occasionally offer the strict Lutheran perspective a genuine basis for a charge of heresy.

Following this piece of work are the writings:

a) *Regarding Jakob Böhme of Old Seidenberg, to be debated at the Soest Athenaeum by the Presiding Chair Dr. Justus Wesselus Rumpaeus, Rector of the Archigymnasium, and the respondent Johannes Christoph Beurhusius of Dortmund, on March 12th, 1714. Soest. Printed by Hermann.* (Here, the chamois dresser and mystic Kotter from Sprottau is called a trifler <note>original Latin: "Anylus" — likely a derogatory term implying a teller of "old wives' tales"</note>). This writing is at most useful for learning the names of some otherwise unknown followers of Böhme.

b) “*Scriptural Report on Jakob Böhme, who, although presented by an anonymous author as one highly enlightened, cannot be called such when illuminated by the light of Holy Scripture; shown upon request by...*”

<summary>This page catalogs and critiques early 18th-century sources on Jakob Böhme, ranging from local chronicles and biographical sketches to hostile polemics by orthodox Lutheran theologians like Abraham Calov.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Gabriel Funcke, Abraham Calov, Görlitz, Seidenberg, Lutheran orthodoxy, heresy, bibliography, historiography, theosophy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the critical bibliography of Jakob Böhme, evaluating 18th and 19th-century biographical accounts. The author maintains a skeptical tone toward "romanticized" or "legendary" versions of Böhme's life, favoring rigorous historical documentation over mystical embellishment.</meta>

Thomas Lüdeken, "Servant of the Divine Word at the Holy Spirit [Church] in Salzwedel. Magdeburg 1715." <note>Original Latin title: "Dienern des göttl. Wortes ad Spir. S."</note> — This work is rendered tasteless by a lengthy justification as to why the author is even authorized to provide such evidence.

5) Equally worthless, though for different reasons, and wafting with a mystical scent of wonder, is:

> The life of a certain Jacob Böhme, who although he was a very lowly man, yet wrote with the most wonderful, deep knowledge of natural and divine things—more than anyone has been known to do since the time of the Apostles—and yet he never read them or learned them from any other man, as may be seen in what follows. Wherein is contained a perfect catalogue of his works. London. Rich. Whitaker. 1644. Likewise: *Memoirs of the Life, Death and Wonderful Writings of J. Böhme*, by Francis Okely, Lackington 1780. <note>The original source lists these titles in English.</note>

6) *Dissertation on the Fanatics of Silesia* <note>original: "Dissertatio de fanaticis Silesiorum"</note>, hardly worth mentioning. (The author is Wernsdorf.)

Much like the writings cited so far, several from more recent times serve only to burden the researcher of Böhme's life with unnecessary reading ballast.

7) *Jakob Böhme, a Biographical Attempt.* Pirna, at the Arnoldische Bookstore, 1801. Written with great breadth in the comfortable writer’s mood typical of that period, it recounts the well-stuffed, traditional legends from Franckenberg <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652) was Böhme’s first biographer and a fellow mystic; his accounts are often seen as more hagiographic than historical.</note> and his associates. Regarding the author's intent—to represent Böhme’s inner life and development through excerpts of his writings—it cannot claim the prize for thoroughness. In this anthology, the underlying system does not shine through; mere excerpts fail to achieve clarity of presentation. The philosophical direction is only hinted at, the theological aspect is neglected, and the "shadow sides" <note>difficult or controversial aspects</note> are avoided, as is common with collections of excerpts. The final judgment values Böhme’s historical position neither deeply nor correctly. Indeed, the author himself admits he did not succeed in gaining a clear view of Böhme's conception of nature, which was supposedly his main goal.

8) An essay by Dr. Bachmann in Belp near Bern, in the *Studies* by Daub and Kreuser, 1809, Vol. 1, which

---

<page-num>19</page-num>

can be found again as an excerpt in the *Morgenblatt* 1809, Vol. 1, Nos. 135 & 136. The author "knows" that Altseidenberg is a market town; despite this, Böhme attends the village school there; his father was supposedly a shepherd, and so on. Bold is the attempt to describe how Böhme achieved his enlightenment. To this author, *Aurora* <note>Böhme's first book, "Aurora: The Rooting of the Day," written in 1612.</note> is the most excellent of Böhme's writings because it breathes with fresh, initial enthusiasm.

9) An essay in the journal *The Biographer* <note>original: "Der Biograph"</note>, Halle 1802, Part 1, pp. 107–132, by the famous Eberhard. He draws attention to Böhme’s connection with the Kabbalah <note>Jewish mystical tradition</note> and alchemy; however, when he tries to explain Böhme’s enlightenment through the reflection of a shiny tin vessel by means of the planet Jupiter, he entirely misunderstands the nature of unmediated mysticism. <note>Legend says Böhme was plunged into a visionary state after seeing a beam of sunlight reflected in a pewter (tin) dish.</note>

10) *Jakob Böhme, a Biographical Memorial Stone* by Friedrich de la Motte Fouqué (the author of *Undine*), Greiz 1831. Popularization seems to have been the purpose of this Romantic author, who indulged more in a poetic-mysterious sentiment than in the drive for historical truth. He turns Franckenberg's narrative into an embellished, blurred legend; instead of grasping the historical significance of the theosophist, he shows only a touching softness and pity for a wounded soul. If he intended to encourage the study of Böhme’s life, the first fruit of such effort would be to overturn this "Memorial Stone" as being novelistic fiction. A correct evaluation of this work has already been provided by G. Köhler in the *New Lusatian Magazine* <note>original: "N. Laus. Magazin"</note>, 1830/31, p. 537.

11) *Jakob Böhme's Life and Teaching*, presented by Dr. Wilh. Ludwig Wullen. Stuttgart 1836. Extremely deficient, even if one were to stand on the ground of Franckenberg’s style of history-writing. The author is unaware of the Dresden proceedings <note>referring to the 1624 examination of Böhme’s orthodoxy in Dresden</note>; there is no mention of historical connections with other theosophists; the Pietist element is exaggerated; hollow phrases take the place of an actual study of Böhme’s works. Surely, the author's hope is misplaced—his wish to gain a favorable verdict for his efforts and to have contributed toward securing Böhme an honorable place among the deep thinkers of the German fatherland.

<sig>2*</sig>

<summary>This section critiques several 18th and 19th-century biographies of Jakob Böhme, dismissing most as romanticized, poorly researched, or overly reliant on legend. The author specifically calls out Romantic writers like Fouqué for prioritizing poetic sentiment over historical accuracy.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, biography, historiography, mysticism, alchemy, Friedrich de la Motte Fouqué, Aurora, Abraham von Franckenberg, German Romanticism</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the bibliography of historical sources regarding Jakob Böhme, transitioning from 18th-century accounts to the more rigorous 19th-century critical studies. It establishes the scholarly foundation for the biography that follows in the next section.</meta>

The following works are based on archival sources and critical research:

1) The manuscript writings of Christian Knauthe, a preacher at Friedersdorf near Görlitz in the middle of the last century, located in the library of the Society of Sciences in Görlitz. Knauthe collected material on various parts of Lusatian history with great care; this material is made even more important because many of the chronicles he used have since been lost, and his painstaking diligence leaves no doubt as to the integrity of his records. He also possessed manuscripts by Frankenberg, von Tschesch, and Reuner—all followers of Böhme—but these were destroyed during the fire in Görlitz in 1754. Significant for our present subject are: a) *History of Schwenkfeldianism in Upper Lusatia* <note>original: "Historia Schwenkfeldianismi in Lusatia Superiore"</note>, b) *History of Crypto-Calvinism in Upper Lusatia* <note>original: "Historia Crypto-Calvinismi in Lus. Sup."</note>, c) *Böhme Library* <note>original: "Bibliotheca Boehmistica"</note> Vol. II. Book III. 98, d) *Genealogy of the Kober Family in Görlitz* Book III. 55. No. 6.

2) With equally honest research, the information in the manuscript chronicle of Superintendent Magister Janke was compiled from local sources. He also provided the notes for the essay by Johann Gottfried Schulz in the *New Lusatian Magazine* 1822, p. 52 ff., which deals with the grave, burial, and followers of Böhme.

3) The first solid foundation for a critical treatment of Böhme’s biography was finally formed by the extremely rich and careful essay by Gustav Köhler in the *Görlitz Guide* of 1837, 6th year, No. 43–48, supported by local and archival sources.

4) The results of this effort, augmented by valuable additions based on Böhme’s own statements, were compiled by Dr. Theodor Neumann in his *History of Görlitz*, p. 366 ff.

Among the larger historical works that mention Böhme, Gottfried Arnold’s *History of the Church and Heretics* gives the most diligent and, through its citation of sources, most complete biography; however, it understandably relies on the old chroniclers. It also cites only those points of Böhme’s doctrine which illuminate his relationship to Lutheran Orthodoxy and mysticism, as well as his seemingly paradoxical views. He was followed by Brucker in his *Critical History of Philosophy* <note>original: "hist. phil. crit."</note> IV. I. Book IV. Chapter III. § 20. pp. 695–706,

<page-num>21</page-num>

Kortholdt, and Uhsen. Since then, the "theosophical shoemaker" <note>A common nickname for Böhme, referencing his trade and his spiritual philosophy.</note> has been mentioned in almost all histories of the Church, mysticism, and philosophy that aim for detail—even in works such as K. A. Menzel’s *History of the Germans in Modern Times*, Vol. 7. In recent times, his doctrine has been presented sometimes more precisely, sometimes more concisely by:

Fr. Ast, *History of Philosophy*.
Hegel, *History of Philosophy*, Vol. 3, pp. 296–327.
Schwegler, *History of Philosophy*.
Carriere, *The Philosophical Worldview in the Age of the Reformation*, 1852.
Ritter, *History of Philosophy*, Vol. 10, pp. 100–141.
Noack, *History of Christian Mysticism*.

Anthologies of Böhme’s works were published by Räze and Umbreit. It is a matter of regret that even modern historians, in touching upon Böhme’s external history, have not taken into account the critical research of Köhler and Neumann.

# Part One.
## Jakob Böhme’s Life.
### 1. Jakob Böhme’s Civic Life.

Jakob Böhme was born in 1575 in Altseidenberg, a village near the small town of Seidenberg in Upper Lusatia, directly on the Bohemian border. His dates of birth and baptism can no longer be determined because the parish registers of Seidenberg only begin in the year 1630. He was the son of respected and well-to-do farming folk, from a lineage that—judging by the name (which according to documents can be written with equal justification as Behme, Behem, Bheme, Böhm, Böhmer, Behmer, and Bohem) and even more so by the proximity of the Bohemian border—had its origins in Bohemia. As early as 1416, a Hannes Böhme in Seidenberg is mentioned in the Görlitz city records, who undoubtedly belonged to the theosopher's ancestors ¹).

---
¹) Hannus Behme of Alt-Seidenberg resigns six [units of currency] concerning all his goods on St. Martin's Day for [the benefit of] Ten-Lichtenberg. <note>original: "Hannus Behme von Alden Seidenberg resignat VI. fird. circa omnia bona sua S. Martini pro X. Lichtenbergynne"</note> see *Görlitz Guide* 1837, 6th year, No. 43.

<summary>This page lists the critical 19th-century sources for Böhme's biography and begins the narrative of his life, noting his birth in 1575 to a prosperous farming family near the Bohemian border.</summary>
<keywords>Christian Knauthe, Gustav Köhler, Theodor Neumann, Hegel, Upper Lusatia, Altseidenberg, genealogy, archival research, history of philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general bibliography of sources into the specific biographical details of Jakob Böhme’s early life and family lineage, correcting historical misconceptions about his family's social status.</meta>

Jakob was the fourth child of his father, Jakob, and his mother, Ursula; his siblings were named Ursula, George, Michael, and Martin. The elder Jakob later married a second time, and from this second marriage came three more daughters: Elisabeth, who married a certain Martin Schubert in Bellmannsdorf; Dorothee, who later became the wife of Matthes Brandt in Görlitz; and Maria, who was still a minor at the time of her father’s death in 1619. On February 7, 1607, because of his second wife, the father had reached a settlement with his children regarding their inheritance matters. Jakob Böhme’s grandfather, Ambrosius—called *Bruse* in the Lusatian dialect—had seven children: Hans, Merten, Ambrosius, Anna, Margaretha, Jakob (the father of the Theosophist), and Dorothea. <note>Wegweiser 1837, No. 43. Neumann, *History of Görlitz*, pp. 336 and 381, where Böhme’s family tree is recorded.</note> If Frankenberg wishes to portray Böhme’s family as "poor wretches," <note>original: "armen Schluckern"</note> <note>Vita 2 and following.</note> this is based on a complete error; it is known even of the grandfather, Ambrosius, that he was a respected man in the village, held the office of <term>lay judge<gloss>Gerichtsschöppe: a local official who assisted in legal judgments</gloss></term>, and owned the farmstead situated between the manorial estate and the Hage farm. Ambrosius died in 1563; according to Upper Lusatian custom, the estate passed to the youngest son, Jakob, who bought it from the other heirs for 600 marks on Martinmas 1563. Michael, the Theosophist’s brother, acquired it for the same sum in 1619 after the death of the father, who, like the grandfather, had been a lay judge and additionally served as <term>church warden<gloss>Kirchvater: a lay overseer of church property and affairs</gloss></term> since 1568.

Unfortunately, precise accounts of Jakob Böhme’s childhood and youth are almost entirely lacking, as only the idle fictions of Frankenberg, which do not withstand critical scrutiny (as we already saw in the introduction), attempt to fill the gap of these years. Jakob was sent by his parents to the town school in Seidenberg, where he enjoyed relatively good schooling under the capable Rector Johann Leder from Schmiedeberg and a cantor; at that time, this was an educational advantage over most peers of his background, which he

---

<column-break/>

owed to the early determination that he should enter a trade. His frailty and sickliness from youth onward made him unfit for running a farm, although as the youngest son, he would have been the rightful heir to the paternal estate. It is in this career path, assigned to him early by his parents, as well as in his physical constitution, that one can recognize the origin of that mysticism which later came to full bloom, and the intense drive toward thinking and <term>brooding<gloss>Grübeln: a characteristically German term for deep, often obsessive, spiritual or intellectual speculation</gloss></term>. During that period, schooling was viewed entirely as a branch and tool of theology, and Holy Scripture was required to serve as the foundation for both sacred and secular science by order of the "theological regime" (especially after the conclusion of the Formula of Concord <note>A Lutheran confession of faith intended to resolve doctrinal disputes.</note>, the expulsion of the Wittenberg professors in 1590, and the filling of vacancies by <term>Flacians<gloss>Strict followers of Matthias Flacius who emphasized the radical nature of original sin.</gloss></term>). Even if this environment—combined with a natural inclination toward inner contemplation—had not yet provided the primary occasion for the deep religious development of his soul, the report that he diligently attended church even as a boy and was quiet and God-fearing makes the early adoption of that spiritual direction understandable. <note>Frankenberg 7.</note> Furthermore, according to tradition, his father is said to have adhered to mystical teachings; <note>Köhler in *Wegweiser* from 1837, No. 41.</note> this report becomes highly credible given that, in his position as church warden, he might well have felt prompted to speak much of religious matters within the family as well as the community—almost as if he belonged to the clergy himself—and to prioritize a God-fearing spirit in his home. Frankenberg goes too far when he adds that even as a boy, Böhme hated the "word-squabbling" of the theological schools; in childhood, he would hardly have encountered such disputatious circles, and his judgment of them could neither be mature, nor could a coincidentally "correct" opinion be credited to him as a virtue (which was ultimately the reporter's motive for adding that detail). Nevertheless, it was very natural—

<summary>This page details Jakob Böhme’s family lineage, proving they were respected local officials rather than poor peasants, and suggests that his physical frailty led him toward education and a trade instead of farming.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, genealogy, Seidenberg, lay judge, church warden, mysticism, education, Formula of Concord, Frankenberg, childhood</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of Jakob Böhme, moving from his family background into the formative influences of his childhood education, his physical health, and the start of his professional life as a shoemaker.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
certainly, when the sedentary lifestyle of the school, the continuous preaching of penance and faith by theological teachers, the diligent church attendance, and the deep reading of the Bible gave a gloomy and darkly confused direction of thought to a spirit that was easily excitable due to the frailty¹) of his physical build. This planted within his breast a restless, unfathomably deep-rooted drive toward the visionary pursuit of a goal whose clarity was lost through the dominance of his emotional world and through an altogether deficient school education, which was least suited to forming speculative minds.

His schooling was inadequate compared to the monumental realms of metaphysics he attempted to conquer; what a fourteen-year-old boy, destined for a trade, had learned in the school of a very small town could never suffice to provide him with the terminology and logic necessary for the clarity of a completely displaced philosophy. Had he been equipped with a higher education, he might have been able to take a recognized place in the learned literary world; instead, he remained a theosophist and did not become a philosophical thinker. The most he carried away from his schooling in the way of scholarship were likely a few scraps of Latin, which he later exploited in all directions to become both understood and misunderstood; yet most of those expressions, so often inserted to the reader's astonishment, are of theosophical origin and the fruit of later social circles. This much, however, is obvious: it was not a miracle—as the superstitious chroniclers would have it—that infused the adult theosophist with the art of writing as if by a magic stroke. On the contrary, his father already knew how to handle a pen; and even if the son was far from the literary dexterity of a writer of our century, the large number of his writings and his talkative, expansive style—

---

¹) That he was of a sickly physical constitution is shown by the description of his outward appearance by Frankenberg, as well as the circumstance that he was often ill, as we learn from the period of his authorship <note>original: "Sendbr. 27" — referring to the Epistles/Theosophic Sendbriefe</note>. His visions prove this even more, which, despite all doubting criticism, cannot be denied, even if popular legend has embellished the truth.

<column-break/>

<page-num>25</page-num>
verbose, broad style, which one similarly finds in men of his era such as Schwenckfeldt <note>Caspar Schwenckfeld (1489–1561), a Silesian reformer and spiritualist</note> and Andreä <note>Johann Valentin Andreä (1586–1654), a theologian linked to the Rosicrucian movement</note>, shows that the difficulty of writing by no means outweighed his delight in spiritual creation.

While the hints regarding Böhme’s childhood already reveal the unmistakable starting points of his later development, it is regrettable that, with the exception of the meagerest notes, all reliable and true sources regarding his years of apprenticeship and travel <note>original: "Lehr- und Wanderjahre"</note> leave us in the lurch. This is all the more unfortunate as it is precisely in these years, when every person tends to decisively set the later direction of their spirit and character, that Böhme’s nature took on its first firm imprint. The turn toward <term>theosophy<gloss>a mystical religious philosophy seeking direct knowledge of God and nature</gloss></term>, the mystical view of nature with its ethical coloring, was certainly conditioned by external influence, through social circles or hostility, and through attractive or repulsive forces.

In his fourteenth year (1589), Jakob began his apprenticeship with a shoemaker in Seidenberg, and in 1599, in the twenty-fourth year of his age, he acquired the <term>master's rights<gloss>Meisterrecht: the legal entitlement to practice a trade as a master and take on apprentices</gloss></term> in Görlitz. From this, it can be concluded that he observed the other periods of time still largely maintained among craftsmen to this day; he likely became a journeyman in 1592 and set out on his travels a year or two later, in 1593 or 1594. Frankenberg's report that he came to Görlitz in 1594 agrees with this, insofar as he was no longer in Seidenberg at that time but was already engaged in his travels. However, the biographer’s claim that he attained his master's rights in that same year of 1594 is completely refuted by official sources. At that time, an article of the shoemakers' guild in Görlitz from March 23, 1574, was still valid, stating: "When one has become a master, the same shall marry within half a year; should this not happen, he shall be obliged to win the master's rights anew¹)." As both the Görlitz marriage register and—

This proves that the account by Frankenberg—which contains the improbable claim that a nineteen-year-old young man (in 1594) who belonged to the artisan class and could only have been a journeyman for a short time had acquired both his master’s rights and a wife at the same time—is an error. Furthermore, Bartholomäus Scultetus <note>A famous mayor and astronomer of Görlitz</note> noted in the Citizen's Registry: "Jacob Behmer from Alt-Seidenberg, shoemaker, has acquired his citizenship upon presenting his birth and release papers. <term>S. C.</term> <note>original: "S. C." - likely 'Senatus Consultum,' meaning by decree of the council</note> April 24, 1599." Since no one could become a master without first obtaining citizenship, the agreement of these documents establishes the facts as stated above and confirms the inaccuracy of Frankenberg’s narrative.

For over twenty-five years, until his death, Böhme lived with his wife in a happy and undisturbed marriage blessed with six children. While the Görlitz baptismal records only list five of them²), Böhme had another son named Jakob, who is mentioned in the 61st <term>Epistle</term> <note>original: "Sendbrief"</note>. In early May 1624, he had returned from his <term>journeyman travels</term> <note>The traditional period of travel for a craftsman after completing an apprenticeship</note>, so that there is reason to consider him the

At the time of the son's homecoming, the father was in Dresden and urged his son to wait in Görlitz so that his mother might have some comfort until he could change the situation (as she was being harassed by the mob due to the incitement of the clergy). To be sure, this exhortation could also support the assumption that Jakob was younger—perhaps born in 1604—since the father’s words clearly imply the son wished to set out on his travels once more; it would be striking if a twenty-four-year-old journeyman returning to his hearth should immediately take up his walking staff again. However, since nothing certain is established regarding this latter assumption, the first is to be preferred. Even if the son had already reached that age, given the bleak circumstances of his family, a stay abroad might have seemed more desirable than the family home, which was so hated by the common people. In any case, he went back to his travels; had he already chosen another location to establish a new home, his father’s letters—which offered richer material during that period—would surely have contained advice to the son, of the kind that even noblemen and scholars did not disdain but, on the contrary, overvalued.

The godparents of the children are all of middle-class origin¹); a fact which indicates that during the time of his trade business, Böhme moved almost exclusively within the narrow middle-class circles of his peers. Those names are never mentioned by even a single syllable in his <term>Epistles</term> <note>original: "Sendbriefen"</note> dating from a later time. The protectors, followers, and friends of the theosophist belonged for the most part to the nobility and the scholarly class. His subsequent activity in the "workshops of the spirit" had removed him from the once-familiar everyday circles of the <term>philistinism</term> <note>original: "Spießbürgerthum" - referring to the narrow-minded or conventional middle class</note> of that time, which had sunk quite low, and led him toward new acquaintances who shared his views and aspirations, unhindered by the shackles of a craft-based social standing and a

artisan-like mindset. His old friends withdrew from him as the agitations of the clergy sought to cast the dark light of a heretic and sorcerer upon him—one who was supposedly in league with diabolical powers.

For a long time, Jakob Böhme pursued his trade faithfully and diligently; indeed, within a few years, he had saved enough fortune that by 1610, he was able to purchase a house. Had he obtained the means to acquire the house from his paternal inheritance (his father was still alive then and had many children to provide for) or from his wife's dowry, he surely would have bought it at the time of his marriage in 1599, or at least during the settlement of the inheritance in 1607. If he had possessed the means without using them in this way, while also conducting his trade negligently or not at all, the sum of 375 marks would undoubtedly not have remained after eleven unprofitable years. This was, in fact, the purchase price¹). Furthermore, a

Böhme’s marriage was happy and undisturbed. Although direct reports do not mention it, this can be proven—aside from the gentle character of the theosophist, which found general recognition and is usually a trait of contemplative mystics—by the fact (see Funke, *History of the Lives of Spiritual Persons*, p. 63) that both his sons, Jakob and Tobias, became capable craftsmen. His wife remained faithfully devoted to him in the later, distressing times of his life and quietly and submissively endured all the suffering that her husband, though blameless, had brought upon himself and his family. But Böhme maintained an equally tender devotion for his family, no matter how much the brilliance of his elevation and the recognition of his name shone around him; evidence of this preserved loyalty is found in the later epoch of his life; but from the beginning, a man can only earn a favorable prejudice regarding his love for family—</margin>
