--- Page 24 ---
<meta>The previous page established Jakob Böhme’s purchase of a house in 1610, suggesting he had been a diligent and successful shoemaker despite later claims of his poverty or neglect of his trade. It ended by noting that a significant change was coming in his professional life as he began to focus more on his spiritual calling.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

completely abandoned his trade (in the year 1613 $^1$)). His newly won friends sent him financial support, so that he could devote himself more undisturbed to his spiritual sphere of influence. Nevertheless, the poor thinker often fell into pressing poverty, because those supports undoubtedly reached him only irregularly, and were of little consequence in relation to the needs of a father who still had a large family to feed for twelve years, and had to settle three payments for the purchase of his house, each of 25 marks; just as little could the 470 marks he received for his shoemaker's stall <note>the "Schuhbank" refers to his professional license and workshop space</note> suffice for his maintenance for long. Even if the unity and harmony of his inner state with his external living conditions afforded him more satisfaction than a comfortable existence—which he could have achieved and increased through his trade by neglecting his urge to solve the inner contradiction—his worries nonetheless occasionally squeezed complaints from him that his spiritual energy could not stifle. Thus he writes in 1619 in the **4th Epistle, section 31**: "But for this time I know no other counsel to nourish the earthly body with wife and child; I will therefore apply myself, and set the Heavenly before everything, as much as is possible for me." Therefore, he saw himself forced, from time to time, to take refuge in another branch of earnings, so that he could sometimes complain of being overloaded with too many worldly businesses $^2$); by which he did not only mean the care for the dispatch of his writings, which he took upon himself by request;

<note>
This provides a glimpse into the heart of a man who cherished and cared for his father's relatives even in the later years of his life. In the **4th Epistle to Christian Bernhard, section 29**, he writes: "For I still have so much to do because of my deceased brother's surviving little daughter, that I must run to the village every week."
</note>

---

$^1$) **Epistle 34, 19.** from December 10, 1621: "I have also left my craft lying for that reason, to serve God and my brothers in this calling, and to receive my reward in heaven, although I must have ingratitude from Babel <note>Böhme’s term for the corrupt, institutionalized church</note> and from the Antichrist."

$^2$) A beginning has been made on this (the *Three Principles* <note>Referring to his work *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence*</note>), but I am almost overloaded with too many worldly businesses, otherwise a large part might already be finished. **Epistle 4, at the end.**

<column-break/>

<page-num>31</page-num>

rather, for several years, he carried on a trade in woolen gloves, which he bought from the farmers of the surrounding area and brought to the market in Prague once a year in the autumn $^1$); he also occasionally took refuge in the shoemaker's craft again. It was on one of those business trips that Jakob Böhme watched the entry of the new King of Bohemia, **Frederick V of the Palatinate** (Lusatia also belonged to the Bohemian crown lands) into Prague, and reported on it to Christian Bernhard on the Thursday after St. Martin's Day, November 15, 1619: "Regarding your request concerning the Prague matters, as I was present at the entry of the new King, you will have well heard of the entry to Sagan <note>now Żagań, Poland</note>, that it has happened. He entered from Schlan <note>Slaný</note> up to the Hradčany <note>the castle district of Prague</note> at the castle, and was received with great finery by all three estates, as it has also been with all kings before."

The **Thirty Years' War**, the weight of which pressed heavily upon Bohemia and its neighboring lands right at the beginning, did not spare our theosophist from its evils. Namely, as a result of King Frederick's accession to the throne in Prague and its unfortunate outcome, an army of national plagues rolled onto the **Six Cities of Upper Lusatia**. The Elector **John George I of Saxony**, in alliance with Emperor Ferdinand, had Lusatia reconquered by the Burgrave **Karl Hannibal von Dohna** $^2$), Lord of Wartenberg—who left a bloody monument to his cruelty in Silesia—from the Margrave John George of Jägerndorf, the then General Field Colonel of Frederick V. Bautzen was forced to surrender after a hard siege; it felt all the horrors of a stubborn and unmerciful enemy. Görlitz also had to give in, and received a Saxon-Imperial garrison.

---

$^1$) **Report on the Life and Writings of J. B. No. 5. § 17.**

$^2$) In November 1612 he had been confirmed as the Burgrave of Upper Lusatia, **Mylius’s Görlitz Chronicle p. 370**: "On November 14, the Bohemians elect Frederick, Count Palatine of the Rhine, as the Bohemian King, thus ousting Ferdinand, whom they had crowned in September 1614. This stirred up a great war, so that Bethlen Gabor <note>Prince of Transylvania and leader of anti-Habsburg forces</note> also involved himself in it." **Ibid. p. 392. year 1619.**

<page-num>32</page-num>
ordinance. Hoë von Hoënegg <note>Matthias Hoë von Hoënegg (1580–1645) was a prominent and controversial Lutheran court preacher in Dresden known for his fierce opposition to both Calvinism and Catholicism.</note> preached at that time in the Bautzen Cathedral for his master, the Elector, and the "only true" Lutheran religion, which at the time flirted so shamelessly with Catholicism and its champions, the <term>Church Militant<note>original: "Ecclesia militans"</note></term>. As early as 1619, Emperor Ferdinand had the Prince-Bishop of Warsaw send brutal Polish robber bands into his rebellious lands. These groups, known and feared at the time as Cossacks, consisted of a gathered rabble that exploited the inhabitants with the most inhuman cruelty, swarming through Silesia, Moravia, and Bohemia to restore the authority of the Emperor and the "only saving" Church. For several years, they remained a burden on the eastern part of the Empire, as new bands, lured by the prospect of loot and the Emperor’s word, continually arrived. Jakob Böhme speaks of these devastations in his **Epistle dated November 12, 1622**: "Regarding the Cossacks, I report to you that they are stationed near Leitmeritz in Bohemia, extending to Leippa, and are greatly ruining the land; it is said they are to pass through our area and turn toward Poland, but we have nothing certain. I believe they will remain in Bohemia or Lusatia and not see Poland, for we shall shortly have new reports. The current peace is not permanent, for the sickness is unto death, and has never been greater, as time will reveal." On December 10 of the same year, he writes: "For what great lamentation and ghastly belief, murder, and unheard-of devilry the traveling Cossacks have now driven among our neighbors in Silesia, will perhaps be known to you." — As a result of these military campaigns, plague and hunger came over the unhappy land, and Böhme lay ill for six weeks from a sickness inflicted by the wicked soldiers, as he writes in his **27th Epistle** to Bernhard in the spring of 1622. In February 1622 (see the **23rd Epistle** to Karl von Ender), it happened that due to the insecurity of the times, the forty-eight sheets of manuscript which Böhme had sent to Mr. Michael Ender in Hirschberg at the request of Mr. Johann Rothe were lost. When Böhme was in Dresden in the spring of 1624, he experienced a festival of joy similar to the entry of Frederick of the Palatinate

<column-break/>

<page-num>33</page-num>
into Prague; the Emperor had just concluded peace with Bethlen Gabor <note>Gábor Bethlen was the Prince of Transylvania and a leader of anti-Habsburg forces.</note>. All the world rejoiced, though from the beginning those better informed could expect no long duration for this state of affairs; Böhme says in the **62nd Epistle, section 10**: "Dresden is a city of jubilee here, just as Prague was a short time ago, and things are proceeding magnificently. From Hungary, it is reported here almost as a certainty that peace has been concluded between the Emperor and Bethlen Gabor, but the news reports run quite differently. In the Netherlands, it is also quiet now, although many people are in preparation there, yet one hears nothing new. Eight days ago, Sweden recruited soldiers here, and the English recruitment also continues; time will tell more."

Böhme was of small stature, haggard, and of poor appearance; his naturally weak constitution may have suffered even more from his sedentary lifestyle, intense mental exertion, and nervous overstrain. His temples were prominent, his nose curved, his eyes blue, his beard short and thin, and his voice weak, but with a lovely sound in his speech. Thus Frankenberg <note>Abraham von Franckenberg was Böhme’s first biographer and a close friend.</note> describes the exterior of the man, of whom no further description exists; the portraits that are still found here and there are not true to life; namely:
1) One in folio size (which Superintendent Jancke once owned) with the inscription **Jacob Boehme, Shoemaker of Görlitz, Fanatic** <note>original: "Jacob Boehme, Sutor Gorlicensis, Fanaticus"</note>. Born in Old Seidenberg in the year 1575. Died in Görlitz in the year 1624.
2) One on a full sheet engraved in copper in 1677 in Amsterdam at the behest of a certain Nicolaus Häublin, with much symbolic imagery. The theosophist has his arms crossed over one another; to his right is darkness, to his left light with rays and lightning. Around the bust winds a ribbon with the inscription: **Jacob Boehme, German Philosopher and Central Theosophist** <note>original: "Jacob Boehme, Teutonicus Philosophus et Theosophus centralis"</note>, along with his birth and death years. A copy of this was lithographed in the *Wegweiser* in 1837.
3) One in folio with the inscription **Jakob Böhme, German Theo-Philosopher** and birth and death years; a copy of this exists in octavo.
4) One in octavo with a serious face; it has the

The fundamental trait of Jacob Böhme’s entire character must be described as a tendency toward contemplative mysticism. From this perspective, all his peculiarities, his virtues, his weaknesses, and his seemingly paradoxical

<column-break/>

<page-num>35</page-num>

externalities become understandable. This fundamental trait was profoundly religious; nervous irritability often stretched his state of mind to the extreme. The drive for reflection and brooding was rooted deeply in this religious mood; he sought God everywhere. The innermost core of all religion—the ethical relationship of man—was the secret he tried to wring from heaven. His entire self was absorbed in this; the center, the primal source of the universe, attracted every fiber of his being. From this starting point, his reflections radiated outward; from here, he viewed all decisions that were approved by mature consideration as outpourings of his religious, godly consciousness. This gentleness and inwardness gave his religious character that color—peculiar to contemplative mystics—that was so captivating to the outside world. Because the Godhead was in his heart, and the conviction of his strong spirit alone could give him the experience of his <term>rebirth<note>A central concept in Böhme’s theology, referring to the spiritual transformation of the individual.</note></term>—the divine fact of inner sanctification—it was impossible for him to obtain this from the outside, as the power of his "I" would not suffer the interference of a foreign will into his sanctuary. Thus, he considered the outward forms of the visible church to be only secondary to the salvation of the Christian. Ceremonial service—as soon as one saw true Christianity within it—as well as formulaic faith and word-quibbling, which the orthodox theologians of the time favored and considered the most important part of worship, were an abomination to him. Thus he once exclaimed: “O you antichristian world, what have you done with your ceremonial service, that you have set it in God’s place! The <term>Councils<note>Ecclesiastical assemblies.</note></term> are only aimed at making you a master over silver and gold, and over the souls and consciences of men! It would be better to have no ceremony, but merely the practice of God’s earnest command 1).” “Dear Christendom has been led away from all apostolic orders or virtues into human laws, and Christ’s kingdom has been made into a kingdom of pomp and hypocrisy in baptism and the Last Supper: ceremonies have been added. O, if only the right faith and understanding had been kept 2)!”

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a description of various portraits and medals of Jakob Böhme, transitioning into an analysis of his spiritual character and his personal, internal approach to Christian doctrine.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

The internal comprehension of Christian doctrine was a strict requirement for him, for the sake of which he surrendered all other goods. A drive for inner truth had spurred him into a laborious spiritual struggle and did not let him shrink back—even where the Holy Scriptures, the foundation of his spiritual property which he so highly revered, showed obscurities and contradictions with themselves and with sound reason—from placing them before the judgment seat of his conviction, which had attained evident clarity. This was quite different from those orthodox shepherds and their devout, will-less, and thoughtless flock, who believed they found the soul-saving mystery of Christianity (or rather, of Lutheranism) precisely in contradiction, in senselessness, and in the blind recitation of misunderstood formulas. Here, Böhme rants against the external conception of the forgiveness of sins:

> “Look, you poor soul, you stand and pray: O God, forgive me my sins, let Your anger subside, and accept me also into grace. This is quite right; however, you do not understand how God accepts the poor sinner. You think it is as if you come before your local prince after having forfeited your life, and you plead with him, and he forgives your misdeed out of grace, and so you are indeed clear; but your sins scold you to your face, and your heart accuses you itself that you are still deserving of punishment. See, that is how you also come before God, and thus many hypocrites are born. You think God, in His essence and Spirit, takes your sins away from you. If it happened that way, God would have to move Himself for the sake of every single person calling upon Him and throw their sin away from them. Look: when God forgives your sin, He takes nothing away from you, nor does He descend from heaven into you, for He has been in your soul from eternity. You must only turn around and go through that same path into the Majesty <note>original: "Majestät," referring to the divine presence or the light-world.</note>.” ¹)

This is certainly one of the most beautiful and sublime passages on this problem! — With very sharp criticism, he even approaches the content of Scripture where it conflicts with science; a spiritual height that did not exactly belong to the character

---

¹) *The Threefold Life of Man* <note>original: "Dreif. Leb."</note>, 11, 61 and following.

<column-break/>

<page-num>37</page-num>

of his time, when a <term>Kepler<note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630), the famous astronomer and mathematician.</note></term> found recognition only as a gold-maker <note>A reference to the fact that scientific pioneers often had to frame their work as alchemy or astrology to gain patronage.</note>. Thus, in *Aurora* <note>original: "Morgenröthe," Böhme's first major work.</note> p. 20 (in Schiebler's edition), he says on the occasion of explaining Genesis 1:6, 7, 8:

> “This description shows once again that the dear man Moses was not the author of it, for it is written quite unintelligently and simply, although it nevertheless possesses quite excellent understanding.”

Furthermore, p. 221:

> “The writer Moses writes: God separated the light from the darkness, and called the light Day, and the darkness Night; then from evening and morning the first day came to be. Because the words 'evening' and 'morning' run entirely contrary to philosophy and reason, it is to be held that Moses is not the author of it, for evening and morning did not exist before the time of the sun,” etc. ¹).

The literalists <note>original: "Die Leute des Buchstabens," literally "people of the letter."</note> call him a heretic; the veneration of the Word without independent conviction and a belief in authority are hateful to him. “For heretics are such people who are born of reason, where one only exchanges words and explains words with words, while the mind never experiences what the power and understanding of the word is.” ²) “Even though reason cries out: 'Bring forth the Scriptures and the letters!', the external letter alone is not enough for knowledge; the living letter, which is God's pronounced Word and Essence, must be opened and read within the human being himself.” ³)

Böhme was decried by his enemies, the orthodox Lutherans, as a heretic and atheist; admittedly, he did not have dead symbols in his mouth from morning until night, and therefore the zealots suspected him. Yet he was a Protestant at heart, even if he...

---

¹) This is one of the few places where Böhme calls reason by the name we are accustomed to. Namely, despite this ruthless use of it, reason <note>German: "Vernunft"</note> is to him something different than what we traditionally understand by it; he distinguishes between external and internal reason. The former is nitpicking, profane science, the mother of the <term>vulgar arts<note>original: "banausischen Künste," referring to mechanical or unspiritual labor/knowledge.</note></term>, of dialectics, word-splitting, and the twisting of meaning; the latter, the genuine kind, he calls <term>Understanding<note>German: "Verstand"</note></term>, the higher faculty of the spirit that is open to the Light.
²) *Apology against Tilke* <note>original: "Apol. Tilk."</note> I. 441.
³) *Introduction to Signatura Rerum* <note>original: "Einl. z. Sign. Res."</note> 4.

<summary>This page explores Jakob Böhme's internal, mystical approach to Christianity, highlighting his rejection of literalism and his intellectual independence. Böhme argues that true forgiveness is an internal shift toward God rather than an external legal pardon, and he uses rational critique to question the Mosaic authorship of Genesis based on scientific and philosophical inconsistencies.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jakob Böhme's spiritual philosophy, moving from his critique of empty rituals to his defense of religious tolerance and his uniquely direct, visionary writing style.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

...ruthlessly attacked the shortcomings of Lutheranism<sup>1</sup>). To him, Catholicism is the Antichrist, the great Babel; thus in *The Threefold Life* 12, 18; *The Three Principles* 23, 1; 26, 24 ff. *Aurora* (Day-Spring) p. 85 in Schiebler’s edition; he takes the field against the Calvinists in the book *Of the Holy Supper* 5, 8. — Böhme adhered to the external Lutheran worship, and although he despised ceremonies devoid of inner meaning, he did not reject the form as a symbol of the state of the soul. The mystic, too, felt the need to generate expressions and signs of inner life within the external world. But he always kept the principle of tolerance pure, a principle which mystics have always so boldly established and so courageously defended; only against intolerance itself and against <term>clerical squabbling<note>original: "Pfaffenzank"; refers to the bitter theological disputes between church officials</note></term> did he know no mercy. One of the most beautiful passages, which would still grace an enlightened age, is found in *The Incarnation of Christ* (I., 13, 3):

> “You struggle over religion, and yet there is no struggle at all in religion; there are only various gifts, but only One Spirit speaks. Just as a tree has various branches, and the fruit has various forms and does not look exactly like one another, and just as the earth bears various herbs and flowers, and she, the earth, is the single mother: so it is with those who speak from God’s Spirit; the true church of Christ has no law.”

Just as Jakob Böhme had to endure scorn and mockery from his companions in his youth because he separated himself from them, led a reclusive life, and diligently attended church, so he lived more and more into the inwardness of his emotional world; this "self-listening" makes the inner eye and ear delicate, and in turn makes every fact of the spirit a matter of the heart. Therefore, his nature toward others was mild, modest, and humble—very rare virtues in those days of the most undisguised egoism; for in what else did that ossified <term>Philistinism<note>Here meaning a narrow-minded, materialistic social attitude</note></term> lie then, if not in the limited assertion of one's own "I," without being inspired by a social or theoretical idea for the whole that would set aside individual-

---

<sup>1</sup>) The opinions that now circulate in Germany regarding the cup <note>referring to the Eucharist/Communion</note> and the person of Christ are also children of the Antichrist.

<page-num>39</page-num>

-ity! He respected the convictions of other subjectivities if they were truly internal, just as he himself did not wish to be disturbed in his own. On the other hand, because he knew nothing but his own innermost self, this manifested in a love of truth, immediacy, and <term>naivety<note>In the sense of a pure, childlike directness rather than ignorance</note></term> that shines through his writings in all places and characterizes his view of the outside world. How often the reader finds him in the most zealous dialogue with the Devil, how often he converses with his own poor soul, how trustingly he recounts what occurred in the deepest depths of his soul. “Listen, Lucifer,” he once addresses the evil principle<sup>1</sup>),

> “what is to blame now that you have become a devil? Is it God, as you lie? Oh no, you yourself. The <term>source-spirits<note>original: "Quallgeister"; Böhme's term for the fundamental forces or "qualities" that make up a being's nature</note></term> in your <term>body<note>original: "Corpus"</note></term>, which you yourself are, they have born you such a little son. You cannot say that God ignited the <term>salitter<note>A term Böhme used for the divine constituent matter of the heavenly world</note></term> from which He made you; rather, your source-spirits did it, after you were already a prince and king of God. Therefore, when you say God created you thus, or spat you out of your <term>place<note>original: "Loco"</note></term> without sufficient cause, you are a liar and a murderer, for the whole heavenly host bears witness against you; that you prepared this grim quality for yourself. If it is nothing more, then go before God’s face and answer for yourself, but you see it well enough anyway, and dare not look at it. Dear one, would you not like to have a friendly kiss from the Son of God, so that you might refresh yourself for once? If you are in the right, then just look at Him once; perhaps you will become well.”

This naivety even rises at times to poetry<sup>2</sup>):

> “If all trees were writers, and all branches were pens, and all mountains were books and all waters were ink, still they could not sufficiently describe the misery and wretchedness that Lucifer with his angels has brought into his <term>place<note>original: "Locum"</note></term>.”

It sounds just as naive when he is enraged at the anatomists and "human-flayers"<sup>3</sup>), or at the lawyers and their henchmen<sup>4</sup>)!

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Jakob Böhme, following the discussion of his views on religious tolerance. It now focuses on his personal character, specifically his defense against the persecution of the head pastor in Görlitz, Gregorius Richter.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

His polemics against the attacks of other writers were entirely in this spirit—initially extremely good-natured. He believed that everyone must be as true to their inner self and the outside world as he was himself; with the most touching mildness, he exhorted his opponents to gentleness. But when naked malice confronted him, as in his dispute with the **Primarius** <note>The "Primarius" refers to Gregorius Richter, the chief pastor of Görlitz and Böhme’s primary persecutor.</note>, and when all his conciliatory warnings ¹) were rejected with scorn, then anger also overcame him—a man true to nature. There, he bravely returned the blows received from his opponent and exposed the man's failings.

> “It is a great shame,” he addresses **Primarius Richter**, “that you allow such untruths to be printed to the disgrace of others; one might well find great flasks and glasses of brandy in your own house. That the Lord **Primarius** says I enjoy drinking foreign wines and brandy is something he judges by himself, thinking that what happens to him happens to another. Oh no, we poor people do not have the money to pay for them; we must make do with a drink of beer, such as we can produce. But the Lord **Primarius** must surely be given foreign wines, even if other people must make do with lesser ones; the Spanish wines cause the Lord **Primarius** to occasionally lose himself; one can also see it by the **wine-blossom** <note>original: "Weinblüthe"; a term for the redness or rosacea on the face of a heavy drinker</note> upon his face, that he drinks strong wines much more than I do, for I have no such signs as he has. In one week, he drinks more foreign wines than I drink in an entire year; but I understand quite well **where the hare's leg is broken** <note>original: "wo dem Hasen das Bein gebrochen ist"; an idiom meaning "where the real trouble lies"</note>: He knows that by divine providence I have often been summoned to great lords and nobles, and so he thinks that when we come together, we sit with one another and drink ourselves full, as he is accustomed to doing with his own company.”

Now, when the opponent was defeated and he himself had attained recognition of his worth, he could not deny himself a triumph in his heart, which overflowed with joy; yet this was as far removed from malicious glee as it was from an over-refined, weak,

---

¹) Cf. the dispute with **Tilke**, especially the 19th Epistle to **Koschwitz** and the 60th to **Krause**.

<page-num>41</page-num>

and characterless sentimentality:

> “If the **Primarius** at Görlitz now had something to complain about against me, he might bring it forward here before the Electoral Councilors (so he writes from **Dresden** to **Dr. Kober** in Görlitz), and leave aside his poisonous slanders before the Honorable Council. Here I would stand ready to answer him and set his lies before his face, which he has poisonously poured out over me before the congregation and in the **lampoon** <note>original: "Pasquill"; a satirical or libelous pamphlet</note>.”

There we see him entirely as a child of his time; in the **Apology** against the **Primarius**, his polemic emerges most sharply. Yet his other writings also contain declamations about general corruption, filled with the powerful expressions of a language that now seems obscene. The Revelation of John and several books of the Old Testament had to provide the material; nevertheless, it remains a coarseness of the age that cannot be excused simply as "natural growth." Moral and spiritual barbarism was a general evil and gave the language that crude coloring. The power and genius of a **Luther** and **Hutten** had once given it justification. That justification was now gone, as the **Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritus"</note> had gone to the devil, and only the **stagnation** <note>original: "Phlegma"</note> remained. **Böhme** still constitutes a respectable exception in this regard because, even aside from his moderation and mystical mildness, he only allowed his undisguised emotion to speak, showing nature and naivety, whereas in others only a displayed demoralization came to light.

The modesty and humility, which Böhme never cast off—neither when noblemen and scholars did not disdain to visit the poor shoemaker in his dwelling, nor when the Electoral Court sent him its recognition—nevertheless knew the limits of a strict morality: he was never afraid to confess the truth freely and openly. Distress and persecution might well squeeze sighs from him, but the noble pride of self-awareness never left him. In his poverty, he proved a nobility of soul. Honorably he proved throughout his whole life what he expressed in his defense against **Richter**: that it was not his habit to play the hypocrite to the rich for gifts and favors.

<summary>This page describes Jakob Böhme's spirited defense against the libelous attacks of Pastor Gregorius Richter, highlighting Böhme's transition from mildness to righteous anger. It also explores Böhme's personal integrity, noting that despite his humble status as a shoemaker, he remained steadfast and un-servile toward both his enemies and his noble patrons.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Gregorius Richter, Görlitz, polemics, humility, shoemaker, religious persecution, Dresden, church history, 17th-century language</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Jakob Böhme, following the account of his temperament and his interactions with his primary persecutor, Gregorius Richter. It now examines his character in relation to public life and his prophetic political visions during the early years of the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

Such a nature was bound, by all logic, to feel entirely at home in the bosom of a close, cozy family life; it was there that his soul built itself a temple. However, when he entered the complexity of public life, his eyes were easily dazzled. His views on his relationship with the Saxon court and the outcome of the entire event in Dresden <note>Böhme was summoned to Dresden in 1624 to be examined by a committee of high-ranking theologians and courtiers regarding his orthodoxy.</note> show him to be a man who was perhaps created for some measure of friendship and the genuine, spiritual exchange of narrow circles, but who, among courtiers and world-weary high clergy, took for **cold hard cash** <note>original: "für baare Münze nahm"; an idiom meaning to take something at face value or as literal truth.</note> that which was intended only as a polite formality devoid of truth. Had his eye looked more sharply, he would have seen it as nothing more than pretense and theater.

For a similar reason, his political combinations—insofar as the stormily pressing events of the time prompted them—were occasionally monstrous:
> "I remind you," he writes in the *4th Epistolary Letter, 39*, to Christian Bernhard, "that you should watch whether the time of the great march upon the mountains of Israel in Babylon will soon be at hand, especially because of the **Transylvanian** <note>original: "Siebenbürger"; referring to Bethlen Gabor (1580–1629), the Prince of Transylvania and a key Protestant figure in the Thirty Years' War.</note>, who will obtain help from the Turks and easily reach as far as the Rhine river. Though I reckon the election of a proper German Emperor must still be delayed a little, and meanwhile great war and strife, as well as the destruction of many cities, castles, and mighty lands, shall follow."

On the whole, his intuition proved correct; yet what is said of Bethlen Gabor and the Turks and the election of the Emperor belongs to the realm of the fantastical.

However, when his judgment concerned the sphere in which Böhme fully lived and breathed, he reveals a healthy mind and an impartial stance that avoids fanaticism just as successfully as it avoids indifference. He correctly perceives the impure motives and the unworthiness of the military leaders:
> "All this, for which men now strive and fight, ruining land and people, is but an empty husk without fruit, and belongs to the 'fire-world' <note>In Böhme’s complex cosmology, the "fire-world" represents a state of harshness, judgment, and separation from the "light-world" of divine love.</note> for the sake of separation. There is no true understanding in any party; they all fight only for the name and will of God, yet no party wishes to actually do His will; they intend nothing but their own honor

<column-break/>

<page-num>43</page-num>

> and lust; were they true Christians, they would have no strife." (Epistolary Letters 46, 48.)

Jakob Böhme is a truly German character, like **Eckhart**, **Suso**, **Tauler** <note>The OCR reads "Tauber," but this refers to Johannes Tauler (c. 1300–1361), a famous Dominican mystic.</note>, and above all Luther; faithfully preserving his God in his heart, and faithfully fighting for Him externally. He is one of those dreamy, God-intimate souls who are seized and deeply shaken by the drive to explore the truth in their innermost depths, in their whole being—a drive that gives them no rest or peace until they have fathomed reconciliation through vision and feeling. Sunken into themselves, they behold God, and because God is All, they behold the world in God. Thus they live even here in another world, and proclaim with fearlessness and courage the truth that has been preferentially revealed to them.

### 2 Jakob Böhme's Spiritual Life and Interaction with His Followers.

The visionary, emotional direction of his entire spirit, which he had adopted early on, prevented Jakob Böhme from viewing his own states with an objective, unbiased eye. Physical frailty gave the play of his imagination far too much room; its activity became so energetic and far-reaching that his thoughts, mixed with glowing images of the imagination that seemed feverish to others, even lifted him into superhuman, ecstatic states. In these moments, all his sensory anchor points and everything earthly in his surroundings vanished, and he gave himself over without reservation to that **supersensory sensuality** <note>original: "übersinnliche Sinnlichkeit"; Böhme's experience of spiritual things as if they were tangible or visible.</note>, that spiritual world of appearances, losing himself entirely in the object of his contemplation.

It is neither necessary nor possible to banish the reports of such states through critical skepticism. **Franckenberg** <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652), Böhme’s friend and first biographer.</note> cannot claim the credibility of a legal document, yet his stories retain a historical core sufficient to recognize the peculiarity of Böhme’s states when compared with his own words. Even as an apprentice, Franckenberg recounts—allegedly based on Böhme's own report—that he had a supernatural vision, though in our eyes it bears a quite natural character. A man is said to have approached him while he was in the shop—

<summary>This page analyzes Böhme's character as being ill-suited for the deception of court life but profoundly insightful regarding spiritual matters and the hypocrisy of the Thirty Years' War. It also introduces his mystical, ecstatic states and begins an account of his early supernatural visions as recorded by his biographer Franckenberg.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, mysticism, Thirty Years' War, Bethlen Gabor, political prophecy, Meister Eckhart, Henry Suso, Johannes Tauler, Abraham von Franckenberg, spiritual ecstasy</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed Jakob Böhme's temperament, his inability to navigate the complex social world of the Saxon court, and his prophetic (though sometimes monstrous) political visions during the Thirty Years' War. This page turns back to his youth, recounting a pivotal meeting with a stranger and the beginnings of his spiritual struggle.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

...was alone in the shop, a stranger wanted to buy a pair of shoes from him; only after long hesitation did he agree, because he did not have the authority <note>As an apprentice, Böhme did not have the legal right to set prices or conduct sales without his master present.</note>. Then the stranger moved away a little, called out with a loud voice: "Jakob, come out!" He took him by the hand, looked deeply into his eyes, and spoke in a prophetic tone: "Jakob, you are small, but you will become great and a completely different person and man, so that the world will wonder at you; therefore be pious, fear God and honor His Word, especially love reading the Holy Scripture, in which you have comfort and instruction; for you will have to suffer much distress and poverty and persecution, for God loves you and He is gracious to you <note>1) original: "Frankenberg 9"; referring to Abraham von Frankenberg, Böhme's first biographer.</note>." — The story reports nothing supernatural, but it testifies to the young Böhme's receptivity to take every encouragement toward further reflection and toward the inwardness of the life of the soul as a serious warning. The youth’s modest behavior, his stature, and his facial features—which certainly announced the significance of his future early on—undoubtedly drew the attention of sensitive and educated men, just as the peculiar fire in the eyes of unusual people often reveals the power of the spirit in an indescribable mastery and captivating of those around them.

The report <note>2) original: "Frankenb. 10."</note> is also believable that his master <note>The shoemaker to whom Böhme was apprenticed.</note> chased him out of the house "because he could not tolerate such a household prophet." In all times, it is the fate of unfortunate talent, denied its proper place in the waves of life, to be treated with mockery and persecution by the profane, who do not recognize the trace of genius. Thus, the healthy spirit, not discovered by a guiding, caring hand, was forced to wither and fall into barbaric detours; no rare phenomenon in those times, when faith in the extraordinary had perished through the simple mockery of the uncomprehended, and the people became estranged from any higher conception of life.

---

1) Frankenberg 9.
2) Frankenberg 10.

<page-num>45</page-num>

Böhme’s spiritual development presents three moments, two of which were primarily conditioned by external impressions and influences, while the third, as the valuable core, laboriously fought its way through <term>barbarisms</term> <note>This refers to Böhme's unrefined, non-academic language; as a self-taught shoemaker, he often invented his own vocabulary to describe complex spiritual states.</note>. Those moments are the directions of nature-wisdom, specific <term>Theosophy</term> <note>A system of religious philosophy that seeks direct knowledge of the mysteries of being and the divine through mystical insight.</note>, and mystical <term>Theology</term>, continuations of <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>, <term>Weigel</term> <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588), a German theologian and mystic.</note>, and <term>Schwenckfeldt</term> <note>Caspar Schwenckfeld (1489–1561), a Protestant Reformer and spiritualist.</note>. Their intimate connection through the center of self-immersion forms the character of Böhme’s teaching.

The contemplation of the ethical rift between evil and good tore apart his inner self; the demand for unity drove him to a violent struggle, which he himself touchingly describes as a terrible fight. His "I" had to have unity, and did not rest until the whole world was forced into the One idea of God through the immense but raw power of his spirit; nature and the human soul, as the nearest revelations of contradiction, were the objects of his contemplation. Having arrived at the experience of ethical consciousness simultaneously with the vision of the inner eye, he swims in a sea of mystical bliss:

> "Since I find within myself a powerful opposition, having felt the urge in flesh and blood and the mighty conflict between the seed of the woman and the seed of the serpent <note>A reference to Genesis 3:15, interpreted by Böhme as the internal war between divine grace and fallen human nature.</note>, I have once set myself so hard in the struggle against the serpent's seed and my own corrupt nature—though through God’s assistance—that I intended to overcome and break that same innate evil will and inclination, and to adopt the love of God in Christ, to hide myself in God’s heart from the cruel storm-weather of God's wrath and the devil’s grimness, so that God's Spirit might rule, drive, and lead me. I also purposed to hold myself as dead in my innate form, until God's Spirit gained a form in me, and I grasped Him, so that through and in Him I might lead my life. I also purposed to will nothing, except what I recognized in His light and will. It should be my will and deed, which was indeed not possible for me, and yet stood in a serious resolution, and in a very serious struggle and fight against myself..."

<summary>This page recounts a prophetic encounter during Böhme's youth and his subsequent struggle for spiritual unity, influenced by earlier mystics like Paracelsus and Weigel. It highlights Böhme's internal battle against his "corrupt nature" to find a singular, divine focus.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Abraham von Frankenberg, prophecy, apprenticeship, Theosophy, Paracelsus, Valentin Weigel, Caspar Schwenckfeld, spiritual struggle, mystical experience</keywords>

<meta>The previous page detailed the early life of Jacob Böhme, focusing on a prophetic encounter with a stranger during his apprenticeship that foretold his future spiritual significance and the hardships he would face.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

himself." "And what happened there, no one but God and my soul shall know, for I would rather have risked my life than let go. I wrestled, therefore, with God’s assistance, for a considerable time for the **knight’s wreath** <note>original: "Ritterkränzlein"; a metaphor for the crown of spiritual victory or enlightenment.</note>, which I afterwards attained with the bursting open of the gates of the deep in the **center of nature** <note>original: "Centro der Natur"</note> with great joy, as a wonderful light dawned upon my soul, which was foreign to wild nature. In this light I first recognized what God and man were, and what God had to do with man, which I never understood before, nor ever sought in such a way, but rather as a child who hangs upon its mother and longs for her, so also did my soul long for this light, but without any prior knowledge of what should or would happen to me, but as a simple child."

How his "I" arrived at the contemplation of nature from an ethical drive for inner unity, and how here too a similar unity of spirit became a necessity for him, he describes in the **Aurora** <note>original: "Morgenröthe"; Böhme's first book, titled "The Daybreak in the East."</note> $^2$): "But when this (the contradiction offered by the common view of nature and God's governance) gave me many a hard blow, doubtless from the spirit that had a desire for me, I finally fell into a hard **melancholy** <note>In the 17th century, "melancholy" referred to a profound spiritual and mental crisis or depression.</note> and sadness when I beheld the great deep of the world, also the sun and stars, as well as the clouds, rain, and snow, and considered in my spirit the whole creation of this world, wherein I found in all things evil and good, love and wrath, in the irrational creatures such as wood, stones, earth, and elements, as well as in men and animals; additionally, I considered the small spark of man, what he might be esteemed before God against these great works of heaven and earth. Because I found that in all things there was evil and good, in the elements as well as in the creatures, and that in this world it went as well for the godless as for the pious, also that the barbaric

---

$^1$) **Apologia against Tilke** <note>original: "Apol. Tilk."; Böhme's defense against the accusations of the pastor Balthasar Tilke.</note> 20, 26.
$^2$) Chapter 22, 51.

<column-break/>

<page-num>47</page-num>

peoples possessed the best lands, and that fortune stood by them even more than the pious, I became therefore quite melancholy and deeply troubled, and no Scripture could comfort me, though it was quite well known to me; at which time certainly the devil would not have been idle, who then often blew heathen thoughts into me, which I will here keep silent. But when in such tribulation my spirit (for I understood little or nothing of what it was) earnestly raised itself into God, as with a great storm, and my whole heart and mind, together with all other thoughts and will, enclosed everything therein, without ceasing to wrestle with the love and mercy of God and not to let go. He blessed me then, that is, he enlightened me then with his Holy Spirit, so that I might understand his will and be rid of my sadness, and so the spirit broke through. But as I, in my set zeal, stormed so hard against God and all the gates of hell, as if even more of my powers were present, intending to stake my life upon it—which certainly would not have been in my power without the assistance of the Spirit of God—immediately after several hard storms, my spirit broke through the gates of hell into the **innermost birth of the Godhead** and was there embraced with love, as a bridegroom embraces his dear bride. But what kind of triumphing there was in the spirit, I cannot write or speak; it can be compared to nothing else but where life is born in the midst of death, and it compares to the resurrection from the dead. In this light, my spirit immediately saw through everything, and recognized God in all creatures, even in herbs and grass, who He is and how He is, and what His will is; also, in this light, my will immediately grew with a great urge to describe the **essence of God** $^1$)."

Thus Böhme’s world of thought struggled in fantastic-mystical visions; here and there, dawning light broke through the misty night of the spirit; from time to time it opened itself $^2$), like the flower of a plant:

$^1$) **Aurora** 19, 5 and following.

<summary>This page describes Jacob Böhme’s intense spiritual crisis and subsequent enlightenment, where he "wrestled with God" to understand the problem of evil in nature and the world. He recounts a mystical breakthrough into the "innermost birth of the Godhead," which allowed him to see the divine presence in all of creation.</summary>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>The previous page described Jakob Böhme’s early spiritual awakening, where he felt a sudden "bursting open" of divine light that allowed him to recognize the relationship between God and humanity. This page continues by describing the long gestation period before he wrote his first work and the local legends that arose about his "mystical" powers.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

But before the day broke, twelve years passed: then it fell upon him like a sudden downpour, as he says—what it hits, it hits. So it happened to him that he wrote down whatever he could grasp to bring into the external world, and as a sign of his inwardly breaking light, he named the fruit of his long struggle: *Aurora* <note>original: "Morgenröthe"; Böhme's first work, meaning "The Daybreak in the East."</note>, his first work. It was meant to herald the day for him, but the sun never shone clear and bright for him; clouds and storms obscured his luminous day. Lightning certainly flashed often, but the **Teutonic Philosopher** <note>original: "Teutonicus Philosophus"; a title later given to Böhme to signify him as the first uniquely German philosopher.</note> remained within mysticism and never achieved speculative clarity. What he felt and experienced was reserved for others of his people to elevate into the mature system of thought of a steadily progressing philosophy.

The inner sun still shone for him sometimes and for a considerable time, but not always persistently; when it had hidden itself, he did not even understand his own work. He knows no more fitting way to compare the bliss of this mysticism, these fertile moments, than with the relationship of the **Bridegroom to the Bride** <note>A common mystical metaphor (the "mystical marriage") representing the union between the human soul and God.</note>, which appears even more decisively in Catholic mystics, such as in his pupil **Angelus Silesius** <note>A 17th-century German priest and physician known for his mystical poetry.</note>. Such visions are not the exclusive property of mystics, except that in poets and thinkers they do not appear in such an immediate, unreflected, almost sensory form. Böhme did not live in the Age of Enlightenment. What wonder is it, then, if what was only the fruit of laborious reflection seemed to him an immediate gift from God? Lacking logical precision, he sought to hold his inner vision together in its entirety using only the powerful fetters of the spirit.

The people of his hometown, however, thought differently about these states of ecstasy; slander and fictional additions built upon a grain of historical truth formed a legend. This legend is repeated in almost the same words in nearly all the chronicles that mention Böhme at all:

"Jakob Böhme, the shoemaker of Görlitz, was for many years the most prominent of the **enthusiasts** <note>original: "Enthusiasten"; in the 17th century, this term was often derogatory, implying someone who was "possessed" by a spirit or suffering from religious mania.</note>; he often had his **trances** <note>original: "raptus"</note> and ecstasies, such that he would sit in a corner and write day and night, though otherwise he could neither write nor read, and brought great books home;

<column-break/>

<page-num>49</page-num>

and when these writings of his were sent to several academies for censorship, they were of such a nature that (as the common folk say) the academic **theologians** <note>original: "Theologi"</note> and **professors** <note>original: "Professores"</note> could not refute them, or else, as I suspect, because of the abominable blasphemies they found therein, they would not look at them further or deign to answer them." He was not alone in this error (personally), but led others away from the right path onto his own erring way, leading them along (like one blind man leading another). But he did not plant his sect or erroneous teaching into people’s minds through writings alone; he also promoted it through breathing and blowing upon them. For when he found someone who wished to enter his company, he led him to a place alone, lay upon him—yes, hand to hand, foot to foot, mouth to mouth—and breathed his breath into him. Then the **student** <note>original: "Catechumenus"; a person receiving basic religious instruction.</note> or **sectarian novice** <note>original: "Novitius Sektarius"</note> became a partaker of the erroneous spirit through that breath; he fell into ecstasy along with him, sat down, and finally wrote such things day and night without ceasing, the hearing or reading of which a Christian and God-fearing heart should rightly loathe and endure with horror. This **act** <note>original: "Aktus"</note> is now called by a Greek-Latin word **Enthusiasm** <note>original: "Enthusiasmus"</note>, a wafting upon, a breathing-on or breathing-into by the spirit, and those who arrived at the mentioned sect and error in such a manner have been called **Enthusiasts** <note>original: "Enthusiastae"</note>, those breathed upon or blown upon by the spirit.¹)"

The immediacy of his spiritual, productive intuition certainly misled him into exaggerations that very frequently occur among mystics. Mysticism is the conviction of the immanence of God in the human subject; one step further and the mystic is convinced that he shares in God's omniscience, that he sees through the future. Hence the prophecies of Jakob Böhme, which are still held today by his followers among the people of his fatherland, partly in the same way as any other superstition—

be used for interpretations of the future. If they are useless for this purpose, they testify all the more clearly that Böhme was not merely a mechanical tool in the hand of a mysterious external power, but had a most personal share in his creations: for in part, the prophecies bear entirely the stamp of the vague apocalyptic prediction of a coming judgment, a downfall of the world, and of the last things. This was a free, wide field for the prophet, which fit perfectly into Böhme’s system; he harvested it boldly, just as today’s idealists still immerse themselves in a prognosis of the future according to world ages. The **third principle**, the world <note>In Böhme’s system, the "Third Principle" is the visible, material world, which acts as a mirror or a field of tension between the divine light and the dark fire.</note>, will one day separate into the first and second, the evil and the good; each will be left to its own essence—the one to continual torment and longing for the other, the good into the immediacy of salvation.

> “When the time of the outer literal constellation is at an end, the **built tower** <note>original: "gebaute Thurm"; a reference to the Tower of Babel, symbolizing human pride and external religious structures.</note> falls over, as the external man, together with opinion, and breaks everything all at once, except for the single soul, which then stands naked and bare before God ¹).” — “To this I hear a howling and a great cry, that all your servants cry 'Woe' over you, because you distress them; moreover, you have forgotten my noble seed and have not sown it, but rather your wild seed, for your great gluttony and splendor. Behold, I have spat you out against **Babel** <note>Böhme uses "Babel" to represent the "confused" and corrupt institutionalized churches of his day.</note> into the cold of my wrath; there I will press you in the winepress and will set my **lily-branch** in my rose garden, which brings me fruit that my soul desires; from it my **sick Adam** <note>Refers to fallen humanity in need of spiritual healing.</note> shall eat, so that he may become strong and go into his paradise ²).” — “Let it be told to you, you Jews, Turks, and nations, you need wait for no other; there is no other time at hand than the **time of the lilies**, and its sign is the **sign of Elijah** <note>A common theme in 17th-century mysticism, predicting a final age of peace and spiritual restoration before the end of the world.</note>; therefore see to it in what spirit you live, that the fire of wrath does not swallow and consume you. Truly, if it is not soon prevented, the fire will burn

---
¹) *Mysterium Magnum* 36, 56. <note>Böhme’s massive commentary on the Book of Genesis.</note>
²) *The Three Principles of Divine Essence* 20, 43.

<column-break/>

out over Babel, then there is no counsel until the wrath consumes everything that has grown within it ¹).”

**Babel** and the **Antichrist** signify the external church, everything that is not mystically religious, the profane existence, dry framework instead of spiritual content—the Catholic hierarchy as well as Lutheran fanaticism. He predicts their imminent downfall because the present gave so little satisfaction to his sense of the truth of the inner Protestant principle; yet, regarding time and reality, he wisely keeps to vague expressions that can be understood as much symbolically as literally. So in the **5th Epistle** <note>original: "5. Sendbr."; Böhme’s "Sendbriefe" or "Theosophic Epistles" were letters sent to friends and followers.</note>:

> “Also, no one should think that the time of the total breaking of the city of Babel will occur now: there will indeed be a very great crack, which people do not believe now, for the Antichrist is not yet fully revealed, though somewhat. People will also think they have now uprooted him, and after some tribulation great joy will follow, and they will thus make laws and covenants, even with heavy and sharp articles of religion, but mostly for the ascent of their honor and power; and people will think the Holy Spirit speaks from heaven, and that it is now a golden world, but it is stuck full of the wrath of God, and is still a Babel, and the true essence of the right life of Jesus Christ is not yet within it. — Also, the rider on the **pale horse** will yet come and cut down much with his scythe. But meanwhile the **lily** greens in wonder, against which the last Antichrist stirs up persecution, and then his end comes; for the appearance of the Lord smothers him. Then Babel burns in the zeal and wrath of God, and it is wondrous, for a time comes from the Lord that is not from the starry heaven.”

Another prophecy of a very similar kind, going into considerable detail and perhaps the most extensive in Böhme’s writings, is found in the **41st Epistle** to **Frankenberg** <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652), a nobleman and mystic who became Böhme’s primary biographer and disciple.</note> from February 23, 1623, as a postscript. — At times, the prophecies descend into the completely incomprehensible: “But we have heard a watchman say: Down with it, the beast with the harlot, which stands on the earth in **Naema’s** <note>Likely referring to Naamah, often associated in mystical traditions with seductive or demonic worldly beauty.</note> stead, has fallen, and the

...winepress of the sevenfold vengeance has been given in seventy-and-seven. The vengeance rises in Lamech <note>Lamech, a descendant of Cain in Genesis, represents the peak of human wrath and earthly corruption in Böhme’s symbolic language.</note>, and goes through seventy-and-seven, and no one can prevent that.” In another place he says: “But when this dawn <note>original: "Morgenröthe"; also the title of Böhme’s first major work, symbolizing the breaking of divine light into the world.</note> shall shine from its rising to its setting, then henceforth there is no more time, but the Sun of the heart of God rises, and R. A. R. A. R. P. is thrust into the cold outside the city, and with him A. M. R. P. ¹)” <note>These letter sequences—R.A.R.A.R.P. and A.M.R.P.—are mystical ciphers Böhme used to represent spiritual forces or judgments in the "Language of Nature."</note> He adds very wisely the remark: “These are hidden words, and are understood alone in the language of nature.” Böhme gives a kind of interpretation of this to Valentin Thirnes in the 28th Epistle:

> “The other interpretation is a figure of the outward kingdom, where there will be great confusion and strife, as the nations stand in conflict; thus the figure indicates the victory beneath it, as it stands in the spiritual figure (the image of a 'midnight crown' serves as the symbolic basis for the comparison), how it will go, which nations will finally triumph, and how meanwhile, in such a sorrowful time, Christ will be revealed and recognized; and in and after such a sorrowful time, the great mysteries will be revealed, so that one will also be able to recognize in nature the great mystery or the hidden God in Trinity, in which knowledge the foreign nations will be converted and become Christians. Also indicated therein is how the sectarian strife in religion will perish in such a revelation, for all doors will be opened, and then the useless chatterers, who now lie like bars before the truth, will be done away with, and all shall recognize Christ, which revelation will be the last; when the sun of life shall shine over all nations, and then the evil beast with the harlot (which is indicated under the character Ra. Ra. RP. AMRP.) goes to its end, as is to be seen in the Apocalypse <note>The biblical Book of Revelation.</note>.”

He shows himself most worthy in his philosophy in the defensive writing to Kaym <note>Balthasar Kaym, an associate of Böhme who held more radical, literal views on the end times.</note>, where he decidedly rejects all temporality of prophecy, especially all fantasies of the Chiliasts <note>Chiliasts (or Millennialists) believed in a literal 1,000-year reign of Christ on Earth.</note>: he says regarding the thousand-year kingdom

---

1) Aurora 26, 110.

<column-break/>

<page-num>53</page-num>

he has no knowledge; he claims the Scripture contradicts itself if one only wishes to interpret it literally; he recognizes indeed that the time is near, but year and day he knows not, and therefore leaves it to God, otherwise he would be found a liar before Him; he cares nothing for prophecies, nor is the world served by them; its salvation lies in the eternal inner Sabbath of the new rebirth.

Yet if Böhme nevertheless involved himself in details, he very often had the misfortune of misunderstanding the "in-breathing" God; the day before his end, he falsely predicted the same for the third day ¹). Yet, due to the vagueness of the form, the prophecy of the death of Primarius Richter <note>Gregor Richter, the Chief Pastor of Görlitz and Böhme's primary persecutor, died shortly after Böhme.</note> came to pass:

> “Because you call me a prophet, albeit with evil intent to my shame, so I shall tell you what the Lord has given me to know: namely, that the time is born when God will demand an account of your strife over the cup of Christ, and punish you for that which you now fear and yet do not repent for; that shall come upon you ²).”

Because the prophecies came from moments of powerful agitation of the soul, they are written in a style that is more important for its poetry than for its entire content; it heralds the tone of Angelus Silesius <note>A famous 17th-century mystic poet.</note> and the Moravian hymnody, but without their dallying and sickliness; gentle and tender, then again deep and sublime, it reminds one of the oriental freshness of the Old Testament poems. Thus there lies a peculiar charm in the words that stand at the end of the treatise *On the Essence and the Signature of All Things*: “For a lily blooms over mountain and valley, at all ends of the earth; he who seeks there, finds. Amen.” <note>The 'Lily' is Böhme’s signature symbol for the coming era of spiritual peace and purity.</note>

The surprise caused by the vision-like inner contemplation was so strong that Böhme, if he did not forget his long struggling and reflection, at least did not think of it in connection with the sudden mystical light. To him, the fruit of study appeared only as divine inspiration, and since he simultaneously understood "study"—after the fashion of the scholars of that time—only as compilation and sophistical...

---

1) Kober's Report § 4.
2) Apology against Richter 25.

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s self-perception as a divinely inspired writer, moving from his prophetic style to his relationship with formal education and his precursors.</meta>

54

...understood quibbling <note>original: "Wortklauben"; refers to the pedantic hair-splitting of academic theologians.</note>, and who lacked the spirit of a free creation, it was quite natural that he strictly distinguished his own spiritual, immediately created insights from "school-wisdom." He presented himself like an underage child who received his unconscious knowledge only from hearing God. He correctly captures this unconscious nature of immediate production—which distinguishes him from other spirits but also bears much of the blame for his lack of clarity and his confusion—in the statement that his knowledge does not make him "blessed" <note>original: "selig"; implying spiritual salvation or happiness.</note>, for he does not "know" it; it is not his own, but God's Spirit knows it within him; God lures him to Himself with it; and if the Spirit departs, he knows nothing¹). — He claimed God had revealed more to him than he sought or understood, and that his science did not rest on delusion or opinions, but in a living contemplation and sensitivity, for which he required no doctor from the schools of this world²). He calls himself a foolish, simple man who did not gain his recognition and high knowledge from art or reason; he never sought great "art" <note>In Böhme's time, "art" often referred to academic learning or technical skill.</note>. "I can write of myself," he says in the 12th Epistle, § 5, "no differently than of a child who knows and understands nothing, and has never learned anything, except only this which the Lord wishes to know in me." However, one can see how much the Spirit of the Lord became identical with his own spirit as a result of immanence <note>The philosophical idea that the divine is present within the material world and the human soul.</note> when he says: "Since it is indeed my work which my spirit drives, I will write it as a memorial in just such a way as I recognize it in my spirit, and then in the way I came to it; and I will set down nothing foreign that I have not experienced myself, so that I myself am not found to be a liar before God³)."

While he often assures us that he did not receive his writings and science from men, we can grant him full belief insofar as his works are reproduced immediately from his own spirit; however, this does not mean he read no foreign authors, or that their opinions cannot be recognized within his teachings.

---

¹) *Apology against Tilke* <note>Balthasar Tilke was a contemporary critic of Böhme.</note> I. 587.
²) ibid. 303, and II. 53.
³) *The Three Principles of Divine Essence* 10, 1.

<column-break/>

55

On the contrary, such foreign views are often found in his work in a very undigested form, where his lack of understanding and his mistakes in grasping Paracelsian theosophy <note>Theosophy here refers to "divine wisdom," specifically the mystical-philosophical system of Paracelsus.</note> shine through glaringly. He himself confesses that he read the writings of many masters and searched through them, hoping to find the "pearls" from the foundation of man; but he could not find that for which his soul longed¹). He also says he understands the opinions of the astrologers, having read a few lines in their writings, and knows well how they describe the course of the sun and stars; he does not despise it, but mostly considers it good and right. In the 12th Epistle, he even gives his own judgment on the views of Paracelsus, Schwenkfeld, Weigel, and Weihrauch; he fights against Stiefel and Meth in his own polemical writings and quotes their opinions word for word. Yet even without this obvious assurance, it would remain beyond doubt that Böhme did not only diligently read the Bible from his early youth—which he quotes often and strikingly, and which forms the core of his worldview—but also eagerly studied Theophrastus Paracelsus, Weigel, and Schwenkfeld, along with several other mystics. Much in his work agrees exactly with their writings, and his entire doctrine is to be seen as the point of union where the naturalistic theosophy of Paracelsus, the Lutheran mysticism of Schwenkfeld, and the decidedly mystical theosophy of Weigel cross and find their resolving unity. But how did Böhme, the cobbler, come across such books?

->Theophrastus Paracelsus Bombast von Hohenheim²)<-

¹) *Aurora* p. 255 in Schiebler’s edition.
²) In any case, he is to be understood as one of the "high masters" whose writings Jakob Böhme ( *Aurora* p. 96 in Schiebler) claims to have read; and indeed, he must have done so long before his period as a writer, since the *Morgenröthe* <note>The Aurora.</note> is his first work, which he began in 1610. Dr. Hans Locher in Zurich ( *Theophrastus Paracelsus Bombast von Hohenheim, the Luther of Medicine*, commemorative publication for the jubilee in Zurich 1851), following the lead of Marx, disputes the authenticity of most of the writings known under Paracelsus' name, and calls only ten genuine, which are purely surgical, alchemical, and natural-historical in content. --- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the intellectual landscape surrounding Jakob Böhme in Görlitz, specifically the influence of Paracelsian thought and the emergence of the Rosicrucian movement.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>

[Paracelsus] had, through his genius, given wide distribution to those views already taught by Agrippa von Nettesheim <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535) was a famous German polymath, physician, and legal scholar known for his works on the occult.</note>. These views were the fruits of a lively investigative drive for truth, but at the same time, they constructed wild, fantastic buildings of thought through strange leaps of logic instead of rigorous science. It was a peculiar weaving together of alchemy, astrology, theology, and <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>original: "Naturweisheit"</note>, at the center of which lived the grand thought of a single idea that guided and embraced the whole. This was intended to make it possible to comprehend the entire universe from a single central point, while in practice, its application to specific details gave rise to the strangest and most absurd doctrines. After the death of its brilliant founder, the school lost its originality and became a rich source of nonsense and superstition. Nonetheless, compared to the religious orthodox, its followers were among the enlightened of that time. As in all of Germany, this school was numerously represented in Görlitz, and the chronicles report that many of the most prominent citizens identified with it—for example, the physicians Kober and Kurz (or Curtius), who were friends of Böhme, and the Mayor Scultetus (or Scholz von Schollenstern), a friend of Tycho Brahe.

The exaggeration of this movement is well-characterized by those famous writings published in 1613 and later in Kassel and Frankfurt am Main under the titles:

> “*Fama Fraternitatis*, or Discovery of the Brotherhood of the Laudable Order of the Rosy Cross, along with the *Confession*”; and: “*Confessio Fraternitatis*, or Acknowledgment of the Laudable Brotherhood of the Highly Honored Rosy Cross, written to the Learned of Europe”; and finally: “Epistle of the Brotherhood of the Laudable Order of the *R.C.* by Julianus de Campis. *O. G. D. C. R. F. E.*” <note>These initials likely stand for a Latin motto such as *Omnia Gloria Deo* (All Glory to God), though their exact meaning was often kept intentionally cryptic by the authors.</note>

---

To deprive those doctrines—which have long been called <term>Theosophy</term> <note>A system of esoteric philosophy seeking direct knowledge of the divine.</note>—of their name entirely by placing their representative in a different light is unlikely to succeed, given the mythical darkness in which his teachings have been shrouded by his followers; just as one can hardly separate Pythagoras from his alleged teachings through historical criticism. Paracelsus will always be thought of in connection with the concept of Theosophy, as countless people revered him as a master, and even if he did not write all those works himself, he certainly provided the occasion for them to be attributed to his name after his death.

<column-break/>

<page-num>57</page-num>

The position of those degenerated theosophists is very aptly described in the second chapter of the *Confessio*:

> “We have no other philosophy than that which is the Head and Summary <note>original Latin: "Caput et Summa"</note>, the foundation and content of all faculties, sciences, and arts; which, if we look at our age <note>original Latin: "Seculum"</note>, encompasses much of theology and medicine, but little of legal wisdom, and simultaneously searches diligently through heaven and earth; or to speak briefly of it, which sufficiently investigates and depicts the individual man, regarding which all scholars who join us and answer our call will find more wonderful secrets among us than they have been able to experience, investigate, believe, or express until now.”

These writings proclaimed, as is well known, a great world reformation that would proceed from a secret brotherhood—consisting first of four, then seven associates—and would cause a wonderfully rapid change in divine and human affairs. They claimed their founder was Christian Rosenkreuz, a Christian sage educated in a monastery who had gathered the treasures of the highest wisdom in the Orient more than two hundred years earlier. Then, grand boasts about the discovered knowledge of the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>The great world or universe.</note> and the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>The "small world," referring to man as a reflection of the universe.</note> (or *mundus minutus*) are served up in the guise of mysterious presentation. Finally, quite adventurous ideas about primitive Christianity, a primitive empire, and a primal language emerge. All the scholars of Europe were invited to investigate the truth of those statements, and indeed, several German and French theologians felt compelled to publicly oppose the writings. They suspected that the proclamation of a single primal religion was an attempt to merge Calvinism with Lutheranism. So many confessors of the new faith were found that the name “Rosicrucian” became the general designation for theosophists in Germany. And yet, the writings were the work of one of the most enlightened theologians of that time, Johann Valentin Andreä of Württemberg, who had previously written works on the same subject with satirical intent, and was now using a grand parody <note>original: "Persifflage"</note> to mock the gullibility and superstition of his contemporaries...

<summary>This page describes the influence of Paracelsian "Theosophy" in Görlitz and the rise of the Rosicrucian movement through the publication of the Fama and Confessio Fraternitatis. It reveals that these influential mystical texts were actually intended as a satirical critique of the era's superstitions by the theologian Johann Valentin Andreä.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Paracelsus, Rosicrucians, Görlitz, Johann Valentin Andreä, Fama Fraternitatis, Macrocosm, Microcosm, mysticism, satire</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Rosicrucian manifestos—the *Fama* and *Confessio*—and their impact on the intellectual and religious climate of Görlitz during Jakob Böhme’s time.</meta>

<header>58</header>

...wanted to bring to light¹). In this, he succeeded admirably with posterity, though his contemporaries were already so deeply caught in their delusion that one half believed in it, while the other half truly considered it the product—albeit a mad one—of a mystic acting in good faith. A multitude of fraudsters traversed the land, vigorously exploiting the gullible on the basis of this supposed Order; Andreas Libau <note>Also known as Libavius (1555–1616), a German physician and chemist known for his critiques of alchemy and the Rosicrucians.</note> (in his well-intentioned reflection on the *Fama Fraternitatis* and *Confessio*) even defended Julianus de Campis, who had composed the "Open Letter of the Brotherhood"; this is proof of how deeply these fantasies had taken root in the hearts of those obscurantists²) <note>original: "Dunkelmännern," a reference to the "Letters of Obscure Men," a famous 16th-century satire against the clergy.</note>.

Without a doubt, Böhme also came across such writings; for in Görlitz, too, there were Rosicrucians. Among them was Gotthard Hellwig, who died as a senior official <term>Primarius</term> in 1662³).

¹) Hoßbach, *Johann Valentin Andreä and His Age*, Berlin, Reimer 1819, has extensively concluded the long-pending question; he completely denies the existence of a society named Rosicrucians before the appearance of the *Fama*. Even in the *Confessio* itself, it says: “But someone might ask regarding the Fraternity, where is the location of said Fraternity, or where might one find the *College* <term>Collegium</term> of the Brothers of the Rose Cross? To this I will give you a brief report and know that the brothers, in their proclamation, give it to be understood—as you yourself can read—that as of yet no incorporated assembly of all Rosicrucians has been established or exists in a certain place; as for the *College* with its officials, time will produce such a thing. I will tell you something, perk up your ears, Pamphilus <note>original: "arrige aures Pamphile," a Latin phrase from the playwright Terence meaning "listen closely."</note>, and consider it well; and if you do not understand it all, nevertheless remain modest <note>original: "modest"</note> and content until its time; then one will no longer speak through proverbs.” — Andreä achieved his purpose; the opponents who publicly entered the battlefield were: Gilbert de Spaignart in the *Theological Sentry’s Horn*; Valentin Grießmann in the *Faithful Eckhardt*; Georg Rostius in the *Book of Heroes of the Rose Cross*; Nicolaus Hunnius’ *Reflection on the New Paracelsian and Weigelian Theologians.*

Year 1649" <note>original: "Mystica tergemino regique hominique Deoque munere dona ferunt sacra ad cunabula reges. Anno 1649."</note>. On the front exterior is an ornamented oval,

<column-break/>

<header>59</header>

If Böhme's healthy and religious sense still preserved him from these excesses of theosophy, it is a sign of his significant spirit, which, under happier life circumstances, could have soared to the height of a philosopher. His medical friends later took great pains to instill a great deal of alchemical junk into him; yet, by a fortunate instinct, he absorbed more from the direction of Weigel, Schwenkfeld, and the <term>Crypto-Calvinists</term> <note>Secret followers of Calvinism in regions where it was officially banned.</note>, who also played no insignificant role in Upper Lusatia¹). In the events that occurred in the history of sectarianism in Lusatia, it is clearly visible that the division of Protestantism into strictly separated dogmatic parties was initially foreign to the feelings of the people. This remained so until a reaction of strict formulas from above taught the people, to their great astonishment, that their faith was heretical, and then fanatized them against the people who did not want to abandon the old, undivided faith. In this state of unconsciousness, even Görlitz could never be considered the main seat of the Schwenkfelders and secret Calvinists²). The former observed divine service and baptism but did not celebrate the Holy Supper. In such isolation they held...

Under the handle is a sign with the inscription: "Christ is my only hope" <note>original: "Unica Spes mea Christus."</note>. — —
