Following the model of the Rosicrucians, societies were even established; in The Hague in 1622, an alchemists' association was founded by Christian Rose; it had as its symbol a golden cross with a rose (see *L. C. Orvius, Preface to Montanus's Instruction in the Hermetic Science*). Arnold strangely assumes that in Württemberg such a society of 30 people existed under Andreä's leadership for the purpose of spreading religiosity. Andreä would then have been mocking himself; moreover, he belonged to an entirely different direction.

<summary>This page explores how Jacob Böhme navigated the "alchemical junk" and Rosicrucian fervor of his time, remaining grounded in the spiritual teachings of figures like Schwenkfeld while the religious authorities in Görlitz grew increasingly dogmatic. <keywords>Jacob Böhme, Rosicrucians, Johann Valentin Andreä, Görlitz, Schwenkfelders, Crypto-Calvinists, Theosophy, Fama Fraternitatis, Gotthard Hellwig, religious dissent, sectarianism</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the influence of Rosicrucian writings in Görlitz and their impact on Jakob Böhme’s intellectual environment. This page shifts focus to the local religious conflicts involving the Schwenkfelders—followers of the reformer Caspar Schwenkfeld—and their struggle against the city’s orthodox Lutheran clergy.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

First among them were the Görlitz families of Schütze, Ender, and Hoffmann. Sebastian Schütze, a learned man and owner of Leopoldshain and Hennersdorf, possessed an irreproachable reputation and a living faith; he had personally made the acquaintance of Schwenkfeld <note>Caspar Schwenkfeld von Ossig (1489–1561), a Silesian reformer who emphasized the "inner word" and spiritual experience over external rituals, leading to conflict with both Catholics and Lutherans.</note>. In 1544, he appointed a shoemaker named Franz Seidel as a preacher in Leopoldshain. When his daughter Ursula died in 1560—she was married to the Council member <note>original: "Rathsverwandten"</note> Hans Hoffmann, the owner of Hennersdorf—the preachers refused to provide her with a funeral escort. On their command, the bell-ringer ceased the tolling of the bells. The Council had to be convened, but they instructed the then Head Pastor <term>Primarius</term> Wirthwein that he should deliver the funeral sermon, as no public acts of heresy had occurred. 

Despite the prohibition against introducing personal attacks into the sermon, he nevertheless began the address by declaring that he was only holding it not because he considered the deceased woman to be anything other than a heretic, but to announce to everyone that henceforth he would refuse to escort any such person who despised the most venerable sacraments and did not hold themselves in conformity with other Christian folk. As a result of the incitement in this sermon, the common people began to point fingers at the Schwenkfelders. The Council threatened to suspend the preacher Misenus, who had not accompanied the funeral procession; however, when two other preachers declared they would share his fate and the people began to grumble, the decision was rescinded. In the meantime, Wirthwein emigrated to Frankfurt on the Oder in 1561. 

Yet the Council also issued an admonition to the families who had a reputation for "Schwenkfeldery" <note>A derogatory term for the followers of Schwenkfeld.</note> to adhere to the customs of the church. In 1567, they requested the preachers to submit a list of suspects and then informed these individuals they must leave the city if they were unwilling to profess their loyalty to the Lutheran Church. Hans Hoffmann had died in 1567; the city's preachers, with the exception of his friend Maschke, refused to give him an escort. Maschke delivered the funeral sermon, and two rural clergymen accompanied him to the grave. The Council's decree made an impression on many Schwenkfelders; the learned citizen Michael Hermann renounced the sect, and even the elderly Bastian Schütze took Holy Communion before his death, which occurred in 1569. However, when Hans Hoffmann's son Georg

<page-num>61</page-num>

died in 1575, the preachers refused once again to go to the grave with the "hidden heretic." This time, the Council’s command could do nothing against the obstinacy of the "orthodox shepherds of souls," except that, in defiance of them, the Council ordered the bells to be rung:

> "The bells belong to the people of Görlitz, and not the priests; not everything will be done as they wish."

Thus, through the firm intervention of the Council on November 15th, Georg at least received an honorable accompaniment from the school choirs, and in the absence of a funeral oration, spiritual hymns were sung for an hour. Michael Ender of Sercha, Hoffmann's brother-in-law, complained about the matter to Emperor Maximilian II <note>Holy Roman Emperor (reigned 1564–1576), known for his relatively tolerant religious policies.</note>, who issued an admonition to the Council stating that the preachers should be rebuked for their mischief and ordered to refrain from it, otherwise His Imperial Majesty would proceed against them with severity. —

---

With this, the open quarrels with the Schwenkfelders ended for the time being, but the sect spread more and more in the countryside among the nobility. As soon as Böhme let his light shine, all the followers of Schwenkfeld flocked to him; such as Karl von Ender, Lord of Leopoldshain, Sercha, and Hennersdorf, and his brother Michael; the Lord of Fürstenau at Zodel, and others. A Schwenkfelder, Haug von Salza, also resided at Zwecka and Lomnitz. The Schwenkfelder disputes were not the only or the last; closely linked to them were the "Cryptocalvinist" <note>A term for Lutherans who were suspected of secretly holding Calvinist views, particularly regarding the nature of the Eucharist.</note> quarrels. These were discussed more frequently and certainly offered less ground for persecution, yet under the guise of an unconscious heresy, they provided a pretext for the fanaticism of orthodox Lutherans to preach the "right doctrine" by force, ultimately stoking bloody battles during the course of the Thirty Years' War. 

In the year 1591, after the death of Elector Christian of Saxony, the so-called Cryptocalvinists and Philippists <note>Followers of Philipp Melanchthon who sought a more moderate path than the "Gnesio-Lutherans."</note>, who did not adhere to the strict wording of Lutheran doctrine, were expelled from Saxony. In place of the old professors came the "Flacians" <note>Strict, militant Lutherans following the teachings of Matthias Flacius Illyricus.</note>—men like Hunnius, Gessner, and Hutter. Suddenly, people began to smell heresy everywhere; this was also the case in Lusatia <note>The region surrounding Görlitz.</note>, where the established old preachers were not initiated into the subtle, argumentative arts of the new zealots. There, people had never looked so jealously upon the purity of doctrine regarding Calvinist teachings, and therefore they had welcomed the Cryptocalvinists who had been banished from Silesia and Saxony.

<summary>This page details the escalating tensions in 16th-century Görlitz between the city's Lutheran clergy and the Schwenkfelder nobility, noting how these religious divisions eventually prepared the ground for the reception of Jakob Böhme’s teachings and the broader conflicts of the Thirty Years' War.</summary>
<keywords>Schwenkfelders, Görlitz, religious persecution, Council of Görlitz, Lutheran Orthodoxy, Cryptocalvinism, Jakob Böhme, Elector Christian of Saxony, Philippists, funeral rites</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>The previous page detailed the persecution of Schwenkfelders in Görlitz, focusing on the funeral of Ursula Hoffmann and the clergy's refusal to provide a Christian burial, which incited public hostility toward the sect.</meta>

...and had granted a place of refuge to sectarians. If a more precisely expressed confession of faith found an audience, it was certainly a mediation between various party views; as evidenced by that praise which, as late as 1620, Micha’s Master, the pastor of Rengersdorf near Görlitz, sent after Mr. Wolfgang von Nostiz to his grave: "Regarding his religion, he was a Christian; he assumed no sectarian name, being baptized as little into Lutheranism as into Zwinglianism and Calvinism, and as little into these as into those. He also had a heartfelt displeasure for the biting and bickering of restless theologians, who, alongside their delusions and unscriptural teachings, provoke discord and strife, confuse the simple-minded, and cause bitterness among them." — Previously, public offense had been taken at a dissenting doctrine only a single time; in 1563, the Council of Görlitz removed the *Primarius* <term>the head pastor or leading clergyman of a city</term> Jauch, because he had once delivered a Calvinist sermon in Dresden to the terror of his listeners. In Bautzen, however, in 1591, in the spirit of the prevailing orthodoxy, Nahrhammer first incited the people against the so-called secret Calvinists; particularly against a certain Nestlein; lampoons <note>original: "Pasquille," satirical pamphlets or public insults</note> appeared, and serious unrest was to be feared. However, the Provincial Governor <term>Landvogt</term> submitted a report in a timely manner to Emperor Rudolph II, who issued the following letter regarding these events:

> "Rudolph the Second, [to] Our Well-Born, Dear, and Faithful [subject], we graciously inform you that a credible report has been brought to us, stating how, in our Margraviate of Upper Lusatia in several places, both in the countryside and in the cities, but especially in our city of Görlitz and at the schools there, the seductive sect of the Calvinists is taking root; particularly at this current time, many of them, having to leave and wander from Meissen and Saxony, are said to seek shelter and residence. — Since we have no intention whatsoever of allowing this offensive and harmful sect to enter our lands. Therefore, this is our final command: that you immediately, alongside the worthy, Our Devout, Dear, and Faithful Gregor Leisentritt, Administrator and Dean of the Cathedral in Bautzen, as Administrator in spiritual matters <note>original: "administratorn in spiritualibus"</note>, investigate with all diligence—not only in Görlitz in the schools and among the citizenry, but also in other places where you have suspicion of such

<column-break/>

<page-num>63</page-num>

> persons—conducting a diligent inquiry in total silence; then, you shall dismiss those same deceivers and sectarians as having been abolished in our stead, and turn them away with the proceedings of our first punishment. Should they, however, not allow themselves to be turned away and warned by your admonition, you are to report to us afterward with a detailed specification of the persons and their arrival, and especially those who give them shelter, and also by what means such erroneous and seductive sects and people devoted to them might be removed from Our lands; just as we have given final command to the aforementioned Cathedral Dean in Bautzen, as Our Administrator in spiritual matters in Upper and Lower Lusatia, to pay close attention to this, and with your help to remove such wandering sectarian preachers, or where they refuse to go, to obediently report the situation to Us for Our further resolution <note>original: "Resolution," an official decision or decree</note>; we graciously do not wish to withhold this from you for your information. It happens according to Our most gracious and final will.
> ->Given at the Royal Castle in Prague. Jan. 15th, Anno 1592. In the 17th year of Our Roman reign, the 20th of the Hungarian, and the 17th of the Bohemian.<-
> ->Rudolph.<-
> ->Adam of Neuhaus<- <note>original: "Adamus de nova domo," a high-ranking Bohemian nobleman and official</note>
> ->Chancellor of the Holy Roman Kingdom of Bohemia.<-
> By the personal command of His Holy Imperial Majesty, Sebold Eingold. — To the well-born, Our dear faithful Hans von Schleinig, Our Counselor and Provincial Governor <term>Landvogt</term> of Our Margraviate of Upper Lusatia."

That meant establishing a Lutheran Inquisition in its finest form <note>original: "in optima forma"</note> using Catholic authorities. The Calvinists in Bautzen were persuaded to leave the city; but Nahrhammer was also exhorted to cease the incitements; however, the fire had now been thrown among the people, who were now embittered against the Calvinists. For this time, the storm of imperial displeasure passed; the Bautzen Council drafted a letter of defense; the Görlitzers sent a delegation, namely Bartholomäus Scultetus <note>A famous polymath, astronomer, and mayor of Görlitz who was a contemporary of Jacob Böhme</note>, the mayor, Dr. Johann Weiss, Elias Dietrich, and Gregor Richter (as a school colleague, the later *Primarius*, who began the quarrels with Böhme) and

<summary>This page describes the escalating religious tensions in Upper Lusatia, specifically the crackdown on Calvinism directed by Emperor Rudolph II in 1592. It highlights the use of Catholic administrative power to enforce Lutheran orthodoxy and introduces figures like Gregor Richter, who would later become Jacob Böhme's primary antagonist.</summary>
<keywords>Calvinism, Lutheranism, Görlitz, Bautzen, Emperor Rudolph II, Upper Lusatia, religious conflict, Bartholomäus Scultetus, Gregor Richter, Jacob Böhme, Landvogt Hans von Schleinig</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion of religious tensions in the region, this page explores the persistent influence of dissenting doctrines like Calvinism and mysticism in Görlitz and Upper Lusatia, setting the stage for Jakob Böhme’s spiritual development.</meta>

Martin Mylius to Bautzen. Since they asserted that they had always stood by the orthodox doctrine, and an investigation of the schools eventually ordered resulted in no unfavorable report being submitted to the Emperor, the city was let go this time.

The Calvinist doctrine, however, still found its adherents; after the death of the Rector of Görlitz, Ludovicus, in 1594, Calvinist manuscripts were found in his estate, and in 1601, Gesner in Wittenberg publicly denounced the preacher Martin Moller in Görlitz as a heretic. A correspondence ensued; they appealed to the decision of the theological faculties, and when in 1602 the judgment came almost unanimously from Wittenberg, Leipzig, Jena, Tübingen, Rostock, and Frankfurt on the Oder that Gesner had rightly accused Mollerus of Calvinism, and that his apologies contained nothing sound or wholesome, Moller preferred to remain silent. His opponents then also rested, as they stood in too distant a relation to Moller (Lusatia belonged at that time still to the Bohemian crown). Finally, in 1620, the people of Upper Lusatia took up arms in good faith for their Calvinist King Frederick <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, known as the "Winter King," whose brief reign over Bohemia helped trigger the Thirty Years' War</note>, without suspecting how dearly they would have to pay for this heresy. —

Finally, mystic enthusiasts in the manner of Stiefel and Meth, as in Thuringia, had also appeared in Upper Lusatia. As early as 1539, an Anabaptist named Johann Ender had sought to spread his doctrine in the vicinity of Görlitz. His eloquence gained him not only success, but his activity was also concealed for a long time, so that it was only late that the Council intervened against these activities and banished several Anabaptists ¹). However, in the open countryside, the mystical doctrine still found resonance and greater distribution through the favor of secrecy. In Böhme's time, a tawer <note>A leather dresser who uses alum and salt to make white leather</note> from Sprottau, Christian Kotter from Langenau, appeared as a mystic; he prophesied from 1616 to 1624 and had to flee to the Moravian Brothers ²).

In 1627, the Imperial Fiscal <term>a legal official representing the Emperor’s financial and judicial interests</term> in Glogau imprisoned him and Menzel; he was thrown into prison, accused of the crime of high treason <note>original: "criminis laesae majestatis"</note>, also treated poorly, finally put in the pillory and banished from the country. In Lusatia he found little support and died in Flanders in 1647. Among the people, his name remained in high regard, and it was said that his accuser died in the same place where he had threatened to hang Kotter." —

<page-num>65</page-num>

Thus it becomes easily explainable how Böhme came to possess mystical and theosophical writings, how he early on became acquainted with the doctrines of Schwenkfeld and Calvin, and how—having been made aware by mystical enthusiasts during that desolate, rigid time which mostly sought its salvation only in dry formulas of faith, in fanaticism, and in narrow-mindedness—he took the path of inwardness; indeed, there is (in Knauthe) the specific report that he took part in Moller's religious house convents <note>These were private religious meetings where laypeople gathered to discuss spiritual matters and prayer, a practice that preceded the Pietist movement</note>, where those who gathered talked about their spiritual states and Christianity without despising public worship. In general, he maintained an association with this preacher, who came to Görlitz in 1600 and caused many new spiritual awakenings through his clever and soulful sermons.

It also becomes explainable how Böhme could quickly find numerous followers, who flocked to him as a new light: alchemists and theosophists, Schwenkfelders, prophetic mystics, and Crypto-Calvinists <term>Lutherans who secretly adhered to Calvinist views, particularly regarding the Eucharist</term> joined in collective opposition, even if it happened only as a sign of their dissatisfaction with the dominant orthodox church. — Böhme owes the distribution of his writings to that descendant of the first Schwenkfelders of Görlitz, Karl von Ender of Leopoldshain...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jacob Böhme’s life in Görlitz, moving from the religious conflicts of the city to his personal emergence as a writer and the reception of his first major work, Aurora.</meta>

his emergence from the darkness of the workshop. The proverb that a prophet is worth nothing in his own homeland has been applied to Böhme, though not quite rightly; even if the common people were incited to fanatical behavior by the orthodox clergy to torment the poor theosophist <note>Böhme's "theosophy" was a mystical philosophy that sought direct knowledge of the divine through nature and spirit</note>—as happened everywhere in those times—this is no reproach to the entire land; for where else would a herald of new doctrines, and in such an indigestible form at that, find such a general and enthusiastic following that even the common folk were swept along—among the lower classes as well as in the withered heights of society?

The book that Böhme had produced after twelve years of struggle <gloss>1)</gloss> was likely intended by its original design to exhaust the entire realm of theosophy, but it is scarcely half completed, and not to the regret of posterity; a crude form stunts the pearls that lie at the bottom of the murky sea. Had not a strange spirit of immediacy and warmth, the earnestness of a strong feeling struggling in vain for clarity, been poured over the style of the whole, the naivety of certain passages would only too much remind one of a puppet show of little angels and demons. Böhme was indeed far from believing in the physical or spatial nature of a kingdom of heaven filled with angelic choirs and a hell of devils with horns and claws; but in the absence of philosophical concepts, and being unaccustomed to holding onto non-sensory ideas, his coarse, energetically sensory nature reached for the vivid images provided to him by his studies, by nature, by the Scriptures, and by the theosophists and alchemists, in order to banish speculative thoughts into his simple layman's head with cyclopean power. The immense effort of his thinking took the help of giant parables, or images that one would have to call childish if the consciousness of divine immanence did not spread a nimbus of solemnity over them. — With continued reading of the writings, the understanding becomes accustomed to the

---

1) *Aurora* 19, 14, 12, 10. Balthasar Walther called *The Dawning of the Morning* by the Latin name *Aurora* <note>original: "Aurora". Balthasar Walther was a physician and traveler who became a key follower and promoter of Böhme's work.</note>. Frankenberg 13.

<column-break/>

strange way of expression, and becomes capable of discovering the deep poetry, the core of a speculation toward which the dawning, dark manner of mysticism works. Böhme himself concealed neither the great lack of clarity in his expression in general, nor the imperfection of the *Aurora* in particular; he cannot advise the reader enough to be cautious when studying his writings: “But you must understand this precisely as it is meant, for when I speak in parables, etc.” “I write only in parables, until the reader might arrive at the right understanding <gloss>1)</gloss>; the great secrets in the *Aurora* are still very much hidden in the mystery <note>original: "Mysterio"</note>, but it was not well possible for reason to grasp it the first time <gloss>2)</gloss>). — The book *The Dawning of the Morning* was only my childlike beginning, thus I wrote in reflection without reason, purely following the vision in an almost magical way <gloss>3)</gloss>). — Therefore, if you do not understand these writings, do not act like Lucifer; do not take up the spirit of pride with mockery and attribute it to the devil, but seek the humble heart of God; that will bring a small mustard seed from the plant of Paradise into your soul, and if you persist in patience, a great tree will grow from it.” — When Böhme took up the pen again after a period of about 5 years, he felt well that his style had changed along with his way of looking at things. In the 12th Epistle <note>original: "Sendbriefe", refers to Böhme's "Theosophic Epistles" or letters</note> he wrote in 1620: “There I attained a better style <note>original: "Stylum"</note> of writing, and also a deeper and more fundamental knowledge; I could bring everything better into the external, as the book of the threefold life shows through the three principles <note>original: "Principien"</note>, and as the divine lover, should his heart be opened, will see.”

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jacob Böhme’s early writing process, specifically addressing his claims that his first work, *Aurora* (The Morning Glow), was intended only for personal use rather than public consumption.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

[Jacob Böhme] composed *The Aurora* only as a personal memorial <note>original: "Memorial"; Böhme used this term to mean a private record or aide-mémoire for his own spiritual progress</note>¹), to possess an anchor point for his further reflection and a comfort in his gloomy hours when he did not understand how he had come to his illumination. When he asserts that he did not compose it with the intention of distribution, these expressions are to be understood as meaning that he intended to bring his writings to the public neither through printing nor through self-promoted copying, yet without harboring the intention to forever bury the creations of his spirit in darkness and never communicate them to the eye of another. This would have already been a contradiction with his inwardly felt calling, which he often liked to represent as the lily that blooms for the midnight lands <note>Böhme frequently used the "Lily" as a symbol of the upcoming spiritual spring or reformation in the "midnight" (northern/dark) lands</note>²); thus, the frequent addresses to the reader show the opposite, not only in the introduction but also in the text of *The Aurora* itself. These addresses cannot even be counted among the insertions of a later revision, as such instances are explicitly highlighted³) by the editors.

---

Written as a memorial, and as a restoration of the dark castle in flesh and blood, moreover with no purpose of making such a work (Missive 10, 2). I intended to keep such writings of mine with me for my lifetime and give them to no man, but it came to pass according to the providence of the Most High that I entrusted something of them to a man, through whom it became public without my prior knowledge; whereupon the first book was taken from me. (Missive 12, 12).

¹) Böhme also composed other writings "for himself as a memorial;" however, this does not mean, as with *The Aurora*, that he did not want them published, but rather marks the contrast to a formal dedication or a response to a submitted inquiry. (*The Three Principles* 24, 1; *The Threefold Life* 4, 3 etc.)

²) *Apology against Tilke* I., 644.

³) *Aurora* 3: "Kind reader, here I will have you faithfully warned, etc.;" in many places: "Here note this well; understand this rightly; here you say, etc." At the end of the introduction he even says: "the first seven chapters deal quite simply and comprehensibly with the essence of God and angels through parables, so that the reader might come from one step to another finally into deep meaning and the true foundation. Many species are often repeated and described ever more deeply for the sake of the reader’s, and also my own, sluggish comprehension." Furthermore 14, 38: "I myself wonder much more than the reader will perhaps wonder."

<page-num>69</page-num>

Karl von Ender, who read *The Aurora* with delight, immediately had it copied and distributed in many copies among his friends¹). He attached himself more closely to Böhme and became his first patron and admirer; in the first missive, theological conversations are mentioned which the two of them held together. By 1613, Ender must have already cultivated Böhme's acquaintance for a long time, since one does not offer a few pieces of writing carelessly written down as a memorial to the first newcomer who happens along, and the nobleman would not have stolen them from the shoemaker. The following missives are also addressed to him: No. 1 from January 18, 1618; No. 2 from October 22, 1619; No. 5 from December 1, 1619; No. 6 from May 4, 1620; No. 37 without a date; No. 54 from the year 1623; No. 52 without a date. At the time when the second missive was written, a theosophical physician from Glogau, Balthasar Walther, is mentioned as a scholar already known to both parties. A remarkable personality, a follower of the Theophrastian school <note>Followers of Paracelsus, also known as Theophrastus von Hohenheim, who combined medicine with alchemy and mysticism</note>, like most physicians of his time, he had such a visionary enthusiasm for the search for truth, for the unveiling of the secrets of nature and man, that he spared neither time nor expense to undertake a difficult and tedious journey to where he hoped to find the richest sources of Kabbalah and Alchemy <note>original: "Alchymismus"</note>, namely to Syria, Arabia, and Egypt. Previously, he had stayed in the Hungarian mines and worked in the laboratories there, then lived at the court of the Prince of Wallachia at Târgoviște, where he wrote a work under the title: *A true description by Balthasar Walther of the deeds of Lord John Michael, Duke and Palatine of Moldavia, Transalpina, and Wallachia, collected by the author himself in his court at Târgoviște* <note>original: "Balthasari Waltheri vera descriptio rerum ab Dno. Jon. Michaele, Mold. Transalp. S. Walachiae Duce et Palatino gestarum in ejusdem aula Terwisana coll. ab ipso autore"</note>²). After returning from the Orient, he stayed for a time in Glogau and then, in an unexpected manner, found in the head of a German shoemaker the insights that Egypt’s parchments had not unrolled for him. For three months he stayed with Böhme and went

<page-num>70, 71</page-num>

...then as director of the chemical laboratory in Dresden (where Hinkelmann, Böhme's host and friend, was his successor). In 1620, he was with Prince August of Anhalt at Pleske (Plötzkau near Bernburg), and later served as a personal physician <note>original: "Leibarzt"</note> at the court of the Count of Gleichen¹). He then went to Lüneburg²), and finally to Paris, where he ended his eventful life³). Walther declared Böhme's book to be the most perfect product of magic. It is only a pity that the side of Böhme's theosophy <note>Theosophy: a system of philosophy or religion that proposes to establish direct, intuitive knowledge of divine things through spiritual ecstasy or special individual relations to the holy</note> which delighted the alchemists is the very same side we would so gladly see absent from it. It is this side that has so often caused people to disregard what is good and useful in his work and to dismiss the theosophist as a half-madman; namely, the astrology and the strange details of "nature-wisdom," the cornerstone of which was that old, absurd pursuit of gold-making. That Dr. Walther, at least, was not of such a sublime spirit as Böhme, and perhaps took very little interest in actual speculation, is suggested by the fact that he placed great importance on ceremonies, fasting, and almost Jewish forms of purification, as well as on external matters of dress and dietary rules; he even criticized Böhme for being so indifferent toward such things⁴). Yet he was a brave recruiter for Böhme's teachings. His zeal once even earned him a well-deserved rebuke from his friend when he revealed mysteries to "unclean ears." Böhme told him "they are not everyone's food; one must not throw the pearl on the path to be trampled underfoot; his writings were not served for a full belly, but for a hungry stomach; he had written them not for idiots, nor for the clever, but for himself and for those to whom God would give the same and understanding." (Missive 7.) From

Böhme's followers in Görlitz itself—aside from Martin Moller, who died in 1606 and had a reverse relationship to him <note>Moller was initially Böhme's mentor/pastor before their relationship soured due to Böhme's heterodox views</note>—were for the most part physicians of the Paracelsian school <note>Followers of Paracelsus, a Swiss physician/alchemist who combined medicine with occult philosophy and chemistry</note>, such as Balthasar Walther. The best friend among those in Görlitz was Dr. Tobias Kober, from a well-known family that went extinct half a century ago³). He was the author of a work titled *Military Observations* <note>original: "Observations Castrenses"</note>. Böhme was in correspondence with him when he undertook the journey to Dresden. Kober had even taken over the guardianship of Böhme's household and cared for the surviving family after his passing. He died of the plague in 1625⁴). To him, as to Balthasar Walther, a significant influence on the theosophist's direction of thought must be attributed. One might even wish that Böhme had not had this association; then perhaps we would be spared many a nonsensical alchemical fantasy and many a detailed absurdity, which emerge more glaringly the longer Böhme enjoyed their conversation. Even Frankenberg says that he [Böhme] had...

<keywords>Balthasar Walther, Tobias Kober, Paracelsianism, alchemy, Görlitz, theosophy, spiritual influence, correspondence</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jakob Böhme’s intellectual circle, specifically his relationship with Tobias Kober and how Böhme integrated "nature-wisdom" and linguistic theories into his theosophical system.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

...used foreign Latin terms especially in his writings, and did not acquire them on his own, but learned them through interaction with the theosophists¹). He often went walking with Kober—as Hegenicht, who generally provides trustworthy reports and can still be called Böhme’s contemporary, recounts—in order to botanize; then, from the outward **signature** and formation, he recognized the inner power, effect, and property, and designated them with the syllables and words of the name that was whispered and granted to him. After this, he would ask for the learned name of the plant, preferably in the Hebrew language; if this name could not be told to him, he requested it in Greek, and finally in Latin²). If the physician had provided a false name, he soon noticed the deception by comparing the property of the name with that of the plant, the **signature** <note>Signature: In Böhme's philosophy, the 'signature of all things' is the belief that the external form of a plant or object reveals its internal spiritual or medicinal essence.</note> with the color, and said that it could not be the right name. Just as men sought a panacea <note>Panacea: a universal remedy for all ills.</note>, an elixir of life, or a philosopher's stone <note>Philosopher’s Stone: a legendary substance in alchemy capable of turning base metals into gold and granting immortality.</note> created from the center of the Deity active in nature to put man in possession of the powers of nature; so too it was believed that a fundamental and primal language <note>original: "Ursprache"</note> could be found, with the help of which every language on earth would be understandable. This is a thought that is neither absurd in itself—since language is the expression of the spirit, of reason, and of sensation, as long as it rests on the fundamental features of an onomatopoeia <note>Onomatopoetik: the formation of words that imitate natural sounds.</note> built upon common analogies among all people and offering a unifying focal point for all other languages, from which they radiate according to the peculiar nature of each nation—nor was it brought into consideration solely by those <term>Paracelsisten</term> <note>Paracelsians: followers of the medical and occult theories of Paracelsus (1493–1541).</note>, since even in the most recent times Wienbarg has made it the subject of a special study. — However, one can see from Hegenicht’s account what false and strange things were reported to him by his learned informants, when he suggests that Böhme felt all the more satisfied by a word

---

¹) Franckenberg, *Life of Böhme*, 20.
²) Hegenicht, 6.
³) *Aurora*, 8, 73.

<page-num>73</page-num>

the more unfamiliar the language from which it was taken was to him and his friends. For Böhme, entirely faithful to the populist German direction that had taken a powerful upswing since the Reformation, remarks¹) that one must only understand the mother tongue, for one has a deeper ground in it than in Hebrew or Latin; even though the learned exalt themselves over it like a proud bride, it matters not, since their art is now at the dregs. — “Now,” he says in another place²),

> “the five senses have their counsel in the power of the whole body, and when the counsel is concluded, the assembled judge speaks it from his center in the middle of the body as a word into the heart, for that is the fountain of all powers, from which it also takes its rising. From whatever quality the word takes its origin, in that same quality it is thrust from itself on the tongue, and the power of discernment goes out from the tongue. — The language of nature is the root or mother of all languages that are in this world, and the whole perfect knowledge of all things stands therein; for as Adam first spoke, he gave names to all creatures according to their qualities and indwelling effects; this is the language of the whole nature, but not everyone knows it, for this is a mystery which has been communicated to me by the grace of God, by the Spirit, who had a desire for me³). —”

This was Böhme’s own view of the <term>Natursprache</term> <note>Language of Nature: Böhme's belief in a mystical primal language where the sound of a word perfectly matches the essence of the thing it names.</note>; a few lines teach us the misconceptions to which he exploited it. It seemed to him that the secret lay not in the sense and sound of the whole word, but in the dissection of individual syllables down to the atom and in the interpretation of the letter—a micrology in which, just as with specialized alchemical wisdom, the understanding escapes every ear other than the finely-tuned ear of the theosophist. Thus in the *Three Principles*, 22, 85:

> “For the word *Heaven* <note>original: "Himmel"</note> has another understanding in the language of nature: The syllable *Him* journeys out from the heart,”

---

¹) *Aurora*, 8, 73. ²) *Aurora*, 8, 73. ³) *Aurora*, 20, 90.

<summary>This page explores Jakob Böhme's concept of the "Language of Nature," where he believed the true essence of plants and objects could be discerned through their physical "signatures" and the sounds of their names. Böhme asserted that this primal language, once used by Adam, was more accessible through the German mother tongue than through learned languages like Latin or Hebrew.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Böhme’s linguistic mysticism, specifically how he believed the very sounds and syllables of words revealed the inner essence of divine and natural things, before transitioning into a directory of his early followers and patrons.</meta>

...as if from the Father’s power, or from the essences of the soul, and thrusts itself upward into the **Holy Ternary** <note>original: "Ternarium Sanctum"; in Böhme's thought, this refers to the divine manifestation or the spiritual "body" of God's kingdom.</note>; there it takes hold with both lips, and carries the name of the angel beneath it, as the syllable *Mel* signifies the humility of the angels, so that they do not lift their hearts flying into the Trinity out of pride, but rather, as Isaiah says in Chapter 6: 2, 3, etc. 

In a similar way, Böhme explains the word "give" <note>original: "gib"</note> in *Aurora* 13, 110: "In the beginning God created heaven and earth" in *Aurora* 18, 48 and following; "Day and night" in *Aurora* 19, 84 and 110; "Water" in *Aurora* 20, 90 and 91; "Spoke" in *Aurora* 21, 18; "Mercury" in *The Three Principles* 1, 13 and so forth.

Another physician and friend of Böhme was Dr. Michael Curz (or Curtius), the author of the Latin funeral poem which begins with the words: *Muses of Nysa, if there are any who do not have hearts of iron* <note>original: "Nissiades Musae si queis non ferrea corda"</note>, and which is usually printed in the editions of Böhme's works. He put much effort into collecting Böhme's papers. Among the Görlitz followers, the following are also known: the physicians Georg Roth and Johann Roth of Baumgarten, father and son¹), who diligently copied Böhme’s writings; furthermore, Martin Möller, a city councilman, and Friedrich Rhenisch. In Zittau, the neighboring **Six-Town** <note>The "Sechsstadt" was an alliance of six prominent towns in Upper Lusatia.</note>, those who esteemed Böhme were Johann Molinus, Johann Hartig, and Matthias Rhenisch; they had a conversation with him when he traveled through to Dresden, during which Melchior Bernt, also from Zittau, accompanied him²).

Among the noblemen of the open countryside, Böhme counted his greatest number of followers; the examples of Hutten and Schwenkfeld <note>Caspar Schwenckfeld was a Silesian Reformer whose followers, the Schwenkfelders, emphasized internal spirituality over external church structures.</note> had acted beneficially upon the knighthood. They turned in part toward literary pursuits, as the trade of the "robber-knight" was now suppressed, and their prestige in the Empire had come to an end along with the old ways of warfare. For the most part, it was Schwenkfelders, such as Karl von Ender, who gathered around the new spokesperson and entered into a lively correspondence with him. Among the Lusatians, mention is also made of Kaspar von Fürstenau, Lord of Arnsdorf, Debschütz, Dittmannsdorf, Gruna,

---
¹) Missive Letter <note>original: "Sendbrief"</note> 63, 12.
²) Knauth's Library of Böhme <note>original: "Biblioth. Boehmist."</note> *Manuscript.*

<column-break/>

Neundorf, Lissa, Zodel, Klingewalde—a "well-qualified cavalier," pious and a chemist¹) <note>In the 17th century, "chemist" or "chymicus" often referred to someone practicing alchemy or spiritual chemistry.</note>—along with his castle administrator Augustin Köppen (Zippe?), with whom Böhme exchanged several letters in the year 1622²).

The Silesian soil was the most fertile for Böhme's teaching, where, following Ender's lead, people worked eagerly for its expansion, especially in the regions of Striegau, Goldberg, Hirschberg, and Schweidnitz. The first Silesian followers were the young noblemen Hans Sigmund and David von Schweinichen; the former of Hohndorf, the latter a famous Silesian poet of religious hymns (died 1667) and governor in Brieg³). Both were "special lovers of the **Fountain of Wisdom**" <note>original: "Fontis Sapientiae"</note>, read his writings diligently, and had a great longing for the little fountain of Christ and noble wisdom, as Böhme says. The 53rd Missive Letter of April 6, 1624, is addressed to Hans Sigmund; Böhme had paid him a visit eight days after Shrove Tuesday and stayed with him for six weeks; in the summer of the same year, he visited his admirer for a second time⁴). Abraham von Sommersfeld and Falkenhain of Wartha, the uncle of Frankenberg⁵), sought the friendship of the shoemaker <note>Referring to Böhme, who was a master shoemaker by trade.</note> through a letter in which he asked him to send his writings. He had read the *Aurora* and sent the used copy to Jakob Böhme with the request to read through it to judge its accuracy. Böhme did so and sent him his work: *The Forty Questions on the Soul of Man*, along with some instructions and hints intended to serve the understanding of his writings. Sommersfeld's nephew, Frankenberg—Böhme's biographer—became acquainted with his writings through his uncle, but met Böhme himself in the winter of 1623⁶), and came together with him repeatedly in the summer of 1624 when Böhme visited the Schweinichens⁷). --- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Jakob Böhme's followers, transitioning from his immediate circle in Görlitz to the influential mystic Abraham von Franckenberg and his associates.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>

Born on June 24, 1593, in Ludwigsdorf near Oels, and praised even by his opponents for his philanthropic spirit, which he proved during the long years of the plague. He possessed extensive scholarship, was a great lover of books, and studied much mathematics, physics, and medicine, but above all, theology. He avoided the intellectual battles of scholars; his soul desired peace, and "**Jesus is my nobility**" <note>original Latin: "Jesus mea nobilitas"</note> was his motto 1). His inner conversion to mysticism and contemplation occurred in the year 1617; through constant vigilance and prayer for the true religion, he was drawn into the quiet Sabbath: he became a partaker of mystical enlightenments. He now diligently studied the Pauline writings, Tauler, Thomas à Kempis, the *German Theology* <note>The *Theologia Germanica*, a 14th-century mystical treatise highly regarded by Luther and Böhme's circle.</note>, Weigel, Arndt, and Schwenckfeldt, as a result of which he was increasingly strengthened in his inner path, and his religion was only further solidified through various temptations and trials 2). From 1642 to 1645, he went to Danzig with Tschesch, where he fell into poverty but received food and lodging from the astronomer Hevelius; then to Amsterdam, where he met the publisher of Böhme’s works, Heinrich Betke (Henrik Betts), and shared his manuscripts with him. In 1650, he returned to Ludwigsdorf and died two years later. As a writer, he appeared under the names Amadeus von Friedleben <note>meaning "Lover of God from the Peace-Life"</note> and **Frederick of the Mountain** <note>original Latin: "Friedericus de Monte"</note>, the **Upright A. V. F.** 3) in faith. However, his way of writing and thinking already leads to an exaggeration of Böhme’s doctrine; wordplay, trifling with magical words, and ambiguities become so prevalent that the good sense is lost even more than in Böhme’s own obscurity; the greater clarity of expression only causes the attractive nature of mysticism to turn into a rigid formula. The spirit of depth is utterly lost in scholarly chatter and scholastic dryness of thought. His numerous writings appeared in Amsterdam between 1647 and 1688 4),

3) i.e., the Upright One; also an anagram of Abraham Von Franckenberg and Amadeus Von Friedleben.
4) Cited above in Arnold.

He was a counselor to the Duke of Brieg and, together with August Fuhrmann, resisted the godlessness of many preachers in the land. Like Walther, he wished to travel to the Orient, but he turned back at Ragusa <note>Modern-day Dubrovnik</note>, went into exile in Danzig, Amsterdam, and Hamburg, eventually became a schoolmaster in Elbing, and died there in 1649 1). He had written: “Princely Christian Reflection on the necessary seizure of means, whereby God’s righteous judgment, gathered wrath, and final punishment upon the current world may be recognized with true regard, and finally, where not averted, yet in some measure diminished.” Amsterdam 1646 (Franckenberg named himself as editor on the title page 1). Prunius, whom Tschesch brought back from Padua, was the translator of Franckenberg’s biography, which was originally written in Latin. Fuhrmann wrote: *Rescue of the True Christian Catholic Evangelical Religion*. Amsterdam 1658. — To the same circle of theosophists—who opposed the ruling orthodoxy in order to spread an inner, heartfelt Christianity, to introduce freedom of thought and tolerance instead of the fanatical persecution of those with different beliefs, and who asserted the love of religion instead of hatred—belonged Hans von Schellenberg, Michael von Ender, Dr. Koschwitz, and Balthasar Tilke (von Tölken) in Striegau. With the latter, Böhme first had a dispute; later, the two drew closer after Jakob Böhme wrote two apologies in response to Tilke’s two attacking letters, the second of which he sent along with a formal letter 2) to Dr. Koschwitz, so that he might induce Tilke to a milder and more favorable judgment, especially since Tilke already held

Böhme had been won over. For this purpose, a conversation was arranged where Böhme’s personality won the heart of his opponent. The dispute had been quite heated; Böhme called Tilke’s attack on the *Morgenröthe* <note>The *Aurora*, Böhme’s first major work, written in 1612.</note> a hostile lampoon that had falsely cited and contested some poorly understood texts. Following Böhme’s first *Apology* <note>A formal written defense of his theological positions.</note>, which did not treat the malicious lampoonist particularly gently, a second polemic by the nobleman followed concerning the incarnation of Christ. In Böhme’s second *Apology*, it is clearly recognizable how deeply he was offended by the attack of the "poisonous lampooner and ignorant zealot"; nevertheless, in a letter to Koschwitz, he adopts a conciliatory tone: he did not wish to shame Tilke and hoped he would "become seeing" <note>original: *sehend werden*; a metaphor for attaining spiritual enlightenment.</note> 1); and for the sake of decency and divine honor, he had replied to him kindly, as he cared more for the children of God than for his own justification. Böhme did indeed ask Dr. Friedrich Krause in the 40th Epistle <note>original: *Sendbriefe*, a collection of Böhme's public letters/correspondence.</note> of February 9, 1623, to persuade Tilke not to treat him so harshly as before; however, the dispute was settled, and a friendly correspondence existed between them.

The news of the brilliant shoemaker also spread to Breslau <note>Modern-day Wrocław, Poland.</note> and beyond; Johann Butowski 2), Bernhard Nitsch 3), and the doctors Güller from Troppau and Brux 4) entered into contact with him after they had been recommended by other pious people and had sent him friendly letters. Other Silesians were: Elias Teichmann, Valentin Thirnes 5), Martin Moser of Goldberg 6)—who spread Böhme’s reputation as far as Lithuania, where an apothecary in the small town of Wildau had read his writings and sent him a letter—Dr. Christian Steinberg 7) and

In Lower Silesia, Christian Bernhard—a young journeyman from the theosophical school <note>The "theosophical school" refers to the informal circle of students and readers of Böhme's mystical philosophy.</note> and toll collector at Sagan—was the most zealous distributor of Böhme’s writings and teachings. It was thanks to him and Balthasar Walther that Böhme took courage once again to pick up the pen, following the forced suspension of his calling by the authorities 2). A large number of Epistles are addressed to him 3). His brother, the assistant headmaster in Sagan, was also welcomed into the circle of theosophists by him. Bernhard facilitated contact with two noblemen, Rudolph von Gersdorff and Friedrich von Kregwiz 4), as well as with Caspar Lindner, a customs official in Beuthen. Rudolph von Gersdorff was among Böhme’s earliest acquaintances; he resided at Weicha in Lower Silesia and enjoyed a wide reputation as a noble, high-minded man. He invited Böhme to visit him; shortly after Easter 1621, Böhme accepted this offer 5), and remained in constant contact with his noble patron from then on. On June 21, 1622, he sent him, via Christian Bernhard, his writings *On Penitence* <note>original: *Pönitenz*</note> or *True Repentance* and *On Holy Prayer* (parts of the *Christosophy*) 6); in 1622, on the same—

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jacob Böhme’s expanding influence in Silesia, moving from his reconciliation with former critics to the formation of dedicated "theosophical circles" and his interactions with various officials and scholars.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>

Along the same paths, several new writings appeared, among which were the *Signature of All Things* <note>original: "Signatura Rerum"</note> and the *Treatise on True Resignation* ^1). In Liegnitz <note>Modern-day Legnica, Poland.</note>, a connection was established with the customs collector Paul Kaym through Karl von Ender. Ender, who as a provincial official for Archduke Max of Styria (the uncle of Emperor Ferdinand II) ^2), had many connections with Silesian officials and noblemen ^3). Kaym is the author of "The Mirror of the Heart," a "Confession of Faith," an explanation of the Song of Solomon and the Revelation of John, and "Of the Cross and Comfort of the Faithful." He died in 1633 and was condemned as a Quaker by the Superintendent of Orlamünde. He asked Böhme for a closer explanation of his views on the end times, to which he received two answers in the two volumes of *Information on the Last Things* <note>original: "Informatoriis Novissimorum"</note> from August 14 and November 18, 1620 ^4).

These theosophical circles, which continued to spin further like a secret society around Böhme's watchword, frequently held meetings with their master. Böhme asserted himself as such despite his humble origins, as can easily be recognized from the veneration shown to him by the nobleman Franckenberg, although he only came to know him in the final period of his life. Conversations and conventicles <note>Private religious gatherings outside the official church.</note> were often held; for instance, with Tilke, Koschwitz, and Staritz in the year 1621 ^5); another time at the beginning of the year 1623 at the prompting of Franckenberg ^6), where they spoke of the divine purpose or will concerning humanity. This inspired Böhme to write his work on *The Election of Grace* <note>original: "Gnadenwahl"</note>. Friedrich Krause was present, whom Böhme, to his regret, could not visit this time in his own home of Goldberg ^7); and

Dr. Staritz, who had already participated in the conversation of 1621.
> "He had a sharp reason and had studied logic well ^1); through this he gave the poor shoemaker <note>Referring to Böhme, who was a shoemaker by trade.</note> much to do, especially since the dispute was carried out over wine, to which he was quite unaccustomed,"
and Staritz sought to shine through his scholarship. There, Böhme's only refuge was to go home quietly, where he had enough leisure to unfold his gifts and diligently bind his thoughts to paper when the deity and his spirit prompted him; for he belonged to those talents who are only able to bring their works to light in silence after mature preparation, and who, in their dealings with the world, constantly provide proof that they were not created for it.

Such an extensive acquaintance, and such a lively correspondence and personal interaction, could not exist without the manifold mutual influence of both parties; the exchange and convergence of opinions was the immediate result. The friends played no small part in Böhme's development; many writings, such as the *Forty Questions on the Human Soul* written at Balthasar Walther's request, and the *Information* written at Paul Kaym's request, owe their existence solely to mutual stimulation; polemics and inquiries sharpened his inner gift of observation. However, the claim that he was merely the trumpet into which others blew lacks any proof. Hegenicht assures us,

---

1) Epistle 15, 6.
> "Regarding our meeting, I was very poorly suited for such disputation, for wine and precious foods obscure the ground of the little pearl <note>A mystical term for the soul's inner light.</note>, especially because I am not used to it, and at home I live quite moderately and soberly; thus Mr. R. was not sufficiently answered."
Likewise at the meeting in the year 1623, Epistle 41, 2:
> "To which Mr. Dr. Staritz was not sufficiently answered on his question this time, because at that time I had not practiced this article in the scholastic manner <note>original: "Schulenart"</note>, and also the banquet <note>original: "Convivium"</note> with such drink, which is not customary for me, obscured the subtle understanding; I was also hindered by their Latin tongues in grasping his ground to encompass it, so that he triumphed with his contained opinion, but without sufficient understanding of the quoted passages of Scripture, and without sufficient basis in the rational conclusions of logic, in which he is indeed excellently well practiced, but in their scholastic manner."

<summary>This page describes Jacob Böhme's growing network of "theosophical circles" and his struggles when debating learned scholars like Dr. Staritz in formal social settings. It emphasizes that while Böhme’s friends and critics stimulated his writing, his mystical insights remained uniquely his own, best expressed in solitary writing rather than academic or wine-filled debate.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Paul Kaym, theosophy, Dr. Staritz, Signatura Rerum, True Resignation, The Election of Grace, Balthasar Walther, mysticism, scholasticism, Silesia, intellectual history</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jakob Böhme’s intellectual circle and the publication of his works, specifically addressing the influence his friends and followers had on his writing style and his reputation.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>

During his lifetime, no one would have doubted that he composed his writings himself¹). One would have to despair over even the smallest word Böhme wrote to suggest that his name was merely a borrowed one: for as soon as one accepts any part of his unique style as authentic, one must believe his solemn assertions that he wished to set down nothing foreign that he had not experienced himself in his own spirit²). To be sure, he did not fully digest everything his spirit experienced; moreover, his writings had to endure many alterations from diligent friends who could not find enough imagery and playfulness within them. From the perspective of a "thirst for wonders" and "adeptism" <note>The pursuit of becoming an "adept," or an expert in secret alchemical and occult knowledge.</note>, the influence of these friends on Böhme was not the most favorable, as was touched upon earlier (p. 72 ff.). One of the strangest proofs of this is the 29th Epistle from January 1, 1622, to Mr. von Schellenberg, where it is quite prettily demonstrated that it is possible for tombstones to weep water if "sympathetic" dead rest beneath them, whose astral element <note>original: "siderisches Element"; referring to the belief that human bodies possessed a celestial or "star-like" force that persisted after death.</note> penetrates the stone. A certain Mr. Valentin Thurnes also receives a strange lesson on July 6, 1622, in the 28th Epistle; he is advised to read the *Water-Stone of the Wise* <note>original: "Wasserstein der Weisen"; a famous theosophical-alchemical text.</note>, a theosophical-alchemical book; there he would find how he could prepare the "philosophical tincture" <note>An alchemical substance believed to transform base metals or heal the body.</note> from any minerals for no more than two guilders. His learned friends misled him more than was healthy for him or his reputation, encouraging him to concern himself with astronomy and alchemy. In response to the whimsical inquiries put to him, even stranger answers came to light³). From these friends come his Latin expressions, which possess little more of Latin than the endings, and which obscure the concept more than they define it; from them comes the endeavor to arrange his categories according to a colorful 

---

¹) *The Three Principles of Divine Essence* <note>original: "Drei Princip."</note> 24, 1.
²) This does not, however, exclude arbitrary censorship by the editors. Stolle also reports in his diary (Schmidt, *Journal for Historical Theology* <note>original: "Zeitschrift für Gesch. Th."</note> Vol. 7, p. 404): concerning Böhme's writings, they were not consistently printed correctly, for when he compared several with the original manuscript <note>original: "Autographo"</note>, he found distinct differences.
³) See Epistles <note>original: "Sendbr."</note> 15 and 42.

<column-break/>

<page-num>83</page-num>

lattice-work, from which only boundless confusion can arise in the mind of the reader who tries to force himself to understand these infinitely refined embellishments and overlapping images with unintelligible meanings. The happiness promised in the 47th Epistle regarding his "principles"—that if one understood all of this, all strife and questions in the reader would cease and "Babel" <note>A common term in Böhme's work for the confused, divided, and corrupt state of official churches.</note> would stand in mockery—seems more desirable than achievable even with the most honest effort.

It reconciles one with Böhme's personality, when one is weary of these absurdities, to hear how he—guided, to be sure, only by his individual reason—knew how to set limits to the exaggerations of such visionary enthusiasts. An anecdote reported by Frankenberg seems all the more credible because it was recorded by a man so prone to believing in wonders and loving symbols, yet it still places that obsession with secrecy in an unfavorable light:
> "A stranger, small and of a sharp wit, once came to Böhme under the impression that he was a professional alchemist <note>original: "Alchymist ex professo"</note>, and demanded that Böhme sell him his secrets for money. Böhme answered him: he lived in God, but in no 'singular' or 'familiar' spirit <note>Refers to a 'familiar spirit' or personal demon/servant-spirit often associated with magic.</note>; if he wanted his secrets, he should return to God in repentance. When the stranger then began to use magical formulas, Böhme threw him out the door, with the warning to never again involve himself in such simony <note>The sin of buying or selling spiritual things.</note> and devilry¹)."

Böhme’s social standing among his friends was highly honorable in relation to his origins; through his religiosity, he was able to set aside the strictly and stiffly observed class distinctions of that time, in which the mindset of his followers met him halfway. He showed no sense of inferiority in his letters; he wrote to the high and the low in the same God-conscious, direct, and childlike-naive style. With a familiarity based on the background of Christian love, he approached the newly-won student of wisdom. In this way, the followers also entered into friendly connections with one another, regardless of whether they were noble or common; noble—

---

¹) Frankenberg, *Life of Böhme* <note>original: "Vita"</note> 22.

<sig>6*</sig>

<summary>This page explores how Jakob Böhme's followers influenced his work with alchemical and Latinate complexities, while also highlighting Böhme's personal integrity in refusing to sell his "secrets" as magic. It notes Böhme's ability to transcend social classes, treating nobility and commoners with the same spiritual familiarity.</summary>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jakob Böhme's social standing and the financial support he received from his followers, transitioning from the authenticity of his authorship to his personal relationships with the nobility and commoners of his time.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
In that age, which strictly divided the social classes like castes, the distributors of Böhme's writings were commoners; yet both classes exchanged letters. Such was the case with Rudolph von Gersdorff, Caspar Lindner, and Christian Bernhard. Böhme was invited to the tables of noblemen and stayed with them for weeks and months at a time; the respect with which his name is mentioned in the writings of those men shows that he was not viewed as a court jester, but rather as a master of **theosophy** <note>A system of religious philosophy or mysticism seeking direct knowledge of the divine.</note>. —

His followers also showed enough consideration for him to faithfully look after him in his distressed circumstances. Through his lengthy stays on their estates, he was relieved of his immediate worries regarding sustenance; from time to time, they granted him direct support in the form of money and provisions, which often could not even be obtained for money during the hardships of the war. At times Böhme was in Weicha with Rudolph von Gersdorff, at times in Sagan with Christian Bernhard, at times in Liegnitz with Paul Kaym¹), at times with Koschwitz in Striegau, and at times in Hohndorf and Seifersdorf near Schweidnitz²). He earned little from writing as such; only once, in the 51st Epistle, Section 1, is there a mention that he was offered money for his manuscripts³). He had also entirely neglected his trade <note>Böhme was a shoemaker by trade.</note>, but Christian Bernhard—whose own life circumstances could not have been the most brilliant—Karl von Ender, the wealthy lord of Leopoldshain and Sercha, and Rudolph von Gersdorff of Weicha competed to provide him with support⁴). Even from Martin Moser in Goldberg came

---

1) Epistle 39.
2) Epistle 15, 6 reports on a conversation with Dr. Koschwitz; since Böhme says: "I was not previously accustomed to wine and delicious foods, and at home I live quite moderately and soberly"; it seems necessary to assume that the conversation took place in Silesia, perhaps in Striegau, Schweidnitz, or Hohndorf.
3) "As soon as I arrived home, I sent a short letter to Zittau regarding your books, and informed the gentleman who had previously offered five **Reichsthaler** <note>A standard silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire.</note> for them that they were with me; however, he was not at home but had traveled to Dresden, and I have no answer from him yet until he returns home." —
4) Thus Böhme gives thanks on December 1, 1619 (Epistle 4, 2), and on May 4, 1620 (Epistle 5) for a bushel of grain from

<column-break/>

<page-num>85</page-num>
a box of confections for Jakob Böhme. During the expensive years of famine during the war, these gifts became a significant help for the poor writer; they were given in this manner only to avoid the appearance of mere alms and to ease the burden of such a relationship—

---

Karl von Ender; on September 12, 1620 (Epistle 8) to Christian Bernhard for a sent **Reichsthaler**; on October 29, he sent three empty sacks to Rudolph von Gersdorff to have them returned full; likewise on November 12, 1622 (in the 32nd Epistle) he offers further thanks for grain sent; the same to Christian Bernhard in the 38th Epistle; in the 37th Epistle to Karl von Ender, thanks for grain, cheese, and turnips; finally, thanks for provisions in the 65th Epistle, without a specific date, to the Fürstenau administrator Augustin Köppe in Lissa. In the 37th Epistle, Böhme sends 10 **Reichsthaler** and three Marks to Karl von Ender as payment for provisions, namely for a bushel of rye. This enormous price is striking; it was during the terrible **Kipper- und Wipperzeit** <note>A period of extreme hyperinflation and currency debasement (1619–1623) at the start of the Thirty Years' War.</note> reported by contemporary chronicles. It began in the year 1621; money had sunk to a very low value, and provisions had risen to unprecedented heights. A gold **ducat** was worth 20 to 24 **Reichsthaler** (compared to its former value), an electoral **Engelthaler** was 5 **Reichsthaler**, a **Gröschel** was 3½ **Kaisergroschen**, a Meissen **Pfennig** was 2 **Groschen**, a bushel of grain cost 20 **Reichsthaler**, a **Dreier** was 4½ **Kreuzer**, and a brass **Pfennig** was 2 **Kreuzer**, a **Dickthaler** 4 **Reichsthaler**. According to Roch’s *Lusatian-Silesian-Bohemian Chronicle* p. 425, grain prices in the neighboring Silesian duchies were twice as high as in Görlitz, where according to Funke’s manuscript chronicle in 1622 a bushel of grain cost 10 to 12 **Reichsthaler**, barley 8 **guilders**, later 10½ **guilders**, oats 4 to 6 **Reichsthaler**, and wheat 8 **Reichsthaler**. Meat was equal in value. A quarter of veal = 4 to 5 **Reichsthaler**, a leg of mutton 2 **Reichsthaler**, a fattened pig 40, 50 **Reichsthaler**; likewise drinks; barley beer 14 **Reichsthaler**, a jug of Rhine wine 3 **Reichsthaler**, a cord of wood 10 to 12 **Reichsthaler**. The currency collapse occurred in Görlitz on October 15, 1623, following an electoral decree; likely earlier than in Silesia, because the Lusatian estates had already sworn fealty to the Elector of Saxony on July 28, 1623. Thereafter, ducats were worth 36 **Groschen**, and the **Reichsthaler** 24 good **Groschen**. The new Görlitz small copper **Pfennigs** were abolished, but the new silver **Groschen** were issued at 6 for one **Kreuzer**; the Liegnitz **Groschen** at 3 **Kreuzer**, and the **Engelthaler** at 24 **Kreuzer**. Nevertheless, it remains striking why Ender accepted payment from Böhme at all; even assuming 12 **Reichsthaler** was the current price for a bushel of rye at the time, the reduction to 10 **Reichsthaler** would not appear to be a great relief among friends. A sale on commission is unthinkable; thus Ender’s nobility of spirit allows for no other explanation than that Böhme, as soon as he was able to make payment, would not allow even the smallest gift to be forced upon him. —

It provides a fascinating look at the "Kipper- und Wipperzeit" through specific prices for grain, meat, and various contemporary currencies.</summary>

to avoid [it], it seems, Böhme occasionally paid the fee to the sender.

### 3. Böhme's Persecution, His Stay in Dresden, and His Death.

The tolerant ones, the <term>Crypto-Calvinists</term> <note>Lutherans who were suspected of secretly harboring Calvinist views, particularly regarding the Lord's Supper.</note> and <term>theosophists</term> <note>Followers of a mystical philosophy that seeks direct knowledge of the divine through spiritual intuition.</note>, from whose ranks Böhme drew his followers, nevertheless formed only a small part of the population; and the very fact that they rallied so quickly around their "new light" was a result of the ruling party exercising a no less oppressive, fanatical power over intellectual freedom and civil life. Philosophical thought was at this time neither popular nor even present in Germany; the two directions that were constantly in conflict had grown on religious soil. One was the party of spiritual progress consisting of the mystics, theosophists, and Crypto-Calvinists—that is, all those who subjected the established norms of faith to their own independent investigation and were therefore regarded as heretics. The other was the party of stagnation, the <term>Orthodox</term>, whether they were Catholics, Lutherans, or Reformed. These first two religious parties often went hand in hand during the <term>Thirty Years' War</term> <note>A devastating series of wars in Central Europe (1618–1648) fought largely over religious and political control.</note> more often than they fought each other; the Calvinists were not included in the Peace of the Empire, as only those who followed the <term>Augsburg Confession</term> <note>The primary confession of faith for the Lutheran Church, presented in 1530.</note> were included in it. 

No matter how absurd the exaggerations and ridiculous behaviors of the <term>adepts</term> <note>In this context, individuals initiated into secret or mystical knowledge.</note> might have been, their movement demands the immediate respect of posterity because of the principle of tolerance and intellectual freedom which they honored as a result of their trust in mystical, subjective revelation. This is not so for the orthodox guardians of the church, whose principle—the strength of a unified church community bound by love—had long since turned into a blind fanaticism by persisting in frozen formulas of faith without reason and without any thought of progress. The value of a good Christian was judged not by their disposition and moral conduct, but by their eloquence in defending the established dogmas; theological and dialectical subtleties were taught in universities and pulpits, thereby sowing hateful discord in the

<page-num>87</page-num>

hearts of the listeners. They did not rely on evidence, but on biblical passages, which were often misunderstood, often torn from context, and often used based on inaccurate translations. Stubbornness and the crudeness of polemics had to serve as evidence of truthfulness. The representatives of the ossified framework of Protestant formulas convinced themselves of a rigid belief in the sense of contradiction; they became entangled in the finely and neatly woven web of a sham science. Just as they themselves, before they were of age and had begun to think, had sworn by the words of the master, they persuaded the people—whose spirit was still sincerely inclined toward the truth of the Reformation—into fanaticism against those of different faiths. Hatred against sectarians was the first Christian duty; slavish submission to the arrogant priesthood was the second. Small indeed was the handful of clergymen who sought to unite love and tolerance with orthodoxy, and those few were exposed to the hatred of their colleagues. 

The Württemberg theologian <term>Johann Valentin Andreä</term> <note>A prominent Lutheran theologian and writer (1586–1654), often associated with the Rosicrucian movement.</note>, famous for his beneficial and mild influence in his high position, for his courageous struggle for Protestantism, and for his witty writings, once complained: the main reason for his "unpardonable crime" was that he had judged <term>Johann Arndt</term> <note>A German Lutheran theologian (1555–1621) whose influential work "True Christianity" emphasized inner piety.</note>, the incomparable herald of true Christianity, along with <term>Johann Gerhard</term> <note>A leading Lutheran scholastic theologian (1582–1637); he is described here as a precursor to Spener, the founder of Pietism.</note> (the Jena professor who, in his mild manner, can be called a predecessor of <term>Spener</term>), the distinguished theologian, more fairly and leniently than some of his own peers, and that he had voiced his opinion both privately and publicly¹).

Thus, it becomes understandable that even he believed he could only ward off the accusation of heresy through the strongest expressions in his written confession of faith; he rejected all doctrines that stood in opposition to the **Augsburg Confession** and the **Formula of Concord**; but he especially abhorred the tyranny of the papacy, the pride of the **Calvinists**, the hypocrisy of the **Anabaptists**, the deceptions of **Schwenkfeld**, the nonsense of **Weigel**, and the trickery—

---

¹) Hoßbach, *Life of Andreä*, p. 131.

<summary>This section describes the rigid and fanatical religious environment of 17th-century Germany, where Jakob Böhme and other mystical "progressives" faced persecution from the established Orthodox church. It highlights the internal conflicts within Protestantism and the rare voices of tolerance like Johann Valentin Andreä.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the religious climate in 17th-century Germany, specifically focusing on the critiques leveled against the formal Protestant clergy before introducing the specific persecutions faced by the mystic Jakob Böhme.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

...frivolities of the <term>Neoplatonists</term> <note>Philosophers who followed a mystical version of Plato's teachings, often seeking union with "the One."</note>; he had always laughed at the fairy tales of the <term>Rosicrucians</term> <note>A secret spiritual society claiming ancient esoteric wisdom, very popular in the early 1600s.</note> and resisted the "Brothers of Curiosity" $^1$).

With such harsh expressions, the same man <note>Johann Valentin Andreae, a prominent theologian and social critic.</note> takes the field against the sectarians—a man who, with a keen sense of reality, did not hide the great failings of his own "orthodox" clergy from himself:

> “who would rather explain the Trinity than worship it; who would rather prove the presence of Christ than honor Him at all times and in all places; who would rather describe repentance for sins than feel it within themselves; who would rather diminish the merit of good works than actually do good works; and who leaf through the holy books more often than they occupy themselves with the practice of Christian love. They turn religion into a science, the knowledge of which—just like the knowledge of logic and metaphysics—is very useful for gaining a reputation for scholarship” $^2$).

The great mass of the clergy, he argued, had grasped only this one thing and were wonderfully pleased with themselves if they could maintain an "armed theology," bristling with the mere thorns of logic, and shout something loud to the applause of the mob. This has no other result than that they, like madmen, speak differently than they feel and believe. When they return from the pulpit to their business, they are no less filled with the common customs of the world than a vessel, from which the water has been drained, is filled with air $^3$). Like anyone who is hired, they anxiously serve the prescribed laws only so that they do not go hungry $^4$). The "Tyrant Dialectic" <note>Here referring to the rigid use of logical argumentation to dominate religious thought.</note> boasts of the name "Philosophy," despises her other sisters, and fills her "parrots" with such vanity that anyone who has merely tasted the subtleties of logic boasts of having swallowed all of philosophy $^5$).” — Once, in a fit of indignation, he cried out: “Yes, farewell Reformation, for on this earth we shall never see you again $^6$)!”

$^1$) Seybold, *Autobiography of Andreae*, p. 205. Winterthur 1799.
$^2$) <note>original: "Veri Christianismi solidaeque philosophiae libertas"</note> *The Liberty of True Christianity and Solid Philosophy.* S. Menzel, *History of the Germans before the Reformation*, Vol. 6. pp. 99–100.
$^3$) <note>original: "Alethea exul"</note> *Exiled Truth*, p. 306.
$^4$) *Menippus*, Dialogue 62.
$^5$) <note>original: "Alethea exul"</note> *Exiled Truth*, p. 330.
$^6$) *Menippus*, Dialogue 47.

<page-num>89</page-num>

So, it was not surprising that a man like Jakob Böhme was bound to become a thorn in the side of the spokesmen of <term>Orthodoxy</term> <note>The dominant, strictly traditionalist branch of the Lutheran church at the time.</note>. Dialectical hair-splitting was repugnant to his nature; for religion, he demanded a pure, honest heart. He fought against intolerance as a blasphemy against God, who had scattered spiritual gifts in various ways; he saw intolerance as the work of Satan, who alone forbids searching and inquiry. Such a way of thinking and the demand for intellectual freedom soon found an opponent with whom it had to measure itself in public. —

It was first in the year 1613 that Böhme suffered persecutions from the orthodox clergy in Görlitz. This clergy had already provided proof that even in eastern Germany, fanaticism had found "door and gate wide open." Above all, the role of ecclesiastical opponent was taken up by the <term>Primarius</term> <note>The head pastor or chief minister of a city.</note>, who was full of a sense of duty to his office. From his orthodox standpoint, he felt he could not let it go unpunished that a member of his own congregation should interpret the wording of Scripture and the history of Creation in such an unheard-of way—interpreting until the material had become entirely the "property" of the creative author. He was outraged that Böhme dared to preach things that deviated from Luther's articles of faith, and even from the <term>Formula of Concord</term> <note>A 1577 document intended to settle theological disputes within Lutheranism.</note>. Böhme interpreted the Lord's Supper not as a "physical miracle"—as the strict <term>Flacians</term> <note>Followers of Matthias Flacius, known for their extreme views on original sin and rigid Lutheran doctrine.</note> demanded—but in a mystical way, as a spiritual union with the heart of God. He dared to attack the doctrine of <term>Divine Election</term> <note>The idea that God chooses specific people for salvation (predestination).</note> as nonsensical and unhesitatingly raised the banner of free inquiry regarding religion, the Bible, and human decrees. 

But the motives of this "shepherd of souls" <note>The Primarius, Gregorius Richter.</note> were not so innocent and pure when he hurled his "thunderbolt of excommunication" against the poor religious thinker: how could a mere shoemaker—one of the lowest members of his congregation—dare to cherish his own view and even try to appear as an author? He had not attended the high schools (universities), yet he wanted to solve problems about which the highest scholars were in disagreement—abysses that the Primarius himself could only cover up through blind zeal and a dead belief in the literal word. Furthermore, the expression of these solutions in Böhme’s writing was dark and obscure, which intimidated the clergyman, to whom the prophetic breath of the work was unpleasant; for as far as its depth reached—
