<meta>This page continues the biography of the figures opposing Jacob Böhme, moving from the general state of the clergy to the specific character of Gregorius Richter, Böhme's primary antagonist in Görlitz.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

[...understood] his reasons not; thus, it had to be the work of the devil, as it was too much effort for him to comprehend something while the author claimed to be inspired by God. He, the <term>Primarius</term> <note>The "Pastor Primarius" was the head or chief pastor of a city's main church, holding significant authority over the local clergy and civic religious life.</note>, claimed this as well, and since he possessed the "correct" God, the other's inspiration must be from the devil. Above all, personal reasons—envy and anger at the boldness of a layman—spurred the priest to purge such a heretic from the community.

Gregorius Richter, Chief Pastor of Görlitz, was born on February 1, 1560, in Ostriz, a small town between Görlitz and Zittau. He was the son of the local monastery blacksmith (from the nearby Marienthal Monastery). While living in a blacksmith's house as a student in Breslau, he was so drawn to his father's trade that he briefly traded his studies for the hammer and anvil. However, he later returned to the study of theology and, in 1584, obtained a position as a teacher at the Görlitz Gymnasium. In 1587, he was appointed by the Council to the pastorate at Rauscha, a village in the Görlitz Heath, and finally became a deacon in Görlitz in 1590. In 1606, he attained the position of Chief Pastor; yet, even then, his vehemence in the pulpit prompted a reprimand from the Council. The mayor, <term>Bartholomäus Scultetus</term> <note>A famous astronomer, mathematician, and statesman who served multiple terms as mayor of Görlitz and was a contemporary of Böhme.</note>, noted this in his diary: “On August 1, 1606, Chief Pastor Gregor Richter was invested in the treasury chamber¹) with an admonition to moderate his intercessions and to speak more clearly²).” Once again, in the year 1618, his vehemence had to be restrained by the authorities. During a time of plague, he was forced to remain at home for an entire month—more out of vengefulness and hostility than any actual danger, for he and his family were perfectly healthy. He also fell into a dispute with Rector Dornavius. During that time, Richter had preached about "flight" <note>This refers to the theological debate over whether a Christian should flee a plague-stricken city or stay to serve, a common point of contention in the 17th century.</note> when Dornavius had escaped the pestilence by going to Sprottau. It was reported to the Rector that Richter had intended the sermon for him. The Captain of Görlitz and Karl von Ender wanted 

---

¹) i.e., the treasury chamber, *Camera thesauri*.
²) Also entered in the manuscript chronicle of Master Superintendent Jancke.

<page-num>91</page-num>

to settle the matter amicably, but Dornavius refused¹). Richter had scolded a plague doctor from the pulpit as a "bad Samaritan" because he kept his patients waiting. To avoid unrest, the Council ordered him to stay in his room. The plague provided the pretext, but the people composed a mocking verse:

> “Why, it is asked, is Richter shut within his halls?
> The Samaritan’s horse has kicked me with its heels.”
> <note>original Latin: "Quaeritur inclusus cur sit Richterus in aedes? Me Samaritani calce petivit equus."</note>

On January 1, 1624, he had prayed for so long for those who had given him New Year’s gifts that he forgot to read the New Year's notice. Consequently, he had to return to the pulpit one more time after the hymn. When <term>Elector Johann Georg</term> <note>John George I, the Elector of Saxony, who ruled during much of the Thirty Years' War.</note> came to Görlitz in 1621, Richter preached very zealously against the Calvinists in his presence, but prayed at length for the Elector, who gave him a token of favor in return²).

These few traits are sufficient to place the Chief Pastor's behavior toward Böhme in the proper light. The image of an orthodox fanatic is completed by these events involving the quiet <term>Theosophist</term> <note>Jacob Böhme, often called a theosophist because his philosophy sought a direct, mystical understanding of the nature of God and the universe.</note>. Through Karl von Ender's zeal in distributing the *Morning Glow* <note>Böhme's first major work, *Aurora* or *Die Morgenröthe im Aufgang*, written in 1612.</note>, a copy had also come to the attention of Chief Pastor Richter. He immediately began, from the height of the pulpit—the place where his influence was most secure and could become most dangerous—to pour out violent insults upon the poor shoemaker, who did not know how he had come to face such hostility. He had not seen his manuscript for a long time and did not know in whose hands it was³). The excitement of the people after the sermon from the

---

Richter was not without a humanist education; his satires on Böhme are written in fluent verse and with wit, though often of a crude sort. Published by him: 1) *New edition of economic principles*, Görlitz 1604. 2) *Historical principles, part III, containing ecclesiastical principles*, 1602. 3) *Appendix to historical rules, continuation of the third century of new principles*, Görlitz 1614.

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the growing conflict in Görlitz. Following the introduction of Chief Pastor Gregorius Richter’s aggressive temperament, the text now details the specific events of July 1613, where the pressure from the clergy forced the City Council to take legal action against Jakob Böhme.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>

July 28th, when Richter had preached about false prophets, was so powerful that the Council could no longer ignore the fact—which had become public and was maliciously blackened by the priest—without having to fear violent scenes from the incited mob. For here, as everywhere else, the mob exercised a significant and fearsome power under the leadership of the priests, before which innovators, as well as city authorities and even princes, had to bow. It was a time when pangs of conscience, convictions, and measures of force were directed by the church lords, and the court preachers of the Protestants and the Jesuit confessors of the Catholics played such a significant role at the courts. The Council of Görlitz¹), which had already striven for a worthy impartiality during earlier religious disputes with the <term>Schwenkfelders</term> <note>Followers of Caspar Schwenckfeld, who emphasized internal spiritual experience over external church ritual.</note> and <term>Crypto-Calvinists</term> <note>Lutherans suspected of secretly holding Calvinist views on the Eucharist.</note>, and now also saw enlightened men—above all the excellent Bartholomäus Scultetus—in its midst, resorted to a measure of prudence. They did this so as not to have to bow to the raging of the fanatical priest and the fury of the mob, and to avoid seeing an innocent member of the city sacrificed to blind revenge through civil destruction, so long as it could be inhibited in its worst outbreaks by a mock punishment. On July 26th, a Friday, the Council had the persecuted man taken into custody by a city official. After an interrogation, he was indeed released, but had to vow to renounce his <term>enthusiastic philosophy</term> <note>The term "enthusiastic" (schwärmerisch) was used pejoratively by the church to describe mystical or visionary beliefs not sanctioned by dogma.</note> and,

---

above all, his writing. Richter showed the book to everyone around, including citizens and friends, to incite them to persecute the author. Often, he achieved the exact opposite effect, because the book was pleasing to many. *Epistles* 10, 26.

¹) It consisted of the following members:
**Consul:** Bartholomäus Scultetus.
**Jurors:** Melchior Steinberg, Bartholomäus Jakobi, Johann Glück (Mayor in the following year), Anton Schmiedt (<term>Third Judge</term> <note>Judex tertius</note>), Heinrich Moller, Michael Büttner, Dr. David Tuchscherer, George Hildrich, Johann Emmerich (<term>new to the Senate</term> <note>novus e Senatu</note>).
**Senators:** George Heinze, Friedrich Schmettig, Caspar Cunrad, Bartholomäus Hagendorn, George Jäckel (<term>Wool weaver</term> <note>Lanif.</note>), Elias Bartsch (<term>Butcher</term> <note>Lanio</note>), Gregor Ulrich (<term>Tanner</term> <note>Cerdo</note>).
**Seniors:** Paul Fideler from the wool-weaving guild, George Würffel from the butchers' guild, Grolmes Thiele from the tanners' guild.

renounce his writing¹). Böhme’s manuscript of the *Aurora* <note>Original: "Morgenröthe" (The Day-Spring or Morning Redness), Böhme's first major work.</note> was kept at the town hall until 1641, when, according to Frankenberg’s report, Dr. Paul Scipio gave it to the Saxon House Marshal Georg von Pflug, who took it with him to Dresden. From there, it came into the hands of Heinrich Prunius—a friend of Tschech whom he had brought back from Padua and a well-known theosophist.

---

¹) The fact of the imprisonment is hardly to be doubted; it is reported not only by one chronicle, as Neumann (History of Görlitz, p. 368) says, but by two, which have as their authority an older chronicle and the diary of Bartholomäus Scultetus, which is equivalent to an official source. This is so even though nothing of it is recorded in the Council’s protocol books—a negligence that in older times not infrequently complicates documentary research, and is likewise to be lamented in the case of Böhme’s interrogation before the Dresden Consistory. In Scultetus, it is written:
> “In the year 1613, July 26th. On Friday, Jakob Böhme, a shoemaker living between the gate behind the hospital smithy, was summoned to the town hall, and immediately his written book in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheets are folded twice to create four leaves.</note> was fetched from his house by the city official; thereafter he was released from prison and exhorted to desist from such matters. In the year 1613, July 28th. On Sunday, Richter held a sharp sermon against him. Tuesday, July 30th, Jakob Böhme, the shoemaker, was summoned by the preachers to the Head Pastor's residence and earnestly examined regarding his confession.” See Köhler’s essay in *Wegweiser* 1837 No. 45. In Funke’s handwritten chronicle it says: Jakob Böhme was summoned to the town hall and questioned about his faith, taken into custody for it, and immediately his book in quarto folio was fetched from his house by the city official, then released from prison again and exhorted to desist from such matters. — In the chronicle of G. Köhler, Vol. III, p. 64, there is the added note: Jakob Böhme was placed in the <term>stocks</term> <note>A device for public punishment; the author later questions the accuracy of this detail.</note>; the city official was named Oswald. — Since this chronicle, according to its appearance and its continuation into the 18th century, is of more recent origin, the report of the stocks may well rest on uncertain information. — Zirschke, *Görlitz Annals* Vol. III, p. 420. — Funke’s chronicle continues: “On July 30th, Tuesday, he was summoned by the preachers to the Head Pastor’s residence and earnestly examined in his confession. Before that, on July 28th, Sunday, when the Gospel was about the false prophet, the Head Pastor Gregorius Richter delivered a sharp sermon against the aforementioned shoemaker.”

Regarding the renunciation: “Thereupon my first book was taken from me, and because quite wonderful things were revealed therein, which were not immediately comprehensible to the human mind, I had to endure much from the ‘reason-wise’ [the scholarly elite].” *Epistles* 12, 12. Frankenberg, *Life* 43. Funke, *Spiritual P.* p. 54.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jakob Böhme's period of enforced silence following the confiscation of his first book, Aurora. It transitions from the history of his lost manuscripts to the internal and external pressures that eventually led him to break his silence and begin his second, most productive period of writing.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

to Amsterdam to the merchant Abraham von Beyerland, and after his death in the year 1641 to his son G. R. von Beyerland. The anonymous collector of reports about Jakob Böhme—a reprint of Gichtel’s <note>Johann Georg Gichtel (1638–1710) was a German mystic and a key editor of Böhme’s works.</note> compilation in the *Theosophia revelata* <note>Latin: "Revealed Theosophy," the title of the comprehensive 1730 edition of Böhme's writings.</note>, augmented with several additions in 1730—says that the autograph <note>The original manuscript written in the author's own hand.</note> finally came to Leiden; now its trail is lost; likely it was destroyed as useless after printing, like Böhme's other manuscripts.

Had the Council intended the complete suppression of the writing, the seizure of Böhme's own manuscript would have been a completely failed measure, since through the care of Böhme's friends very many copies of the "miracle book" <note>original: "Wunderbuche." A contemporary nickname for Böhme's first work, *Aurora*.</note> were put into circulation. This punishment also pained the author less than the prohibition against writing. This meant killing him spiritually, because the expression of his inner world of thought had become a duty and an unavoidable necessity for him, which asserted its demands ever more powerfully. For a time he controlled himself. The persecution had dampened his courage; he even asked God, if his gift did not come from His counsel, to take it from him, and to let him recognize nothing in this way 1); he resolved to write nothing more, but as an obedient man to keep still before God; but so many a storm passed over him; "what he suffered, could not well be told," his reason was weak and timid, as the light of grace <note>original: "Gnadenlicht," a central concept for Böhme referring to divine illumination or spiritual insight.</note> was withdrawn from him for a considerable while, "and smoldered in him like a hidden fire, so that there was nothing but anguish in him—mockery from without, a fiery drive from within 2); but like a grain that is sown in the earth, it grows forth in every storm and tempest, against all reason, when in winter everything is as if dead, and reason says: Now all is gone; thus the noble mustard seed <note>A biblical reference to faith starting small but growing into something immense.</note> greened forth again in every storm under disgrace and mockery, like a line, and came again with a hundredfold fruit 3)."

In that time, when his spirit did not drive him to write (for 5 years, for already at the beginning of the year

---

1) Epistle 10, 5, 6. 2) Epistle 12, 17. 3) Epistle 10, 7. <note>The "Sendbriefe" or Epistles are Böhme’s collected correspondence.</note>

<page-num>95</page-num>

1617, on January 18th, he reports that he has three books under the pen) he had not remained without contact with the mystics and theosophists; more and more friends flocked around him, and exhorted him to let his talent hold sway again 1). Bernhard and Walther <note>Balthasar Walther and Abraham von Sommerfeld, noblemen and physicians who encouraged Böhme.</note>, as already mentioned, knew how to work upon him with such convincing power that he—the conscientious, soulful Böhme, who could not bear to see his divine truth condemned to a deathly sleep without being able to create light for his neighbor—resolved in the highest excitement to take up the pen again despite the command of the authorities. "Because I recognize in power and light that it is a pure gift from God, who still gives me such a driving will for it that I must write, I shall be more obedient to God than to men, so that my office <note>original: "Bisthum," literally "bishopric," but Böhme is quoting the biblical verse "let another take his office" (Acts 1:20) to describe his spiritual duty.</note> be not taken from me and given to another, which I should indeed eternally regret 2)." The despondency that had previously weighed heavily upon him had vanished, so that he now held as an intolerable evil that for which he had recently prayed fervently. The Council’s stern reprimand, the priest's rage, the dark looks of the people—who stared at him as a heretic and sorcerer and shamed his reputation with foul fabrications—were no longer so fresh in his memory, just as the storm had in fact somewhat subsided. Silesia, Saxony, Meissen, and the Marches <note>Territories within the Holy Roman Empire, covering parts of modern-day Germany and Poland.</note> were already full of his fame 3), even though, with the exception of his followers in his home city, no one knew his book. The breath of the Most High came to his aid again and awakened him to new life. His first work was now: *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence*; on January 18, 1618, he had not yet finished it, and on October 22, 1619, he sent the conclusion of the 22nd chapter to Karl von Ender; so that one can suspect he worked on the text for nearly a year. His second writing period had dawned, where he in rapid

<meta>This page continues the account of Jakob Böhme’s literary output and the external pressures he faced. It moves from the physical loss of his manuscripts in the chaos of war to the internal struggle he felt between his drive to write and the legal prohibitions placed upon him by the local authorities in Görlitz.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

In sequence, he composed thirty larger and smaller writings, one of which—concerning the Last Judgment—was lost during the fire in Glogau ¹) according to an uncertain report; and, what is more to be regretted, the greater part of his correspondence was lost in the turmoil of the Thirty Years' War <note>A devastating series of conflicts in Central Europe (1618–1648) that occurred during Böhme's final years.</note> ¹).

The mystic here succumbed to a self-deception that arose from too great a certainty of divine influence. The resumption of his writing was clearly against the command of the authorities, and his behavior cannot stand before a strict court of judgment. There were other ways to satisfy his inner desire; Böhme’s friends could have facilitated a change of residence for him, so that he would have been freed from the shackles of the prohibition. The day had now overtaken the "Dawn" <note>original: "Morgenröthe." A play on the title of Böhme's first book, *Aurora*, which translates to "Day-Spring" or "Morning Glow."</note>. Although Böhme initially did his utmost to prevent the publication of his activity and to persuade his friends to remain silent, this was impossible given his wide acquaintance and their overly-obliging zeal; perhaps there were also traitors among the adepts <note>Followers or initiates into Böhme's esoteric circle.</note> who viewed the rise of a layman with displeasure ²) .

Here and there, foul rumors arose once more; it was not long before the Primarius <note>The head pastor of the city, Gregor Richter, who was Böhme’s primary antagonist.</note> began again to hurl the anathema <note>A formal curse or excommunication by the church.</note> at the unfortunate man. The common people were grumbling again; the priest spared neither time nor effort to brand Böhme a heretic by spreading his writings...

---

> ¹) *De vita et Scriptis J. B.* <note>Latin: "On the Life and Writings of Jakob Böhme," a biographical source.</note> 5, 34 says this book is nothing but *Psychologia vera* <note>Latin: "True Psychology."</note>.
> ²) "And although it is my habit to keep somewhat quiet about it, it is nevertheless obvious, and I hear it with my own ears, how it is desired by proud people, among whom the enemy might hasten to the end like a destroyer; for I know what an enemy I have against me in the Devil, as an opponent. Therefore I ask you to proceed wisely with it; I will demand it back if I need it." *Epistle* 2, 12. — "I hear mockers who are creeping in, etc." *Epistle* 4, 34. — "Regarding the copying of my future writings by N. N., I do not know how to deal with him for the best, for he does not keep silent, and I often hear frivolous people interpreting my current writings, which, as I guess, comes from him, for I have shown them to no one else. Since he is almost worldly and born only from the school of this world, we might be poorly protected."

<page-num>97</page-num>

...to denounce as a heretic ¹). A vexing story, which admittedly an unreliable source reports, may have contributed to embittering the relationship between the clergyman and the parishioner. The narrative comes from a dutiful friend of Böhme’s, Cornelius Weißner, who forged it from uncertain reports to provide a physical, tangible starting point for the second dispute with Richter. Böhme himself mentions nothing of the incident in his existing letters. Yet the story is not to be rejected on internal grounds; it provides another image of the character of an orthodox clergyman who, despite a humanist education, displayed a magnificent wildness in his fanaticism, even if the author may have applied the colors too brightly here and there. If it only preserves a folk legend, it has all the more credibility for that, as it would be the only one of its kind to report something favorable about the theosophist ²) .

---

> ¹) "But know this as news, that slander and lies have only publicized and revealed my little book, so that now almost everyone—nobility and scholars, and also simple people—desire to read it and hold it dear." — *Epistle* of March 15, 1624, to Schweinichen.
> ²) The content of the story is this: Böhme’s brother-in-law had borrowed a thaler from Primarius Richter for "Striebel-baking" <note>A "Striebel" is a traditional fried pastry, like a cruller or funnel cake.</note> and, instead of interest for fourteen days, had brought him a "Striebel" (a Christmas pastry—a lousy expression). The clergyman, enraged by this, threatened him with the punishment of heaven. The young man, a baker, fell into a deep melancholy and finally confessed the cause to his brother-in-law Jakob Böhme. Böhme went to the Primarius, spoke with gentleness on behalf of his relative, and offered him the interest. But the Primarius shouted: "What business does this rag-patcher <note>original: "Zerrfleck." A derogatory term for a cobbler, Böhme's profession.</note> have with me, to disturb, molest, and perturb me!" Jakob Böhme begged for mercy, but the Primarius showed him the door: he should pack himself off. Broad, pompous, and comfortable, he sat upright in his easy chair; as Jakob Böhme was about to go out the door and said, "God keep Your Reverence," the priest threw his slipper at him. Yet Böhme respectfully retrieved it and placed it back under his foot, despite the Primarius saying: "Why should you, godless rogue, wish me a good night, or what do I care for it!" Böhme replied: "Sir, be not angry, I do you no harm, stay in God's keeping," and departed. Now the Primarius began to denounce him as a heretic and condemn him from the pulpit on Sunday, to curse his books, to threaten the city with heaven's punishment, and to command revenge upon the Council so that a heavy judgment might fall upon him; as upon the company... <note>Likely referring to the biblical "Company of Korah" who were swallowed by the earth.</note>

It highlights a famous anecdote involving a dispute over a small debt and a Christmas pastry, illustrating Böhme's humility versus Richter's fanaticism.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the escalating conflict between Jakob Böhme and the religious authorities in Görlitz, specifically the head pastor Gregor Richter, following the publication of Böhme's devotional works.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>

The immediate and true cause of the newly erupting strife was that the Lords of Schweinichen had published the writings “Of Eternal Life and of True Repentance” ¹), which were later included in the series of treatises bearing the name *Christosophia* <note>original: "Christosophia," meaning "Wisdom of Christ."</note> or *The Way to Christ*, in the year 1623. This appeared to the Head Pastor <note>original: "Primarius," referring to Gregor Richter.</note> as an open rebellion against the prohibition issued in 1613, and as a presumptuous encroachment upon the rights of a scholar. He did not shrink ²) from accusing Jakob Böhme of blasphemy, portraying him as a traitor to the church and the sacraments; he claimed Böhme drank himself full of brandy every day, as well as beer and other wines; and that he was a scoundrel, “all of which, Böhme adds, is not true, and he himself [Richter] is a drunken man.” In short: “he raged as fiercely against the printed booklet as if it had murdered his son and burned all his property, and he poured out a heap of lies against me alongside frivolous character assassination” ³). He wrote to Pastor Fries in Liegnitz

> Korah, Dathan, and Abiram <note>Biblical figures from the Book of Numbers who led a rebellion against the authority of Moses and Aaron; Richter is comparing Böhme to these archetypal rebels.</note>. After the sermon, Böhme approached the Head Pastor and asked what harm he had done him; he said he would gladly do penance if he could only remember his misdeed. Instead of an answer, he received only furious looks; finally, Richter broke out with curses and blasphemies: "Get away from me, Satan! Be gone into the abyss of hell with your unrest! Can you not leave me in peace? Must you insult and molest me here? Do you not see that I am a clergyman and am going about my office?" — At this, Jakob Böhme became saddened and replied: "Yes, venerable sir, I see well that you are a clergyman; I also heard it in the church, how things stand with it, and I have also seen that you stand there in your office; I hold you rightly and without further contradiction to be a clergyman; yet would you tell me what I have done to harm you?" — Then Böhme turned to the other clergyman, the Chaplain, and begged him: "Venerable dear sir, please help me entreat the Lord Preacher regarding my concern, to tell me in your presence what I have said or done against him, over which he was so zealous in the pulpit and commanded vengeance from the Magistrate?" -- At this, the Head Pastor became even grimmer, turned to his servants, and ordered them to call the city guards to have him thrown into prison, which the Chaplain, however, prevented. —

1) Epistle 55, 10. *Of the Life and Writings of J. B.* 5, 35.
2) Epistle 52, 1.
3) Epistle 53, 5. Zirschke, *Görlitz Annals* Vol. III. 79, p. 456.

and demanded of him that he should not only stir up such matters from the pulpit, but also have them printed; he was to sue Böhme before the Council at Görlitz, and bring the lawsuit in such a way as if it had been prepared in the name of all the priests in the Liegnitz jurisdiction ¹). Fries agreed to this and submitted the complaint to the Görlitz Council. Richter himself diligently fueled the fire; he even stooped so low as to go frequently to the most prominent gentlemen of the city ²) and slander him; he demanded that he should be put in prison as soon as he came home, and then be driven out of the city. He himself finally submitted another letter to the Council, in which he “had well-heated hell for Böhme and prepared a bath for him.” <note>A German idiom meaning he had made things extremely difficult or set a trap for him.</note>

Although the Councilmen had now read that printed booklet, they could find absolutely nothing offensive or heretical in it; some even praised it, and it found no small approval among the citizenry. The Head Pastor's demand to impose a judicial investigation upon him therefore seemed unjust; they argued against him; for “this religion was nothing new after all; it was simply the foundation of the old holy fathers, where one would find more of the same” ³). But now the Council could no longer evade the pressing fanatic in a mild manner; the crowd was already in such an irritated mood that unpleasant scenes were to be feared, which indeed did not fail to occur in the following period; moreover, the complaint had the formal legal basis that Böhme had ignored the prohibition against writing. The poor persecuted man was summoned before the session of the Council as soon as he had returned from his journey in Silesia, where he had been staying with Master Hans von Schweinichen ⁴). Böhme went there with the firm resolution—

Despite the City Council's initial appreciation for the book, Richter's relentless political pressure and accusations of heresy forced them to take legal action against Böhme.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the legal and religious persecution of the mystic Jakob Böhme in his home city of Görlitz during the spring of 1624, leading up to his eventual departure from the city.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

conclusion, to tell the truth from its foundation, and to regard no creature, even if it should cost him his life. He believed the hour of the Reformation had come. The council decree in the memorandum book <note>original: "Memorialbuche," the official record of the city council's proceedings</note> of the Görlitz council reads:

> “Anno 1624, March 23. Regarding the local shoemaker, called Jochen Böhme, it is dedicated <note>meaning: resolved or recorded</note> that due to manifold complaints of evil alleged teaching, he should be summoned before the Council, and he should be ordered to move his staff further.” <note>original: "ihm ſein Stab fürder zu ſetzen," a contemporary idiom meaning he was being ordered to leave the city.</note>

Three days later, on March 26, Böhme appeared before the session and was asked whether he had written and published the book "Of Eternal Life." In the memorandum book, the protocol of the proceedings from March 26 reads as follows:

> “Jochen Böhme the shoemaker and confused enthusiast or dreamer <note>original: "Enthuſiaſt oder Fantaſt," terms used by the orthodox church to dismiss those who claimed direct divine inspiration</note> says that he prepared the book for eternal life, but did not have it printed; instead, a member of the nobility, Hans Sigismund von Schweinhaus ¹), had it printed. He has been warned by the Council to move his staff further, or in the absence of a peaceful departure, such shall be reported to His Electoral Grace. Whereupon he declared that he would take himself away at the earliest opportunity.”

Through this declaration by Böhme—that he would depart at the earliest opportunity—a story reported by Weißner gains credibility, though Weißner otherwise deserves no absolute trust. Admittedly, Böhme himself completely overlooks this report, even though in his 53rd Epistle <note>original: "53. Sendbr." or Sendbrief</note> he describes the history of his sufferings, the legal proceedings, and the hostilities of the priest in considerable detail. The Council is said to have banished him from the city (this is already in the memorandum book), even refusing to let him take his family or arrange his affairs; several councilmen, however, reportedly refused their consent and left the session hall ²). Thereupon Böhme is said to have gone out of the city overnight (into March 27).

<note>
he should no longer resist the holy office of preaching and bring the punishment of Korah, Dathan, and Abiram <note>Biblical rebels who were swallowed by the earth for defying Moses</note> upon the whole city.
¹) The ancestral castle of the Schweinichen family near Bolkenhain.
²) In 1624, the Council consisted of the following members:
Mayor: Wolfgang Stolberger.
Consuls: Friedrich Schwettig, Barthol. Jakobi, M. G. Staude.

<column-break/>

<page-num>101</page-num>

On the next morning, the councilmen rescinded the decree and had the exile brought back into the city by court messengers; but as the bickering and strife continued because of him, the Council asked him if he would, if possible, leave the city.

The Council’s “warning” signified a definitive banishment; Böhme interpreted the command somewhat mildly: and yet the stern expression “at the earliest opportunity” <note>original: "eheſten Tages"</note> leaves no doubt about the report that Böhme left the city overnight. He recounts in his 53rd Epistle:

> “The Council threatened him, saying he should see to it that the Emperor or the Elector were not perhaps incited by the priests to lay hands on him. When he left the Council, they advised him to step aside for a while, so they would not have unrest because of him.”

Böhme composed a written defense (the *Apology* or *Protective Writing against Gregorius Richter*, April 3, 1624) and submitted it to the Council. In it, he plainly recounts the course of circumstances that likely gave the Head Pastor <note>original: "Primarius"</note> cause for complaint; “he had his wisdom from the Savior alone, who was in love and betrothed to him in the inwardness of his soul; the first book had come into the hands of the Head Pastor by divine providence without his will. The latter had interpreted it with a foreign understanding and slandered it; finally, he admits that he promised not to write anymore after the first judgment, yet the Head Pastor himself was partly to blame for his wide reputation, since he had lent his book out to foreign places, cities, and villages, and had spread it without his knowledge until it reached even the Duke of Liegnitz ¹). It was being eagerly read at his court and in Dresden, as well as by other Princes of the Empire. Many scholars and noblemen had come to him in person, and he had been threatened with God’s punishment if he did not develop his gift, so he had to [write] again...”

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the legal and religious persecution of the mystic Jakob Böhme in his home city of Görlitz during the spring of 1624. It describes Böhme’s failed attempt to defend himself before the City Council and the escalating personal attacks from the city’s head pastor, Gregorius Richter.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>

begun writing, yet he had no part in the publication of his printed book. The accusations of drunkenness and godlessness were, he claimed, unfounded; the <term>Primarius</term> <note>The "Primarius" was Gregorius Richter, the head pastor of Görlitz and Böhme’s chief antagonist.</note> should set out his complaints point by point, and if the Council would only hear him out calmly just once, they would easily recognize his innocence. He was no heretic, no despiser of the Church, no <term>fanatic</term> <note>original: "Schwärmer," a derogatory term used by the orthodox to describe those claiming direct divine revelation</note> or scoundrel; he did not despise the sacraments but rather worshiped Jesus Christ, from whom he received his religion.

The Council did not accept this written defense because the Primarius stormed in during the proceedings. He feared, as Böhme noted, that he might have to answer for his lies. The Council warned the unfortunate man once more to step aside—or, since other people would be happy to have him, to go to them so that they might finally have peace. To this he replied: “Since no one wishes to hear my answer so that I may lament my innocence, and I can find no protection against the Primarius’s impositions and unfair insults, I must commit it to God and see where God might lead me to some pious people, and grant me a final little place where I might at last be out of the Primarius’s sight.”

As he went home from the Council, several sharp-tongued mockers—the Primarius’s followers, perhaps even sent by him—stood at the Town Hall door in the outer room and mocked him; “one among them,” Böhme relates, “a loose knave, anatomized me from the crown of my head to the soles of my feet, from my clothes to my gifts, and attacked the Spirit of God so fiercely, mocking and finally saying: the Holy Spirit would eventually become as common as the fur scraps at the furrier’s.”

On March 27, Richter had a lampoon against Böhme published by Rhambaw in Görlitz, which shows the daunting priestly fanaticism of that time in a clear light. He conjured all the powers of heaven and earth to punish the offender. He paid little heed to refinement of expression:

> The Shoemaker’s book smells of nothing but shoemaker's pitch,
> And the black color, which they call shoemaker’s ink.
> Fie, fie! Let the foul stench be far from us.
<note>original Latin: "Liber Sutorius nil nisi picem redolet sutorium, / Atrum et colorem, quem vocant sutorium. / Pfuy, pfuy! teter sit fretor a nobis procul." Richter uses "Sutor" (Shoemaker) as a recurring insult against Böhme's trade.</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>103</page-num>

->Further in the Propempticum:<-
<note>A "Propempticum" is traditionally a poem wishing a traveler well, though Richter uses it here as a mocking "send-off" for Böhme’s exile.</note>

> Your filth has defiled our city, shoemaker;
<note>original Latin: "Nostram incestarunt urbem tua stercora sutor;"</note>

->further:<-

> Therefore be gone, never return, may you perish miserably, Shoemaker,
> Let a shoe be in your hands, not a pen. —
<note>original Latin: "Ergo abeas, nunquam redeas, pereas male, Sutor, / Calceus in manibus sit tibi, non calamus."</note>

In response, Böhme wrote an extensive defense on April 10, in which Richter's insults were taken up sentence by sentence, rejected, and repaid with counter-accusations. It is written in a sharp and bitter tone, showing that Böhme, too, could be entirely a child of his time in polemics. “Dear Mr. Primarius, Satan has blinded you and led you into wrath. — It is a sign that the way to Christ, which leads to repentance, stinks to you like dung, etc.”

Böhme would have done most wisely to remain silent. The Council saw more clearly by ordering him to leave the city. It was a command of caution that the weaker party, who was also guilty of an overstep <note>referring to Böhme breaking his previous promise not to write</note>, had to give way to the stronger to avoid unpredictable bickering and dangerous public riots. Jakob Böhme may have felt the inappropriateness of his defense himself, as he excused himself at the end for having written it at all: “Dear reader, I consider the lampoon not even worth an answer, as only vain untruth and blasphemy were found within it; however, I wished to make an answer for the sake of those who understand it but have not read my booklet, nor know my person.” The Council deliberately allowed time to pass so that the execution of the order was not rushed; the banishment decree had been prepared on March 26, but the condemned man only thought of leaving the city in mid-May. The storm had dissipated for a time ¹). Even the Primarius had to hesitate to act against—

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jakob Böhme’s persecution in Görlitz. The previous page detailed his failed attempt to defend himself before the City Council against the accusations of the head pastor, Gregorius Richter. Böhme expressed his willingness to leave the city to find peace, as the Council offered him no protection from the public mockery and the pastor's insults.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

so many voices that rose in support of the maligned <term>theosophist</term> <note>A "theosophist" here refers to one who seeks direct knowledge of the divine through mystical insight, a title Böhme’s followers embraced.</note>, they still sought to brand him a heretic. The priest <note>Referring to Gregorius Richter, the head pastor of Görlitz.</note> had only the opinion of the mob on his side. In the streets, and even in his own house, Böhme had to endure insults, slurs, and mockery. He writes that although some said, "He is pious and a prophet," the common talk was: "He has the devil." ¹) His physical condition was still tolerable, for which he thanked God, but he was so thoroughly covered with "Pharisaic burrs" <note>An image suggesting that the religious authorities (likened to the biblical Pharisees) had attached sticky, prickly accusations to him</note> that the common mob hardly recognized him as a human being. Under such circumstances, staying in his homeland could not seem desirable to him. His steadfastness was put to a hard test, and the sensitive, nervous ²), and <term>sanguine</term> <note>In the medical theory of the four temperaments, a sanguine person was considered social and creative but prone to emotional shifts.</note> man was again close to losing his courage, just as he had the first time in 1613, when the breath of the Most High had seemingly left him. It was simply his way to view all his states of being in a religious light, as directly subject to the influence of the Godhead. Thus, he now sometimes pours himself out in bitter and almost melancholy complaints about the perverse state of Christendom; only now and then does a defiant joy awaken in him when, amidst the darkness of the hostilities, a ray of recognition kindly illuminated him and the shouting of the power-hungry priest fell silent for a time before the testimony of his friends.

¹) Missives <note>original: "Sendbriefe," the title of Böhme's collected correspondence</note> 59, 2.
²) How easily excitable and susceptible to shocks his nervous nature was is shown by his report when the Görlitz bridge collapsed before his eyes: "We cannot now go into the city because the bridge has fallen in with an entire trestle in the middle of the bridge, from the top down to the ground, which happened in a flash and a whoosh, as if someone had fired a gun, which I saw myself and felt God's great power almost supernaturally, which gives much cause for reflection, of which I wished to speak with you in person. (Happened on July 18, 1622.) For seeing such a thing struck me hard, as I was lying in the window not three <term>ells</term> <note>An "ell" was a unit of measurement roughly equal to 2 feet or 60 cm; he was about 6 feet from the collapse.</note> from the break, looking into the water; but I ran away in terror, only seeing it in a glimpse, and before I could look around, everything was at the bottom in an instant. (Missives 66, 8, 9.)

<page-num>105</page-num>

Thus he complains in the 58th Missive: "Alas, it is now only a mouth- and title-Christendom; the heart is worse than the heathens were. Alas, dark night! Where is Christendom? She has become a fragile whore! Where is her love? She has turned entirely to copper, steel, and iron! By what shall one recognize Christendom now? What difference has she from Turks and heathens? Where is her Christian life!" etc. His reputation had also drawn the attention of the court at Dresden, since in 1621 the <term>Lusatias</term> <note>Lusatia (Lausitz) is the region where Görlitz is located; it had recently been transferred from Bohemian to Saxon control.</note> had paid homage to the Elector of Saxony following the Accord of Dresden (they had been handed over to him as a pledge by the Emperor until they definitively became Saxon lands in the Peace of Prague in 1635). The mere fact that a shoemaker had become an author, and moreover had acquired a significant following, was bound to pique the curiosity of the high clergy in whose diocese he lived to the highest degree, to get to know this simple and wondrous man. — Furthermore, Richter had made a tremendous noise as if the theosophist were a loathsome heretic; the <term>consistories</term> <note>Administrative bodies of the Lutheran church responsible for maintaining orthodox doctrine.</note> at that time were very jealous of the orthodoxy of the subjects. Thus, there was one more reason to subject the shoemaker—who, seen through Richter's glasses, could already appear as a sect leader and threatened the church constitution through his mystical teachings—to an interrogation. Finally, the Saxon court, like most of that time, harbored that well-known preference for gold-making and alchemical arts. The Elector maintained a laboratory, the director of which was first Balthasar Walther, then Benedikt Hinkelmann, a physician and court chemist <note>original: "Hofchymikus"</note>, just as Emperor Rudolf had kept the great Kepler. Böhme now belonged among these alchemists; his letters provide unmistakable testimony that he held gold-making and the discovery of a material <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>A legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life.</note> to be possible. Hinkelmann had undoubtedly been in close connection with Walther, that zealous follower of Böhme; so it was perhaps believed at court that some benefit could be drawn from the gold-maker. Clergymen, scholars, and noblemen therefore called the wonder-worker, not to haul him before an ecclesiastical court interrogation, but to sound him out whether nothing but...

<summary>This page describes Jacob Böhme’s growing psychological and social distress in Görlitz, his lamentations over the decay of true Christianity, and the rising interest from the electoral court in Dresden. The court's curiosity was driven by both his religious notoriety and his perceived knowledge of alchemy and the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the mystic Jakob Böhme. Following his persecution in his hometown of Görlitz by the local pastor Gregorius Richter, Böhme has been summoned to the royal court in Dresden. This transition marks a shift from his local struggles as a shoemaker to his recognition as a significant spiritual figure among the Saxon nobility.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

rumors circulated as to whether he was an orthodox man who had been slandered, a heretic and false prophet, or if perhaps a "gold-maker" <note>an alchemist</note> was to be found in him. People seemed most inclined toward this last view. The court alchemist Hinkelmann offered him his home as a guest. Certainly, no one suspected the significance that posterity would find in Böhme; the most that was expected of him was likely a genius who had wandered onto wrong paths through religious "enthusiasm" <note>original: "Schwärmerei," a derogatory term used by the orthodox to describe radical spiritualists who claimed direct inspiration from God</note>. Böhme viewed the invitation in a rosy light; until now, he had only been invited to friendly conversations by followers and people who sought his acquaintance out of prior respect. Now, uplifted by his successes, he overlooked the immense distance between himself and that High Consistory <note>The governing body of the Lutheran church</note> at whose head stood a man like Hoe von Hoënegg; to him, the court appeared only as a glittering residence from which new recognition beckoned. — Even before March 15, he had received the invitation; before he had been summoned before the Görlitz Council by the Head Pastor <note>original: "Primarius," referring to Gregorius Richter</note>, and even before Richter sought to blacken his name at the court, his reputation had reached Dresden through his followers. Böhme had promised to follow the summons after the Leipzig Fair ¹); on May 9, he began his journey, which took him through Zittau. Some friends had invited him there, where he found a hospitable reception. A conversation was held, in which Johann Molinus, the doctors Johann Hartig and Matthias Rhenisch, and Mr. von Fürstenau, lord of the manor of Lissa near Görlitz, took part. They also expressed their disapproval of the Head Pastor’s behavior and remarked that they could find no "good spirit" in him. Finally, Böhme received from them a small sum for travel expenses <note>original: "Zehrpfennig"</note> and was asked to continue cultivating their acquaintance. Melchior Berndt, his host, accompanied him to Dresden. A few days later, both arrived in the Electoral residence city and were received by Benedikt Hinkelmann, who was a Christian gentleman ²), with all Christian love and

friendship. Through him, as the physician to the Elector <note>The prince-elector of Saxony</note>, Böhme was now introduced to the courtiers and high-ranking clergy; the long-unrecognized <term>theosophist</term> <note>one who seeks direct knowledge of the divine through mystical insight</note> found ever-widening interest, likely also exciting curiosity through his personal character. Almost everyone with whom he became acquainted read and loved the printed booklet, recognized it as a gift from God, and used it daily. In particular, the Electoral Councilor Joachim von Loß took an interest in him; Loß’s family had earned a significant name in Saxon court history, and he had personally been very active in the diplomatic negotiations regarding the Bohemian affairs ¹). First, on Whit Sunday <note>Pentecost</note>, May 25, he paid Böhme a visit at Hinkelmann’s residence, accompanied by the Electoral House Marshal, the Master of the Stables, the Grand Chamberlain, and another councilor; on the Thursday after Pentecost, May 29, he had Böhme and Hinkelmann picked up in a carriage to go to Pillnitz Castle. On both occasions, Böhme won great acclaim through his conversations ²). — The Saxon clergy, whose members were not all as <term>orthodox</term> <note>strictly adhering to the official, rigid Lutheran doctrine of the era</note> as Hoe von Hoënegg and Richter, had now also gotten hold of the printed booklet of the supposed heretic. Fortunately, the two writings *Of True Repentance* and *Of the Supersensual Life* contained nothing of Schwenkfeld’s teachings, nothing of <term>Theophrastic</term> <note>referring to the alchemical and medical theories of Theophrastus Paracelsus</note> chemistry or views on nature, nothing of the decided, blasphemous mysticism of Weigel, and finally, nothing philosophical. In them, Böhme had only described what he himself had experienced ³); it was merely a description of the surrender of the self, the doctrine of entering into God and the immersion of the entire will, of heavy repentance and the struggle of the Christian who wishes to attain divine intimacy; of the thorny path of one who, through the "I," is still tempted to fall away from the heart of God; and of the blessedness of union with the body of Christ. This was nothing heretical; it was the Lutheran doctrine of justification by faith, as Tauler and the *German Theology* <note>original: "deutsche Theologie," a famous 14th-century mystical treatise</note> had already held, from which Luther

<summary>Jakob Böhme travels to Dresden under the protection of the court physician Hinkelmann, finding favor among high-ranking nobles and officials who admire his spiritual writings. Despite his previous persecution as a heretic, his works are found to be consistent with the mystical traditions of Lutheranism rather than radical sectarianism.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of Jakob Böhme’s time in Dresden, where he found unexpected support among the high-ranking clergy and nobility, contrasting sharply with the bitter persecution he faced from the pastor in his hometown of Görlitz.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>

drew the internal reality of his reformation. It is no wonder that the clergy did not understand how the author of such orthodox writings could be so severely persecuted by another clergyman ¹); no wonder that Böhme himself heard how Hoë von Hoënegg <note>Matthias Hoë von Hoënegg was a powerful and strictly orthodox Lutheran court preacher in Dresden.</note> taught the doctrine of rebirth and the "inner man" from the pulpit in exactly the same way it was written in Böhme’s own little book ²). Then the Superintendent of Dresden, Aegidius Strauch, praised Böhme's work loudly and publicly; he even granted the author a conversation in his home ³). Generally, people looked upon the lampoon <note>original: "Pasquill," referring to the libelous pamphlet written by Böhme's enemy, Gregorius Richter</note> written by the Head Pastor with strangeness; "some even believed that the wretched evil spirit had dictated it ⁴);" it was despised by the priests, who said that Richter was stepping entirely outside the bounds of his office by writing it. 

The transition from such harsh persecution to such glorious recognition was too sudden for a man unaccustomed to the world, and Böhme could not restrain his joy. He exulted loudly and mocked his defeated opponent—a behavior otherwise not his way at all, and which can only be explained by the shock to his temperament, having been torn out of his everyday circles. He even threatened Richter with punishment from the Electoral Court, which was, in truth, still a long way off. "One could well bring him to justice for his lies and shameful slanders ⁵); now let him try to complain to the Electoral Councilors; I would stand before the law and confront him with his lies, which he has so venomously poured out over the congregation and in his lampoon against me ⁶). He brings shame and mockery upon the city of Görlitz—his mouth might even

be stopped entirely; it would be good for him to keep his mouth from blaspheming. If he only wishes to endure me, he has time to call a council and undertake a reformation."

Through his deep religiosity and his modest and quiet conduct—which was far removed from prophetic or millenarian <note>The belief in a future thousand-year reign of Christ on earth, often associated with radical religious groups</note> arrogance—Böhme won many friends. They promised him goodwill and advancement. Joachim von Loß suggested he wanted to promote him to the Elector ¹) and see to it that he received support and peace to further his talent. Daily, his "acquaintances" came for conversation; daily, the praise of his views resounded. The circle of his reputation in the capital expanded further and further. The peak of his good fortune seemed near when, after waiting for more than six weeks, a formal interview with the High Consistory <note>The governing body of the Lutheran church in Saxony</note>—perhaps even in the presence of the Elector, who had been traveling for a long time with many councilors—was announced ².

Unfortunately, Böhme’s Epistles, which provided such precise reports on the preceding events, leave us at this point. An unlucky star has presided over all the sources to such an extent that Cyprian, Erasmus Franzisci ³), and after him Engelschall, were able to claim that the entire proceeding of the colloquium <note>A formal academic or theological discussion</note> with the High Consistory was a fabrication. This doubt, however, is by no means justified. While Weißner, whose credibility is admittedly not beyond question, remains the only detailed source, Hegenicht made precise inquiries on the spot and found the fact confirmed ⁴). Weller, who can still be called a contemporary of Böhme, harbors no doubt about it ⁵), and the preacher Abraham Hinkelmann says he saw Jakob Böhme living in his father's brother's house during his youth ⁶).

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the radical elements of the Reformation and the shift from institutional church structures to internal, mystical experiences.</meta>

of their innermost experience, of their God Himself. Hence Karlstadt's <term>iconoclasm</term> <note>original: "Bilderstürmerei"; the practice of destroying religious icons and images, which Andreas Karlstadt advocated as a rejection of external ritual</note>; hence the refusal of the Lord's Supper by the followers of Schwenkfeld in cooperation with the Orthodox; hence the many other symptoms of the disdain for church communion. But the mystics also succumbed once again to the ethical-social drive; they wanted to represent the inner church already here on Earth. A state of religious fervor carried the enthusiasts beyond the boundaries of finitude, and they expected the reality of a thousand-year kingdom. Hence the movements of the Anabaptists, Thomas Müntzer, and other <term>Millenarians</term> <note>original: "Chiliasten"; from the Greek 'chilioi' (thousand), referring to those who believe in a literal 1,000-year reign of Christ on earth</note> ¹). Their wild, fanatical rage for the destruction of all social forms—upon whose space the new house of God was to be built—immediately entered into a life-and-death struggle with every established confession, because the general need for formal unification was not to be sacrificed for the sake of a few enthusiasts. Local coloring and concentration made them appear as a continuation of medieval sects, which the Reformation had revived like a fertile rain, even though they deny any connection with the Waldensians, Albigensians, and Patarenes <note>These were medieval groups often labeled as heretics by the Catholic Church for their alternative views on poverty and clerical authority</note>. The feeling of a decaying state and social life also influenced their political formation. Even Schwenkfeld, who does not exactly belong in the category of the Millenarians, leaned toward the formation of <term>conventicles</term> <note>private, often secret religious meetings outside the established church</note> as a result of the need for structure, and he advised church discipline and excommunication.

Both directions—the strictly mystical and the millenarian—were bound to lose their historical significance once the principle of tolerance and the emancipation of free inquiry had gained the upper hand over rigid belief in traditional formulas and over ecclesiastical fanaticism; once a mystical center—the union of the idea of God with humanity, the identity of both, and the possibility of identity for the entire human race—had been established, rather than perishing in blind and senseless ceremonial service and pure externality. Prophets, Millenarians, and those who "see God" become ridiculous figures and

<meta>footnote: ¹) See pages 2 and 3.</meta>

---

<sig>10*</sig>

<term>pious zealots</term> <note>original: "Muckern"; a derogatory term for people who practiced a private, often secretive or hypocritical form of piety</note> who have slipped out of a long-vanished time and escaped their own graves. The births of the becoming and the decay of a spiritual development become absurd when their one-sidedness steps forward with claims for recognition in classical times of harmonious, general education.

Mysticism is very closely related to <term>a priori speculation</term> <note>philosophical reasoning that proceeds from theoretical deduction rather than from observation or experience</note>. Both develop their results from the evidence of their own spirit, and yet mysticism in its pure form cannot be called a veiled speculation, although it can transition into it. The essence of mysticism is perception—that is, feeling or image; that of speculation is the concept. Mysticism, as the inner experience of communion with God, expresses itself either in the form of intense sensation—which experiences different phases and stages up to the highest bliss and, by virtue of the sensory-irritable nature common to most mystics, passes into an almost physical relationship. The mystics themselves describe it as a love relationship of the bridegroom to the bride—such as Eckhart, Suso, Tauler, Böhme, and the Moravians <note>original: "Herrnhuther"</note>—which carries a character that is sometimes masculine and sometimes feminine. In this state, even the agonies of Christ are reproduced in one's own self, and the annihilation of the self effects a kind of spiritual ecstasy. The highest, unnatural intensification of this feeling is the complete "becoming nothing" in God, where, however, through an involuntary reversal, the subject mistakes itself for the identity of God. Upon this were based the heresies of Weigel, Stiefel, and Meth, who claimed that man could quite literally become Christ. The other form of mysticism is internal contemplation, as the activity of the spirit that comes closest to feeling. Here it borders on pantheistic philosophy, with the difference that the latter comprehensibly deduces the <term>immanence</term> <note>the idea that the divine is present in the material world</note> or identity of God with the world, while the former, in the expansion of the subject over the objective world, finds the Godhead again through experience in feeling or in contemplation. Hence the so decidedly pantheistic views of mystics like Eckhart, Isaac of Stella, and Giordano Bruno; hence the same form of the <term>dialectical triad</term> <note>original: "dialektischen Dreitheilung"; likely referring to a three-fold progression of the soul or the Trinity</note> which has persisted since the oldest mystics of Christian times—Markos, Valentinian, and Dionysius the Areopagite—through all...

<summary>This page discusses the decline of radical Reformation movements like the Anabaptists and Millenarians as rationalism and tolerance grew, while also defining the psychological nature of mysticism as an "inner experience" of God. It highlights the transition of mysticism from pure feeling into a sense of identity with the divine, bordering on pantheism.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mystical thought, moving from the social and political movements of the enthusiasts to the intellectual evolution of mysticism into speculative philosophy, Gnosis, and Theosophy, specifically highlighting Paracelsus.</meta>

...runs through mystical teachings; hence the phases of the development of the Godhead according to stages, and hence the clearly emerging speculative thoughts, such as those held by <term>Eckhart</term> <note>Meister Eckhart (c. 1260–1328), a German theologian and mystic whose work focused on the individual's union with God</note>, that God is the Universal, the Everything, and therefore the Nothing, the substrate of all particularity; that the finite is an illusion and the Universal is Truth. Or as <term>Sebastian Franck</term> <note>Sebastian Franck (1499–1542), a radical reformer and humanist who promoted an internalized, "spiritualist" Christianity</note>—who developed speculation on the ground of mysticism most boldly—put it: that every existing thing is in God, that evil does not exist in itself because it occurs within God, that concepts are relative, and what seems to be God to us is the Devil before God.

But before mysticism becomes speculation, it still has a medium to pass through, which, like every true philosophy, is grounded in the <term>empiricism</term> <note>the theory that all knowledge is derived from sense-experience</note> of the external world. This forms the preliminary stage upon which conceptual thinking can first gain a firm footing: namely, <term>Gnosis</term> <note>original: "Gnosis"; from the Greek for "knowledge," referring here to mystical insight into the divine nature and the world</note> and <term>Theosophy</term> <note>original: "Theosophie"; meaning "divine wisdom," a system of thought seeking direct knowledge of the mysteries of being and nature</note>, predominantly the latter. The former brings the experience of the historical worldview to mystical consciousness, where it appears as an immanent idea of God; insofar as it has its home in the past, it is a sign of the desperate decay of a spiritual development. In contrast, Theosophy, which elevates the observation of natural science to an inner mystical experience, appears during flourishing periods of development. It is the fruit of the upward surge of general education—the thought of the unity of the existing world, of nature, and of the Self, which outpaces the laboriously acquired individual facts. Gnosis and Theosophy are no longer products of pure, primitive mysticism. They renounce the immediate consciousness of the immanent Godhead by seeking communion with it not solely through inner contemplation, but through the mediation of the sciences. Precisely because they are essentially a reflection of external empiricism within the subject (while forgetting this act of reflection), they form the transition to overt philosophy, which completely discards the self-deception of failing to acknowledge its own reflection. However, they are not yet philosophy as long as they present the truth of science—acquired before others—in mystical experience as the result of a temporary illumination of thought by the Godhead. From this middle standpoint, the form of Gnosis and Theosophy

<column-break/>

141
11

bears clear witness. The logical form of the concept is shunned; image and concept intersect, confuse, and obscure one another as a result of the attempt to identify a mystical wealth of imagery with naked speculation.

In the general upswing of spiritual endeavors during the age of the Reformation, a bold innovator also emerged in the fields of medicine and natural sciences in general. He attempted to overthrow the belief in the authority of <term>Hippocrates</term> and <term>Galen</term> <note>The dominant ancient Greek medical authorities whose theories had ruled medicine for over a millennium</note> and, instead of learned compilation from traditional maxims, broke a new path based on independent experience. He was a child of his time, grown from the people according to his spirit, and remained true to that folk-character, just as the ecclesiastical Reformation was in its origin. **Theophrastus Paracelsus Bombast von Hohenheim** was one of the first who praised Luther's "new light" because he fought so boldly against the Pope, the representative of rigid faith. His fundamental outlook was deeply religious and therefore reform-oriented; he transferred the work of the Reformation to his own science: Philosophy, Virtue, Astronomy, and <term>Alchemy</term> <note>original: "Alchymie"; the medieval precursor to chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter and the search for a universal cure</note> were to be the pillars of medicine, but Theology preceded them all. In a stroke of genius, he grounded experience through experiments; but because he was a theosophist—that is, he saw God as immanent in the manner of a mystic, specifically in the extension of the subject over the totality of the empirically given—he simultaneously outran experience. He elevated the subjective opinion within his mind to the same level of evidence as the scientific parts of his worldview. Thus, he shared the superstitions of his time because the Idea that animated the Whole appeared to him again in the most diverse natural things, albeit in a barbarously abstracted way. He leaped beyond the empirical knowledge of natural laws by following the precedent of the <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>original: "Kabbala"; a tradition of Jewish mysticism</note> (which had been made palatable to the German people by <term>Reuchlin</term> <note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a humanist scholar who introduced Hebrew studies and Kabbalistic thought to Christian Europe</note>) and <term>Agrippa von Nettesheim</term>, as well as that which constituted the superstition and black magic of the Middle Ages: the connection between the **Macrocosm** and **Microcosm** <note>The idea that the universe (macrocosm) is reflected in the human being (microcosm)</note>, between stars, metals, and human affections. Instead of being refined into individual concepts, the living idea of nature was personified for him into celestial bodies...

<summary>This page explores how mysticism evolved into Gnosis and Theosophy, bridging the gap between pure spiritual experience and formal philosophy through the study of nature. It highlights Paracelsus as a key figure of the Reformation who applied this mystical-scientific approach to medicine, blending empirical experiment with alchemy and Kabbalistic thought.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the examination of Paracelsus, specifically critiquing his linguistic style and diving into his alchemical and cosmological theories regarding the composition of the universe and man.</meta>

...and individualized: he knew spirits of mountains, forests, water, wood, smoke, and fire, as well as "stone-people," <term>homunculi</term> <note>artificial tiny human beings created through alchemy</note>, and every other monstrous birth that the wild imagination of a youthful race had hatched. Furthermore, Paracelsus was not truly original in that he did not abandon the learned, secretive style of his predecessors. Although he qualified as a lecturer in the German language—despite fierce resistance from the old school at Basel—and wrote the majority of his works in German, he still disfigured his writing with foreign-sounding expressions. These later became an unavoidable nuisance for German <term>theosophists</term> <note>original: "Theosophen"; philosophers seeking direct knowledge of the divine and nature</note>.¹) His efforts can be called a failure because, in his zeal for independent research and his abhorrence of formula-addicted compilations, he also rejected the scientific form of his own knowledge. This resulted in a multitude of inconsistencies and shifts in his doctrine, just as his broad, turgid style is the consequence of his boundless conceit.

According to him, true wisdom must be founded on the teachings of Christ: we reach him through faith; truth is found only in the eternal spirit, in the inner God. There we grasp the divine secrets, and therein we become masters of all other secrets. According to this law, his entire view of nature is intimately connected with Christian mysticism. This view of nature—which wavers between categorizing things in pairs (<term>dichotomy</term>) or in threes (<term>trichotomy</term>)—does not have the Godhead itself as its object (which is the domain of theology), but rather the **Macrocosm** <note>the "great world" or universe</note> and the **Microcosm** <note>the "little world" or man as a reflection of the universe</note>. At the foundation of both lies **Chaos**, which God created, and from which soul, spirit, and body were separated. The core of all matter is the **Quintessence** <note>original: "Quintessenz"; the "fifth essence," the purest spiritual core of an object</note>, which is bodily extracted from all plants²) and from everything in which there is life; it is the nature, power, virtue, and medicine. The chemical constituents of matter are **Salt** (*Sal*), **Sulfur** (*Sulphur*), and **Mercury** (*Merkur*). These are not literal salt, sulfur, and quicksilver, but rather their spiritual essence, which their element

produces itself; it is placed into three things: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.¹) From these three comes a "conjunction" that provides a body and unites their essences. Now, to know what form is: one is **Liquor** and is called Mercury; one is **Oiliness** (*Oleität*) and is called Sulfur; and one is **Alkali** and is from Salt. — The elements are to humans what the soul is; not all elements are in every being, but rather each thing has only one **Complexion** <note>original: "Complexion"; the specific temperament or elemental balance of a living being</note>.²)

The other part of the world is the invisible and intangible,³) the "star-spirit" or **Cagaster**, while a third component, the **soul**,⁴) resides in man. The human is "incorporated" together from all of these: the **Iliaster**,⁵) which is the sacramental or eternal, divine spirit or body.⁶) The creation of individual beings occurs according to the spirit that dwells within them;⁷) this is their **Impression**, their **Predestination**, which is determined at birth and depends on the stars—specifically, on the way their essences (which are identical to the metals)⁸) mix and take shape.

Man is also subject to this predestination if he allows his "star-spirit" to gain the upper hand. This star-spirit represents the reason—which we call "intellect"—that has produced the arts, crafts, and sciences that do not come from God.⁹) But if his **Imagination** forms itself in the soul after the image of God and holds sway, then he is free from this celestial "impression."¹⁰) Paracelsus expands at great length on the science of the lower spiritual component of man; his **anthropology** is rich with brilliant insights,

but also in superstitious absurdities <note>original: "Abgeschmacktheiten"</note>.¹) However, the soul is immortal and does not enter the earth with the body.²) The spirit is bound to the body,³) which becomes <term>Mumia</term> <note>the vital balsam or "mummy" substance inherent in the body</note> and, when separated from the spirit, naturally passes into decay.⁴) The peculiar quality of this view is its complete materialism and its focus on chemical natural processes; for even the soul—the sacramental, divine part—possesses or is something corporeal.⁵) This is specifically the <term>Iliaster</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for the first chaos or prime matter; here referring to the spiritual body</note>, the body of Christ. To achieve a mediation with the mystical view of Christianity, the Eucharist, and Baptism, the contrast between Good and Evil (which occupied <term>theosophy</term> <note>the study of divine wisdom through nature and revelation</note> so much) rests for him on this logical consequence of chemical processes.⁶)

Now, while the character of this theosophy—which <term>Agrippa von Nettesheim</term> <note>a famous 16th-century polymath and occultist</note> also pursued—presents a strict and almost slavish immersion into primary, unreflective empiricism in such a way that, on one hand, mysticism itself is subordinated to the practical purpose of medicine, and on the other, it displays a materialistic, physically mystical view of the world representing God; it becomes more spiritualized through the decisively emerging consciousness of mysticism, of divine <term>immanence</term> <note>the idea that God is present within the physical world</note>, and even identity in **Valentin Weigel** (1533–1588). Weigel was the pastor at Zschoppau in the Ore Mountains, whose writings only appeared after his death. He stands at the high point of theosophy. It is of no interest to trace how Weigel brings the teaching of <term>Theophrastus</term> <note>Paracelsus</note> to clearer expression: how the world also splits into three for him (soul, spirit, and body); how man originated from all three; how **Salt**, **Sulfur**, and **Mercury** are the components of things; and how the stars rule the middle world. All this is repeated with Paracelsian expressions. However, what must be emphasized is his theological-mystical view,

The fact of Christ’s appearance, the God-man, is repeated in all believers.¹) Man is not his own, but God’s; his judgment, recognition, knowledge, understanding, and ability are not of himself, but of God. Where God recognizes Himself in, with, and through man, there God Himself becomes everything in man; and that is called Christ, since God Himself is the man, as He sees, speaks, and acts everything.²) Christ is in us; to seek Him, one must not sail across the sea, nor wander to Rome or Jerusalem;³) rather, spiritually (even if not physically), God and I are One in will.⁴) This decidedly mystical consciousness now places weight on the fact that God, Heaven, and Hell are not capable of local determination; they are everywhere, for the theosophist sees God in the smallest thing of nature, and the mystic finds Him in the small human heart. Weigel is a theosophist and, at the same time, a decided mystic. God is not enclosed in any boundary <note>original: "termino"</note>,⁵) and encompasses all invisible things; outside of this God there is no spirit—neither angel nor devil, neither world, neither visible nor invisible—just as there is no physical creature outside the world. All things are eternally with God, and are God and in God.⁶) Good and Evil are only in the human subject; it is here that ethical-subjective mysticism claims its right. The creature is split; it carries Good and Evil within it—on one side, "doing-nothing-of-its-own," and on the other, the essence it has received.⁷) To become good, one must seek God; but He is nowhere but in the "I."⁸) He— <meta>The catchword "He" suggests the next page begins by expanding on how God reveals Himself within the individual.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Valentin Weigel's mystical theology, specifically his view on the internal presence of God, before transitioning to the life and conflicts of the Silesian reformer Caspar von Schwenkfeld.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>

know yourself, and so you find Him; in supernatural knowledge, God is the eye and the light within man, and if God is to work within him, then man must stand still and wait for God¹). Salvation lies in the imprisoned will²). God is the eternal rest, the bliss, and the end of all creatures³). As glorified beings in eternity, in the vast expanse, we shall soar with a supernatural, heavenly body, needing no external place nor element, as a spiritualized and divinized flesh of the body of Christ⁴). Weigel still stood entirely on mystical ground, within which he had attained clear intuition and perfected expression, but there was still a long way to go toward philosophical speculation; it can already be called progress that clarity had overcome the tangled lack of system and many inconsistencies. His successors were forced by this clarity to draw conclusions that dug the grave of <term>theosophy</term> <note>a philosophical system seeking direct knowledge of the divine through mystical insight</note>. Weigel’s teaching was also not without contradictions. The identity of God and the world, of Christ and man, could not be pursued to a satisfying goal even in a mystical way, much less satisfy ordinary consciousness, given that this mystical identity does not experience the divine vision face to face. The contradiction between good and evil remained unresolved within the boundary of the Godhead; with it, the doctrine of <term>predestination</term> <note>original: "Gnadenwahl," the theological concept of God's sovereign choice in salvation</note>; indeed, in its unresolved state, it did not even enter Weigel’s consciousness. Here, another direction now approaches; driven from conclusion to conclusion from the side of a strict churchliness, with a consciousness originally no more mystical than Luther himself had at the beginning. The Reformation had occupied itself with these very same questions, but sought to solve them not through mystical perception, but through a faith—first anticipated and later regulated by formulas—in the identity of God and humanity in Christ, in the sacraments, and in predestination. At the same time as Luther, a re-
