former had arisen in Silesia whose difference from Luther’s and Zwingli’s teaching was not yet apparent. **Caspar von Schwenkfeld**, Lord of Ossig, from an old noble family of Silesia (1493–1562), had chosen a literary career in his twenty-fifth year and worked with body and soul on the task of church reform. Out of enthusiasm for his cause, he traveled to Wittenberg in 1522, aligned himself closely with Luther, and entered into a friendly correspondence with him. In Wittenberg, he became acquainted with Carlstadt, Münzer, and the <term>Zwickau prophets</term> <note>a group of radical reformers who claimed direct revelations from the Holy Spirit</note>. In Silesia, Fabian Eckel, Valentin Krautwald, and Sigismund Werner in Liegnitz stood faithfully by his side. Schwenkfeld only learned Greek in his thirtieth year, but then studied the Church Fathers so diligently that, although lacking formal systematic theological training, he achieved an immense breadth of reading, as a result of which his works teem with the most diverse citations. Like Carlstadt, Arndt, Andreaͤ, and Gerhard, he belonged to the Lutheran mystics of the scholarly direction. Through his teaching on the Sacrament, he soon caused offense to Luther; the orthodox in Silesia made him hated, so that even his patron, Duke Friedrich II of Liegnitz, indicated to him that he should leave the country; he turned to Swabia, but soon fell into disputes with the Upper German Reformers; at the Strasbourg Synod, the breach with Capito, Bucer, and Zell first came to light; in vain did a meeting in Tübingen promise a reconciliation—in 1540, after the deaths of Friedrich II and Duke Ulrich of Württemberg, the <term>Schmalkaldic League</term> <note>a defensive alliance of Protestant princes in the Holy Roman Empire</note> directly labeled him a <term>Eutychian</term> <note>a heretical view that Christ has only one nature, which Schwenkfeld was accused of because of his "glorified flesh" doctrine</note>, <term>enthusiast</term> <note>original: "Schwärmer," a derogatory term for those who prioritized inner spirit over outward scripture and church structure</note>, and heretic. Persecuted, he died in Ulm in 1562 at the home of his friend Streicher. His followers in Swabia and Silesia called themselves **Confessors of the Glory of Christ**. Now, if his teaching—which stands in the middle between the three emerging confessions and, through its rational-resolving conception of Reformation questions, provides more satisfaction than Luther’s or the Catholic miracle-seeking view, or Calvin’s external conception—has found so little recognition, the reason lies in the rejection of church norms, the effectiveness of the preaching office, and the external sacraments as necessary signs of inner sanctification; in the mystical...

consciousness of subjective self-sufficiency, and finally also in the prolixity and breadth of his writing style. The common people also demanded external forms of identification and the organization of religious confession; like Weigel, Schwenkfeld also stood outside of this popular need. The few who felt satisfied by his teaching fell into an inevitable struggle with the masses of church-goers. Schwenkfeld assumed a spiritual body of Christ, the so-called <term>Glory</term> <note>original: "Glorie," referring to Schwenkfeld's doctrine of the "Celestial Flesh," where Christ's humanity is progressively divinized</note> ¹), which unites itself spiritually and inwardly with the believing soul of every Christian, provided the proper preparations have been made through faith and the mystical will ²). Ceremonies, dead faith in the word, and the external office of preaching do not help toward true salvation ¹). God has two wills, a hidden counsel and a revealed one; the former is unchangeable, and without it nothing happens, except for sin; according to it, God knows who shall be saved and who shall be damned; for He is omniscient; the revealed will shows what we should do and leave undone, and wills that all should be saved, and not the death of the sinner. With particular emphasis, Schwenkfeld teaches that God, Heaven, and Hell are not local, but are everywhere and nowhere, and that free inquiry and tolerance must be demanded; for whoever uses external force in spiritual matters is a heretic ³).

The consistent development of the doctrine of the Glory of Christ, the spiritual kingdom, and the <term>divinization of the flesh</term> <note>original: "Vergeltung des Fleisches," likely a transcription error or archaic variant for "Vergöttung," meaning the deification of Christ's humanity</note> leads to the same view of the threefold world and the life of man ⁴) (which Schwenkfeld also knows by this name), which must be called the fundamental feature of <term>theosophy</term>. He himself is still far from the systematic completion of his mysticism; his doctrine, laid out in countless writings, of which scarcely half are printed, contains only great fragments and starting points of a coming holistic conception, while he himself still places all emphasis on the views of the Lord's Supper, Baptism, and the office of preaching in detail, as the Church had challenged him through its polemics. In the divinization of the flesh of Christ, he aligns himself closely with

<meta>This page continues the discussion of 16th and 17th-century mystical movements, moving from the foundational ideas of Valentin Weigel to his radical successors and the eventual synthesis of these ideas in the works of Jakob Böhme. It follows the previous discussion of Caspar von Schwenkfeld’s theology of the glorified body of Christ.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>

Weigel (or reversed according to chronology); in the doctrine of the <term>immanence of God</term> <note>the belief that God is present within the physical world and the human soul</note> and of <term>election by grace</term> <note>the theological concept of how God chooses those who will receive salvation</note>, there were still unresolved contradictions and dark passages that had not yet received a perfected expression¹). Schwenkfeld still stands with one foot on ecclesiastical-theological ground; Weigel frees himself into a specifically mystical-theosophical consciousness and approaches <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>the precursor to modern science, seeking to understand the laws of nature as reflections of the divine</note>. His rigorous mysticism found its continuers in Esajas Stiefel, Ezechiel Meth, and the little-known Hans Weihrauch—whom Böhme mentions in his 12th <term>Epistle</term> <note>original: "Sendbrief," a formal public letter</note>—and in several other phenomena of the early 17th century, whose origin from the popular spirit Julianus de Campis indicates in the *Epistle of the Brotherhood of the Order of the R. C. (Rosy Cross)* <note>The Rosicrucians, a mysterious secret society claiming ancient esoteric wisdom</note>. "Let us consider the innovators, who are not as old as the old Hildebrand, Dannhäuser (of whom I previously sang a couple of songs) and other blowhards <note>original: "Schnauzhahnen," a derogatory term for arrogant or boastful people</note> who arrived many years after the Roman wars (p. 65)." Stiefel claimed that Christ was in him according to power and essence, and that he even saw him physically; consequently, he signed his name: God, Jesus Christ, Son of God and Mary, the living Word of the Father in heaven, full of his holy spirit, and my holy flesh and blood at his right hand in heaven and in all my limbs in all chosen, called unbelievers and also in this my dear bride, full of the holiness of our Father in us inseparably in all eternity²).

Meth called himself Ezekiel Christ, or the Angel Michael. Both considered themselves sinless and denied baptism, the Lord’s Supper, and the office of the ministry. Their following amounted to about

---

1) For example: "that there is found in the whole Christ no inequality, neither imperfection nor weakness, but pure power, strength, and divinity—yet I do not hold because of this, nor does it in any way follow, that Christ should consist of two natures, namely God and man, much less that his humanity was thereby mixed with the divinity or abolished and extinguished: the truth of the human nature in Christ was not emptied out, nor taken from Christ." *Epistles, Vol. II, Part 2, Vol. 49, p. 665.* <note>The author adds:</note> Where is any understanding to come from here? — Cf. *Of Faith and Knowledge of the Holy Trinity*, Vol. 1.
2) Arnold, *Theological History*, Part III, ch. IV.

<column-break/>

<page-num>151</page-num>

200 heads¹). — These are caricature-like developers of the Weigelian doctrine of the identity of God and man, which had not yet emerged in this absurdity due to a lack of emphasis.

All these threads and directions, which are represented primarily by <term>Theophrastus Paracelsus</term> <note>a famous Swiss physician and alchemist who blended science with mysticism</note>, Schwenkfeld, Sebastian Franck, and Weigel, were woven into a great painting by the <term>theosophy</term> <note>divine wisdom or mystical insight into the nature of God and the world</note> of Jakob Böhme. He possesses the Paracelsian <note>original: "theophrastische"</note> total view of the world; he combines it with Weigelian theology and finds the center that keeps the stages of Paracelsian materialism and Weigelian spiritualism in balance. He simultaneously connects to the Schwenkfeldian direction by drawing reconciling forms of faith from his doctrine, which he carries one step further in its consequences. From all three, he in turn rejects something: from Paracelsus, the crude materialism; from Weigel, the doctrine of the complete identity of the mystic with God; from Schwenkfeld, the doctrine of the <term>non-creaturely nature of Christ</term> <note>the idea that Christ’s humanity was not a "created" thing like other humans, but of divine origin</note>—for the same reason that Schwenkfeld cites²). His doctrine, which unites all mystical and religious currents that had emerged from the people and the learned—with the exception of "frozen" <term>orthodoxy</term>—simultaneously brings the mystical view to the surface of speculative thought. Philosophy breaks through the darkness of the mystical world in his work; the struggle between the contradictions of sensory-supersensory perception and the conceptual nature of the term constitutes the "barbarism" of his expression—the titanic wrestling for the utterance and resolution of inner conflict³). Even within this standpoint of mediation on the border between theosophy and philosophy, Böhme struggles through various phases until the consciousness of the necessity of thought, of the

2) Namely, "because otherwise he could not redeem humanity."
3) Or is it more fitting to describe not the naive struggle, but the pain he and the reader felt during it, as titanic?

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s theosophical system, moving from its historical roots in Weigel and Paracelsus to the internal contradictions of its metaphysical and ethical structures. It explores how Böhme attempts to synthesize the struggle between good and evil through a "dialectical process" that remains caught between abstract thought and vivid imagery.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>

in theosophy <note>a system of religious philosophy seeking direct knowledge of the divine through mystical insight</note> as an unprocessed, vivid image, now emerges like a flash of lightning. The metaphysical problem of the split into soul, spirit, and body has identified itself with the ethical problem of good, evil, and their struggle; both, in their unification, are brought toward a solution in speculation through the thought of the **dialectical process** <note>the process of resolving contradictions to reach a higher truth</note>—the internal necessity of opposites. However, this struggle achieves only fragmentary clarity; the dialectic is not yet able to overcome the sensuality and crudeness of the metaphysical (or here, still physical) intuition, and the ethical question is in danger of being explained away by a metaphysical truth. The logical process of thought still lies in a theosophical **twilight of the gods** <note>original: "Götterdämmerung"; here meaning a state of transition where clear logic is still obscured by mythic imagery</note>, because conscious deduction is replaced by a theosophical mannerism.

Finally, and worst of all, the developmental phases of the doctrine do not stand sharply apart but are layered together in the same writings—much as <term>plutonic</term> <note>forces originating from deep within the earth, like volcanoes</note> powers scramble <term>neptunian</term> <note>layers formed by water or sedimentation</note> strata—and thereby create a wretched confusion, worse than what the barbaric expression, torn between image and thought, would naturally cause. What can be distilled as common ground from this unsystematically transmitted doctrine is roughly as follows:

There are **three principles** of the deity and, along with it, the world in which it is <term>immanent</term> <note>existing within or remaining within; the idea that God is present in the physical world</note>: a positive one, a negative one, and one struggling between the two and composed of them:
*   God, Devil, World
*   Soul, Body, Spirit
*   The Kingdom of Angels, the Kingdom of Lucifer, the Kingdom of this World
*   Paradise, the Elemental World, the <term>Sidereal World</term> <note>the world influenced by the stars and planets</note>
*   Good, Evil, Free Choice

Man stands in all three principles; the mystery of his position in this triple life is solved simultaneously with the positing of the first three principles—the necessity of the distinction of the Idea and its opposite. Everything exists within these three distinct principles; **trichotomy** <note>a three-fold division</note> runs through all that exists, appears, and perishes. Man stands either under the influence of the elements, and is devilish and animalistic; or under the influence of the stars—the alchemical powers—and his own reason, in which case he is "sidereal" and lives in earthly arts and sciences, in the externality of religion, in heresy, and ceremonial service without penetrating into the inner sanctuary. This is only achieved through the third

---

<page-num>153</page-num>

world (or as will be shown later, the second principle): the soul-world. In the hands of the theosophist, this realm receives a breath of sensuality, similar to the works of **Theophrastus** <note>referring to Theophrastus Paracelsus, the famous 16th-century physician and occultist</note> in accordance with the demands of theosophy as a visual mysticism. The body of Christ is this soul-realm, and whoever penetrates it is a member of this body: here we see the transition to theological mysticism.

Through the very first division, man is given the free choice to follow one of the principles; but since the "Eternal Virgin"—the dominion of the divine principle—fled during the **Fall of Man** <note>the biblical transition of man from innocence to sin</note>, the idea of the divine soul had to regain dominion over the other two through the **mystical**, rather than the **historical**, Christ. By this, the "Virgin of Wisdom" was once again moved back into the realm of human possibility. The internal sacraments are the symbol of the mystical immanence of the body and soul of Christ, which are not strictly, and essentially not at all, distinguished from one another.

These are the characteristic foundations of the doctrine. In describing them, the appearance of **idealization** could not be avoided, because these are only the generalities; the specific details offer a **reason-killing study**, and the generalities themselves frequently alter their internal order and their relationship to the specifics. These details are for the most part poorly digested opinions of **Paracelsus**; some are further developed thoughts, such as the doctrine of the **signature** <note>the "Signature of All Things," the idea that every object in nature has a physical sign revealing its spiritual essence</note> and human knowledge of things through it; some are not Paracelsian but uniquely executed Kabbalistic <note>relating to Jewish mysticism</note> views, though of minor importance, such as the doctrine of the seven "source-spirits," dualities, or forces of nature.

The breakthrough of thought-clarity shows itself in the effort to establish categories, but their adventurous, crudely sensual form is at once a testimony to the weakness of the attempt and the inability of the mind to think in abstract concepts. Idealization was also unavoidable because Böhme's doctrine can only be made clear through its starting point and its historical completion—by looking at the moment its standpoint was eventually overcome—unless one wishes to fall into the same confusion as the theosophist himself.

The whole of Böhme’s outlook provides the...

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Jakob Böhme’s philosophical system, moving from the internal contradictions of his "triadic" logic to his role as a foundational figure in German thought. It examines Böhme not just as an individual, but as a representative of a specific German mystical tradition that balances folk-poetry with deep spiritual speculation.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>

inner satisfaction of synthesis into a unity of reason and totality. But because this unity of reason is nothing more than the necessity of a conceptual positioning in Three <note>referring to the Trinity or a triadic logical structure</note>, as the One of the highest contradiction, such a doctrine of principles can provide even less complete satisfaction; for, even setting aside the highest contradiction of the faculties of logical, ethical, and metaphysical thought—postulated as a unity—it is capable of causing only greater confusion.

Jakob Böhme was called the **German Philosopher** <note>original: "Teutonicus Philosophus"</note> by Balthasar Walther <note>a physician and early follower of Böhme who helped spread his ideas</note>, and he signed his name as such, perhaps not without a sense of self-worth. He seems to be worthy of the name. It is not merely that mysticism in its purest and deepest form—with all its shudders and delights—is rooted in the spirit of the German nation more than in any other people; in the Middle Ages, a mystic was called a **German Philosopher**. It is not merely that pervasive mood which, from the earliest days of our history until the most recent times, has unlocked a multitude of superstitious fables and magical arts, but also a rich glory of fairy-tale poetry, legends of elves, fairies, and giants, and a world of premonitions and boundless emotional feelings. Nor is it merely the religiosity implanted in the innermost soul, which so advantageously distinguishes the German from the externality, passion, and frivolity of other peoples regarding religion. It is not merely these admittedly peculiar German traits that secure an honorable name for the Christian thinker who knocked at the gates of German philosophy but sank down from exhaustion before they were opened. It is, above all, the premonition of the world-encompassing, cosmopolitan spirit, which in its grandeur elevates the "I" to the thought of the **World-God** or the **God-world** <note>the idea that God and the universe are inextricably linked</note>. Attempting to behold the Godhead beyond the individual experiences of science, he struggles to develop and comprehend the entire world from the necessity of human spiritual forms. This was a bold stroke of genius to build systems which must often vanish in their details before the scrutiny of precise science, but which, through their very anticipation, mark the path of science for centuries, providing researchers—through the divination of the plan—with

---

<page-num>155</page-num>

courage, perseverance, and hope for reaching the goal. Even if they were only moments in the struggle for a truth, they would be worthy of attention as errors recognized later. Now, it is true that Böhme neither cleared a path for a specific science or method, nor did he immediately raise the intellectual standing of his people in general; yet, even if unfavorable circumstances prevented him from reaching a higher place, he still possessed the titanic power of a spirit who—from a meager workshop <note>Böhme was a shoemaker by trade</note> and a modest natural environment—beheld the twilight of the light in a brilliant striving for a vision of the Godhead and the Universe within his own "I," in the depths of the human soul. He grasped with a lucky hand what more powerful scientific minds of their century would attempt to hold up as a goal two hundred years after him—attaining it, however, not through mystical vision, but through conceptual speculation and disciplined thought. Taken all together, this constitutes the "Germanness" of the theosophist <note>Böhme</note>, whose origin was the soul of the people, whose development was a struggle for concepts, and whose historical continuation and brilliant completion was the **Idealism of the 19th century** <note>referring to the great German philosophical movement of Hegel, Schelling, and Fichte</note>.

The few traditions regarding Böhme's external life provide neither a vivid picture nor sufficient clues and indications of the developmental stages in which Böhme's spiritual world changed and evolved. One might most readily expect an insight into the connection between his external appearance and internal life from the **Epistles** <note>original: "Sendbriefe"</note>, numbering 66, dating from the most significant years of his life, 1618–1624; but this hope is thwarted by the insignificance of the fragments, which may provide light here and there and general hints of external stimulations, but are unable to explain a necessary and grounded progression of his vision. Just as the entire mystical and philosophical current of thought in the literature of the 17th century stands in almost no noticeable connection or visible interaction with the politics and external history of the German people, Böhme's vision also structured itself without any demonstrable external influence. Therefore, the individual...

<summary>This page explores Jakob Böhme's title as the "German Philosopher," linking his mystical visions to the broader character of the German nation and its folklore. It argues that while Böhme lacked formal scientific method, his "titanic" spiritual intuition anticipated the complex systems of 19th-century German Idealism.</summary>

<keywords>Jakob Böhme, German Philosophy, Mysticism, Balthasar Walther, German Idealism, Theosophy, Folklore, 17th Century Philosophy, Epistles</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into Jakob Böhme’s intellectual legacy, moving from his identity as the "German Philosopher" to the practical difficulties of analyzing his writings. The author explores why it is so challenging to trace Böhme's spiritual and philosophical evolution through his bibliography.</meta>

Writings in their chronological order provide the only foundation for discovering a grouping, order, and structure in Böhme's spiritual development. However, even this effort is made difficult by various external and internal circumstances.

In itself, it lies in the nature of **theosophy** <note>Theosophy here refers to Böhme’s "divine wisdom," a mystical system seeking direct knowledge of God and the universe</note> that it does not become conscious of the reasons for its own transformation in the same way as a philosophical thinker. A philosopher might be moved to set up a new system through the consistent pursuit of previously undiscovered contradictions; theosophy, however, forgets the "becoming" of thoughts and intuition at the moment of their birth and throughout the duration of their existence. Böhme's doctrine displays various "finished" theosophical paintings whose plans and figures are thrown over one another, without one being able to determine with certainty why the artist consciously changed his view, or why he did not erase the old painting so that it would no longer shimmer—and so blindingly at that—through the new one. 

A second difficulty lies in the chronological sequence of the writings, which is not precisely established and perhaps cannot be. The **Epistles** <note>original: "Sendbriefe"</note> do not provide sufficient information on this either. It is not enough that the smaller writings are only vaguely dated by year; even the significant work *On the Birth and Designation of All Beings* <note>Original: "Geburt und Bezeichnung aller Wesen," better known by its Latin title "De Signatura Rerum" or "The Signature of All Things"</note> is uncertainly dated to either 1621 or 1622, depending on when the 12th Epistle was written. This is all the more important because a change in Böhme's perspective occurred between the years 1620 and 1623. Not many writings originate from the year 1621, while 1620 and 1623 mark the high points of Böhme's career as a writer.

Finally, Böhme's language—aside from the essential confusion of systems mentioned above—is an almost insurmountable obstacle to separating his views with certainty and judging them as one would with philosophers of pure thought. It is not without justice that people praise his beautiful sentence structure, the grace and brevity of his expression, and the depth of feeling and spiritual enthusiasm in the theosophist's language when compared to his contemporaries. Yet this favorable view, which anthologies are only too eager to spread emphatically, finds

<column-break/>

application only in the relatively rare passages of specifically mystical content. In these moments, enthusiasm and the bliss of "divine visitation" transport the theosophist's perceiving "I" out of the common mood and carry it on wings toward heaven. There, he moves through beautiful poetic images—sometimes naively among lilies, roses, angels, and sweet fragrances; sometimes in a prophetic tone, darker but more magnificent in its premonitory form, soaring above the limits of finiteness and comprehensibility. Or, in the speculative sections, where theosophy—unfaithful to itself—produces sharp, lightning-like thoughts such as <term>I-ness</term> <note>Böhme’s term "Ichheit," referring to the self-centered will or ego</note>, **comprehensibility**, **idea**, **primal causes**, **quality**, **essence**, and **separability**. These are expressions that breathe the spirit of a highly advanced intellectual activity, leaving one hardly able to suspect how quickly dark night follows the lightning. 

A third form of his language is the occasionally soulful "theological-mystical" style, which stands closest to the first but bears the sober, oppressive stamp of dependence on an external deity. A breath of the kind found in even the most insignificant sermons and ordinary prayer books wafts from this form of expression. To this category also belong the sanctimonious maxims and mottoes, for example: "Our Salvation in the Life of Jesus Christ in us," which Böhme usually placed as an abbreviation <note>Original: "U. H. J. L. J. C. J. U." for "Unser Heil im Leben Jesu Christi in uns"</note> at the top of his Epistles; or: "May the open fountain of the grace of Jesus Christ be with us," etc. Only one more step and one stands in the midst of orthodox formula-seeking. Only in the rhyme:

> "To whom time is as eternity,
> And eternity as time,
> He is delivered
> From all strife."

lies a deeper and more thoughtful meaning.

A complete "stone desert," however, is the language of his alchemical, astrological, and physiological views. In their specialization, these cause an unbearable headache through their indigestibility, their subtle and imagined splitting of images, and their insoluble, absurd contradictions. They represent a continuous, mind-numbing confusion of the image and the reality it represents...

It also critiques his varied linguistic styles, ranging from sublime poetic mysticism to dense, "indigestible" alchemical jargon.</summary>

...of thought, bringing into the world a flourishing nonsense by tossing together different concepts and keeping identical ones apart—a style that must be overcome with patience or skipped over page by page if one does not wish to be surrendered to a total disgust for Böhme’s entire theosophy <note>Böhme’s "theosophy" refers to his mystical system of divine wisdom, which combined theology with a unique form of natural philosophy</note>. Unfortunately, these later sections are of such great size that in many writings, the grains of philosophical gold can only be found with great effort, and thus the overall study of the doctrine has significant obstacles to overcome.

It then begins a chronological analysis of his writings, starting with his first work, the *Aurora*.</meta>

...and Company in 6 volumes (7 parts). Vol. 1 (Parts 1 and 2) *The Way to Christ* and the *Aurora*; Vol. 2 (Part 3) *The Three Principles of Divine Essence*; Vol. 3 (Part 4) *The Threefold Life of Man*, the *Signature of All Things* <note>original: "Signatura Rerum"</note>, and *The Election of Grace* <note>original: "Gnadenwahl"</note>; Vol. 4 (Part 5) *The Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note>; Vol. 5 (Part 6) the smaller writings: *True Psychology* <note>original: "Psychologia vera"</note>, *Theosophy*, *Pansophic Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Pansoph."</note>, *Six Mystical Points* <note>original: "Sex pta Myst."</note>, *Six Theosophic Points* <note>original: "Sex pta Theosoph."</note>, Table of the Principles, *The Key* <note>original: "Clavis"</note>, and the Epistles; Vol. 6 (Part 7) the polemical writings and instructional letters to Kaym.

Werdenhagen translated *True Psychology* into Latin and published it in 1632 in 24mo <note>A very small book size where the paper sheet is folded into 24 leaves</note> in Amsterdam.

The *Signature of All Things* saw a French translation, which was not as successful as the two English ones published by William Law and Taylor in London in 4to <note>A medium book size, "quarto"</note> concerning the "177 Questions." — Abraham von Beyerland translated the complete works into Dutch and published them between 1634 and 1642.

In 1700, an excerpt appeared titled "Jakob Böhme Epitomized for the Simple."

In 1718, "Hearty Excerpt from Jakob Böhme’s Writings," Amsterdam ¹).

### First Stage of Development. Preponderance of the Ethical.

#### 1. Day-Spring at its Rising, or Aurora.

This writing—the first, and one might say, the youthful work of the author—is to be completely separated from those that followed, as a period of five years, which passed in inactivity, significantly changed the theosopher’s <note>A "theosopher" in this context is a mystic seeking direct knowledge of the divine through spiritual intuition</note> way of looking at things. It was begun on January 1, 1612,

---

¹) The author had before him the editions by Glusing, *Theosophia Revelata* <note>Theosophy Revealed</note> from 1715, and by Schiebler 1831–1847. The page numbers for the citations from the *Aurora* refer to the Schiebler edition. The arrangement of chapters and paragraphs does not differ between these editions.

<page-num>161</page-num>

and continued into the next year¹); in 1619, Böhme added an epilogue as the 27th chapter. The *Aurora* is a great curtain-painting: it hints at what the stage will bring, but it conceals the clarity and life-character of the drama; a veil still lies upon Böhme’s world of thought. What later comes close to being a pure expression of a concept still surges here in the colorful, misty images of a childlike way of imagining things. Regarding what Jakob Böhme says in the introduction § 107 about the first 7 chapters: "they are dealt with quite simply and palpably²)" <note>Böhme uses "begreiflich" (comprehensible) to mean "tangible" or "sensory"</note> concerning the essence of God and angels through parables, so that the reader might move from one stage to another and finally reach deep meaning and the true foundation. In the 8th chapter, the depth of the divine essence is introduced, and from then on, the further one goes, the deeper it gets. Many a *species* <note>original Latin term for a "kind" or "outward appearance"</note> is often repeated and described ever more deeply for the sake of the reader’s—and also my own—stubborn comprehension;" this can be said of the entire work: namely, that it is written in parables.

The beginning is made by a vision in the introduction, wherein Böhme spreads out his views on the history of the development of the human race. *Theosophy*—the contemplation of the Godhead in the world and the struggle and search for it—is seen as the core task of life; within it, all other concerns for body and spirit must be absorbed. Its object is compared to a tree standing in the garden of the world, whose fruits are human beings. The good and evil qualities struggle within this tree, and so that the fruits—the humans—should not fall entirely to the latter, God frequently moved nature to open up the knowledge of good and evil to mankind. Finally, he let a small branch grow—Christianity—which became a great tree from the rising to the setting of the sun <note>From East to West</note>. The fruits of the tree—here Böhme clearly leaves the earlier parable—were eagerly gathered by the nations; however, the prince of wrath <note>A reference to the dark, stern aspect of nature or a personification of evil/Lucifer</note> came and sold them for money and mixed false ones among the genuine ones. But when God...

---

¹) Epistles 2, 11.
²) i.e., sensory, *palpabilis* <note>Latin: "palpable" or "tangible"</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the bibliography of Böhme's works and begins an analysis of his first major text, the Aurora (1612), describing it as a foundational yet immature work filled with parables and "misty" child-like imagery. It introduces Böhme's metaphorical "tree of the world" to explain the struggle between good and evil in human history.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s first work, the "Aurora" (Day-Spring). ...also allowed those people to prosper who were granted the false fruits, because He saw their hot desire as a good tree. The Prince of Fierceness <note>original: "Grimmigkeit," Böhme’s term for the wrathful, harsh aspect of nature and divinity</note> planted a tree in the Midnight <note>symbolizing the North or the realm of darkness</note> and caused much damage with its fruits (perhaps the Papacy?). But then God let a little green branch sprout in the Tree of the Midday, and the Light of Nature opened up: the knowledge of both qualities <note>the fundamental forces of good and evil; perhaps referring here to theosophy?</note>, and then the tree in the Midnight withered—until finally the Prince of Fierceness began the last battle; but the Ancient One moved Himself, in whom is all power and every creature, and Hell was banished.

In a similarly unclear and confused manner—as the parable is carried out, where one does not know whether the *point of comparison* <note>original: "tertium comparationis," the shared trait in an analogy</note> is meant to be theosophy, Christianity, Protestantism, the world, or something else—the content of the *Aurora* is then announced according to Philosophy, Astrology, and Theology.

> “Through philosophy, the divine power is treated: what God is, and how nature, stars, and elements are constituted in God's essence, and from where all things have their origin, how heaven and earth are constituted, as well as angels, humans, and devils, plus heaven and hell, and everything that exists in both qualities in nature, from a true ground in knowledge of the spirit, an impulse and will of God. — But also

> “astrology treats the powers of nature, the stars and elements, how all creatures have come from them, and how they drive, rule, and work everything, and how evil and good are worked through them in humans and animals, from which it comes that evil and good rule and exist in the world, and also how the kingdoms of hell and heaven consist within them.”

In philosophy, too, Böhme wanted to speak of both qualities in nature, namely of good and evil; here again, heaven and hell appear in both sections, and are also found in the third; for:

> “through theology, the kingdom of Christ is treated: how it is constituted, how it is opposed to the kingdom of hell, and also how it fights and struggles in nature with the kingdom of hell, and how humans through

<column-break/>

163

> faith and spirit can overcome the kingdom of hell and triumph in divine power and attain eternal salvation, bringing it forth as a victory in the struggle. Also how man, through the effect of the quality itself, throws himself into corruption, and finally how it will come to an end with both.”

Although much in this section is tangled together across the main groups, one can nevertheless recognize that these three parts correspond to the three principles as they appear in Böhme’s other writings. The author follows a different worldview here and admits himself in various places that the *Aurora* was a very deficient work, which he wrote as a young child in the *mystery* <note>original: "Mysterio"</note> of God, who had only then come to school and was still clinging to the *ABC* <note>1)</note>. “The book is still to be better explained in many places, because the concept was still stuck within it <note>2)</note>. The great secrets still lie very deep therein in the *mystery*, and were well recognized by the *author* <note>original: "Autore"</note>, but it was not quite possible for the reason to grasp it the first time; although it was recognized in the depths, the *author* was still quite unaccustomed to this, and when heavenly joy met him, he simply followed the spirit <note>3)</note>. The *Aurora* still rises out of childhood <note>4)</note>.”

The philosophical genius in the “Day-Spring” <note>Aurora</note> still has a good deal of twilight; the childlike, fantastical presentation of a divine comedy, where angels play music and devils roast, prevails here more than elsewhere; it is the author’s "infant school" with undeveloped pictorial representations. The devil and the realm of darkness are still more “tangible” here than in Holy Scripture; and the philosophical concepts, as they later emerge—especially the traces of the later-appearing metaphysical-dialectical progress (Being, Non-being, Becoming; Desire, Desired, Will)—appear here in personal, human-demonic forms.

***

¹) *Apology against Tilke I, 356.* ²) *Epistle 8, 61.* ³) *Epistle 10, 36.* ⁴) *Epistle 12, 66.*

<summary>This page analyzes the structure of Böhme's "Aurora," dividing it into Philosophy, Astrology, and Theology, while noting Böhme's own later assessment of the work as an immature, "childlike" first attempt at describing his divine visions.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Aurora, Day-Spring, Philosophy, Astrology, Theology, Theosophy, Fierceness, Grimmigkeit, dualities, divine mystery, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s first work, the "Aurora," specifically contrasting its early theological structure with his later, more developed system involving the "Ungrund" (the Abyss) and the three principles.</meta>

But it is not only this childlike conception of the kingdoms of Heaven and Hell that distinguishes the *Aurora* from the writings of later periods (though it still appears there from time to time); rather, the very foundation of the system is clearly different. While later on, the Kingdom of Joy—the second principle—develops out of the *Abyss* <note>original: "Ungrund," Böhme's term for the primal, groundless void preceding God's self-manifestation</note>, the Nothing, and both together bring about the third principle, Nature or the world—where the realm of Satan is nothing more than God’s fire of wrath, a phase or side of the divine power—here, a God is presented who is the ruler of all natural forces, the seven qualities. His Heart, the cohesive center, is called the Son, and His working and governing is the Holy Spirit. His seven qualities form three angelic kingdoms; one of these becomes the Kingdom of Darkness through the will of its sovereign <note>referring to Lucifer</note>, and now struggles with the Kingdom of God in the newly created world, which God brought forth in the place of the fallen Lucifer. Here, the second principle of falling away, that which develops itself—Satan, Lucifer, the principle of evil—is what in later works becomes the fire of wrath in the Abyss, the first principle.

It is to be regretted that the author did not finish the work—not because of its philosophical value, but because of the total difference in its perspective.
> “The devil intended to call it a day <note>original: "Feierabend machen," to stop work for the evening</note> with this, because he saw that the day was about to break within it.”
Half of the section that one could call "Astrology" in Böhme’s manner, and the entire "Theology"—amounting to some 30 quires—are missing. Thus, the depiction of the good principle in the Godhead is absent, even if we have many descriptions of the ideal world in the peculiar confusion of the book (which is also counted toward the Godhead in a broader sense); only an incomplete depiction remains of the actual corporeal world, of nature, and of human life, which stands under the stars <note>referring to the astrological influences Böhme believed governed physical life</note>.

First, Böhme speaks of the qualities—the natural forces, as one might translate the word, which he himself explains as: the mobility, welling up, and driving of a thing. But the qualities, as they are named here, are not yet as distinct in number and form as in the later writings. Here, there are cold, heat, bit-

<column-break/>

<page-num>165</page-num>

terness, sweetness, sour, and harsh qualities. If one immerses oneself in the details of this natural-philosophical view, one falls into a labyrinth of nonsense that repeats itself again and again, almost with the same words. How the qualities then form a *curriculum* <note>original: "Curriculum," a course or cycle</note>—an interaction similar to the planetary system, which is also compared to the turning of a wheel—in which the actual power of God exists (here in the *Aurora* a natural force, but ruling just as much in the world of forms, or ideas, as one must suspect¹, as in finite nature), is what the second chapter teaches. In the third, the Godhead is broken down into the three forms: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.
> “The Father is the self-sustaining essence of all powers; He is Himself Everything in eternity, and everything that exists has come from His power, which goes out from Him; the Father is the total divine power from which all creatures have come; He has no beginning, nor end. The Son is in the Father, the Father’s heart or light, and the Father begets the Son from eternity to eternity forevermore, and the Son’s power and brilliance shine back into the whole Father, just as the sun shines in the whole world². If the Father were now to stop begetting, the Son would be no more; and if the Son no longer shone in the Father, then the Father would be a dark valley, for the Father’s power would not rise from eternity to eternity, and the divine essence could not consist³. The third person in the essence of God is the willing spirit, which arises from the rising in the *terror* <note>original: "Schreck," Böhme's term for the sudden flash or "shock" of birth or transition from darkness to light</note> where life is born; it surges in all powers and is the spirit of life⁴; the Holy Spirit forms everything and builds it⁵; he is the spirit of the first will toward nature.”

From the 4th to the 17th chapter, the subject is the three angelic kingdoms, which are formed as modifications of the Godhead (unclear in what manner) from the seven qualities that he occasionally presents and explains. In one place, angels and qualities are the same to him⁶. Without stating it openly, he apparently forms this tripartite division according to the three principles; yet a won—

---

¹) Aur. 14, 10. ²) Aur. 3, 33. ³) Aur. 23. ⁴) Aur. p. 276.
⁵) Aur. p. 279. ⁶) Aur. 14, 10—13.

<summary>This page analyzes the structural differences between Böhme's "Aurora" and his later works, specifically focusing on his early conceptualization of the Trinity and the "seven qualities" as natural forces.</summary>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s "Aurora," transitioning from the theological structure of the Godhead to the specific cosmological and astrological details Böhme used to describe the creation and the fall of Lucifer.</meta>

There is a peculiar contradiction that Böhme avoids exposing—and is even less able to resolve—in that he mixes the psychological division of the Godhead according to Power, Will, and Deed (Father, Son, and Spirit) into his system. Lucifer represents at one time the realm of the Son, and then again represents negation and the evil principle; as the Son then takes Lucifer’s place, a conflict arises in the central realm. In this section, one finds a rich selection of biblical-poetic and childlike, fantastic passages, particularly where he speaks of the creation of the world, of substance, essence, and character, of "God-likeness," and of the location and the entire "body" of an angelic kingdom and the fall of the angels. The 9th Chapter, concerning the delightful, friendly, and merciful love of God—the great heavenly and divine mystery—is partly a repetition of what was said before and partly an anticipation of what follows. The 10th and 11th Chapters deal once more with the "qualities" or "source-spirits" <note>original: "Quellgeister," Böhme's term for the seven fundamental forces of divine nature</note>, especially the 6th and 7th: Sound or Tone (*Mercurius*) and the concept that forms the entire "body" (*Corpus*) of the qualities, the *Salitter* <note>Böhme's term for the divine "earth-matter" or the crystalline salt of heaven; a word he likely corrupted from *Sal Nitri* (saltpeter) and the "language of nature"</note>. The former word is derived from Mercury, the latter from *Sal Nitri* <note>original: "Sal Nitri," Latin for saltpeter</note>, both distorted according to Böhme's "language of nature."

Chapters 12–17 deal with the kingdom of Lucifer and his fall. That realm is also formed from the seven qualities, but through the fall, they have become hard, fierce, and thorny. The author’s naive outbursts against the malice of the devil for having fallen away from God are very frequent.

> “Why do I still complain about you? You stinking goat! Oh, you cursed, stinking devil, how you have corrupted us! What excuses will you make, or what will you throw in my face? You say that if your fall had not happened, then man would never have been conceived: Oh, you lying devil; even if that were true, the *Salitter* from which man is made—which is also from eternity, just like that from which you are made—would have stood in eternal joy and clarity, and would nonetheless have ascended into God, and would have tasted the delightful love in the seven spirits of God, and enjoyed heavenly joy. Oh, you lying devil, wait just a little longer; the Spirit will 

---

¹) *Aurora* 14, 10–13.

<column-break/>

reveal your shame; wait just a little while longer, and you will have had your parade¹).”

Is this not a clear testimony to how much the subject matter of Böhme's writing was a matter of his heart?

From Chapters 18–28 is the beginning of "Astrology," the presentation of the relationships, properties, and workings of the stars. The "qualities" are the source-spirits of God, and from these, the stars are corporately formed; the "stellar body" forms the matter of nature, the real world. Therefore, the creation of the world is treated here. However, Böhme only gets as far as the fourth day, when the stars are created, which gives him the occasion for detailed descriptions. On the whole, he adheres to the creation story of Moses; however, where the Bible will not fit into his intuition, he either denies that Moses was the author, or attempts to lift the "veil" that lies over the eyes of the "good man Moses," or outright accuses him of errors².

The view that the stars form the foundations of natural life and also exert a direct influence on human beings is drawn from Paracelsus <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist whose ideas on the correspondence between the macrocosm (universe) and microcosm (man) deeply influenced Böhme</note>. Therefore, to gain a base of operations for further natural philosophy, Böhme first speaks at great length about the Sun³, the planet Mars⁴, Saturn, and Venus⁵; Jupiter is mentioned in passing as the originator of sensuality⁶.

This summary of contents, however, only hints from afar at what is in each chapter, for the material lies in an immeasurable, confused jumble; Böhme himself admits that he has repeated much. He was never so completely a master of expression as to be able to present his thoughts clearly and in an orderly fashion—least of all in the *Aurora*. Here, one has the utmost difficulty holding onto certain ideas in order to visualize the skeleton or the plan of the whole; one cannot always trust the headings: in the details, it surges even more wildly. To get a single thought onto paper, the theosophist <note>A term for a mystic who seeks direct knowledge of God and the cosmos through inner revelation</note> repeats himself...

---

¹) *Aurora* 8, 100.  ²) See *Theosophische Sendbriefe* I. p. 61. *Aurora* 19, 79; 20, 2; 21, 1.
³) *Aurora* 25, 42.  ⁴) *Aurora* 25, 72.  ⁵) *Aurora* Chapter 26.  ⁶) Chapter 26, 18.

<summary>This page analyzes the structure and themes of Böhme's "Aurora," highlighting his struggle to organize his mystical visions of the qualities of God, the fall of Lucifer, and the astrological creation of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Aurora, Lucifer, Qualities, Source-Spirits, Salitter, Astrology, Paracelsus, Creation Story, Theosophy</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the stylistic analysis of Jakob Böhme’s "Aurora," contrasting its early linguistic simplicity with the more complex terminology Böhme adopted later in his life under the influence of his more educated friends.</meta>

...in the most obscure manner the earlier material, then wraps it in a multitude of parables, and once it has become thoroughly unclear through this process, it finds its fulfillment.

Meanwhile, the author is not yet very deeply immersed in theophrastic nature-wisdom <note>original: "theophrastische Naturweisheit," referring to the mystical philosophy of Paracelsus (Theophrastus von Hohenheim) which blended chemistry, medicine, and theology</note>, so that the great flood of barbaric foreign words—the use of which his friends particularly advised—does not yet seem so overwhelming. How Böhme’s later language differed from that of the *Aurora* is shown by the subsequently inserted remarks from the revision of 1619, which have a peculiar similarity to the *style* of Franckenberg <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652), a mystic and Böhme’s primary biographer</note>, which is sharper and clearer, but drier, flatter, and less refreshing than Jakob Böhme’s own poetry. Along with the foreign words, the actual *alchemy*, *magic*, *astrology*, and other incomprehensible secretive posturing has remained absent; only once in the introduction is there a completely *crass prophecy*, and a second one (Chapter 26, 120), which was already cited along with its explanation in the 28th Epistle to *Thirnes* in Part I, pages 50 and 51. But even if the *Aurora* is one of the most "German" writings of the Teutonic Philosopher <note>original: "Teutonicus Philosophus," a Latin title commonly given to Böhme to honor him as the first great philosopher writing in the German language</note>, there is nonetheless a lack of German philosophical conceptual terms, though not a lack of glorification of the mother tongue as a "natural language."

> “Therefore we also pray in the Our Father: Give us our daily bread; so that the same tone or word ‘Give,’ which we thrust out from our center <note>original: "Centro"</note> of light through the animal <note>original: "animalischen," meaning the vital or living spirit</note> spirit from the mouth into the divine power, shall help to form our daily bread in the divine power as a co-shaping or co-birthing, which the Father then gives us for food ^1).”

> “In the beginning God created heaven and earth. These words one must actually consider for what they are, for the word *In* <note>original: "Am"</note> takes hold in the heart and leads to the lips, where it is captured, and goes sounding back again to the place from which it issued. — The word *beginning* <note>original: "Fang"</note> travels quickly from the heart out to the mouth, and is also held at the back part of the tongue by the palate, and when it is released, it gives a further quick pressure from the heart out to the mouth. That signifies...

---

^1) *Aurora* 13, 110.

<column-break/>

...the rapid rejection of the refuse of the devils together with the corrupted *Salitter* <note>Böhme’s term for the divine "earth-matter," derived from saltpeter</note>, for the strong and rapid spirit thrusts the breath <note>original: "Oden"</note> strongly from itself, and retains the correct tone of the word or the pronunciation at the back of the palate, that is, the true spirit of the word. This signifies that the corrupted wrath is eternally thrust out from the light of God, but the inner spirit, which is burdened with it against its will, shall be set back into its first house. The last emphasis, *ning* <note>original: "Ang," the final syllable of *An-fang* (beginning)</note>, signifies that the innermost spirits in the corruption are also not entirely pure, and therefore require a cleansing and a consuming of the wrath in the fire, which will happen at the end of this time ^1).”

In this *style*, Böhme exerts himself for many pages to make a fifth type of biblical interpretation clear to his reading public, in addition to the four types customary in the Middle Ages <note>The four traditional senses of scripture: literal, allegorical, moral, and anagogical</note>, without posterity being able to gain the same insight into this mystery as he seems to have had; it is unlikely that our knowledge would have been enriched by such a science, even if it had been communicated. Here, certainly, that Socratic word is least appropriately applied—which, once judging Heraclitus’s writings favorably, has been applied by lovers of Böhmean theosophy to his writings and their dark passages with more sentimentality than criticism: namely, that what is easily understandable contains excellent sense and grand truths, and as for what is misunderstood and incomprehensible, one must live in the conviction that it is of equal value regarding the depth of its wisdom. Better to say: for its abyss.

### Second Stage of Development. Predominance of Physics.

It has been noted previously how extremely difficult it is to strictly separate periods, phases of development, groups of thoughts, and various directions from one another...

---

^1) *Chapter* 18, 8. *Compare* 19, 48 *and* 110; 20, 90.

<summary>This page analyzes Jakob Böhme's linguistic style in the "Aurora," specifically his "language of nature" where he interprets the phonetic sounds of words like "Give" and "Beginning" as mystical revelations of divine and demonic processes. The author criticizes Böhme's obscurity and compares his early work to later, more technically complex revisions influenced by his followers.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Aurora, Teutonic Philosopher, nature-wisdom, language of nature, Salitter, Franckenberg, biblical interpretation, phonetics, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s theological evolution, moving from his early work "Aurora" toward his more systematic metaphysical writings. It explores how Böhme’s understanding of the Trinity and the nature of evil shifted as his "spirit-view" matured.</meta>

...since in almost every writing several modes of thought run through one another, and only a predominance of one or the other mode of representation can be recognized in the fragmentary masses. But if we try to distinguish the stage of the *Aurora* from the following period through a characterization—and despite its immense confusion, it deserves consideration—one can describe its way of looking at things as ethical. Evil stood un-reconciled against Good; the struggle occurred in the third principle. The irreconcilability and immediacy of the opposites showed itself in the personification and in the coarse sensuality of the conception. The strong ethical feeling, which asserted itself in poetic praise of the angels and in harsh verbal battles with the devil, did not notice the logical and metaphysical contradiction that had arisen within the first principle's indeterminacy of the always-good *God* the Father-Deity and in the creation of *Christ* in place of the second negative principle within itself. The third principle arose through the renewed activity of the first in the hardened and scorched *Salitter* <note>original: "Salitter," a term Böhme used for the divine substance or "spiritual earth" of the heavenly realm, often likened to saltpeter but representing the fundamental building blocks of existence</note>.

1) The struggle of this perspective could not stop here; the first principle was soon presented as negative: Hell. The previous second principle aligned itself with this because the good deity could no longer maintain its exclusive character in the first principle, as the pure principle of *Christ*, the highest positive, had moved into the second position. Such a contradictory split within the second principle had a greater right to be considered primitive than the opposition between the now-indistinct idea of the positive deity of the first principle and the second, which encompassed *Christ* and *Lucifer*.

2) In the *Aurora*, the doctrine of the Trinity could not be integrated. The third principle received nothing from it, nor did *Lucifer*. *God the Father* was the first principle, *God the Son* the second—partially. The inequality of this distribution demanded further development.

Therefore, another series of ideas fought its way through, showing a more objective, metaphysical way of looking at things, in which evil is no longer presented as something absolute and un-reconciled, but rather receives immanence in the God-

<column-break/>

head. The metaphysical unity of the overall vision is thereby established. This developmental stage is mainly marked by the "*Three Principles of the Divine Essence*," "*The Threefold Life of Man*," and the "*40 Questions on the Human Soul*." A predominance of natural problems, *alchemism*, and *physiology* is noticeably in accordance with its character. The transition to the next stage is formed by the "*Incarnation of Christ*" through the momentary flashing of consciousness, which develops the next stage from this very perspective.

### 2. The Three Principles of the Divine Essence.

Encouraged by *Balthasar Walther* and *Christian Bernhard*, *Böhme* began this writing, as mentioned above, at the beginning of the year 1618 (or the end of 1617) and writes of it in the 2nd *Epistle* <note>original: "Sendbrief," a formal letter or treatise</note> in October 1619 as a completed work. From the 10th *Epistle*, we learn that he composed this and the two following writings within nine months; a productivity that, as with the writers of the Middle Ages, can only be explained by the fact that *Böhme* only put the vision of his spirit to paper without being concerned about external form, solely for the purpose of a *memorial* <gloss>1</gloss>), which he also assures here—although he did not expect, as with the *Aurora*, that it would not come to the light of publicity; and without having to process an extensive literature with an expenditure of time and effort like today's writers. It is not surprising that *Frankenberg* <note>Abraham von Franckenberg, Böhme’s biographer</note> reports that *Böhme* wrote everything in one go, without crossing anything out. The form of his style also bears witness to this.

In the introduction, the theosophical <term>theosophy: "divine wisdom," referring here to Böhme's specific mystical system for understanding God and the universe through direct spiritual intuition</term> task of life, the knowledge of God, is established. One achieves it not only through the contemplation of nature but also through entering into the interior and experiencing one's own spirit in mystically immersed vision. The inwardness of the...

<summary>This page analyzes the transition in Jakob Böhme's philosophy from the "ethical" struggle of the Aurora to a more metaphysical system in the Three Principles of the Divine Essence. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Jakob Böhme’s evolving theology, specifically focusing on how the "ethical" struggle between good and evil described in his earlier work, the *Aurora*, begins to transition into a more structured metaphysical system involving three distinct "principles."</meta>

Knowledge of God and self simultaneously effects the inner ethical sanctification of the whole person. Just as **Schwenkfeld** <note>Caspar Schwenkfeld (1489–1561), a German theologian and spiritualist who emphasized the "Inner Word."</note> in the treatise "On the Christian Man, Report from Holy Scripture, p. 877 in the 1st part of the Christian Orthodox Books and Writings" sets the task for man, "he should first recognize what he is by nature, that he is miserable and a sinner; secondly, that he should learn where all such things come from, namely from **Adam’s** transgression, so that he may become a different person and learn to recognize God in Christ;" and just as **Weigel** <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588), a predecessor to Böhme whose mystical writings emphasized internalizing the divine.</note> expresses even more clearly than **Böhme**: that nothing is more beneficial and useful than to know oneself ¹); so **Böhme** expresses even more clearly than **Weigel**: "Whoever looks upon and knows himself must know the eternal God, whose image he bears and from whom he has come; thereafter he also knows the great world out of which he is made according to his mortal part; he also knows the *stars* <note>original Latin: "Astra"</note> and **celestial bodies**, also the four **elements**, and all creatures and thus man, for man is created from the clod of earth, that is, from the whole world (*Glory to God alone, etc.* <note>original Latin: "Soli Deo Gloria etc."</note> Part II, Chapter 1, p. 15): in the same way, **Böhme** develops the idea that nothing is more useful and necessary than to know oneself, what man is, out of what or from whom, and for what purpose he was created; in such earnest contemplation he will find that he can look into the heart of all things, what **essence**, **power**, and **property** they have; moreover, God has given him understanding and the highest sensibility, so that he can know God his Creator—what, how, and who He is, also where He is, and from what he was created and has come. He hereby learns to know himself, what sort of **matter** and **being** he is, and also how he was created out of God’s being. In such contemplation, divine knowledge and love for God grow in man, as between children and parents: so if we now know ourselves, how we are created in God’s image, being, and property out of God’s own being, then it is indeed right that we live in His obedience and follow Him." The highest task of life for the mystic is contemplation, and by immersing himself in himself, he—who has narrowed himself to the Absolute and expanded himself to the world—finds therein his

God, who inspires and sanctifies his being. Thus, contemplation becomes the **rebirth** of the ethical self.
Now the new **theory of principles** is developed, which results from such contemplation:
"That there is a difference, that the **evil** is not called and is not God, that is understood in the first *principle* <note>original Latin: "Principio"</note>, for there is the earnest source of **fierceness** <note>original: "Grimmigkeit," Böhme's term for the wrathful, dark, or consuming quality of the first principle of existence</note>, according to which God calls himself an **angry**, **fierce**, **jealous** God; for in this **fierceness** consists the **original source** <note>original: "Urkund," implying a fundamental record or primal evidence</note> of **life** and all **mobility** ¹). There is indeed no difference in God, but when one searches for where **evil** and **good** come from, one must know what is the first and original source of **wrath**, and then also **love**, because both come from one **original source** ²)." And so the following **principles** ³) arise: I. the **darkness**, which longs for the light, is the first principle; II. the **power** of the **light**, through which you see in the mind without eyes, is the other principle. III. And the longing power "which goes out in the mind and draws to itself and fills itself, from which the **material** **body** grows, is the third *principle* <note>original Latin: "Principium"</note>;" these are the **principles** in man, but the **Godhead** has exactly the same; Böhme cites man only for the sake of illustration.
**Böhme** says indeed explicitly ⁴), that he cannot say otherwise than that the first *principle* is God the Father himself; but yet **evil** arose from the first *principle*, not through a fall, but in the **fierceness**, in the **darkness** of the first *principle*. In the Godhead of **wrath**, through its sting, the **light-sun** of the **Son**, of the **Heart**, has risen; the opposites of the first two **principles** mutually condition one another, and to speak in a creaturely way ⁵), one is the first, the other the second. The doctrine of the **Trinity** now identifies itself easily with the three principles ⁶), because **evil** is no longer regarded as being outside

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme's complex theological system, transitioning from the internal self-knowledge of the believer to the structural "Principles" and "Qualities" that Böhme uses to describe God, nature, and humanity.</meta>

is considered as standing in the Godhead. It is only to be regretted that the fundamental views of the *Aurora* <note>Böhme’s first major work, *Aurora: Daybreak in the East* (1612), which laid the groundwork for his mystical philosophy.</note> appear alongside the indications of later developmental stages, alternating with the view just presented; these views do not lose their viability over time, which causes infinite confusion.

The first chapter deals with the first principle, which still contains the realm of darkness latently or *in potentiality* <note>original Latin: *potentia*</note> within itself—the "primordial God." Chapters 2 through 4 deal with the second principle, the realm of light, the true eternal nature, or (according to our modern linguistic usage) the positive Godhead of Christianity. The doctrine of the qualities <note>Böhme’s "Seven Qualities" or "Source-Spirits" are the fundamental forces that drive all existence, from harshness and bitterness to light and sound.</note> is repeated in Chapter 2, Section 12, and Chapter 3, Section 18, without being in the least bit clearer than in the *Aurora*, since the change in the fundamental view—aside from the pervasive obscurity of these fragmented ideas—branches down even to this point. While all seven qualities in the *Aurora* worked together in every principle, here the first principle only goes up to the fourth quality¹), because at the fifth quality—which Böhme calls "sweet love"—it must have occurred to him that it could not possibly belong to the principle of "fierceness" <note>Böhme uses the term *Grimmigkeit* (fierceness or wrath) to describe the dark, rigorous aspect of God’s nature.</note>, just as the fourth quality must occupy a middle position because "fire" both burns and shines.

Chapters 5 through 27 deal with the third principle. This section takes up so much space partly because Böhme, in all his writings, loves to give free rein to his fantasies more carelessly the further he progresses, so that prolixity, repetition, obscurity, and confusion—in short, the weakness of the entire work—increase. It is also partly because the field of investigation—nature and the human world—is infinitely wide and rich, while the first two realms are actually naked ideas, populated only with abstractions from the perceptions of the third principle, as Böhme himself suggests in the heading to the fifth chapter:

---

<gloss>
...other principles would break forth and rise in the birth of the Son, then the Father would be a dark valley; thus you see indeed 2) that the Son—in his birth—shuts out another principle and reconciles the angry, fierce Father, making him lovely and merciful, and is a different person than the Father. — Now you see indeed 3) how the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son, etc.

<page-num>175</page-num>

->“Of the third principle or creation of the material world together with the stars and elements, through which the first and second **principles** are more clearly understood.”<-

Chapters 5 through 9 recount the creation of the world: how out of God arose the *Matrix* or *Fifth Essence* <note>original Latin: *Quinta Essentia*</note> or *First Matter* <note>original Latin: *Materia prima*</note> (in Chapter 5); how from these the elements, the heavens, and the beasts above and below the water were formed (Chapter 6); and of the true and the material heaven, the true Paradise, and the Garden of Eden (Chapter 9). Without disregarding the Mosaic creation story <note>The traditional account of creation found in the Book of Genesis.</note>, following the sequence of the text in **Genesis**, Böhme nevertheless prefers the mystical-pantheistic view of the world’s origin found in the Gospel of **John** ¹).

In Chapters 10 through 19, one finds the doctrine of the three principles in man, starting with the pre- or supra-worldly ideal state: the nature of **Adam** before the Fall. Chapters 13 through 16 in particular offer a rich abundance of anthropological, physiological, and psychological views; yet the effort required to wind through the undigested Paracelsian <note>Refers to the influence of Paracelsus (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist whose complex terminology Böhme adopted.</note> views is immense. Regarding the creation of man and woman (Chapters 10 and 13), the fall of **Adam** (in Chapter 12 and repeated in Chapter 17), the struggle of the principles in **Adam** before and after the Fall (Chapters 11, 32–40 etc., and again in Chapters 15 and 16), the sleep of **Adam** after the victory of the first principle (Chapter 12), man's birth and procreation (Chapter 14), their expulsion from Paradise (Chapter 18), and the "lesser" and "greater" death (Chapter 19)—wondrous things are proclaimed here.

The next chapters (20–27) first describe the struggle of the three principles in earthly life and depict the consequences for human culture. This is framed, of course, from that theological standpoint according to which the inventions and progress of the human spirit—sciences and arts—are brought forth by the activity of the third principle (the world of the stars). A strict distinction is made between this earthly bustle—cherished by "letter-scholars" <note>A derogatory term Böhme used for theologians who focused on the literal text of the Bible rather than spiritual experience.</note>, the worldly-minded, heathens, and "mouth-Christians"—and the holy life lived in the "divine..."

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme's theological works, specifically examining the chapters of his middle period where he describes the cosmic struggle and the development of humanity through his "Three Principles." The previous page ended with a discussion of how the "qualities" of God’s nature shift between the first and second principles.</meta>

...the Heavenly Virgin." <note>Böhme uses the "Heavenly Virgin" or "Sophia" to represent Divine Wisdom and the pure, spiritual mirror of God.</note> Yet the doctrine of the development of humanity through the will of the Godhead and the internal struggle—through the **Incarnation of the Word** <note>The "flesh-becoming" of the Logos.</note>, that saving act before and after Christ which is understood in a mystical way as being sublime above all concepts of time and place—still reveals a very refreshing and exalted conception. This is because one can clearly recognize the effort to banish all abrupt, inorganic development from the midst of human history, such as the orthodox myth of redemption seems to represent historically.

In Chapters 20 and 21, the division of humanity is described according to the **Churches of Abel and Cain** <note>In Böhme’s system, Abel represents the spiritual, light-oriented person, while Cain represents the worldly, "fierce" or self-willed person.</note>, according to spiritualized mystical Christianity and Heathenism, as it has persisted from Adam’s fall—that is, from the beginning of humanity—until the separation of principles at the Last Judgment. This is a historical-philosophical view that we are accustomed to finding not only among the mystics but among the majority of medieval historians. From Chapters 22 to 27, the work deals with Christ’s work of redemption, rebirth, Christ’s testaments, his temptation, penance, suffering, death, and exaltation to God, and finally the Last Judgment, according to a mystical interpretation of Holy Scripture. An exhortation to mankind to find themselves in God, and a parable executed quite poetically, concludes the whole; in the Appendix, the doctrine of the **triple life of man** <note>The idea that a human being lives simultaneously in three worlds: the dark world of fire, the light world of God, and the external world of the four elements.</note> is briefly repeated as it was depicted from Chapter 10 onwards; and, what is very necessary, an apology for the "confusion of language" is added in the opening sentences, with the explanation:

> "Because in our previous writings there are several words in which we might be misunderstood by the reader, we wish to report somewhat more thoroughly to the reader of the second book of our writings, so that he may take note of the sense and spirit of what is meant when one speaks of the divine life, and therein does not employ merely one kind of word and name, since sometimes three or four different names understand exactly the same single divine life."

In fact, not only this writing but this entire stage of development is rich in the fruits of interaction with **Paracelsians** <note>Followers of the physician-alchemist Paracelsus, who blended chemistry, medicine, and mysticism.</note> and **Schwenkfelders** <note>Followers of Caspar Schwenkfeld, who emphasized the "inner word" and the spiritualized flesh of Christ.</note>. The character of the

<page-num>177</page-num>

physical asserts itself down to the smallest detail; and unfortunately, Böhme has not yet processed or overcome the teachings of Kober, Walther, and others here. The foreign words of the Basel medical school <note>Referring to the terminology popularized by Paracelsus and his followers.</note> accumulate; the mystical ideal world merges with Paracelsian materialism and with the Schwenkfelder legends of the Kingdom, the Body of Christ, and the human-sensory deification.

Böhme followed Paracelsus so faithfully in the natural-philosophical, alchemical, and astrological sections that one can only understand Böhme’s confused words with the help of <term>Theophrastian</term> <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim was the birth name of Paracelsus.</note> explanations. There, the Theophrastian expression <term>limbus</term> <note>original Latin: *limbus*; used here to mean a primordial spiritual substance or reservoir of matter.</note> reappears; as do <term>Iliaster</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for the primeval force or "star-spirit" that gives form to matter.</note> and <term>Ternarius Sanctus</term> <note>The "Holy Ternary," which Böhme uses to describe the spiritual "body" or manifested realm of the second principle (Light/Heaven).</note>. The latter is the body of the second principle, which is conceived entirely spiritually as the "Realm of Joy," or "Divine Pomp," or "Angelic Body." The sensuality of the ideal world (behind which lies nothing but the despair of attempting to grasp concepts as such, leading him to reach for images) designates the Body of God. In Böhme, the soul of the man naturally partakes in the *limbus* from which it arises; the female element is the <term>Matriz</term> <note>The Matrix or "womb" of nature.</note>, called <term>Kagaster</term> and <term>Aquaster</term> <note>Paracelsian terms for spiritual or elemental substances that are not purely material.</note> by Theophrastus, and named the same by Böhme. A troublesome complication now arises from the fact that in Böhme, the *Ternarius Sanctus* signifies the Holy Kingdom of God (namely, Christ’s Body), and the seed or spark of this kingdom escaped from man to God during the Fall; only Christ could reunite it with humanity, just as the mystical act is nothing other than the entering into the Godhead, into the second principle, the sinking into the *Ternarius Sanctus*. — In Theophrastus, the *limbus* remains; only the *Matriz* belongs to women. Thus the question arises: from where does Böhme provide women with entrance into the *Ternarius Sanctus*?

Paracelsus explains what the "Tincture" signifies¹):

> "The Tincture, which stands in the first <term>Arcanum</term> <note>A "secret" or vital principle in alchemy.</note>, gives its behavior like the *Rebis* <note>original Latin/Alchemy: *Rebis* ("two-fold thing"), referring to the alchemical hermaphrodite or the union of opposites.</note>; it tinctures the body in such possibility, takes away its 'un-being' <note>its corrupt or non-essential nature.</note> and turns it all into the most pure, noble, and permanent."

Accordingly, "Tincture" is the concept of the transition from the first or third principle into the second, divine principle, as is also clear from the passage...
