<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s theological system, transitioning from his "Three Principles" to the specific influences of alchemical thought and the radical reformer Caspar Schwenckfeld on his later chapters.</meta>

178

as shown in the Appendix to the *Book of Azoth* <note>original: "Liber Azoth," an alchemical text attributed to Paracelsus</note>, page 15: "the Word *fiat* <note>Latin: "let it be done"</note> keeps life fragile, unless it be tinctured a second time."
In a similar way, Böhme explains the **Tincture** <note>In alchemy, a substance that transforms metals; for Böhme, it is the spiritual force that permeates and enlivens nature</note> in Chapter 12, 27:
> "the Tincture is a thing that separates, and brings the pure or clear out of the impure; it brings the life or essences of all spirits up to the highest degree; yes, it is the cause of the shine or luster; it is the cause that all creatures see and live, etc."

Also, Paracelsus's obsession—which was rooted in folk superstition—to transform not only those gifted with individual existence, but also qualities, concepts, and complexes of things into "spirits," returns in Böhme; the greatest of these is the **Spirit of the World** <note>original: "Spiritus Mundi"</note>, who rules over the world of stars, a descendant of the great Platonic **Demiurge** <note>The "artisan" or creator figure of the physical universe in Platonic philosophy</note> (Cap. 20, 11. Cap. 24, 40. etc.).

Since Schwenckfeldian views <note>Referring to Caspar Schwenckfeld, a 16th-century reformer who emphasized internal spiritual experience over outward ritual</note> also appear here for the first time in greater detail, it is to be concluded that the association with Carl von Ender was not without influence on Böhme. The final chapters are rich in views on the **Incarnation of Christ**, on baptism, the Lord's Supper, and penance; on the internal and external church.

Without claiming that in the darker passages of Chapters 21, 62, 77, 78, etc., where the incarnation of the Godhead is discussed, Schwenckfeld's doctrine can be recognized literally—regarding the divine body of Christ, which supposedly lost its humanity through the resurrection but had to be divine to redeem mankind—one still cannot overlook that the **Holy Ternary** <note>original: "Ternarius Sanctus," Böhme's term for the manifestation of the Trinity in the spiritual world</note> signifies the same as the **Glory of Christ** in Schwenckfeld's work; and that the **Divine Virgin** in Böhme, who withdrew into the Holy Ternary after Adam's fall, is nothing other than Christ, the Son of God, in Schwenckfeld.

"In the Lord's Supper, Christ gave the body from the holy element, the Holy Ternary (Cap. 23, 11.), which is incomprehensible and immeasurable (23, 13.), so that the soul receives the Godhead, and the new man receives Christ's true body and blood—not as a thought in faith, but in essence, incomprehensible to the outer man—

<column-break/>

179

who is only the outer bread and drinks outer wine, which is not the body of Christ; and in baptism, one receives the water of eternal life and the external water" (Cap. 23, 50—23.); likewise Schwenckfeld says: "In the Lord’s Supper, the body of Christ is present, as heard, but not in the bread, nor there upon the altar, just as it can be enjoyed or received neither in, with, or under the bread¹); there are two types of bread and drink²), the spiritual cannot be eaten except for eternal salvation, and it is eaten in no other way than through true living faith³); Christ does not distribute himself through the external sacramental bread, but through the power of his almighty Word⁴)". Just as Schwenckfeld explains the **Sitting at the Right Hand of God** as the entering into the essence of God⁵), so also does Böhme: "the Right Hand of God is where Love quenches Wrath and brings forth Paradise; that is rightly the 'Right Hand,' where the wrathful Father is called God in the love and light of His heart, which is His Son; but if you ask: Does Christ sit, or stand, or lie down? Then you ask like the donkey asks about the sack—how the tailor had made the sack that he must carry; yet one must give the donkey fodder so that he may carry the sack longer⁶)."

From this mysticism, it is not far to a breaking through of **Pantheism** <note>The belief that God and the universe are one and the same</note>; and after overcoming alchemical barriers, one certainly rests with pleasure at such passages which, because they strive to strip away the imagery of the senses, carry the inkling of a philosophy in themselves, and have disposed more recent critics—especially **Hegel**—in Böhme's favor.

> "The true Heaven, where God dwells within, is everywhere in all places, even in the midst of the earth. He encompasses Hell, where the devils dwell, and there is nothing outside of God: for where He was before the world's creation, there He is still, as in Himself, and is Himself the **Essence of all Essences**; everything is born from Him and bears witness to Him⁷)." —

It highlights Böhme's transition from mystical metaphors to a foundational philosophy that later influenced thinkers like Hegel.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s theological system, transitioning from his "Three Principles" to the specific influences of alchemical thought and the radical reformer Caspar Schwenckfeld on his later chapters.</meta>

"Behold, God the Father is everywhere, and His heart and light are everywhere in the Father; as He became man, so His humanity has indeed been everywhere that His divinity was: for you cannot say that there is a place in heaven or in this world where God is not; where the Father is, there is also His heart in Him, and there is the Holy Spirit in Him" <note>original: "Cap. 23, 10"</note>.

Yet Böhme still struggles terribly with his expression; his striving for **dialectics** <note>The art of investigating truth through the resolution of contradictions</note> is crowned with little success. Only occasionally is there an echo of a dialectical process, such as in the analysis of the Will in Chapter 14, 63. On the other hand, in terms of feeling, the intimacy of his mysticism threatens to turn into a certain sentimental softness. The fiction that the Spirit of God—the "Idea" of man that rose to divinity after the Fall and then descended into the man Jesus—is a pure **Virgin** <note>Referring to "Sophia" or Divine Wisdom, a central figure in Böhme's thought</note>, so that in Christ or the heavenly soul we have a bride to love, is not far from the views of Meister Eckhart or the **Pietists** <note>A movement within Lutheranism that emphasized individual piety and lived religious experience</note> at the turn of the 17th and 18th centuries, who so loved to think of themselves as the "bride of the bridegroom Christ." This kind of "spiritual lust" was already exploited by Scheffler <note>Johannes Scheffler, also known as Angelus Silesius (1624–1677), a famous mystic poet</note>, and upon Böhme’s foundation a type of poetry was built that reveals itself here—though in prose—in long love-dialogues between the heavenly Virgin and the Spirit or Reason as the bridegroom <note>original: "Cap. 12, 38; 21, 62"</note>. Scheffler also follows Böhme in the idea that this Virgin could easily become the Virgin Mary; Böhme struggles in vain with Protestant zeal against the veneration of Mary and the saints <note>original: "Cap. 18, 67"</note>. Mysticism involuntarily unites opposing confessions, so much so that even **Purgatory** is not rejected by Böhme. He explains it by the falling away of the "third principle" <note>the material, physical world</note> at death, where a struggle between the other two (light and darkness) arises; "here is the great life and the great death, for the soul must go into one of them, and that is thereafter its homeland" <note>original: "Cap. 19, 16 and 18, 104"</note>.

If we consider along with what has been said that the **Language of Nature** <note>A mystical "original" language where the sound of a word reveals the essence of the thing named</note> also makes its appearance in several places <note>original: "Mercurius Cap. 1, 13. Immanuel-Jesus-Christus Cap. 22, 84 and following"</note>,

<column-break/>

then the praise Böhme grants his own writing cannot remain unchallenged, as when he calls it in his 12th Epistle (67) a "key and alphabet" for all those who wish to understand his works; although one must still prefer it over his earlier work *Aurora* <note>original: "Morgenröthe" (The Morning Redness), Böhme's first major work</note>. Day has broken, but Böhme never truly saw the sunlight.

Despite the theosophical-alchemical passages, the language achieves a sense of harmony and beauty through its sentence structure. However, as a result of leaning on the "Oriental" perspective of Holy Scripture through the lens of magic and astrology, this book contains too much allegory, poetry, and absurdity to serve as a primary source for researching Böhme’s philosophy. Neither clarity, nor order, nor conceptual precision is found here. Yet a plan—if not a full system—can be seen through the many digressions. The fact that alchemy has not yet reached its peak in this writing is one of its merits, though that is not to say it is not the work most distorted by Böhme's friends <note>A reference to later editors who may have added their own interpretations to Böhme's manuscripts</note>.

### 3. On the Threefold Life of Man.

Written at the end of 1619 and the beginning of 1620. This work applies the doctrine of the three principles, as we know them for this period, to the life of man—a view that was already present in the biblical division of man into soul, spirit, and body, and found in Paracelsus and most mystics. Therefore, man is also called a **microcosm** <note>a "little world" that perfectly reflects the "macrocosm" or the entire universe</note>: he is not only an image of the macrocosm but is also "corporealized" from the <term>limus</term> <note>Latin: "mud" or "clay," referring to the "dust of the ground" from which Adam was created</note> or earthy clod of all three worlds: the realms of light, darkness, and the stars. He can live according to all three principles. Now he needs the grace of God to be absorbed into the <term>Ternarius Sanctus</term> <note>Latin: "Holy Triad" or "Holy Ternary," representing the divine manifestation within the material world</note>; the testaments of Christ must come to the aid of the struggling soul to enable its **resignation** <note>original: "Gelassenheit," a key mystical term for yielding one's will to God</note> and immersion in God. The doctrine of the principles marks the beginning:

> "The first will is not called God, but Nature; the other will is called A and O, Alpha and Omega, beginning and end, from eternity to eternity. And in the first will, nature would not be manifest; the second will makes it manifest..."

<vocab>Jacob Böhme, Scheffler, Angelus Silesius, Mysticism, Dialectics, Microcosm, Macrocosm, Holy Triad, Language of Nature, theosophical, alchemical, Pietism, Purgatory, Epistles</vocab>

<summary>This page analyzes Jakob Böhme’s mystical concepts of the "Divine Virgin" and the soul's "spiritual bridegroom," while critiquing the lack of clarity in his alchemical and astrological writing. It introduces his 1619 work "On the Threefold Life of Man," which defines humans as a "microcosm" composed of the realms of light, darkness, and the stars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s complex theological system, moving from his emotional mysticism to the structural "Forms of Nature" and his attempt to describe how existence emerges from the void of the "Eternal Will."</meta>

for he is the power within the strength, and one would be nothing without the other ¹). God is also an angry, jealous God, and a consuming fire, and within that same torment lies the abyss of Hell, and the devil’s wrath and malice, as well as the "poison" of all creatures; and it is found that without poison and wrath <note>original: "Grimm" — often translated as "fierceness" or "fury," Böhme uses this to describe the necessary friction or "contraction" required for life to exist</note> there is no life. From this arises the opposition of all conflict, and it is found that the most rigorous and "grim" thing is the most useful; for it creates all things and is the sole cause of movement and life ²).“

Here in the first five chapters, where the connection of the **Principles** with the **Microcosm** <note>The "small world"—referring to the human being as a mirror of the entire universe</note> is treated, this difficult struggle for expression occasionally gains **philosophical light**. This occurs where the development of the first will into the second—of the **Ideal** to the **Real**, of **Spirit** to **Matter**—is depicted:

> „Now this same will is eternal, and originates from nothing but itself; and if it were not, then all would be a nothingness—neither darkness nor light. And since something *does* exist, it is the eternal will, which is astringent and desiring, namely as the wonders of creation. Since there is now a desire, that desire draws into itself, and that which is drawn in makes the will full, so that the desire becomes satisfied. For the will is thin as a nothing, but that which is drawn into the will makes the will thick, and is its darkness; thus the eternal desire stands in darkness. Now as the will in its desiring draws into itself, this drawing is a sting of movement <note>original: "Stachel der Rügung"</note>; for the will is thin as a nothing and is still as a nothing. But since the will is an eternal desiring, it also draws eternally into itself, and as it has nothing to draw in but itself, it impregnates itself so that out of the nothing a darkness is formed. This drawing-in creates the 'sting' of the first essences, so that there may be movement and a manifestation of mobility. But now the will cannot endure this drawing-in, for it wishes to be free; yet it cannot be, for it is desiring. And since it cannot become free, it goes into itself with the drawing-in, and conceives within itself another will to go out from

---

¹) Ch. 2, 10. &emsp; ²) Ch. 2, 8.

<column-break/>

the darkness into itself; and this same second-conceived will is the eternal Mind <note>original: "Gemüthe" — the seat of consciousness and disposition</note>, and it goes into itself, etc. (**Ch. I. 27—29.**).“

> „So too there must be an opposition <note>original: "Widerwille" — literally a "counter-will"</note>, for a bright and still will is as a nothing and gives birth to nothing. If a will is to give birth, it must be within something wherein it may form and bring forth: for nothing is nothing, but rather an eternal stillness without movement, where there is neither darkness nor light, nor life nor death ¹).“

From the "source-spirits" or **qualities**, these have now become "forms of nature." If one therefore replaces "forms" with the more common foreign term **metaphysical idea**, which translates Böhme’s word for us, one can certainly see here a further elevation toward conceptual thought. At any rate, the consistency of an emerging **Idealism** is preferable to the **Materialism** that is evident in **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous physician and alchemist whose work deeply influenced Böhme</note>. The doctrine of the seven forms stretches through to the 5th chapter, entirely within a theosophical tangle, adorned with the barbaric expressions of the **alchemical school** <note>Referring to terms like Mercury and Sulfur used as metaphors for spiritual processes</note>. In the 6th form, **Mercury** <term>Mercury: in Böhme's thought, the principle of sound, speech, and the "vital" movement of the senses</term> or Sound, one might discover the sensibility or perceptibility of objects—or reality itself. For "therein, in the birth of love, we find tone, resonance, and song, as well as the five senses: seeing, hearing, smelling, tasting, and feeling, through which life is then understood, as well as pain and torment, along with joy and love, the desire for good and the desire for evil." Yet the 7th form can also claim the same interpretation, for this is "comprehensibility, essence, and corporeality." These theosophical concepts are split so infinitely that the distinctions necessarily blur. In the conclusion of the 4th and in the 5th chapter, the doctrine of the **Holy Ternary** <note>original: "Ternarius Sanctus" — referring to the manifested "body" or kingdom of God</note> begins: the mystical body of God, the body of Christ, the true Heaven, the Water of Life, the **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum"</note>, the **Tincture**, and the Virgin **Sophia** <note>Divine Wisdom</note>—expressions which all designate the same thing.

The path into this second **Principle** is given in the 6th chapter, along with how **Adam** lost it and **Christ** regained it.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s theological system, transitioning from his structural descriptions of the "Forms of Nature" to the increasingly complex—and at times bewildering—numerical and alchemical symbolism found in his later chapters. The text explores Böhme’s attempt to map the entire cosmos, including human biology and the "Second Principle" of divine light.</meta>

(7. 8.). In Chapter 9 begins the doctrine of the **interaction of the three principles**; a table is intended to explain the visual concept, but this—along with an exceedingly confused explanation that loses itself in the most monstrous fantasies regarding theosophical creation, spirits of the air, planets, sidereal <note>Refers to the influence of the stars and celestial bodies on earthly life.</note> influence, alchemical forces, and unintelligible number interpretations—serves as the clearest proof of his errors. This occurs, as it often does, whenever Böhme seeks to clarify his intuitive "flashes of thought" through diagrams and descriptions. Here, one already senses the frenzied mysticism of Kuhlmann <note>Quirin Kuhlmann (1651–1689), a later visionary poet and follower of Böhme who was eventually executed for his radical beliefs.</note>, who sought to illuminate the lack of clarity in his own views through numbers and calculations. This trait is especially prominent among the Silesian mystics <note>A group of 17th-century thinkers and poets from the Silesia region (now mostly Poland) who blended mysticism with high-baroque literary styles.</note> and undoubtedly forms a parallel phenomenon to the obsession with form and outward artifice of the Silesian school of poetry, even matching the artificial style of Lohenstein <note>Daniel Casper von Lohenstein, a Baroque writer famous for his incredibly dense and ornamental language.</note>.

„Since the number twelve contains two realms, with a double number—one angelic and one human, each having the number 6, making 12 together—these two realms also have another two sets of six: the fire, which is the realm of the abyss, and the desire <note>original: "Lust"—here meaning a lower craving or animal instinct.</note>, which is the realm of the cattle and all earthly beings. Each of these also has a six-fold number at its center: six according to the earthly planets, and six according to the fiery planets, making 24 in total; these are the 24 letters in the languages from which they originated. If one then counts the number according to the Trinity (3 × 24), since the Trinity reveals itself in three realms and persons, and according to the Trinity everything is threefold (though according to the creatures it is twofold), one arrives at 72 numbers; this signifies and constitutes the 72 languages, and signifies Babel—a confusion and a wonder $^{1)}$.“

It is almost touching when Böhme, naming his "child" by its right name as he does here, labels such brooding with the predicate of **confusion**; indeed, this passage—as well as the heading for Chapter 10: *How man may find every secret, up to the number 9 and not beyond*—is a worthy companion piece to his agonizing attempt at a "Language of Nature" <note>Böhme’s theory that there is a "natural language" where the sound of a word reveals the true essence of the object.</note>. Rightly, Böhme warns the reader here $^{2)}$ not to delve any deeper into these profound writings, nor to swing his will

---

$^{1)}$ Chapter 9, 67, 68. $^{2)}$ Chapter 10, 48.

<column-break/>

deeper than he can grasp; „he should at all times be satisfied with the concept.“ By this, however, only the sensory analogy is to be understood.

From Chapters 11 to 18 follows the instruction on how to reach the **Second Principle** <note>In Böhme’s system, the "Second Principle" represents the world of Light, Love, and God’s grace, contrasting with the "First Principle" of Fire and Wrath.</note>: first, in the manner of Weigel <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588), an earlier German mystic whose ideas about internal spirituality heavily influenced Böhme.</note>, concerning the true knowledge of man and how one should free oneself from the dominion of the stars; of the forgiveness of sins as the actual act of this liberation; of Christian life and conduct. „Nothing is more necessary or useful to man than that he learn to know himself: for outward conduct remains in this world, but what the heart grasps, the man takes with him $^{1)}$;“ the chapters on the Testaments of Christ, on the two paths of life $^{2)}$, on prayer, fasting, and preparation $^{3)}$, on the blessing of God $^{4)}$, and on death and dying $^{5)}$ continue to expand upon the view of the path to the Second Principle.

However, far from presenting a concise and cohesive doctrine based on these headings and main viewpoints, an unbridled imagination wanders here through all heavens and worlds. It mixes, repeats, scatters, and confuses the sections in the most disruptive manner, perhaps more so than in any other of his writings. The darker the paths in nature were to the research of the Paracelsists <note>Followers of the alchemist Paracelsus who sought to find spiritual and chemical secrets hidden in the natural world.</note>, the more Böhme descends into their depths, into the foundations of human procreation and reproduction. Like Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus.</note>, he claims that the sexual organs and intestines only came into existence after the **Fall of Man**; as with the former, he suggests that since Adam’s sin, humans are fed with "angel bread," manna, or the body of the ideal creation—the same which Christ was to eat in the desert and which he distributes in the Last Supper $^{6)}$.

He who asks this knows nothing of the Fall of Adam and his image, of how humans were and looked before the Fall. <meta>This page transitions from Böhme’s earlier attempts to categorize nature into a deeper discussion of his "Great Mystery." It explores how Böhme merges alchemical language—like the search for the Philosopher's Stone—with the spiritual process of becoming one with Christ. It also highlights his sharp critique of organized religion and "historical faith" in favor of a personal, quietist mysticism.</meta>

By virtue of the physical coloring of the whole, specific mysticism—the immersion into the Second Principle, divine serenity, and contemplativeness—becomes actual magic, the *Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"; Böhme’s term for the chaos or "nothingness" from which God creates all things.</note>, which is to say the true *Philosopher’s Stone* <note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum"</note>. This is the second will of the Deity: the incarnation of Christ or the union with the divine body, flesh, and blood. This explanation, praiseworthy for its spiritual conception as found in Chapters 6, 98 and Chapter 13, becomes obscured by alchemy; so far was Böhme from a philosophical liberation from the superstition of gold-making:
> "For whoever rightly understands our writings regarding the *Center of Nature* <note>original: "Centro Naturae"</note>, from its impulse to the Trinity on the Cross, and to the radiance of Majesty, can indeed find it in metals; it is not difficult. Let him only learn the right entrance, and he is near the end, of which we shall report nothing here, for that belongs to the Magi who are born Magi ¹)."

Lively and fiery are the exhortations to mysticism, which inexhaustibly bring new outpourings in every chapter. These are a consequence of the task: that the Third Principle <note>The physical/material world.</note> must be absorbed into the Second Principle <note>The divine world of light and love.</note>. Here, ethical views develop in rich abundance, though one must frequently translate sermons of punishment, visions, and speeches of disapproval into a positive doctrine of duties, and be content to perceive the highest good

in quietistic annihilation <note>The mystic practice of "dying to the self" or emptying the ego to let God enter.</note>. There, he soon warns against the hypocrites, the "shining Christians" <note>original: "Gleißner"; literally "glimmerers"—those who put on a false outward show of holiness.</note>, who wish to understand the mysteries only externally and are merely believers in the "letter" of the law: 
> "Look there at the hypocrites, the proud, the boastful, who call themselves masters and sufficient discerners of the writings of the saints, who say: 'We have studied the saints in the secrets of the scriptures, we understand them enough, and even better'; who make conclusions: 'Thus we will believe, thus we will understand the scripture,' and who pronounce heavy sentences and punishment upon those who will not adhere to their sentences, which they bring under the arm of worldly power. Yes, truly, that is called rising above God in one's own honor and lust, as the prophet Daniel says of the hypocritical Antichrist: They are the true murderers and wolves who shed blood and wage war for their own conceited opinions; they are the great proud Whore of Babylon who rides in the hearts of princes: through them is poured out the cup of the wrath of God, and yet they call themselves the little sheep of Christ ¹)."

The apocalyptic tone of zeal, which unfortunately often becomes crude, nonetheless uses this polemic only for the good cause—against intolerance and "letter-Christianity"—at a time when the great mass of scholars wanted to know nothing of tolerance. — How beautiful is the exhortation Böhme gives in Chapter 14:
> "Dear children, our struggle for the highest good does not stand in sword and blow, that we should war for God's sake and kingdom and persecute and murder one another, nor in much knowledge, but merely in a simple, childlike obedience. Dear brothers, look out for yourselves now in this world; you are being led on hypocritical paths; much is boasted of faith, and people are led into *historical faith* <note>Böhme’s term for believing in the Bible as a mere historical record rather than experiencing the living Christ within.</note>, which is only a science; and whoever does not adhere to it is held for a heretic."

In a truly Protestant spirit, Böhme sets himself powerfully against the rigid edifice of hierarchical power, against councils and the "priesthood" <note>original: "Pfaffenthum"; a derogatory term for the clergy.</note> ²); he warns against the outward nature of the church and against idolatry; yet he does not mean to say that the stone churches should be torn down ³). He complains bitterly about the false shepherd

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s early works, transitioning from his "Mysterium Magnum" to his "The Threefold Life of Man" and "Forty Questions on the Soul." at Rome¹), and pours out his wrath over works-righteousness²). <note>The belief that human deeds and religious rituals, rather than faith or divine grace, earn salvation.</note> How fervently he exhorts Christendom to abandon reason that is outside of God; princes should lead their office rightly, for they will one day have to give an account; priests are merely elders of the community, not its lords (a Calvinist view), and the community should live with them in love, so that it may be One Spirit and One Heart.

But one is suddenly torn away from this beautiful, soft, intimate mysticism in its poetic immersion in God and self-forgetfulness when Böhme explains why the Jews were not permitted to eat pork; namely, because the ignited pig fat mixes with the soul-fire—and was God's light supposed to unite with this fat-fire³?! Such passages, numerology, and the Language of Nature <note>original: "Natursprache"; Böhme's theory that sounds and syllables reveal the inner essence of things.</note>—which here interprets the word "Mercy" <note>original: "Barmherzigkeit"⁴)</note> and the entire Lord's Prayer⁵)—ensure that the philosophical value of *The Threefold Life of Man* is to be esteemed no higher than that of *The Three Principles*; with which this writing also shares the entire circle of thought belonging to that same stage of development. A third writing belongs precisely to this group, where the theosophist's vision still wavers under the influence of foreign opinions, and only rarely does pure mysticism work its way up to thought, namely:

4. <term>True Psychology</term> <note>original: "Psychologia Vera"</note>, Forty Questions concerning the Soul, its Original State, Essence, Being, Nature and Property, what it is from Eternity to Eternity, composed by Balthasar Walther, lover of divine mysteries, etc.

Balthasar Walther had posed forty questions about the human soul and asked many scholars and high schools for answers; but since he, as Frankenberg <note>Abraham von Frankenberg, Böhme's friend and first biographer.</note> relates, was not satisfied with the responses, he presented them to Böhme, who answered them. Now, when Balthasar Walther declares that nowhere has he found the true hidden wisdom so perfectly, deeply, and purely grounded as with Böhme, this judgment stands in complete

As in the previous writing, Böhme strives here again to represent mysticism pictorially, having provided a drawing for the first question that offers much that is mysterious to the uninitiated eye. Numerology appears in the same wandering fashion as in *The Threefold Life*: "Also, this government stands no longer than in the number of creation: for every day of creation is a circle of a cycle in the eye, and has its number, of which ten is the cross, the highest number; and man has 10 times 10 as his number, which is 100, and in the crown of paradise the number 1000, and in the essence in the divine center of majesty <note>original: "Centro der Majestät"</note> he has no number at all."

Finally, as a consequence of such a "soul-body," an entire spiritual-corporeal nature is assumed, which one cannot entirely identify with the <term>Noumenon</term> <note>A term from philosophy referring to an object as it is in itself, independent of the senses.</note> or a world of pure concepts. Question 11, 5: "the essence outside of her (the soul) is Paradise: a greening, blooming, and growing of all beautiful heavenly fruits, just as we have all kinds of fruits in this world which we eat earthly; so also there are all kinds of fruits in Paradise that the soul may eat. They are, in color and power, an actual essence, not just a thought; but they are as thin and subtle as a thought, yet substantial, discernible to the soul, capable, powerful, and juicy from the water of life—all from the heavenly essence." — As evidence of how Böhme uses the expressions of Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus.</note> without necessity, one may compare Chapter 6, 12 on <term>Necromancy</term> <note>original: "Nigromantie"</note> with the Paracelsian explanations in the Appendix to *The Book of Azoth* <note>original: "Liber Azoth"</note>, p. 7, regarding *Netrocomicum* and *Necrococomicum*. Furthermore, Question 1, 196 in Böhme: "And from Magic arises Philosophy, which grounds Magic and seeks and finds therein <term>Astrology</term> eternal; and Astrology seeks again its master and maker as <term>Astronomy</term>, the <term>Sulphur</term> and the <term>Mercury</term> <note>In Böhme’s alchemy, Sulphur represents the "fire-spirit" or drive, and Mercury represents the "water-spirit" or the medium of manifestation.</note>, which has its own principle; and within it is the third Magic, the Physician <note>original: "Medicus"</note>, who seeks the breaker and wants to heal him; but he finds the fourth Magic as the Theologian <note>original: "Theologum"</note>, who seeks the <term>Turba</term> <note>original: "Turbam"; Böhme's term for the spirit of destruction or the "uproar" that shatters divine order.</note> in everything and wants to heal the Turba, but he finds the eye of the first Magic"—and to this, compare Paracelsus's Introduction to *Sagacious Philosophy*, p. 3: "Where the Astronomer ends, there the true Physician begins, there the right Philosopher begins, there the eternal wisdom begins, there all arts begin; and note this: that the excellent religions begin there, and without Astronomy no art may be well completed; after it begins the divine wisdom, after it begins the Light of Nature."

<column-break/>

<page-num>191</page-num>

The relationship of the divine spark to spirit and body mediates the possibility of entering into the Body of Christ. — This is mysticism resting upon Valentin Weigel, certainly the theosophist whose reading had the most favorable influence on Böhme. <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588) was a predecessor to Böhme who emphasized the "inner light" and the presence of God within the human soul.</note> Pantheism, by virtue of the generality of its propositions, provokes reflection and the beginning of philosophy. As with Weigel, so too with Böhme: God is Everything and yet nothing (for if man is to leave his freedom, he must enter into God and become a nothing); He is the eternal standstill, the eternal rest, the great expanse. <margin>On the Place of the World, Chapters 19 and 22.</margin> Sin is not essence or substance, but <term>Accident</term> <note>original: "Accidens"; in a philosophical sense, a property that is not essential to the nature of a thing.</note>; <margin>Weigel, *Know Thyself* (Gnothi Seauton), Part II, Ch. 19, p. 116.</margin> for the torment of souls is nothing but the striving and wrestling for the lost God, a sensitive anxiety <note>original: "Aengstlichkeit" or "Angst"</note>, which is a striving and yet no striving; an anxiety in the very same essence that had been introduced into God's will. <margin>Chapter 19.</margin> Naturally, the physical orientation tolerates no such contradictory opposition as the ethical one does. The soul stands in its light, as Böhme already describes in the Appendix on the "turned-around eye," when it clings to God; but it stands in anxiety, in wrath, and in darkness when it is a "larva" <note>A mask or phantom.</note> or image of the devil.

Because of the described position of this writing, Böhme's own praise cannot find approval when he considers it <margin>Epistle 10, 37.</margin> to be his clearest work: "it is an almost bright mirror, in which one recognizes the Great Mystery <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note> visibly enough, for those who are children of it; the world should rightly rejoice over it, if the devil's wrath and malice do not prevent it." <margin>10, 12.</margin> He even considers himself here to be a complete prophet; <margin>Question 38.</margin> he speaks "magically" and not with plain words, for it often happens that a thing is evil in essence, yet soon a little branch grows out of the malice that breaks the <term>Turba</term> and brings about a conversion; yet it is spoken more clearly here than in the prophecies of David and Ezekiel, "for the time is now at an end, and the beginning has found the end. Yet the secret belongs only to the children of God." <margin>Question 38.</margin> Many words held to be prophetic, but—

## 5. On the Incarnation of Jesus Christ. *On the Incarnation of the Word* <note>original: "De incarnatione Verbi"</note>.

The specific mysticism of Christianity, which in earlier writings had been presented—albeit broadly and extensively—only as a secondary purpose, becomes here the subject of a dedicated work. Because philosophy arose from mysticism—from the idea of the annihilation of the "I" and the sinking of oneself into God—the closer examination of this relationship gives the present work its position as a point of transition. It links just as closely to the Theophrastian <note>referring to Theophrastus Paracelsus</note>, physical direction by virtue of its vision of the *Holy Ternary* <note>original: "Ternarius Sanctus"; a term Böhme used to describe the divine manifestation in nature</note>, as it announces the intellectual leap of a new stage of development in the doctrine of the *Groundless* <note>original: "Ungrund"; Böhme’s term for the absolute, undifferentiated void or abyss from which God and the world emerge</note>, which appears here for the first time and into which man must immerse himself.

The divine virgin, the ideal of humanity that had to depart from man through the Fall of Adam, was reopened in the Virgin *Mary*, so that *Christ* was indeed a creature, but like the first man before the Fall, like the archetype in the Spirit of God. Böhme learned from the Schwenkfelders <note>followers of the reformer Caspar Schwenkfeld, who emphasized the "inner Word" and the progressive deification of Christ's humanity</note> how one can immerse oneself in this mystery so deeply that the way out is difficult to find. Both love to recount the most precise details of the flesh of *Christ*, of the divine limbus <note>spiritual matter or "matrix"</note>, the earthly matrix, the deified body and blood. Through *Christ*, it becomes possible for man to enter the body of God again; the means of union are the sacraments, which Böhme treats in more detail in the book on the *Testaments of Christ*. Here, the difference from the teachings of Weigel and Stiefel <note>Valentin Weigel and Esaias Stiefel were contemporary mystical and heterodox thinkers</note> is not as sharply emphasized as in the 12th Epistle and in the *Writings Against Stiefel* <note>original: "Antistiefelien"</note>. He carelessly allows passages to run through, such as:

> "Thus the will-spirit walks rightly in God, for he is one spirit with God, and works divine works with God²)" — your will receives God's power, *Christ's* body, and the new body grows in you in God; then you are God's child, and *Christ's* goods belong to you; His merit is your merit,

---

¹) Epistle 10, 50.  
²) Chapter 6, 8.

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>193</page-num>

> His suffering, death, and resurrection is all yours; you are a member of His body, His spirit is your spirit; He leads you on the right path, and everything that You do, You do for God¹). God is the Ground and the Groundless <note>original: "Grund und Ungrund"</note>, freedom and also nature, a light and darkness, and man is also everything, if he only seeks and finds himself as God²)."

The work is divided into three parts. The first is titled: *How the Eternal Word Became Man*; the second: *How We Must Enter into Christ's Suffering, Dying, and Death*; the third deals with: *The Tree of Christian Faith*. The value of the parts is also graded according to this content. In the first part—which, beginning from the origin of all things, describes the creation, the emergence of man, his actual paradisiacal form, the creation of woman, the flight of the *Tincture* <note>the spiritual life-force</note>, the divine virgin *Sophia* at the Fall, her return in *Mary*, the incarnation of *Christ*, its utility, the rebirth of the will-spirit, and the difference between the old and new birth, between *Adam* and *Christ*—one finds the old Paracelsian manner of presentation, as is appropriate for the physical stage of development. In wild, fantastic conceptions, an attempt is made to solve the obscurities and contradictions of the religious mysteries present in Holy Scripture according to the natural sense. — The second and third parts depict the mystical path of immersion in God and the symptoms of divine life. First, it is explained that man must enter into a new *Principle* through the *Groundless* <note>original: "Ungrund"</note>, through fire into light, so that after the dying away of the old self through death, he can sink into the likeness of God. — The essence of this state, faith, is said to be no historical science, but a power of God, one spirit with God, a true love, a will toward God.

> "It is not such an easy thing to be a right spirit; rather, it is the most difficult thing of all. The will must become a knight and fight against the corrupted will. It must be as if dead, and as it thus

²) *Chapter II. 10, 6.*

Böhme describes a process of spiritual rebirth where the believer's will is united with God's through a "knight-like" struggle against the self.</summary>

finds, so he finds himself in God and works with God—not according to what reason wants, but in God, doing what God’s Spirit wants. For the earthly life protects nothing, so that he may live in God, and God’s Spirit may be in him, both the willing and the doing; he is as if he were a nothing, and is yet *everything* in God; he is an ornament and crown of the Godhead, a wonder in divine magic <note>original: "göttlichen Magie"; Böhme uses "magic" to describe the creative, transformative power of the divine will</note>. He creates where there is nothing, and takes where nothing is made: He works and no one sees his essence; he exalts himself, and yet requires no ascending. He is great and mighty, and yet is the very lowest humility: he has everything, and yet grasps nothing more than gentleness." Through this struggle, through death in Christ’s death, he attains forgiveness of sin, "which is not like a worldly court where one's guilt is forgiven out of mercy while one remains a rogue <note>original: "Schalk"</note> in his heart. If one learned all the scriptures by heart and sat in church his whole life, but remained an earthly and beastly man in the image of his soul, striving only for falsehood in his heart; then his hypocrisy helps him not at all; he is a false shepherd as long as he still carries an earthly image in his soul."

In this profound mysticism, which is carried by a beautiful flow of language and a rare religious poetry, Böhme feels entirely like himself; happy in the contemplative immersion in God, he breathes out his convictions and his own experiences with heartfelt anointing <note>a term for spiritual grace or inspiration</note>, and refreshes the reader through the power, freshness, and beauty of his style. — One gladly forgives the fact that the effeminate tone of mysticism occasionally rings out here:
> "Thus we become pregnant with the kingdom of heaven, and thus enter into Christ’s death while in a living body, and receive the body of Christ as the divine essence;" or when he again introduces his figurative, materialistic view:
> "Thus our will longs for a flesh, not for an earthly and transitory one in torment <note>original: "Qual"</note>, but for a permanent one without duality <note>original: "Dual"</note>; for we clearly understand that we have gone out from the Eternal into the fragile, and that we have drawn the matter <note>original: "Materiam"</note> into the slime <note>original: "Limum"; a reference to the primeval clay from which Adam was formed</note> — — for Adam's imagination drew the earthly torment of the stars and elements into the slime,

<column-break/>

and the stars and elements have drawn in the earth's sickness, and thus the heavenly matter of the heavenly flesh has become earthly" etc.

After this writing formed the transition to a subordination under specifically mystical immersion in God—explained through the process of overcoming alchemical subject matter—and after the mystical conviction lying directly in the spirit could thus gain the upper hand in the theosophical view; the breaking consciousness of the mystic demands a new development, even if only for moments, conditioned by the errors of the old physical view. This development is expressed primarily in the next small writings. It is precisely the smallest writing that has almost the greatest value for philosophy; perhaps because our theosophist <note>a person seeking direct knowledge of the divine through mystical insight</note> kept his thoughts more in check during these small works due to the limited scope, so that the overgrowing sprouts of alchemy found no room; and because at the same time he grasped them, the foreign doctrines were sufficiently processed to allow a primitive wrestling of the heart and thought to grant vital force once more before the images.

### Third Stage of Development. Predominance of Dialectics.

As Böhme immersed himself ever deeper into the metaphysical separation of the three principles, the necessity of the primordial division struggled ever more clearly into his consciousness. The principles became more spiritual and conceptual—mysticism had prepared the way for this—suddenly the lightning-bolt of the absolute primitive positioning of Thesis, Antithesis, and Synthesis <note>the three stages of a dialectical process: a proposition, its contradiction, and their resolution</note>—the dialectical process, which is essential as distinguishability <note>original: "Schiedlichkeit"</note> in the Godhead—leaps forth. The wrestling makes way for clarity, but only for a short time. — The sharpness of this stage, the consciousness of a necessary organization of everything thinkable according to this unconditional first schema, forms its characteristic—and Böhme wonders at his own achievement, marvels, and sinks back again into the old darkness, because his dim eye cannot endure the lightning-bolts.

<summary>This page analyzes Böhme's transition from alchemical imagery to a more refined mystical and dialectical philosophy, focusing on the soul's union with God and the early emergence of a Thesis-Antithesis-Synthesis structure in his thought.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, mysticism, dialectics, Thesis, Antithesis, Synthesis, alchemy, theosophical, Godhead, Limum, spiritual development, forgiveness</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s philosophical development, moving from his early mystical visions to a more structured "principle-theory" where he attempts to separate the nature of God from the origin of evil.</meta>

In the previous stage (insofar as such a strict separation is possible given the confusion and obscurity of Böhme’s style of presentation), the first principle included the vague intuition of a Godhead—a primary Will—united with the principle of darkness, or Satan, as something inherent within it, though not identical to it. — Emerging philosophical consciousness must have made Jakob Böhme realize that, on one hand, this view bound two concepts into a single principle in such a way that one part necessarily belonged to the second principle; on the other hand, such a jumbling of intuitions and ideas, which are recognized as original and absolute, cannot be tolerated because of the required simplicity of their foundation. Thus, it followed that:

1) The principle of evil, or negation, now stood in sharp contrast to the second and third principles as the first principle. This is required by the nature of the relative concepts of Being and Nothing, of Good and Evil (which had lost its physical reality in the previous stage and can now only stand as the negation of the Good), and their synthesis in the third principle;

2) The indefinite first principle, as the Godhead, separated itself from the three principles as a result of the mystical-pantheistic view of the God-All. It now stands as the <term>Boundless</term> <note>original Greek: Ἄπειρον (Apeiron), meaning the infinite or indefinite</note>, as the completely undetermined, as the <term>Groundless</term> <note>original: "Ungrund"; Böhme's term for the absolute nothingness or the primal depth of God before creation</note>, as a "Nothing" where thoughts vanish—thoughts which can only grasp the structure of the three principles. It stands as a universal, infinite Godhead; a unity postulated by reason but unthinkable and imaginary. — Through this reshaping of the doctrine of principles, the ethical and physical stages are reconciled within the dialectical development: the ethical view finds satisfaction in the fact that the Godhead is no longer yoked to the evil principle as the "wrathful side" of God the Father, but can hover above all three principles like the Spirit over the waters; and that the opposites of Good and Evil now spring forth in sharp hostility. The physical view is satisfied by the doctrine of the Groundless and by the necessary positing of Being within the three principles as the ultimate foundation. — Because the Groundless is now separated from the three principles as the primal Godhead, this view approaches the one found in the *Aurora* <note>Böhme’s first major work, written in 1612</note>, where the second

---

<page-num>197</page-num>

principle split into Christ and Lucifer. It is easy to recognize that the first principle has now become the Groundless, and that Lucifer has taken the place of the first principle as the element that immediately conditions the opposition. Unfortunately, this approximation of his earlier view is the cause of manifold confusion, especially between the Groundless and the evil principle, the sharp separation of which only rarely appears.

While the shorter writings attempt to develop the dialectical process of what had previously been a physical doctrine of principles, the work *On the Signature of All Things* <note>original Latin: "de Signatura Rerum"</note> promises to carry this process through everything that exists and can be thought; the book *On Election by Grace* <note>original German: "Gnadenwahl"</note> seeks to reconcile the contradiction between Good and Evil, the necessity of a good Godhead, and the freedom of choice. Other writings of this period, such as the polemics against Stiefel, fall behind in the clarity of their thought development; they either carry out individual views of the theological dogma of Böhme’s mysticism without focusing on the original distinctions, or they expand upon Gnostic views regarding the developmental history of religions, or finally, they provide supplementary explanations of points that remained obscure to individual friends or to everyone. This latter mass of writings, which already reveals the decline of his theosophical genius, does not represent a new stage of development, but rather mixes all the earlier ones in a strange way. Among them are the writings "On the Testaments of Christ" and the *Great Mystery* <note>original Latin: "Mysterium Magnum"</note>, which are primarily Gnostic in content; furthermore, the *177 Questions*, the *Key* <note>original Latin: "Clavis"</note>, and the theological treatises.

In this second version, the third period extends until 1624; however, if one restricts it to the major works, these occur in 1620 and 1621. The period of stagnation and decline falls in 1623 and 1624.

### 6. *Six Theosophical Points. Six Mystical Points.*
### *The Pansophic Mystery.*

These three small writings form a natural group, as they were all composed in the year 1620. Drawing on pure mysticism and staying far removed from alchemical and astrological doctrines, they maintain an almost philosophical tone, such that they are of greater value than

...many an extensive work ^1). The ideas that previously appeared in symbolic dress finally become concepts. Mysticism already taught that heaven and hell are not capable of spatial representation, but rather have their place in the small human heart; every person carries them within themselves; whichever quality a person awakens, that same quality burns within them, and the soul is capable of its fire; and when the body dies, the soul need not travel anywhere, but instead it is cast into the hellish dominion to which it belongs by its nature; the abyss has no location, neither time nor space. ^2) If a person wishes to enter into the Nothing, into freedom, they must surrender themselves to the fire; thus they sink into the death of the <term>Principle</term> <note>original Latin: "Principii"</note>, and so they bloom out of the <term>fire-anguish</term> <note>original: "Feuer-Angst"; Böhme's term for the agonizing tension or "friction" within nature before it breaks through into the light of God</note> into the light; for when they surrender themselves, the eternal will toward nature—which is God the Father—leads them through the fire into Himself. For by surrendering, the person falls back to the first will toward nature, which then leads them—through the second will, which is His Son or Heart—out of the nature of anguish, and places them with the Son's will into freedom, outside the torment of the fire. God is then their will and action within them; but whatever seeks to be master within the fire, that enters into its own essence and falls prey to the fire-will of God. ^3) God is here called Freedom; outside of God, in nature, is Necessity; the former—

^1) An exception can be made for the type of prophecy found in the 6th text of the *Pansophic Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Pansophicum"</note>, which seems intended only for immortals and <term>Theosophers</term> <note>A term for those seeking "divine wisdom" through direct spiritual experience rather than just academic theology</note>:
> "Thus also the <term>Turba</term> <note>original: "Turba"; Böhme's term for the "turmoil" or "wrath" that disrupts the harmony of the divine order</note> is sought as the wrath of all creatures, for it has run its course even with the disgust of the creatures, and now becomes manifest, as if at the goal in the middle of the 'crown-number'; in the 6,000th year, a little above, not below. In the day and hour when the creation is completed in the <term>Mystery</term> <note>original Latin: "Mysterio"</note> and has been placed into the wonders in the Mystery (as a mirror of eternity). That is on the sixth day past noon; there the Mystery stands open with its wonders, and is seen and recognized. Then 'Selfhood' <note>original: "Meinigkeit"</note> will drive out the *Turba* for a time, until the beginning steps into the end—'then the Mystery is a wonder in figures.'"

In the 7th text as well, Böhme delves somewhat into magical things, where he develops a theory of colors and deduces the four alphabets (as in *The Threefold Life of Man*) along with the 77 languages. For example: "This alphabet of the <term>Language of Nature</term> <note>original: "Natursprache"; the belief that there is a primal language where names perfectly match the inner essence of things</note> lies hidden in the color black beneath everything else, for the color black does not belong to the count of colors; it is Mystery, and misunderstood except by the one who possesses the language of nature, to whom it is opened by the Holy Spirit."

<column-break/>

<page-num>199</page-num>

—is the Nothing, the light of love; the latter is the burning torment. One sees here the—albeit unconscious—progression of these series of intuitions. The beginning is ethical: the killing of desire, the destruction of the "I," of the self, of egoism; the devotion to the "Non-I," the immersion in the Idea of God is his highest pleasure, his light, his salvation. Torment lies in the desire for "essence" (meaning for the real/material), in the definable, sensory goal and drive. Thus the Idea of God became a "Nothing" in contrast to all creatures—indescribable, a <term>Groundless</term> <note>original: "Ungrund"; the absolute depth of the Godhead that precedes all existence, even the distinction between good and evil</note> in which the light and heart of God arise. Thus the ethical consciousness and mystical experience become a metaphysical principle. "When the body is broken in death, the divine fire ignites in its essence, and the divine image is figured once again"; ^1) one step further, and instead of the divine image, we see a Nothing that is God Himself in contrast to the creature. Thus we stand on mystical-idealist ground: that death is a merging into God, a destruction of the "I," and a surrender in love to the "Nothing-All," involving the abandonment of personal immortality. Thus, it is not surprising that Böhme’s pantheistic passages have found such a great resonance.

Here, Böhme is at the height of his philosophical development; the *Groundless* is the substrate of the three principles: "The *Groundless* is an eternal Nothing, yet it makes an eternal beginning as a longing; for the Nothing is a longing for 'Something,' and yet there is nothing that could give the 'Something'; rather, the longing is itself the giving of that which is yet also nothing but a mere desiring longing. And that is the eternal understanding of <term>Magic</term> <note>original Latin/German: "Magia"</note>, which creates within itself where there is nothing; it makes 'Something' out of 'Nothing,' and that only within itself; and since that same longing is also a nothing—merely a will—it has nothing and is nothing that could give it anything, and it has no place where it might find or lay itself down. This same will is a spirit, and we recognize the eternal Will-Spirit as **God** and the stirring life of the longing as **Nature**. For nothing is earlier, and both are without beginning, and indeed one is the cause of the other and an eternal bond."

^1) *Six Theosophical Points* 5, 7, 43. ^2) *Six Theosophical Points* 5, 7, 43.

<summary>This page explores Jakob Böhme's transition from mystical symbolism to the philosophical concept of the 'Ungrund' (the Groundless), where God is defined as an eternal Nothingness and a primal Will that precedes both Nature and being. It highlights his view of heaven and hell as internal states of the heart rather than physical locations.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Ungrund, Groundless, mysticism, Turba, nature of God, selfhood, fire-anguish, Language of Nature, theosophy</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s philosophical development, moving from his earlier mystical symbols toward a more structured conceptual framework. The previous page discussed the soul's surrender to the "fire-anguish" to reach God's light.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>

And thus the <term>Spirit of Will</term> <note>original: "Willengeist"; Böhme's term for the active, conscious aspect of the divine will</note> is an eternal knowledge of the <term>Groundless</term> <note>original: "Ungrund"; the "abyss" or "non-ground" prior to all existence, a central Böhme concept</note> and the life of seeking is an eternal knowledge of the will¹). — "How the <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "Principium"</note> is opened out of the eternal Groundless has been perceived as the essence of the three Principles, according to the likeness of the Holy Trinity, with their wonders, as if in an unfathomable depth, and this from eternity²)."

The *Six Theosophical Points* have a clear, logical division, unlike few of Böhme's other writings; the first three points deal with the Groundless and the three Principles and their struggle in general: 1) each by itself, 2) the mixing of the first two, 3) their struggle in the third Principle. The final three points deal with specifics: 1) the Light-principle, 2) the earthly Nature-principle, 3) the Kingdom of Darkness.

Of the *Six Mystical Points*, the first deals with the blood and water of the soul, the second with the <term>Election of Grace</term> <note>original: "Gnadenwahl"; the theological concept of predestination</note>, the third with sin, the fourth with the Last Judgment, the fifth with <term>Magia</term> <note>original Latin: "Magia"; not "magic" in a modern sense, but the creative power of the will</note>, and the sixth with the <term>Mysterium</term> <note>original Latin: "Mysterium"; the "Mystery" or hidden plan of God</note>. While the first yields little profit for philosophy, and the second asserts the necessity of Good and Evil as opposites but does not deduce them, and the fourth treats its subject in a purely biblical manner—by contrast, the third point, which provides a philosophical analysis of sin, reminds one vividly of **Spinoza's doctrine** when it says: "Love and enmity are only one thing, but each dwells within itself, which makes two things"; and the thoughts expressed here are truly refreshing.

"The 'Something' is dark and obscures the light of life, and the 'One' is light, for it loves itself and has no desire for more." The two final sections provide explanations of the words *Magia* and *Mysterium*, which actually mean the same thing: the necessity of the conceptual process of the three Principles which, because it is presented as the ultimate ground, must be ungrounded and therefore incomprehensible—thus, a "Mystery."

The *Pansophic Mystery* <note>original Latin: "Mysterium pansophicum"</note>, concerning the heavenly and earthly Mystery, deals with Böhme's fundamental doctrine and maintains complete speculative purity; despite its brevity, it is a jewel among Böhme's writings.

---

<meta>Footnotes at bottom of page 200</meta>
¹) *Pansophic Mystery*, Chapters 1 and 2. ²) *Six Theosophical Points*, Book I, 2, 24.

<page-num>201</page-num>

### 7. Theosophia. On Divine Contemplation.

Although several other works lie between the previous writings and the *Theosophia*—which was only begun in 1622 and never completed—it is so closely linked to those three that its value justifies placing it before weaker products. **Divine Contemplation** is exactly what the terms *Principle*, *Great Mystery* <note>original Latin: "Mysterium magnum"</note>, *Magia*, *Science* <note>original: "Scienz"; here meaning a living knowledge or sapience</note>, <term>Differentiation</term> <note>original: "Schiedlichkeit"; the process of things becoming distinct from one another</note>, and <term>Perceptibility</term> <note>original: "Empfindlichkeit"</note> denote: namely, that "Something" becomes what it was not: the conceptual process, the turning of the Godhead into its negation and its return into itself in the third Principle. Since this work, as far as it exists, is outstanding in its clarity, thoughtfulness, and lack of <term>theosophical-alchemical additives</term> <note>Böhme often used alchemical metaphors (sulfur, salt, mercury) which can be confusing; this work is more purely philosophical</note>, it offers a quintessence of the most digestible teachings of Böhme’s theosophy, wherein he interprets his fundamental secret, and shows the striving toward a philosophy—even if it remains a dogmatic one. The points of contention are traced back to an intense degree of contradiction, more so than in the larger specialized works like *The Three Principles*, *The Threefold Life*, *The Election of Grace*, or the *Great Mystery*; and this alone is a great gain for an honest and non-fantastical philosophy. Its transparency is immediately apparent in its division into only four chapters.

1) Böhme deals with the <term>primordial process</term> <note>original: "Urproceß"</note> of the Godhead, and how Good and Evil originate within it; "thus we can also philosophize about the single good will of God and say that it can desire nothing within itself, for it has nothing in or of itself that could give it anything, and therefore it leads itself out of itself into a **differentiation**, into **centers**, so that an opposition <note>original: "Widerwärtigkeit"; literally "against-ness" or resistance</note> may arise in the outflow—as in that which has flowed out—so that the Good becomes perceptible, active, and willing within the Evil; namely: it wants to separate itself from the Evil and return into the single will of God. But because the outflow of the single eternal will of God always proceeds out of itself toward its manifestation, the Good also flows out—as the divine power—from the eternal One with such an outflow, and enters into the differentiation and into the centers of multiplicity. Thus, the perpetual outflow of the will causes the Good within it, through its movement, so that the Good again..."

<summary>This page analyzes Böhme's transition into structured philosophical writing, particularly his "Theosophical" and "Mystical" points, and introduces his work "Theosophia" which describes God's self-manifestation through differentiation.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Groundless, Ungrund, Three Principles, Theosophia, Divine Contemplation, Magia, Mysterium Magnum, Spinoza, primordial process, Good and Evil</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s works. Note that there is a gap between the previous provided page (200) and this one (202); the text here picks up mid-sentence discussing the movement of the "One" from stillness into desire and multiplicity.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>

...longs for stillness and becomes desirous to penetrate back into the Eternal; and in such a penetration into itself, the One becomes mobile and full of desire, and in such an operation there exists a sensitivity, knowledge, and willing ^1).”

Thus, evil has emerged from the good—that which, as the One, entered into multiplicity by willing according to its essence. Unity is desired once again, and in this contradiction between the Universal and the Particular—in the sting, in the falling away of reality from the Idea—lies evil; it is therefore only a necessity of dialectics, a relative thing. —

2) The second chapter speaks of the mind, will, and thoughts of human life, “how the same has its original state and the will of God, and how it is a <term>counter-image</term> <note>original: "Gegenwurf"; Böhme's term for the created world or being as a reflective projection or "rebound" of the divine</note> as the image of God, in which God wills, works, and dwells”—thus, concerning man.

3) The third chapter deals with nature: how it too is a counter-image of divine science, through which the eternal will makes itself sensitive, visible, active, and willing via the unfathomable supernatural science; how all things are from, through, and in God; how God is so near in all things and fulfills everything. The visible world with its host and creatures is nothing other than the outpoured Word, which involves itself in properties, since in properties a self-will has arisen. The spiritual world of fire, light, and darkness stands hidden within the visible elementary world and works through the <term>Separator</term> <note>original: "Separatorem"; the archetypal principle of differentiation and division in Böhme's cosmology</note> with its influence on all things, according to the nature and property of each thing.^2) Here one finds a decided idealism, a good preliminary study for Hegel; only the Idea exists, and from its process, the world, reality, "something," and appearance result. Finally, the fourth chapter contains only a few paragraphs. It deals with the interaction of God and the world, the Idea and <term>tangibility</term> <note>original: "Greiflichkeit"; the material quality of being graspable</note>, as well as the purpose of the world order. —

### 8. On the Four Temperaments.

This treatise was composed “by request” ^3) in March 1621, and can be viewed as a supplement to the *Forty Questions*

<gloss>1) Book I, 14–16. 2) Chapter 3, 14. 10. 3) Epistles 12, 72.</gloss>

---

<page-num>203</page-num>

concerning the human soul. It treats, in a theosophical but otherwise sensible and clear manner, the theory of the four temperaments that is still generally valid today; and Böhme even agrees with Kant in considering the sanguine temperament to be the happiest.^1) This is hardly surprising, as the entire doctrine has survived with few changes from Galen through all schools of thought until the most recent times. The theosophers merely delved a bit deeper into the connection with the four elements and the stars: the sanguine temperament is dominated by Air, the choleric by Fire, the melancholic by Earth, and the phlegmatic by Water. The entire explanation is organized accordingly. Included are good prescriptions for each temperament on how it can protect itself from the dominion of the evil principle.

### 9. Two Apologies against Balthasar Tilke.

These were composed in the year 1621. The first defends the *Aurora* <note>original: "Aurora"; Böhme's first major work, meaning "The Day-Spring" or "Dawn"</note>, the second defends the *Incarnation of Christ* against Balthasar Tilke’s attacks; however, only the first is addressed directly to him, while the second was written to Dr. Koschwitz.^2) Both can be encompassed in a single consideration because of their equally minor significance for the totality of Böhme's vision, because of their very similar tone and content, and finally, because of the same deficiency of using symbolic and sensory-poetic expressions instead of clear concepts.

Both writings are of little importance because they show the propositions treated in earlier works in neither a changed nor improved form. Instead, they merely seek to ward off or refute the hair-splitting interpretations of the attacking opponent through repetition and by expanding upon individual points of what was said previously. (In any case, one will prefer to get to know Böhme's theosophy from writings where a systematic path and a plan shine through, at least suggestively, rather than in polemical writings where, due to the manner of the attack, individual points are torn out of context and placed in a harsher light than elsewhere.) Nor do they achieve clarity...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s polemical works, specifically his responses to critics like Tilke. It explores the tension between Böhme's call for spiritual tolerance and the harsh, visceral language he used when defending his "inner light" against literalist opponents.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>

...reach, which in the previous treatises had already attained a significant degree of perfection. Flashes of thought and brilliant insights into philosophy are almost entirely absent; indeed, they would be wasted on an opponent who, living entirely in the manner of his contemporaries, could neither understand nor produce a living spirit, but instead delighted in subtle twists of words and thoughts and blatant misunderstandings; one who, as Böhme ¹) says, pulled the Scriptures by the hair so that they had to serve him as he wished, regardless of where the foundation and cornerstone remained—whether such understanding existed in the cited <term>terms</term> <note>original: "Terminis"</note> or not—provided only that he wielded Scripture and letters, exchanged words for words, and cloaked his erroneous opinion with such a facade, as long as he cited Scripture, made Böhme's meaning suspicious, and attacked it with hostility.

Based partly on a narrow interpretation of the Bible and partly on an imitative, literalist <term>Schwenkfeldianism</term> <note>A religious movement following Caspar Schwenckfeld, which emphasized the "inner word" and the spiritual nature of the sacraments, though Böhme found their followers too tied to rigid formulas.</note>, he attacks Böhme, who in these <term>Apologies</term> <note>original: "Apologien"; these were formal written defenses Böhme produced to answer accusations of heresy.</note>, as he frequently likes to express himself, does not cast his pearls before swine. Indeed, how can polemical writings have value for the essence of a mystical philosophy, since polemics demand sharpness of thought, proof and counter-proof, but above all, a common ground from which both parties can proceed? It is the peculiarity of mysticism to rely solely on the conviction of an inner fact of divine illumination, so that by virtue of this subjectivity, the commonly granted starting point is missing, and an unconditional tolerance demands its recognition. Moreover, the mystic's intuition is not deduced, proven, or reflected upon, which means it completely lacks the conditions of a polemical and critical philosophy. Böhme feels this quite rightly: that the time of disputation and chatter is over; he and Tilke get no further through their arguing,

> "but with rebirth in the Spirit of Christ, they would reach and obtain the little pearl, so that they would never need to quarrel over it; each should only seek within himself and see what he is.²)"

Thus the po-

---

¹) Apology II. 14. ²) Apology II. 323.

<page-num>205</page-num>

lemic consists for the most part of declamations against the blindness and stubbornness of the opponent, curses against the sinful world and the external spirit of the letter, and in sermons of punishment and repentance. Even if Böhme recommends tolerance—because an illustrious mind with practiced senses recognizes that God's gifts are without end or number, that God leads His children in wondrous ways and gives to each a different gift to express than to the other¹); that one should not despise what God gives to another, because enlightened senses walk kindly and lovely, and instruct man in what he should do and leave undone, recognizing themselves in brotherly debt, and are modest in punishment and teaching with good instruction—yet he himself by no means shows that he could control himself or discard coarseness and harshness; as when he addresses his <term>scandalous pamphlet</term> <note>original: "Pasquill"</note>:

> "O great Babylonian holiness, bethink yourself better, or you will not eat of Christ's <term>prey</term> <note>original: "Aas"; literally "carrion" or "carcass." In Böhme's visceral theology, this refers to the spiritual nourishment or "flesh" of Christ that the believer must "consume."</note>: the author is no devil, but seeks his prey, Christ²)",

as he declares him outright to be a liar because he, Böhme, is a child of God³); Tilke cannot look him in the face in these "trousers," he must take off the fur coat to behold the spirit of the mystery; he runs against Babel, and would rather run into hell, as Lucifer did, than see the root of the tree⁴); quite apart from how often he calls him a shameful pamphleteer and a proud Pharisee, and how often he includes him in reckless speeches of punishment against his wretched age,

> "in which the devil stalks us with ropes and nets, where one brother shames, slanders, despises, and kills the other, so that he may catch us in God's wrath."⁵)

Such controversial writings are all the more conducive to the knowledge of Böhme's intellectual stage, his innermost intentions, and his development of thought, because the conflict brings all these sides to light in a very natural course, and the outpouring of the heart reveals the experiences of the soul.

Tilke's attacks arise from the misunderstanding of Böhme's somewhat unclear mode of expression; Böhme

O blindness and self-reason, who has forbidden us from searching? The Devil forbids it to us, so that we might not search out his kingdom; otherwise, we might flee from him; how conceit has blinded you! <term>Pasquill</term> <note>original: "Pasquill," referring to an anonymous satirist or critic.</note> must not forbid searching <sup>9</sup>); those are heretics who exchange words for words and explain words with words, while the mind never experiences what the power and understanding of the Word is <sup>10</sup>)."

# 10. First Anti-Stiefel,
<note>original: Antistiefelius Primus</note>

->or: Reflections on Esaias Stiefel's Booklet: Of the Threefold State of Man and His New Birth,<-

->and<-

### Second Anti-Stiefel,
<note>original: Antistiefelius Secundus</note>

->or: On the Error of the Sects of Esaias Stiefel and Ezechiel Meth, Concerning the Perfection of Man.<-

Stiefel, along with his brother-in-law Meth, had pushed <term>Weigelian mysticism</term> <note>Followers of Valentin Weigel, who taught that God is known only through internal illumination.</note> regarding the identity of Christ and the mystically enlightened, pious man to the extreme <sup>1</sup>). They claimed that Christ descends into every person and identifies himself entirely with anyone who gives themselves over to him mystically, so that Stiefel himself said: "I, the living Word of God, speak or do this"; indeed, he even signed himself: "Christ, Son of God." He taught that holy parents must also produce a Messiah, that Christ would be born often, and that a person so born or absorbed into Christ already possesses a glorified body here on earth, in which he will be resurrected, and which is already immortal here. —

Böhme did not fail to recognize the kinship of Stiefel's mysticism with his own; he says: he was indeed unknown to him according to the "outer man," but not a stranger in spirit, but rather born out of his "Mother's essence and power" <sup>2</sup>); also, that things were as the author wrote, and he had written correctly in his knowledge regarding what the author said of entirely "fleshly Christendom," which carries nothing more than the name in its mouth, while the heart and mind are recognized before God only as a mocker of the name of Christ; it [Christendom] has now borrowed and lent the mantle of Christ with his suffering, death, and dying, and tickles itself with Christ's suffering and atonement, but does not wish to enter into his death <sup>3</sup>); but he decisively guards himself against this "Identity Mysticism"; he first instructs Stiefel that man lives in <term>three principles</term> <note>Böhme’s system of the dark world (fire), the light world (love), and the external world (nature).</note>, and that only one of these, as long as he wanders in earthly life, can give itself entirely over to Christ, while the others constantly burden him with sin, so that it is folly to think a man could be fully...

---

<sup>1</sup>) See Part II. p. 235.   <sup>2</sup>) I. 12.   <sup>3</sup>) I. 93. 94.

<summary>This page details Böhme's fierce defense of spiritual inquiry against Tilke's literalism and introduces his critique of Esaias Stiefel's radical belief that enlightened humans become identical to Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Esaias Stiefel, Antistiefelius, mysticism, identity mysticism, Election by Grace, Ezechiel Meth, Schwenkfeld, Tilke, Eternal Nature, Pasquill</keywords>
