<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme's polemical writings, transitioning from his defense against Tilke to his critique of Esaias Stiefel (the "Antistiefelien"). It specifically addresses Böhme’s rejection of the idea that Christ can be "born" out of the natural reproductive process of holy parents.</meta>

come to be born if his parents were faithful and holy, for since Adam’s fall the three <term>principles</term> <note>Böhme’s cosmological system consists of three "Principles": 1. The dark fire-world (God's wrath/nature), 2. The light-world (God's love/Christ), and 3. The external visible world (nature/humanity).</note> have struggled with one another with equal strength; finally, he also defends the historical Christ and denies that He can be born anew many times, although he knows how to preserve his mystical Christ as the second principle of the Godhead—the Light or Heart of God—without compromise. Finally, he says "in good faith from his gifts," that a person should take great care if they shall or wish to speak with the Lord’s mouth. "For if one wishes to say: I, the living Word of God, speak or do this, then the Lord's will must be present, and the Spirit of God must arm the tongue, heart, and mind, and join in the action itself; otherwise it is not the Lord’s word, but that of the external man." —

In the second polemical writing, there follows a further elaboration of these views: on Adam’s nature; whether Abel’s death is Christ’s death; on Jacob’s struggle with Esau; and regarding Christ’s death, whether He died as the triune God. —

The *Antistiefelien* <note>Böhme’s writings directed against Esaias Stiefel (1561–1627), a radical perfectionist who claimed a state of sinless union with God.</note> are of mystical-theological content; therefore, natural-philosophical fantasies and alchemical broodings are as scarce as the purity of philosophical expression. Without major highlights, but also without having decidedly absurd or confused passages, they mostly move in a finer structure of periods. However, in the second writing, the names *Jehovah* and *Jesus* are dissected according to their components, and a <term>Paracelsian</term> <note>Referring to the ideas of Paracelsus (1493–1541), who blended medicine, chemistry, and theology.</note> explanation of human reproduction finds its place. Although it is certainly a beautiful, noble thought to submit the union of the sexes to the longing for the vanished jewel of the Ideal—the Divine Virgin—the closer and deeper investigation of how the <term>Limbus</term> <note>In Böhme’s usage, the "Limbus" is the masculine, essential seed or "dust of the earth" from which man was made.</note> seeks the <term>Matrix</term> <note>The "Matrix" refers to the feminine, receptive, or formative principle/womb of nature.</note> leads into the overly tangled snares of theosophical figments of the mind. Many excellent views on the human soul, its relation to the body, and the essence of God are veiled by such natural-philosophic or theosophical fantasies; in vain does Böhme struggle here for a clarity that only a "favorable hour" might bring him. —

The first writing, which bears the date of the first Sunday after Easter <note>original: "Quasimodogeniti"</note> 1621, is only an opinion on Stiefel's doctrine—which had come to his ears at that time—addressed to some 

<page-num>208</page-num>
<page-num>209</page-num>

friends, and is kept in a calm, friendly tone. He rejects the notion that he wishes to suppress the Author's <note>Referring to Esaias Stiefel.</note> little book or his high spirit; rather, in love toward him, he wishes to offer his own gifts and understanding, as one limb of the body to another. For he recognizes the Author’s spirit well enough and finds it dear to address him, because he has suffered much for the sake of Christ’s name; but he should not be ashamed to learn to know himself better, and to learn more in the spirit within the Bride of Christ. Böhme says he himself claims no perfect knowledge; he considers himself as a nothing; therefore, he admonishes his friends to understand such writing in no other way than as Christian and brotherly, for he is not a lord over their spirit and knowledge, but their helper in the Lord.

The second writing, in April 1622—thus one year later—composed on the occasion of a newly published work by Stiefel, is a companion piece to the second apology against Tilke. Böhme seems to have been irritated that Stiefel did not want to abandon his errors and stubbornly continued to present himself as the "new Messiah of Langensalza"; Böhme meets him with the harshest expressions: it would not take much for a "new Antichrist" to be born in Stiefel ¹), and when the Author says that holy, believing parents could give birth entirely to Christ as the product of human reproduction, Böhme remarks that almost every cow would laugh at that if she saw her own kind coming from the "external man"; and that he [Stiefel] often acts more foolishly than a beast. Finally, he mocks him: Stiefel must "put on different boots" <note>A pun on the name Stiefel, which is the German word for "boot."</note> if he wanted to ride with Christ's spirit over death and hell.

While the four cited polemical writings are of minor significance for Böhme's overall development—because they do not illuminate the fundamental doctrines more closely, and only draw isolated consequences from earlier writings that were provoked here and there—a larger work attempts to carry the three principles, or the dialectical process, through all forms, beings, and names. This was certainly a grand idea that had already existed in the theosophical school but had been put forward without decisiveness. Unfortunately, the success...

...not to the thought itself. Within the necessity of the dialectic, alchemical caricatures were immediately mixed in. Internally, this writing forms a primary document of Böhme’s third, dialectical stage of development. This is:

### 11. On the Birth and Marking of All Beings.
->*De Signatura Rerum* <note>original: "De Signatura Rerum" — Latin for "On the Signature of Things." This is Böhme's famous work on how the internal essence of a thing is revealed through its external form.</note><-

This treatise was finished in February 1621 or 1622, depending on whether the 12th Epistle <note>Böhme's *Sendbriefe* or Epistles are a collection of his correspondence which help scholars date his works.</note> is dated to May 10, 1621 or 1622. Although the end of the preface in the *Theosophia Revelata* <note>The "Revealed Theosophy," a major posthumous collection of Böhme's works.</note> from the year 1715 gives February 1622 as the time, reasons of probability favor 1621. This is because, in the list of previously written works in the aforementioned Epistle, the polemical writings against Stiefel are not mentioned; indeed, one cannot even count these among the "several small tracts" that Böhme lists alongside his larger works, since other writings of the same size, such as the *Forty Questions on the Human Soul*, are included. —

In this writing, the concept—the essence of every thing, of the world, of man, and of every individual as well as vegetable body—is conceived as an inherent organic principle: thus physics merges with dialectics <note>Here, "dialectics" refers to Böhme's system of understanding how opposites (like light and dark, or inner and outer) interact to create life.</note>. This essence now drives its object toward the process of becoming and perishing—that is, of life and existence. —
> "And there is no thing in nature that is created or born that does not reveal its internal form externally as well; for the internal constantly works toward revelation, as we see in the power and manifestation, how it has revealed itself in its forms and manifestations, just as we see and recognize this in stars and elements, in creatures, as well as in trees and herbs."¹)

In the struggle of concepts, of essences, of spirits, of powers—or whatever one may call the internal principle ²)—in this development and opposition lies

---

<note>¹) Chapter 1, 15.</note>
<note>²) Böhme therefore also calls this metaphysical-dialectical principle "possibility": *I am only noting the possibility of all things, alongside the practice of the new births, and I give guidance to those gifted by God for external things, since the time for the opening of all secrets is indeed approaching and dawning.* Introduction.</note>

the movement and order of the world: "for eternal nature has born nothing but an equality out of itself with its desire, and if there were not an everlasting mingling, there would be eternal peace in nature; but thus nature would not be manifest; in struggle she becomes manifest, so that every thing rises up and wants to flee from the struggle into still rest, and thereby only runs out of itself into another, and only thereby awakens the struggle ¹)." — The doctrine of the <term>Signature</term> <note>The "Signature" is Böhme’s belief that every object in the natural world carries an external "sign" or "signature" that reveals its inner spiritual character or medicinal use.</note> rests upon this thought, though in its details it admittedly cannot always preserve the dignity of thought and reason. —

> "For though I see that someone speaks, preaches, teaches, and writes of God, and I hear and read the same, it is still not enough understood by me; but if his resonance and his spirit enter from his signature and manifestation and mark his manifestation into mine, then I may understand him in his true depth, whether it be spoken or written, provided he has the hammer that can strike my bell. By this we recognize that all human properties come from one, that they have only a single root and mother; otherwise, one human being could not understand another in sound."²)

— Thus, that which is "birth" in the realm of reality is now called the "marking" (or signature) of all beings. Here originates the <term>Language of Nature</term> <note>Böhme believed in a "natural language" where the sound of a word corresponds to the essence of the thing it describes.</note> and the theory of sound:
> "for it is that from which every thing speaks in its property, and always reveals and represents itself, for what it is good and useful; for every thing reveals its mother, who thus gives the essence and the will to the manifestation."³)

Medical science, too, is an application of the principle of struggle among manifestations and the signature of essences:
> "And then we also understand medicine herein, how one thing heals another and brings it to health; and it is for us to understand how the physician stands in the equality of every thing; for

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s "De Signatura Rerum," specifically examining the second chapter and Böhme's transition into a more complex, often confusing, alchemical and magical framework.</meta>

"...in equality stands the fulfillment of the will, as its highest joy; for every thing desires a will of its own kind, and is pained by opposition; but if it receives a will of its own kind, it rejoices in that equality and sinks into rest within it, and from enmity, a joy is born."¹) —

In the course of this presentation, the theosophist <note>The "Theosophist" refers to Böhme himself, as his followers called his philosophy "Theosophy" (divine wisdom).</note> becomes a completely incomprehensible magician, for which the subject matter of the writing offers him ample occasion. The plan of the work—so far as it is possible to extract it from the tangle of alchemical and magical doctrines—is roughly as follows:

After the introduction (much like those in the "Three Principles of Divine Essence" and the works of Schwenkfeld and Weigel <note>Caspar Schwenkfeld and Valentin Weigel were influential German mystics who preceded Böhme and emphasized internal spiritual experience over external church ritual.</note>) states the task of the mystic—namely, that he should contemplate what he is, whence good and evil come, and that he should find God and the world through self-knowledge to attain final salvation through serenity <note>The German term is "Gelassenheit," a central concept in German mysticism meaning a state of letting go or yielding to God's will.</note>—the first two chapters develop the foundation of the doctrine as previously communicated. However, as Böhme describes the primal process of the will—the movement emerging from the "Nothing"—two systems intersect and cause the reader no small confusion. According to the first, a physical system, the Second Principle develops out of the First (the <term>Ungrund</term> <note>The "Ungrund" or "Abyss" is Böhme's term for the "groundless" state of the divine before any creation or differentiation—a void of absolute freedom.</note>, the Nothing, the Kingdom of Darkness ²); according to the other, the dia-

---

¹) Chapter 2, 2. 3.   
²) "We understand that outside of nature there is an eternal rest, as the Nothing; and then we understand that in the eternal Nothing an eternal will arises, to introduce the Nothing into Something, so that the will may find, feel, and behold itself; for in the Nothing, the will would not be manifest to itself. Chapter 2, 7. — Thus, that which is found now desires to return into the will of the still Nothing, so that it may have joy and rest therein; and the Nothing is its medicine. 1) The desire for freedom is gentle and light, and is called God; and the desire for nature is still dark within itself, dry, hungry, and fierce: this is called God's wrath, and the dark world as the First Principle. The light world as the Second Principle is indeed not a separate being, but one holds the other enclosed within itself. Chapter 2, 28. — One time the fallen will springs from the Nothing, which is longed for again as light-deity; another time the light-world springs from the Nothing of the First Principle, the dark-world, or both establish themselves simultaneously. — 2) Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), was a Swiss physician and alchemist whose vocabulary deeply influenced Böhme.</note> on the three primary essences. 10. p. 15. <meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s "The Signature of All Things" (Signatura Rerum). It transitions from the previous discussion of the "nothingness" of the will into the specific chapters dealing with medical, alchemical, and ethical applications of his mystical system, focusing particularly on the concepts of selfhood versus serenity.</meta>

...the essence lies (Chapter 8), and how the physician should heal diseases (Chapter 9), is presented with the most meticulous precision and reinforced through astrological and alchemical discussions based on the most strange, half-thought-out experiments.

From Chapters 10–16, Böhme returns to "Humanity," and, as usual, the mysticism here is among the more tolerable portions of the writings, although it is still constantly intermingled with alchemical views. When he speaks of the internal and external cure of humans, it says:
> "God must become man, man must become God, heaven must become one thing with the earth, the earth must become heaven; if you wish to make heaven out of earth, then give to the earth the food of heaven, so that the earth may receive the will of heaven."

The signature, the concept, the figure of man is <term>predestination</term> <note>original: "Prädestinatio"</note>, the <term>impression</term> <note>original: "Impression"</note>, the nature of which is determined by the degree to which one of the three realms predominates in man; only in this sense does the doctrine of fate apply. But so that man may become free from it, Christ reunited his body with the divine Virgin, and therefore the <term>Election of Grace</term> <note>original: "Gnadenwahl"; the theological idea that God chooses who will be saved</note> is a false doctrine. This is treated in Chapters 12–15. The "impression" is <term>I-hood</term> <note>original: "Ichheit"</note>, the <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: "Selbheit"</note>; whoever wishes to enter into freedom must therefore completely renounce "I-hood" and enter into the "Nothing," into God, in total <term>serenity</term> <note>original: "Gelassenheit"; a key mystical term for yielding one's ego to God</note>. For
> "all sins arise from selfhood, for selfhood swings itself with desire into its own, making itself into greed and envy. Right, true serenity is the dying of the disgust against God; whoever entirely leaves his selfhood and gives himself over with mind and desire, senses and will into God's mercy, into the striving of Jesus Christ, he has died to the earthly world in his will, etc. Everything that grieves and distresses you is your selfhood; you make yourself into your own enemy and lead yourself into your own death. If you wish to go out of death again, you must completely leave your own desire, which leads itself into foreign beings, and become as a nothing in your selfhood and own desire."

Where Böhme draws the consequences of this mysticism in Chapter 15, the language is immediately beautified, the sentences flow

<page-num>215</page-num>

smoothly along, and an intimate, heartfelt spirit breathes over the whole:
> "Therefore, dear brothers, guard yourselves against strife and contempt, where people argue over literal forms; a true Christian has nothing to argue about, for he kills off his rational desire; he desires only to make God's knowledge within himself. The outward form is only a guide; God must become man, or man becomes God ¹). — All self-claimed rights and power with which the wretched are tormented, these all come from selfhood, whose origin is in the expressed form, which have introduced themselves into a selfhood with the form and been led away from God," etc. ²)

The crown of this mysticism is the concluding chapter, where Böhme expresses with true fervor the worthy idea that love will finally overcome wrath; that is the eternal signature, that is the heavenly joy:
> "for a lily blooms over mountain and valley, in all ends of the earth; whoever seeks there, finds. Amen."

The *Signature of All Things* <note>original: "Signatura rerum"</note> unites strange opposites. Its basis is the mixing of metaphysical and physical perspectives with the dialectical <term>process</term>, the confusion of the thing with the concept, of the power with the name and the designation. Therefore, on the one hand, one finds great philosophical clarity, much like in the small writings of 1620 and in the *Theoscopia* <note>Böhme’s work "Theoscopia" or "The Highly Precious Gate of the Divine Contemplation"</note>; but on the other hand, the "conflict" is attempted to be explained according to the <term>position</term>, <term>negation</term>, and <term>sublation</term> <note>original: "Aufhebung"; the process of a concept being preserved but transformed by its opposite</note> of the concept through chemical and mechanical experiments of the most tasteless kind. The contrast between the incomprehensibility of <term>Theophrastian alchemism</term> <note>referring to the chemical-mystical theories of Paracelsus</note> is all the more glaring, the more promising the beautiful mystical introduction and the way of thinking in the first two chapters are. Evidence for the <term>natural language</term> <note>original: "Natursprache"; the idea that words should perfectly mirror the essence of objects</note> is missing here; but its theory, as being closely connected with the doctrine of the designation of all beings, becomes more grounded.

It now remains to solve the ethical problem of the contradiction between good and evil at this stage. This question is the same as that of the Election of Grace, the pre...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s theological development, specifically transitioning from his earlier physical-chemical explanations of the universe to his more mature dialectical and ethical works. It focuses on how Böhme attempts to reconcile the existence of evil with a singular, all-powerful God.</meta>

...doctrine of predestination; for the principle of Good, conceived as the Godhead and the All-Encompassing, cannot tolerate the emergence of a dualistic power as an abstract independent entity. He must have established Evil itself, pre-determining the fates of men according to their worth by virtue of His omnipotence and omniscience. Through this, reason forced a sense of unity into the worldview, but dualism was not yet driven from the field: for the contradictions in which human free will and Böhme's concept of God's creation entangled us were too harsh to be outweighed for long by the only half-satisfying feeling of a forced unity. — The problem that divided Calvinists and Lutherans, which earlier theosophists <note>Theosophists in this context refers to mystical philosophers who sought direct knowledge of the divine, often through nature.</note> had tried to cut through with physical and chemical explanations, also had to drive Böhme toward a solution. This occurred after his primal doctrine of the principles and their dialectical execution in detail had been attempted, leaving only the ethical contradiction as the main point requiring treatment. — Once before, the solution to this task had been more set aside than actually found in the physical stage of development, where Evil—the kingdom of darkness—lay immanent within God the Father as a "reverse side" or a shadow; but attention has already been drawn to the inadequacy of this solution, which was reminiscent of the Paracelsian explanation <note>Referring to Paracelsus, a Swiss physician and alchemist who influenced Böhme’s early thought.</note> through chemical processes. — Other, new prospects are opened by the dialectical stage of development, and the following writing marks the attempt to shape these prospects into a present reality, always moving through the confusing interference of the earlier stages.

### 12. On the Election of Grace. *<note>original Latin: "De Electione gratiae"</note>*

This work—not to be confused with the second apology against Balthasar Tilke, which Böhme calls the "Treatise on the Election of Grace" in his 15th Epistle § 21 and 16th Epistle § 1—was completed on February 8, 1623.1) Already there, a decided opposition to Calvinism was evident.2) Here, for the first time from the standpoint of a mystical pantheism <note>The belief that the universe is identical with divinity.</note>, Böhme addresses the view that God, before the times of the creation of creatures and this world, held a counsel

---

1) Epistle 39. 8. Claris 147.   2) S. S. 317.

<page-num>217</page-num>

within Himself in His Trinity through Wisdom regarding what He wished to make and what all being should become. According to this view, He drew a purpose into Himself as to where He wished to order each thing, so that by purpose He had chosen one part of humanity for the kingdom of heaven in His holy delight, and the other part for eternal damnation. Consequently, all things would necessarily have to happen in this way, and the portion of wrath would be so hidden and rejected by God's purpose that no possibility of God's favor remained, nor any possibility of damnation for the other part. Böhme argues that this error arises because people consider God to be something distant and foreign, who dwells high above the stars outside the location of this world, and rules now only through His Spirit. —

Now, in the first three chapters, the attempt begins to resolve the contradiction between the possibility of sin, the existence of evil, and the freedom of the will with the omnipotence and holiness of God. In the works of Weigel <note>Valentin Weigel (1533–1588), an earlier German mystic.</note>, this contradiction was simply avoided; God was good, God was Everything, and thus Evil was also in God. Schwenkfeld <note>Caspar Schwenkfeld (1489–1561), a Protestant Reformer who emphasized the "inner" word of God.</note> attempted a solution in a very clumsy, unphilosophical way by assuming a hidden and a revealed counsel of God. — Böhme now first develops the doctrine of his Godhead: the <term>ungroundable will</term> <note>original: "ungründliche Wille"; a core concept in Böhme’s work describing the absolute origin of the divine, which has no "ground" or cause beneath it.</note>, which is only one, and has nothing before it nor behind it, which in itself is only One, which is as a Nothing and yet is Everything. This "One God" is neither evil nor good; He "gives birth within Himself to the eternal, unique Good, as a tangible will, the Son of the ungroundable will." In this divine contemplation, in the <term>science</term> <note>original: "Scienz"; here meaning a mystical, formative knowledge</note>, the splitting of the Godhead, the process of the Idea in negation (which is established simultaneously with its position)—in this <term>Great Mystery</term> <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note>, in the divine <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Magia"</note> lies the origin of Good and Evil. However, this is not so that Evil exists in and of itself, but only in the reciprocal relationship, in opposition to the Good:

> “Everything is good in itself, and only evil when it runs in a foreign mother (Chapter 6, 25).” <note>The "foreign mother" refers to a thing acting against its own nature or proper place in the divine order.</note>

From this foundation, Good and Evil are derived; Chapter 4 deals with the Creation, and Chapter 5 with the origin of man in the usual physical manner of contemplation; the seven species, source-spirits, or forms...

<summary>This page introduces Böhme's 1623 treatise "On the Election of Grace," where he rejects the Calvinist view of predestination. He argues that Evil is not a pre-ordained decree from a distant God, but emerges from the "Great Mystery" of God's own self-unfolding process.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of Jakob Böhme’s philosophical system, specifically how he transitions from describing the physical and chemical nature of the universe to addressing the ethical problem of evil and its relationship to God.</meta>

<page-num>218</page-num>

(Ideas) are brought forth, and it is recounted how the third principle develops through the fiery <term>science</term> <note>original: "Scienz"; in Böhme’s usage, this refers to a spiritual or intuitive knowledge or a "knowing-power" rather than modern laboratory science.</note>, how all three principles struggle within man, and how the <term>Spirit of the World</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Mundi"</note> works in the third principle. After this comes the fall of man and his state before the fall, for which God was not in the slightest the cause; on the contrary, it lay in the very concept of man to fall in his <term>imagination</term> even before the deed occurred. The 7th chapter speaks of the animal revelation—how after the fall, man’s eyes were opened, namely through <term>science</term>, which revealed the distinctness of the three principles. The remaining six chapters seek to prove this view by referencing passages of Scripture that are usually cited as supports for the doctrine of <term>predestination</term> <note>original: "Gnadenwahl"; the theological idea that God chooses certain individuals for salvation by grace alone.</note>. —

So the writing falls into two parts: a dogmatic one and a polemical or applied one. The first is split into the doctrine of principles and the doctrine of man; and the expression adjusts itself in the well-known gradation according to the varying content. The fundamental doctrine of the principles has gained a fairly definite, philosophic-mystical expression, especially if one is satisfied to see the same doctrine repeated almost literally and frequently without new flashes of light; in the second part, the purity of thought is less frequently encountered, owing to the nature of the subject. It sounds indeed like sudden enlightenment when <term>Böhme</term> says: “The <term>Mind</term> <note>original: "Mens"</note> lies in the <term>Being</term> <note>original: "Ens"</note> as the soul in the body; the <term>Mental Word</term> speaks out the <term>Essential Word</term> <note>original: "Entalische"; relating to the inner essence or being.</note>, heaven encloses the <term>Mind</term> and the fantasy the <term>Being</term>; in the <term>Mind</term> is understood the divine holy power in the framing of the Word, as the Word of powers enframes itself into a spiritual essence, where the Word of powers is substantial” ^1); or when he compares the world to a clockwork ^2), in which the upper and lower mutually condition and desire each other; “in which reciprocity also

---

1) How wavering the expressions are here again. The <term>Abyssal Ground</term> <note>original: "Ungrund"; Böhme’s term for the absolute, undifferentiated divine depth that precedes all existence.</note> is not sharply separated from the first principle; and yet it is neither evil nor good. The second principle is here made comprehensible, and what is more natural than to call reality—as something comprehensible—the negation? Here, comprehensibility is intended only to designate the position within the <term>Abyssal Ground</term>, not existence or being. —
2) Chapter 5, 4. ^3) Chapter 5, 26.

<page-num>219</page-num>

man was created;” yet the <term>Paracelsian</term> <note>Referring to the teachings of Paracelsus, a Renaissance physician and alchemist who used chemical analogies to explain the soul and nature.</note> doctrines help philosophical knowledge not at all, and the sensuality of expressions such as <term>Spirit of the World</term>, <term>Tincture</term>, <term>Limbus</term>, and <term>Matrix</term> <note>Terms borrowed from alchemy: "Tincture" is a spiritualizing life-force, "Limbus" is the formative matter of the world, and "Matrix" is the receptive or "mother" principle of nature.</note> run directly counter to pure thought. The third part has a specifically theosophical direction, whereby one cannot hide the fact that the author knows how to use the Bible for his purposes with an extremely bold grasp, to no less damage to its natural sense than ecclesiastical orthodoxy and literalism had inflicted upon it. The scripture must be understood mystically and magically: thus its value is recognized. But <term>theosophy</term> knows excellently how to first draw a veil over the passages by declaring that reason cannot comprehend them, and only showing them again to the faithful after a complete <term>metamorphosis</term>: all in divine-truthful enlightenment.

<term>Böhme</term> is not yet a philosopher; he only forms the transition to philosophy, and indeed to a strictly dogmatic one, as all mysticism is. Therefore, one should never demand of him that he solve a problem sufficiently for reason or for the critique of thought. It lies in the nature of intuitive <term>theosophy</term> not to ask about contradiction and its resolution. It may brood and meditate before it reaches intuition; but in this moment alone lies the consciousness of the theosophist, not in the brooding and meditating. What enlightenment grasps, stands; and that in which the theosophist is absorbed is for him unshakeable truth. Just as the senses never discover or solve a contradiction, but only the understanding does, so the mystic's thought is an inner sensuality and passive intuition. But people have wanted it to be precisely this problem—the problem of Evil—that was solved most satisfactorily for the philosopher by <term>Böhme</term>. Against such a widespread and completely erroneous opinion, arising from ignorance of <term>Böhme’s</term> writings, it is a duty to counter that despite unspeakable struggling, despite enormous exertion of strength, he only arrives back where he started: namely, at the contradiction of Good and Evil. The success of his Herculean effort is limited to pushing the contradiction back from earthly human life onto God, the...

<summary>This page critiques Jacob Böhme’s transition from alchemical terminology to a mystical theology of man and God. It argues that while Böhme is a vital precursor to philosophy, his work remains a form of intuitive mysticism that shifts the problem of evil onto the divine rather than solving it through reason.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Paracelsus, Theosophy, Spirit of the World, Science, Abyssal Ground, Predestination, Mysticism, Problem of Evil, Doctrine of Principles</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s philosophical development, focusing on how he addresses the "primordial contradiction" within the divine nature and transitions into his later Gnostic and sacramental writings.</meta>

<page-num>220</page-num>

to unite the totality of all possible problems into a single one, and to explain all others through the absolute positing of a supreme contradiction. In this *concentration*, in becoming aware of this supreme contradiction, lies his merit—the apparent solution when compared to the crude, sensory, or illogical conceptions of his predecessors. Only those can find satisfaction in *Böhme's* view who are willing to stop at the necessity of an absolute contradiction: that the Idea, and thus also that of the Deity, posits an *antithesis* <note>original: "Antithesis"</note>, and that *thesis* <note>original: "Thesis"</note> and *antithesis* together form a *synthesis* <note>original: "Synthesis"</note>; only those who believe they have lifted the veil of truth by recognizing this primordial contradiction. If, after the positing of this contentious triad of the Idea, all life—whose essence lies in the struggle of concepts and in their *progress* <note>original: "Progresse"</note>—develops naturally according to its original state, then a second contradiction immediately leaps forth: how the freedom of the will can lead man to good or evil and from whence his moral accountability arises. Böhme himself, whom ethical feeling had driven to research, believes—trapped in the physical—that he has solved all small mysteries through the "Great Mystery," yet he forgets that final point: that man, despite the positing of that distinctness, can still stand within a will-less *causal nexus* <note>original: "Causalnexus"</note>. He seeks to unlock his "Great Mystery," his great primordial contradiction, through many words and countless repetitions in which he struggles in vain for light, and through parables which unfortunately are based on the very same problem that is supposed to be explained ¹). Perhaps, however, this writing serves best to trace the connection of such mysticism with *Hegelian* philosophy, because on the whole, while lack of clarity prevails, there is no confusion within it.

With this, the main questions regarding the dialectical stage of development are also settled. A few more small writings

<note>¹) Böhme often even becomes incensed at reason for not wanting to listen to the mystery at all—how the fiery *science* <note>See previous page: "Scienz," referring to an intuitive knowing-power.</note> leads from the will of the *Unground* <term>Unground</term> <note>original: "Ungrund"; Böhme's term for the "groundless" or the absolute void/abyss from which God and the world emerge.</note> to the utterance, to the second will, and how this in turn leads to the distinctness in the third *principle*; he perhaps felt himself that only the lulling of reason into mystical annihilation could mirror satisfaction to him here. (Ch. 6, 20.)</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>221</page-num>

provide interesting, clear repetitions; in two larger ones, however, an aspect emerges that has not yet been treated as the main subject of a work, namely *Gnosticism* <note>original: "Gnostik"</note>: the historical-philosophical conception of the mystical process, first in the reality of the historical *Christ* as the *God-man*, then the repetition of this *identity* in every human being—the second *principle* in the others—and the sequence of development toward the same in history. However, because *Böhme's* view here has a strictly theological coloring in the first point and aligns closely with *Schwenkfeld's* doctrine <note>Caspar Schwenkfeld (1489–1561) was a German reformer who emphasized the internal, spiritual "living Word" over external sacraments.</note>, and in the second point brings forward no original ideas regarding the *history of religion and mystics*, but rather ensures the original doctrine of the *principles* receives a less bright and clear illumination than in the previous writings, both works are of neither great philosophical significance nor importance for the spiritual development of *Böhme*, whose pivot is the mediation between *theosophy* and *philosophy*. Nevertheless, the sheer volume of the second work seems to have inspired such awe in many readers that it has often been regarded as his masterpiece. The first work forms the theosophical basis of the *Gnosis*, while the second attempts to carry that thought through according to biblical history.

### 13. *Of Christ's Testaments* <note>original: "De Testamentis Christi"</note>.
->(Consisting of two treatises.)<-

Written in May 1623, and sent with an epistle to *Karl von Ender*. The fact that this specific writing is addressed to him, which aligns so closely with *Schwenkfeld*, suggests that the doctrine of the Silesian reformer had been preserved in *Ender's* family since the events in the middle of the 16th century ¹). The communication of the *sacraments*, the *dogma* that is inseparable from Christianity and which, even according to the most orthodox conception, rests on a mystical foundation—namely, on the *fact* of the *immanence of God* in the human *subject*—is the subject of this writing as the mediation of the identity of God in humanity within the individual: the mystical body of Christ and the water of the soul in the Godhead.

<note>¹) Part I, pp. 96–98.</note>

<sig>16</sig>

<summary>This page analyzes Böhme's dialectical method of "Thesis-Antithesis-Synthesis" and his transition into Gnostic and sacramental themes, specifically his work "Of Christ's Testaments" and its connection to the reformer Schwenkfeld.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the examination of Jakob Böhme’s theology, specifically moving from the theoretical "Great Mystery" of the divine contradictions to the practical application of these ideas in the Sacraments (the Testaments). It explores how the "Second Principle"—the realm of light and love—interacts with the fallen human condition through the mystical body of Christ.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>

**16***

In the second principle <term>Principle: In Böhme's system, the "Second Principle" represents the world of Light, Love, and the Son, contrasting with the "First Principle" of dark fire and wrath.</term> is the object of the Sacraments; the divine Virgin of the second principle—as we know from earlier writings, in which the views treated here are already present as fragments—fled after the Fall of Adam. So that she might return, the Body of Christ or the "Water of the Soul" must come into us again and help us; but before this, the other principles—the elemental and sidereal <note>Refers to the "star-fire" or the astrological and physical influences of the material universe.</note> fire of the first principle—must consume [the vanity], so that the soul can enter purely into the second. This was indicated by the sacrifice of the Old Testament as a true figure of the sacrifice of Christ: through the sacrifice of his body, he offered our humanity to the wrath of God, and in this sacrifice, God smelled his sweet love in the humanity of Christ and reconciled his wrath in the fire. ¹) In the fire, the animal vanity of the human will burned away in the fire of wrath: thus the pure human will then penetrated into God's Fire of Love as a sweet fragrance. The water of Baptism signifies the same thing: namely, the rising and the annihilation of the "I" in the second principle—the same suffering and dying as the sacrifice of Christ. He communicated only the mystical body and the blood to the disciples, but the sacrifice must come first; one must experience suffering and dying within oneself to participate in the mystical process of becoming a member of the Body of Christ. He gave himself to them to enjoy in both his human and divine attributes at once, so that they should enjoy the grace (which had entered into humanity, strangled death, unlocked human life again, and led it out through death) in a new human life; he gave it to them under an elemental medium, so that faith might grasp it through a medium ¹). They ate and drank with the essential, longing mouth of faith, not with a circumscribed, natural concept ²). The same flesh, the eternal element, an essence of Paradise, as a spiritual mummy <note>In Böhme’s vocabulary, a "mummy" is not a corpse but a concentrated spiritual essence or a "virtue" that preserves life.</note>, from which man—according to the visible body—took his understanding in the Word of Creation <note>original: "Verbo Fiat"</note>, which became blind in Adam regarding the Kingdom of Heaven; this fleshly essence is filled with heavenly living essence in Christ's incarnation

---

¹) The Lord's Supper 2, 26. ²) The Lord's Supper 3, 3.

<column-break/>

and is made alive again; this is eaten by the true Adamic man <note>The "inner man" or the spiritual nature that corresponds to the original, unfallen Adam.</note> who is hidden within the coarse shell of the four elements. ¹)

Since this act is purely internal, the well-known consequences follow. First, the forgiveness of sins does not happen through the ritual of the Sacrament or absolution; unless man turns away from sin so that he adopts another will—to depart from falsehood—then Christ becomes a light in the man's life and transforms the eternal night into a bright day. ²) Therefore, the godless mouth is not capable of love and receives only Christ's suffering and death, and not his resurrection. He is only capable of judgment; Christ’s Sacrament does not erase sins for him so that he could hide beneath them and cover himself with "Christ’s purple mantle" <note>A critique of the idea that one can be "justified" by a legalistic covering of sins without an internal transformation of the heart.</note>. ³) The enjoyment take place internally, so that on the one hand, an external food and symbolic action is not strictly necessary, and on the other hand, an unconditional blessing is not connected with the consumption, as is the case with the godless. — A child of pious parents who dies before baptism is nonetheless within the anointing of Christ; for it has put on the essence of the parents, since it arose from their anointing—from their baptized soul- and body-essence. The external elemental water is not the ground of baptism, but rather the spiritual water, which is in covenant with the Word and connected with faith; the covenant with baptism exists so that every person shall themselves, with their own will—as a particular branch on the tree and as an individual life—put on the covenant of Christ through the external means ordained for that purpose ⁴).

The power of this inwardness extends so far that even if the baptizer is an unbeliever, the covenant still works and baptizes; the baptizer is then nothing more in the high work of baptism than the baptismal font <note>original: "Taufstein"</note> which holds the water—he is silent. ⁵) Likewise, it is not in the priest's power to bring the body and blood of Christ into the bread and wine with his blessing ⁶); a godless priest cannot absolve and receive,

⁴) Baptism 4, 16, 43, 15. ⁵) Baptism I. 4, 12. ⁶) The Lord's Supper 4, 19.

<summary>This page explains Böhme’s inward-focused view of the Sacraments, where true Baptism and the Lord's Supper are spiritual processes of the soul rather than mere external rituals. It emphasizes that without a genuine "turning of the will" and the internal light of Christ, the outward forms of the church remain ineffective for the godless.</summary>

<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Second Principle, Divine Virgin, Sophia, Sacraments, Baptism, Lord's Supper, spiritual mummy, Word of Creation, inwardness, wrath of God, Fire of Love</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s writings on the Sacraments (Baptism and the Lord’s Supper), comparing his views to other radical reformers like Schwenkfeld, and then transitions into a discussion of his monumental commentary on Genesis, the "Mysterium Magnum."</meta>

it is only an external, unreal tool for itself.

As for the external arrangement of the three writings of quite similar content, the first concerning Baptism deals first with the self-contemplation <note>In Böhme’s thought, this refers to the human will turning inward upon itself rather than toward God.</note> of the human will, through which it has departed from its Mother—the Divinity in the second Principle <note>The "Second Principle" is Böhme’s term for the realm of Light, Love, and the Son.</note>; then (Chapter 2) it deals with the institution of Holy Baptism to heal this fact, which has its model in Christ's sacrifice; His suffering and death, representing this very likeness, are treated in Chapter 3; and the fourth chapter deals with the external act of baptism and the internal mystical consciousness of rebirth in the Body of Christ. The second book on Baptism, begun by the author for the simple-minded but not completed, has exactly the same content as the first. — First, Böhme seeks the reason for the external understanding of baptism in "rational faith"—the belief that Christ is far from us and thrones high in heaven at the right hand of God <term>locally</term> <note>original: "localiter." This refers to the theological debate over whether Christ’s body is physically in one spot in heaven or spiritually omnipresent.</note>, without Him being in our hearts and without us forming members of His body. In Chapter 2, he interprets circumcision as a likeness of baptism, as a symbolic act in the Old Testament; in Chapter 3, he presents Christ's death as the archetype of baptism. — The short treatise on the Holy Supper first compares the Old Testament sacrifice with Christ's death and the mystical sinking of the soul into "Nothing," into God; it then speaks of the Supper of the New Testament and its purpose; in Chapter 4, there is a warning against an externalized understanding; finally, Böhme gives instructions for preparation and, in Chapter 5, deals with the differing views of the religious parties.

Just as the entire doctrine presented in this collection of writings agrees not only in content but also literally with the views of Schwenkfeld <note>Caspar Schwenkfeld (1489–1561) was a Silesian nobleman and mystic who emphasized the "Inner Word" and the spiritual nature of the sacraments, often clashing with Luther.</note>, Böhme comes particularly close to them in this last chapter during the polemic, in which he fiercely attacked the equally external opinions of the Lutherans, Calvinists, and Catholics. Schwenkfeld filled thick volumes of epistles and treatises on this: on the "Papist" teaching and faith; on the Lutheran and Zwinglian likewise. Böhme also warns that through the mindless parroting of a sectarian formula, one loses sight of the true, inward spirit of 

<column-break/>

Christianity, and everyone who argues over the "How, Where, and When" of Christ does not feel Him within themselves; no salvation can be earned through the praying of formulas. —

The *Testaments of Christ* show nothing original in this Böhme-esque interpretation that would claim a preference over the other writings of the same author or other mystics, especially Schwenkfeld. The expression is usually theological.

Through them, sufficient emphasis is placed on the spiritual interpretation of the Christian doctrine of salvation, so that now the bearer of the act of salvation himself, Christ—who was previously presented as the founder of Christianity in a historical person—can now appear completely mystically in proper consequence as being in all eternity, before and after his incarnation in history or myth. He represents the spirit of the inner Church in contrast to the external, heretical one.

## 14. Mysterium Magnum. Explanation of the First Book of Moses.

Written in the year 1623, finished on September 11 of the same year. According to the title, the work deals with the revelation of the divine Word through the three principles of Divine Being, also with the origin of the world and creation, wherein the realm of Nature and the realm of Grace are explained; for a greater understanding of the Old and New Testaments—what Adam and Christ are, and how man should recognize himself in the light of nature, what he is, and wherein his temporal and eternal life, as well as his salvation and damnation, consist. Although one would hardly expect to encounter less than Böhme's entire system of teaching based on these words, the core of the book is a philosophy of the history of humanity, as far as such a thing is possible for a mystic who does not take into account the rich abundance of all human history, but rather makes the inner mystical fact and the tradition of the divine Word the object of his investigations. Böhme seeks a single dominant fundamental idea in all the narratives of Holy Scripture, especially in Genesis, with a sensible comparison to the New Testament. Throughout the Middle Ages, the Bible had already been interpreted mystically because people

<summary>This page analyzes Böhme's "Testaments of Christ," emphasizing his spiritualized view of the sacraments and his alignment with Schwenkfeld against externalized, "formulaic" religion. It also introduces the "Mysterium Magnum," Böhme's expansive mystical commentary on the book of Genesis.</summary>

<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Baptism, Holy Supper, Caspar Schwenkfeld, Mysterium Magnum, Genesis, mystical interpretation, rational faith, inner Church, three principles</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s works, transitioning from his shorter sacramental treatises to a detailed critique of his massive commentary on the Book of Genesis, titled "Mysterium Magnum" (The Great Mystery).</meta>

could not understand how such a holy book, inspired by God, should be understood in the same way as secular literature—according to its naturally simple sense. In his view, it had to offer deeper sources for the understanding of the Godhead and for the salvation of the soul, for theosophical struggle and inner enlightenment, than a poetic, legendary history of the fates of the Israelite people back to its very beginnings—where a mystical-religious cosmogony <note>A theory regarding the origin of the universe.</note> and anthropogony <note>A theory regarding the origin of humanity.</note> forms the starting point—is able to provide.

Just as God, before all beginnings, is split in His concept into a duality, and this duality is united and tied in the third Principle, so this "knot" runs through humanity between the visible and invisible world, the outer and inner church, sham-Christianity and the children of God; for this world is a revelation of the invisible essence of God, in whom all forms and appearances are prefigured in a magical way within the concept. This reproduction of the visible world according to the archetype of the Godhead—this pervasive, perceived unity of the All, of spirit and bodies—is called the *Mysterium Magnum* <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"; translated: The Great Mystery.</note>, the "breathing out of the eternal One" $^1$), the eternal spiritual nature that is hidden in the visible $^2$), the "outflow and counter-reflection of divine science" $^3$). It remains, of course, unclear why this specific book received the name *Mysterium Magnum*; it could be given with almost equal right to every other writing of the Theosopher, according to the explanation he himself voiced; for all of theosophy gathers its rays into this one point: the Trinity of the Godhead, which alone is truth. It seeks to fathom this one point, and the "Only Thing" into which the human will should surrender and let itself go—it is the core and shell of theosophy, the Mystery which it claims to unveil.

The mystical idea—that humanity is continuously guided through a steady development; that the Body of Christ was not first effective at His incarnation, but lived on and on in spiritual men, the god-fearing, since the "Divine

---

$^1$) Introduction 11. $^2$) Introduction 9. $^3$) Extract 1.

<page-num>227</page-num>

Virgin" <note>In Böhme’s system, the "Divine Virgin" represents Wisdom (Sophia) or the immanent mirror of God's glory.</note>, the immanence of God, had fled from fallen man and could only be reacquired through a reaching-down from above—lived and breathed the idea that Christianity, from the beginning, found its true essence in inwardness, sublime above external signs and sensory-estranged ceremonies. It was not to be found in the theological bickering of limited, denominational scholars of the word and scripture. Yet, the more precise execution of this in every single chapter of Genesis lacks a higher interest for anyone who cannot feel at home in the strained interpretations of mysticism. Böhme's strained interpretation possesses as little originality or aptitude for philosophy as his hair-raising alchemical and astrological knowledge. Almost every page shows how, with the slightest care, a parallelism between the persons of Genesis and Christ is forcibly dragged in. If it were known exactly how many interpolations in the *Mysterium Magnum* are to be attributed to his subsequent friend Franckenberg <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652), a Silesian nobleman and mystic who was a devoted follower and biographer of Böhme.</note>, we would be better able to judge the emergence of that peculiar Silesian mysticism—which was already mentioned in "The Threefold Life of Man," p. 284. This movement sought to make mystical intuition microscopically clear through symbols, pictorial representation, structural diagrams, calculations, and wordplay, from which the greatest absurdity arises: an immediate result of the contradiction of trying to illuminate internal facts, mystical sensations, and lightning-like intuitions through reflection, cold forms, and the supposed precision of a report.

For example, in Chapter 2, 4: "When the flash or terror rises, it is in the point, and in a glance makes a triangle $\perp$ or $+$ cross, and this is the correct interpretation of the character ♁ <note>The symbol for Earth or the 'Globus Cruciger' (orb and cross).</note>; it is firstly the sharpness of all things and the revealed God in Trinity. — The upper cross signifies the unformed Word in Trinity outside all nature, and the character stands thus ♁, and this character signifies the formed Word, as the Angelic world <note>original: "Englische Welt." In the German of this period, 'englisch' often meant 'angelic' (from Engel), not 'English.'</note>."

In the 6th Chapter, a table of principles and qualities is recorded, which in no way contributes to easing the understanding of his mysticism, unless its very essence is meant to be confusion; especially the first column, which...

is neither the first nor the second Principle, but contains both; the same is repeated in the second column, and if one wanted to ascribe the first to the *Ungrund* <note>The "Groundless" or "Abyss"—Böhme’s term for the absolute, undifferentiated nothingness out of which God and the world emerge.</note>, one would be embarrassed by the concern of how pure indifference—the abyss of all thought and intuition, the eternal Nothing—attains the seven Source-spirits. — Furthermore, a passage in Chapter 11, 34: "But in the Point of the Sun <note>original: "Puncto Solis"</note> the eighth number is open, as eternal nature, the eternal magical fire, and within the fire the eternal Tincture, which is the ninth number, and in the Tincture the X Cross, where the Godhead reveals itself, which is the tenth number, and beyond this revelation the eternal understanding, as the One, that is the God JEHOVAH, as the *Ungrund*." In Chapter 30, a drawing is included showing six intertwined rods with three crowns above them, a burning piece of wood, a sword, and a rod—"the sign of the end"—which is meant to conclude the sixth age (according to the division into six world ages common to the mystics of the Middle Ages), "there the external wrath falls, and with it that same external structure, the city of Babel."

Completely incomprehensible in Frankenberg’s <note>Abraham von Frankenberg (1593–1652), Böhme’s friend and biographer who shared his interest in mystical symbolism.</note> manner, or perhaps having become a model for him, is the interpretation of letters in Chapter 35, 48–53, the conclusion of which reads as follows:

> "And these five (Vowels) <note>original: "Vocales"</note> AEJOV, which are contained in Jesus and Jehovah, are enclosed within the comprehension of three, as in such a word, which is AOV: Father, Son, Holy Spirit. The triangle indicates the trinity of the properties of the persons, and the V on the triangle indicates the Spirit in the H, as in the breathing <note>A reference to the "H" in the name of God, representing the breath of life</note>, where the whole of God reveals Himself in the manner of a spirit through His proceeding out of Himself. The other letters besides the five vowels proceed from the name Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH.</note>, as from the Center of eternal nature, from the Principle, and indicate and express the distinctions of the formed wisdom, as of the formed word in the three principles, etc."

But even apart from these exercises of the "sensual tongue" <note>original: "sensualischen Zunge"; Böhme's belief that the physical sound of words expresses the inner essence of the things they name.</note>, the interpretation of numbers, and the like, it can easily be explained from what has been said so far how the book could not achieve great significance, since its only merit lies in the overall intuition placed in the center, while the details, as everywhere, only cause confusion.

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>229</header>

The close adherence to the biblical text and its expressions, and the method of mystical explanation, make it understandable how Böhme allows himself to indulge entirely in an unphilosophical style of writing. The apocalyptic tone is present in abundance; the Revelation of John <note>original: "Offenbarung Johannis"</note> has become a source for the most wondrous interpretations and outpourings. In the *Mysterium Magnum*, the language remains at a very low level, where it does not shrink from even the coarsest obscenities to the point of repulsiveness in its incessant repetition and deep degradation ¹). Only rarely—where theosophical brooding ceases and the outpourings of the heart gain unhindered course in the ethico-mystical admonitions and in the polemic against externalized views—does the phenomenon repeat that Böhme’s writing style unfolds a genuinely German, Lutheran power and heartiness in beautiful periods: but by no means are these passages of the *Mysterium* of such excellence that others from different writings could not be placed alongside them.

Just as philosophical expression is displaced by figurative language, fantastic words, and mysterious interpretations, so too does the theme of the writing itself offer little occasion for the purely speculative philosophy for which Böhme’s mysticism otherwise shows a talent; even the mystical process of entering into God's body, the Holy Ternary <note>original: "Ternarius Sanctus"; the divine manifestation or "body" of God.</note>, and the fall from it—from the Divine Virgin—is explained either through alchemical means or shrouded in a tone of revelation. Chapters 16–25 provide a great deal of material for this study, dealing with Adam’s fall, Eve, Christ’s Essence <note>original: "Ens"</note> and the serpent’s Essence, and heavenly and earthly food. The *Mysterium Magnum* can therefore hardly be grouped with the previously mentioned writings of the dialectical period; Böhme’s struggle had found its satisfaction in the works already completed; his...

<meta>This page continues the critique of Jacob Böhme’s later work, "Mysterium Magnum," contrasting its perceived "Gnostic" decline with the vigor of his earlier writing, "Aurora." The previous page discussed Böhme’s complex use of numerology and letter-symbolism.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>

Productivity was exhausted; the decay is unmistakable. In perfect harmony with the historical phenomenon that Gnostics <note>Followers of ancient mystical movements who sought salvation through secret knowledge (gnosis).</note> emerge during times of declining culture—as a sign of despair for the future and a retreat into the past—Böhme’s own decline is marked by the Gnostic direction of his vision. The ***Mysterium Magnum*** <note>Latin: "The Great Mystery," Böhme’s 1623 commentary on Genesis.</note> deserves no higher place than the ***Aurora*** <note>Latin: "The Daybreak," Böhme’s first work (1612).</note>; it seems night is falling again when long and broad investigations are conducted into the animalistic "maggot-sack" of the intestines, the internal principal organs of earthly life instead of the feminine **Matrix** <note>Used by Böhme to mean the "womb" or the creative, receptive principle of nature.</note>, "stinking hair," and so forth. The parts concerning "feminine playfulness" belong here among the refreshing resting points $^1$); for the power of a breakthrough philosophy is almost entirely absent. Only the beginning of the writing offers an understanding of the guiding fundamental vision as a whole. While the repetition of the primordial process of the Godhead—as the archetype of the "Mystery" in the Third Principle, the struggle between the internal and external Church, and between true and sham Christianity—is by no means new, it still offers some interest to see how the Three Principles identify themselves as decisively with the Trinity. One sees how the ***Ungrund*** <note>The "Groundless" or "Abyss"—Böhme’s term for the absolute, undifferentiated nothingness before God’s self-revelation.</note> imperceptibly separates itself as the unthinkable Unity of the Trinity, whereas in other writings this view (belonging to the dialectical stage) is still obscured by the confusion that God the Father, the First Principle, is himself the *Ungrund*. Thus, in Chapter 7, 13:

> "Therein one understands three properties in a single Being: as the Father with the **Fire-World**, and the Son with the **Love-Desire** in the Light (as with the **Light-World** or with the great gentleness in the fire), and the Holy Spirit with the **weaving life**, and so forth. — When I consider what God is, I say: He is the **One**

1) "And in this anxious gate of true repentance stands the angel with the fire-sword, and the virginal branch presses through this fire-sword into Paradise, as into the life of Christ, and grows through this sword. There now stands the Virgin-Child with its beautiful roses in the new growth in Paradise; and the poor soul, which gives birth to this child, stands all the while within the fire-sword, tied fast with a bond to the external world, to the coarse animals, and so forth."

<page-num>231</page-num>

against the creature as an eternal **Nothing**; He has neither ground, beginning, nor place, and possesses nothing but Himself; He is the will of the *Ungrund*. — In this eternal birth, three things are for us to understand: 1) an eternal Will, 2) an eternal Mind <note>original: "Gemüthe"</note> of the Will, 3) the Outflow from the Will and Mind, which is a Spirit of the Will and Mind $^1$)."

Insofar as the ***Mysterium Magnum*** attempts to spin out the thought of the "Inner Church"—which is founded on inner experience—of a mystical Christianity existing from the beginning of humanity, there is no lack of passages with ethical-mystical content. In these, as occurs in other writings in the same way, a warning is given against the view that external worship is necessary for salvation; "for where there is living knowledge of Christ, there is the altar of God in all places, where the hungry soul may sacrifice the right, pleasing, holy offering in prayer"—though this does not mean that the **stone-walled churches** <note>original: "Mauerkirchen" — Böhme's term for institutional, physical church buildings, which he contrasted with the "church" of the heart.</note> should be abolished $^2$). Faith is nothing other than uniting the will with God and receiving God’s Word and power into one's will, so that both become One substance and being $^3$). If the free will with its desire is to move toward God, it must first go out of its "false Something," and it exists merely as if in the initial "Nothing," for to go to God, one must leave the **Selfhood** <note>original: "Selbheit" — the egoistic, self-centered state of the soul after the Fall.</note> $^4$). The person is then like God’s **carpenter’s axe**; internally, His temple is built; externally, the hands must serve, because the external kingdom belongs to the heathens in God's wrath. Therefore, we must serve godless lords and nations and be their bondsmen, but we think that we serve God thereby, and that the powerful are from the realm of nature—yet God wants these rulers also to recognize themselves as His officials $^5$). Before God, all are nothing but **cattle-herds** <note>original: "Viehhirten"</note>: the Emperor and his servant, the nobleman and his subordinate, the powerful, the rich, and the learned $^6$); all the treasures of princes and the powerful should be collected for the common benefit, for the maintenance of good orders and offices, and also

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Böhme's "Mysterium Magnum," shifting from its structural critique to its thematic treatment of social justice, universalism, and a chapter-by-chapter summary of the work's contents, before moving on to his devotional collection, "The Way to Christ."</meta>

of the wretched and the weak, but that the powerful one now tortures and sucks the marrow from the wretched for his own honor, for lust and pride—this happens in the wrath of God, and he serves Satan"¹). — The worldly judges and the "titled Christians" <note>Nominal Christians who hold the name of Christ in title only but do not follow his teachings.</note> are an abomination to him; to him, a heathen, a Turk <note>In the 17th century, "Turk" was often used as a general term for Muslims.</note>, or a mouth- and title-Christian are all the same. However, if the Turks worship the Father, then He hears them in the Son and accepts them through him into childhood²); not that they are justified because of this, but they are equal before God with the "painted" Christians who found a church in stone houses and bring nothing but quarreling and disputing into it³).

Since the manner in which the idea of the "two churches" is carried out through the Old and New Testaments has little importance due to the pettiness of the style, it is sufficient, instead of following the course of the scripture, to append a brief overview of the contents at the conclusion.

The introduction develops the thought that the visible world is the reflection and outflow of the invisible, and states the purpose of the ***Mysterium Magnum*** <note>Latin: "The Great Mystery."</note>. From Chapters 1–7, Böhme deals with the Godhead and its necessary separation into the three principles; Chapters 8–15 cover the work of Creation. Having followed the implementation of the fundamental vision in the pre-worldly, non-sensual invisible substance of the ***Ungrund*** <note>The "Groundless" or absolute abyss of the Divine.</note> and its change up to this point, the formation of this developing principle of all being and thought is now represented in humanity. First, Adam and Eve up to Chapter 25; of the internal and external Church in Cain and Abel up to 28; from Enoch to 31; from Noah to 36; Abraham to 45; Isaac and Ishmael to 56; Jacob and Esau to 63; and Joseph to the end. — The short extract appended at the back remains more on the boundary between mysticism and philosophy; it repeats the primordial process of the Trinity and the ***Gelassenheit*** <note>Translated here as "resignation" or "yieldedness"—the state of surrendering the individual will to God.</note> of the human will in God as the "Great Mystery."

### 15. Christosophy or The Way to Christ.

Under this name, seven small writings are usually grouped together, originating from different times, and their sequence in the editions is not consistently observed. The first small treatise, ***De Poenitentia Vera*** <note>Latin: "On True Repentance."</note>, was published in print by Schweinichen in 1624 along with ***De Vita Mentali*** <note>Latin: "On the Mental [Supersensual] Life."</note> under the above title; later, the name was also applied to four other small tracts written with entirely similar content, in the same tone, and for the same purpose. In the ***Theosophia Revelata*** <note>Latin: "Revealed Theosophy," the title of a collected edition of Böhme's works.</note> available to the author, the sequence is as follows:
1) ***De Poenitentia Vera***. On True Repentance; two books. 1622. 1623.
2) ***De Aequanimitate*** <note>Latin: "On Equanimity" or "Resignation."</note>. On True Resignation.
3) ***De Vita Mentali***. On the Supersensual Life. 1622; a dialogue between Disciple and Master.
4) ***De Regeneratione*** <note>Latin: "On Regeneration."</note>. On the New Rebirth. June 24, 1622.
These four are contained in the first part of the Glusing edition; in the second part, there are two more writings which are grouped with the previous ones in the Schiebler edition.
5) ***Colloquium Viatorum*** <note>Latin: "Dialogue of Wayfarers."</note>. Dialogue of an enlightened and an unenlightened soul; preceded by a dialogue with the Devil. 1624.
6) ***Suplicia Viatorum*** <note>Latin: "Prayers of Wayfarers."</note>. On Holy Prayer; the beginning of a prayer collection for the days of the week with an introduction; the theory of prayer. 1624.

All these treatises have as their subject the mystical way to Christ, the entering and letting-oneself-into the second principle <note>In Böhme's system, the Second Principle is the realm of Light, Love, and the Son, contrasting with the fire/wrath of the First Principle.</note>; they give us a natural history of mystical experience and are important because they open a field entirely or partially unknown to other men, offering the subject the same vivid diversity that external life offers others. Precisely because of their mystical content, ***The Way to Christ*** is written only for "natural mystics"; for to other ears that do not know divine enlightenment, the instructions on how to cast oneself into the Nothing, and through the Nothing into...

<summary>This page concludes the summary of Böhme’s "Mysterium Magnum," highlighting his views on social oppression and universal salvation, before introducing his influential devotional collection, "The Way to Christ."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jacob Böhme’s devotional writings, specifically the collection known as "The Way to Christ" (Christosophia). It transitions from a general critique of the work's accessibility to a detailed description of the stages of mystical progression: Repentance, Resignation, and the Mental Life.</meta>

surrendering to Christ is incomprehensible; and even the natural mystic does not need these counsels, because he has already experienced their truth within himself. Thus, the core of these treatises retains value only through its natural description of mysticism, through its purely religious content, the profound mood laid down within it, and through the inspired feeling of inner bliss. The entire writing style of the <term>Christosophia (Wisdom of Christ)</term> <note>A collection of Böhme's devotional tracts intended to guide the reader toward spiritual rebirth.</note>, the frequent insertion of prayer forms, the biblical-theological phrasing with its often unclear references and deep-echoing sounds, and the obscurity of exuberant feeling cannot fail to be very edifying for devout souls—all the more so as only once in the treatise *On Regeneration* <note>original: "de Regeneratione"</note> are the creation and the fall of Adam explained in the usual alchemical manner.

To reach mystical immersion, annihilation, and enlightenment, <term>repentance (Buße)</term> is first necessary; this is the first stage of mysticism, which the first booklet deals with particularly, without excluding what is necessary for its understanding in the other writings. Contemplations precede this regarding sinfulness, the heavy judgment, the torment of the damned, and the "plaything" of the world. "If a person now feels a hunger for repentance, and does not yet have the right remorse within himself, he can do nothing better than to gather his senses and mind with all reason into one, and immediately, in earnest contemplation when he feels the desire for repentance, make a powerful resolution that he will enter into repentance this very hour and minute and depart from the ungodly way, regarding neither the power nor honor of all the world and leaving everything; and let him set such a hard and strict mind for himself that he will never again depart from it, even if he should be the world's fool for it; he should firmly imagine, and wrap his soul entirely within it, that in this resolution he will attain the love of God in Christ ¹)." — The second stage is described as <term>resignation (Gelassenheit)</term> <note>original: "Gelassenheit." A key term in Böhme’s thought, referring to the total surrender of the individual will to God's will.</note>; it has its model in Christ's suffering and death; it is the burning away of sensuality and the letting in of God; a stage whose inner history is observed especially

---

¹) B. I. 12, 13, 14.

<column-break/>

<page-num>235</page-num>

by the mystics of the feminine direction. "The own-will of <term>selfhood (Selbheit)</term> must die away and must become as nothing; the resigned will trusts only God, and the Spirit of God breaks through the desire of resigned humility; trembling and joy accompany resignation, and in total self-surrender the spark of divine power falls, where the soul is then an instrument of the Spirit of God, only speaking what the Spirit of God says, and is no longer its own property ¹)." Here begins the actual mystical, supersensory life (*mental life* <note>original: "vita mentalis"</note>, the third booklet), which, however, for the sensitive, self-observing mystic, is again divided into various stages of the deepest inner process. This infinitely fine gift of observation is shown exceptionally in the dialogue between the enlightened and unenlightened soul, after the treatise *On Regeneration* <note>original: "de Regeneratione"</note> repeats the content of the previous ones and presents it as being in harmony with the Paracelsian school of thought. In the first resolution, the soul still finds no rest, despite its earnestness and practice; once again it gives itself to God and dares to sink in completely; then came the bitterest remorse for the sins committed, and it wept for its deformity; then it was drawn into the abyss of cruelty and felt as if it were losing its mind, so much so that it surrendered itself to death. Then the face of God's love appeared, and it tasted God's sweetness; but the blasphemer <note>Refers to the Devil or the spirit of doubt and temptation.</note> still stepped toward it, and it had to suffer great temptation, so that earthly reason found itself completely abandoned; it, the soul, desired to come to rest before the mockery; it became mightier and stronger from day to day until it was placed in a great kingdom of grace and the kingdom of heaven was revealed to it. Then it came into true rest.

The last booklet, as already mentioned, shows the mystic without observation, without contemplativeness, in the immediacy of prayer. —

Apart from this complex of specifically mystical treatises, there are a few more writings from Böhme's final years—
