...nothing left over, which bear witness to the fact that his best creative power had already dried up, and that he did not die too soon for the knowledge of his philosophical development—indeed, that he himself regarded his work as a completed whole, capable only of minor exegetical additions. A lack of originality and a wealth of absurdities—perhaps a result of closer interaction with Frankenberg <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652), a nobleman and mystic who became Böhme’s student and biographer.</note> and his friends, which took place particularly in 1623 and 1624—characterize products of this kind; the catalog-like nature of one identifies the final link in the chain of his spiritual development.

### 16. One Hundred Seventy-Seven Questions on Divine Revelation.

That only 15 of these questions were answered is no great loss for posterity. Shortly before he laid his head to rest forever, Böhme was prompted—perhaps by his theosophical friends in Silesia—to indulge in the most curious science of <term>Magic (Magia)</term> <note>In Böhme’s context, "Magic" refers to the spiritual power of the will to manifest something out of the "nothing."</note> and the interpretation of signs; however, death interrupted the completion of those answers. To see their low value, one only needs to read the remaining questions, which stray far from philosophy through their mystical-theosophical interest. For example: What does Noah's drunkenness signify when he cursed his son Ham? Furthermore: Why did Lot's wife become a pillar of salt; how is this to be understood? Question 110: Why did Christ walk the earth for 30 years before beginning his ministry? Why did he have to be nailed to the cross, and why was his side opened with a spear, from which blood and water flowed? What is this figure? —
In the second question, the name **Jehovah** is interpreted according to the <term>sensual language</term> <note>original: "sensualischen Sprache." This refers to Böhme’s "Language of Nature," where the very sounds of a word are thought to reveal the physical and spiritual mechanics of the thing described.</note>: the unity as the **I** [J], goes into itself into a threefold being, which is called **IE**, and the **IE** is the Father, who leads himself with his breathing will into the **HO**, as into a vessel of love, and in **HO** the Word of all powers is understood, for it makes a circumference or enclosure of itself, as the eternal "Something" or "Aught" [Ichts]; from this the love-delight proceeds, which output is the Spirit, who grasps and forms himself into the **VA**, for the **V** is the Spirit as the output, and **A** is the wisdom, in which the Spirit grasps itself into an active life, etc.

<column-break/>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<sig>17</sig>
<meta>catchword: — Soon</meta>

— Soon after, this name of the <term>Great Mystery (Mysterium Magnum)</term> <note>Böhme’s term for the "chaos" or the hidden spiritual source of the entire universe.</note> is designated as the high name **Tetragrammaton** <note>original: "יהוה" (YHWH). The sacred four-letter name of God in Hebrew.</note>. In the third question, the word Jehovah is contracted into *Jah*, in order to gain the interpretative trick of setting *Jah* [Yes] against *Nein* [No] as immediately conditioning each other, and this playfulness does not cease in the following questions. The most prominent feature of the writing arose involuntarily from a passion for foreign words, as Böhme had a lucky strike with one of them that has propagated itself in philosophy with the same meaning, namely: **Idea**. — “Before the creation, God was with two central fires, with great powers, as an eternal infinite birthing of wonders, colors, and virtues, when the angels and the soul of man, along with all creatures of this and the inner angelic spiritual world, lay within an **Idea** or spiritual modeling, in which God has seen all His works from eternity, not in a creaturely, formed manner and shape, as in a separation, but in the formality of powers, where God's Spirit played with itself” <note>1) Question 4, 1.</note>.

Despite these aforementioned dark sides of the theological and alchemical phrasing, which hinders and confuses thought, many passages nevertheless achieve a quite decisive flight toward an idealistic outlook, which could have been perfected by a man with a stronger awareness of speculation and greater confidence in his own strength; God and the Idea lie solely within man; the human spirit, the **I** [Ich], is the true and only seat of the <term>Ungrund</term> <note>Literally "the Groundless." Böhme's term for the absolute, undifferentiated divine nothingness that precedes all existence.</note> and its three principles, its “Idea.”

The first four questions deal with God, the Abyss, love and wrath, and the **Idea**; 5 and 6 deal with the angels; the rest with the devil: how his fall became possible given the omnipotence of God; whether his "Idea" was already evil in the eternal *Ungrund*; the battle of Michael with Lucifer; God's counsel, that He had not intended to bring forth the dark world; what the devil’s office and the foundation of hell might be, and whether it has existed for eternity.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jacob Böhme’s late-period works, moving from his speculative questions to the systematic diagrams and keys he produced at the request of his followers to clarify his complex mystical system.</meta>

### 17. Table of the Three Principles.

The peculiar inclination to capture visions, feelings, and mystical knowledge in images, words, and structured frameworks moved Böhme at the end of his career—at the request of Frankenberg <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652), Böhme's friend and first biographer.</note> and Schweinichen <note>Hans von Schweinichen, a Silesian nobleman and supporter of Böhme.</note>—to record the tables of his "Principles" and accompany them with explanations. First, the schema:

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| AD | Father | Will | Je |
| O | Son | Delight | Ho |
| V | Spirit | Knowledge <note>original: "Scienz" from the Latin *scientia*; in Böhme’s usage, it refers to a spiritualized "science" or the root of perception.</note> | Va |
| A | Power | Word | Life |
| J | Color | Wisdom | Virtue; |

Through this, God outside of nature—in Himself, where He is silent—is meant to be explained. The first table contains the <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH.</note>, representing what God's world is within eternal nature and the first two principles. It is arranged in columns according to the seven qualities, and from top to bottom according to the <term>Novenario</term> <note>The number nine, which Böhme viewed as a sacred number of completion.</note>, the "nine-fold" order in which all life exists. Over the sections for these qualities, the name **TINCTUR** <note>Tincture: A central Böhmean term referring to the spiritual "dye" or essence that transforms and gives quality to matter.</note> stretches out in seven letters. The second table contains the qualities and modes of the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>The "great world" or the universe.</note>, and the third contains those of the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>The "small world," referring to the human being as a reflection of the universe.</note> in a similar framework.

One might suspect that the goal of this theosophy was to think of nothing at all, if the framework itself did not reveal that this school of theosophy earnestly wanted to make understanding possible through these means. However, the categories—especially the "qualities"—are overheard from the depths of theosophy with even more refinement and artifice than the processes of the mystical soul, so that earthly reason loses the ground beneath its feet. This school believes it has produced a masterpiece of clarity by placing strange words into a sharply articulated framework. A great idea underlies this addiction to tables, categories, and frameworks, just as it does the "Doctrine of Signatures"; namely, that the human spirit develops its concepts according to regular series and laws analogous to numerical progressions. As a consequence of this fundamental idea, our own time sees Hegel’s fully realized <term>trichotomy</term> <note>The three-part logical process of thesis, antithesis, and synthesis.</note> in the developmental process; before him, Kant’s division according to the four categories; and finally, Herbart’s

---

<page-num>239</page-num>

calculation of psychological phenomena according to mathematical laws.

### 18. Clavis—The Key to the Principal Mysteries.

A companion piece to the Tables of the Principles, written with the exact same intent, this <term>Clavis</term> <note>Latin for "Key."</note> is intended to shed light on the obscure passages and words found in his previous writings, which were now regarded as a completed cycle. However, it may be of little use to those already familiar with Böhme’s other works, as the old explanations and descriptions of his inner visions are merely repeated here. The only difference is that on these few pages, one finds condensed what must otherwise be laboriously gathered from various sources. To a stranger to Böhme’s writings, they may serve as a bridge to explain his expressions using our modern philosophical terminology; however, only an approximation is possible in this operation. The language of the theosopher possesses too much originality and uniqueness to be successfully reproduced through other words—just as translations, especially French ones, could only do harm to the German spirit. The approximation will always consist only in being able to guess the solution, or often merely the existence, of the veiled contradictions. The *Clavis* mostly contains explanations of names that clarify nothing about the essence of the matter, or metaphorical expressions where the explanation makes the same claim to being a definition as the thing being explained.

### 19. Theosophical Epistles.

Under this name, sixty-six of Böhme's letters to his friends, admirers, and opponents have been collected. These are important records of the active theosophical correspondence and sources for understanding Böhme's development, private circumstances, and fate. Their historical significance and content have been taken into account throughout this present work. The following letters of greater scope and more important content seem to deserve being highlighted once more:
1) Böhme's defense against Gregorius Richter <note>The pastor of Görlitz and Böhme's primary persecutor, who viewed Böhme's mystical writings as heresy.</note>, which Böhme submitted to the city council, but which was not accept—

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of Jacob Böhme's writings, specifically focusing on his apologetic works and his extensive correspondence with fellow mystics and seekers.</meta>

...accepted, and usually (as in the Glusing edition of 1715) listed in the series of **apologetic books** <note>original: "libri apologetici"; these were works written to defend his ideas against specific theological critics such as Tilke, Stiefel, and Meth.</note>.

2) Regarding the characteristically theosophical letters, which were prompted by friends' requests for clarification: as is always the case when Böhme strives to be particularly clear, these often lack his most immediate genius, which is only confused by reflection. These letters provide only minor contributions to the extensive body of doctrines and visions piled up in his other writings—sometimes brilliantly, sometimes more confusedly. However, two epistles from August 14 and November 18, 1620 (numbers 8 and 11) to Paul Kaym, an imperial tax collector in Liegnitz, are rightly highlighted for their content ¹). They also bear the name **Instruction on the Last Times** <note>original: "Informatoria novissimorum"</note>, regarding which Paul Kaym had requested enlightenment. — Paul Kaym adhered to a form of mysticism that found no full satisfaction in the sinking of the mind into itself, or in pure inwardness and subjectivity. Since he did not recognize the external forms of a church community with its symbols and confessions as a justified expression of inwardness—nor as a spiritual necessity in that form—he placed his hope for the earthly realization of a future kingdom, founded by Christ Himself, as a substitute for the lacking church community. Dissatisfied with the dark, symbolic hints of Scripture, he sought to confine that uncertain future within numbers and measurements. Much as Müntzer and John of Leiden <note>Leaders of radical, sometimes violent, millenarian movements during the Reformation.</note> thought to establish such a kingdom, the **Chiliasts** <note>From the Greek "chilioi" (thousand); those who believe in a literal 1,000-year earthly reign of Christ.</note> now also believed—without yet wishing to set their own hands to the work—that the year 1630 would bring the beginning of the kingdom, the goal, and the destruction of Babel <note>In Böhme’s vocabulary, "Babel" refers to the confused, institutionalized, and corrupt state of the established churches.</note>. Toward these hopes of a thousand-year Sabbath and the doctrine of the four hundred years intended for the reform of the godless, Kaym directed the efforts of his natural or forced prophetic gift. Gog and Magog <note>Biblical figures representing the ultimate enemies of God’s people in apocalyptic prophecy.</note> were to enter into battle, and a double resurrection was to bound the period, though it was uncertain in what manner. Fantastic...

---

¹) See Theosophical Works, Vol. I, pp. 125, 126.

<column-break/>

<page-num>241</page-num>

...excitations of the mind, which often intensified into the certainty of mystical enlightenment, along with dreams, coincidences, and the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note>, were the foundations of the "science" of such prophecies. Kaym expected from Böhme, as an enlightened prophet of the Lord, a closer explanation of these mysteries, since Böhme had also scattered prophecies in poetic, obscure words throughout his writings. But Jacob Böhme rejected the follies of such a demand: he himself had no knowledge of the thousand-year kingdom; Scripture, he argued, could be interpreted in various ways, however one wished; the time was indeed near, but knowing it was of no importance ¹).

> "If the godless man were certain that he still had 400 years until the end, how he would strive only for his children and wealth ²)!"

Böhme sought the true Kingdom of God only in the mysticism of his own heart, and in the certainty of the immanent God, he had no need for human community in a thousand-year kingdom.

3) Of importance for Böhme's inner development are Epistle No. 10 to Abraham von Sommerfeld and No. 12 to Caspar Lindner. Distinguished by their eccentricity are No. 28 to Valentin Thirnes, No. 22 to the Lord of Schellenberg, and No. 43 and 47 to H. Freudenhammer in Glogau, who arranged Böhme's principles and categories into tables.

### **Conclusion.**

In the preceding sections, all works recognized as authentic by the Dutch editors have been treated, without the possibility of identifying the mutilations and insertions made by theosophical enthusiasts who sought to embody their own views with a tasteless brush and nonsensical interpretation. — Whatever mentions are found of "lost writings" are undoubtedly based on error and duplicate titles. The "Book of the Last Judgment" is nothing more than the **True Psychology** <note>original: "Psychologia Vera"</note>, also known as the *Forty Questions on the Soul* ³). In Tenzel's monthly...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jakob Böhme’s bibliography and shifts into a developmental study of his theosophical system, moving from his early work, "Aurora," to his later, more refined metaphysical structures.</meta>

...conversations of 1692, a list of various mystical writings is found under the title:

<margin>1) S. Knauth, Manuscript regarding the Bohemist Library Rep. II.</margin>
> **Theological Manuscripts collected with great diligence and much expense around the end of the past and the beginning of the current century by N. N. from here and there,** <note>original Latin: "Theologica Manuscripta summa diligentia multisque sumtibus circa finem defluxi et principium curr. Seculi a. N. N. hinc inde collecta"</note> where 29 pieces of Böhme’s writings are listed, including 10 as original manuscripts; some of these are said not to appear in any other list 1), for example, "Consolation in Sadness, 1620, and on the Heavenly and Earthly Mystery"; furthermore, Jakob Böhme’s various spiritual writings, etc. — The first, however, is nothing other than the treatise on the **Four Temperaments** <term>Complexions: Referring to the four temperaments—choleric, sanguine, phlegmatic, and melancholic—which Böhme linked to spiritual states</term>; the second is the *Pansophic Mystery*, and the third is likely the *Christosophia* or other writings of theological-mystical content.

Moreover, we possess a sufficient number of writings to become acquainted with Böhme’s spirit; indeed, his repetitions, long-windedness, and confusing incidental material make a more condensed style and a lesser productivity—paired with more careful criticism—appear desirable.

The attempt to construct a complete picture of Böhme’s development, based on the descriptions that emerge from the manifold, layered, and intersecting views often found within one and the same writing, brings about roughly the following result:

1. **The Rising Dawn** <note>original: "Morgenröthe im Aufgang," also known as the "Aurora"</note> forms a period of its own. This is the period of a childlike intuition, which was the first product of a powerful ethical drive. This drive outpaced his other intellectual faculties and prevented him from bringing the world—which appeared to exist in contradiction—into a coherent and satisfying unity through progressive thought. — His theosophy <term>Theosophy: Here meaning "divine wisdom," a system of speculation seeking direct knowledge of the mind of God and the mysteries of nature</term> is pictorial and crudely sensory; the emerging concepts in *The Rising Dawn* have the appearance of angels, devils, and dolls. Divinity and the world are not essentially different. This pantheistic deity consists of three principles: a heavenly, a sidereal <term>Sidereal: Relating to the stars or the celestial world of influences</term>, and an earthly kingdom. The first and second (the earthly, which is the fallen kingdom of the devil) are locked in a struggle, the stage for which is the third

---

<page-num>243</page-num>
kingdom. The first two principles are: Good and Evil. Their origin is derived here according to the biblical myth. The main point is: First there is God, then the Devil, and thirdly the World. Because of the ethical demand for distinction, these opposites are unreconciled; the pantheistic unity lacks a clear, visual connection.

2. When, after five years, Böhme’s vision once again found expression, many inconsistencies beneath the surface of his consciousness had reached a remedy and a change of views—quite apart from the greater skill in expression, which had previously remained stuck in the sensory. In the *Aurora*, Christ had been put in the place of the fallen Lucifer; this formed a fourth principle, although it had not yet appeared as such; it maintained its place as the second. Because it is the purest expression of Good, the Devil became the first principle. At the same time, God the Father still held his position in this place, and he remained because in the Old Testament he is the God of wrath and the world of fire—because he created everything, and therefore evil must also have proceeded from him. Evil became an immanence <term>Immanence: The idea that the divine (or in this case, the root of evil) exists within the very structure of the world or the deity itself, rather than being entirely separate</term> of the first principle, yet not an identity. Through the sharp opposition of the pure second principle—the Heart and Light of God—the following order arose:

->I. The principle of God the Father, of Wrath,<-
->II. The Heart of God, the Absolutely Good,<-
->III. The struggling world of the third principle,<-

which the Holy Spirit moves. The Trinity is established and brought into harmony with the three principles; the evil principle has become a modification of the first principle. Reason had asserted its right to unity within the One Deity; this stage of development is primarily physical or nature-philosophical, while the fractured ethical standpoint of the *Aurora* appears to be set aside. There, the fall of Lucifer was lamentable—a miserable, cursed fact grounded in the selfish will of the proud angel; hence the origin of evil, to which Adam fell victim through selfish imagination. God stood tragically outside of evil, and his principle fought against evil within the third principle. The ethical consideration...

<summary>This page catalogs obscure Böhme manuscripts and analyzes the evolution of his philosophy from the early, sensory "Aurora" to a more complex three-principle system that incorporates the "wrath" of God the Father as a foundational element.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Aurora, Three Principles, Theosophy, Divine Wrath, Lucifer's Fall, Pantheism, Christosophia</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the chronological analysis of Jakob Böhme’s intellectual development, moving from his middle period—influenced by alchemy—into his mature dialectical stage and final theological reflections, ultimately comparing his unreflective "beholding" to the systematic philosophy of Spinoza.</meta>

...viewpoint did not disappear but rather took on a reconciling form, in which evil became a subjective, one-sided phase of God in His wrath. The main writings of this epoch are the *Three Principles*, the *Threefold Life of Man*, and the *True Psychology* <note>original: "Psychologia vera"</note>. This was the period marked by the influence of his alchemical friends. —

**3.** Now, dialectical necessity forces its way forward; the opposites sharpen and clarify themselves. Outside of these opposites, nothing exists, because they are the simplest of concepts; and because a transition or mediation is not present, they are viewed as existing and necessary. Strangely, however, negation—as we imagine it—is the "first principle" for Böhme, though not in a temporal sense, and this clarification serves as his excuse. This capacity for distinction <note>original: "Schiedlichkeit," Böhme’s term for the power of differentiation</note> separates position, negation, and sublation <note>original: "Aufhebung," the process where a concept is both canceled and preserved</note>; or antithesis, thesis, and synthesis. The necessity of this supreme distinction, of the highest contradiction, explains all other problems: the metaphysical forms of the Deity in the three worlds, the phenomena of anthropology, the free choice between good and evil, and the separation of the two as mutually and necessarily conditioning one another. At the same time, reason demands a unity for this supreme contradiction; as a sign of the point where sense ends and the unthinkable begins, the imaginary unity of the entire Deity is called the **Groundless** <note>original: "Ungrund," referring to the nothingness or absolute abyss before creation</note> or Nothing, the Abyss and Darkness, where reason is lost.

This is the highest stage of Böhme’s development, where problems are—at least apparently—solved by concentrating them into a single point. This solution was reached through "beholding" <note>Anschauung: a direct spiritual intuition</note>, which comes closest to pure speculation. The writings of this period are the shorter works of 1620, the *Theoscopia*, the *Signature of All Things* <note>original: "Signatura Rerum"</note>, and the *Election of Grace* <note>original: "Gnadenwahl"</note>. This stage also takes on a hint of systematization; however, in the details, there is still a prevailing lack of refinement, confusion, and unintelligibility—the simple consequence of a theosophy that is in the very moment of becoming philosophy. Only the highest peaks reach into the world of pure thought; the supporting material falls back into the darkness of a dead mysticism.

**4.** Although the later writings form a natural group following the completion of this dialectical development, they are characterized by an exhaustion of phi-

<column-break/>

losophical power; because the final fundamental intuition remained the same, they cannot be ranked as a "fourth stage" alongside the first three. The writings of this final period of the theosophist are partly Gnostic in content, such as the *Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note> and the *Testaments of Christ*, analogous to the historical position of the Gnostics in declining stages of development. Others are mystical-theological, while some provide a conscious conclusion through tabular and interpretive summary works. In the theological writings, represented especially by the *Christosophia*, the expression is—as is often the case—not so sharp as to prevent one from suspecting a change in perspective. Specifically, when it is said that the mystic should "enter into the Nothing" in order to be deified, the misunderstanding is not far off: that "Nothing" (which designates the **Groundless**) might be set as a positive, good principle, which would cause a new layering of thought and confusion. However, the "Nothing" here is merely the Groundless acting as the path through which one can reach the "Heart of God," which is the positive. The "I," the **selfhood** <note>original: "Selbheit"</note>, must first be put to death before a new life can be born in the Second Principle. Yet, an ambiguity remains regarding the word "Nothing," because the evil principle is just as much a negation of the positive second principle as is the Groundless. One must, therefore, remove the emphasis of opposition to the "Heart of God" from the "Nothing" (as Groundless) in order to get beyond the contradiction.

Spinoza, the philosopher who was historically closest to Böhme in the direction of speculation that Böhme pursued, did not take the theosophist—who saw only the "dawn" of philosophy—into account. But however much one may place the sharpness, clarity, consciousness, and consistency of Spinoza's thoughts in a favorable light, he and Böhme nevertheless form the opposite poles of one and the same development of philosophy, which was the foundation for later advancement in history. They stood on common ground; namely, the teachings of both had an essentially dogmatic form. Böhme "beheld" without demonstrating how he arrived at his theosophy; Spinoza set down axioms and definitions without proving or deducing them. The difference consisted in the fact that Böhme did so without reflection and only in fragments.

<vocab>Jacob Böhme, Baruch Spinoza, Theosophy, Mysticism, Dialectics, Groundless (Ungrund), Antithesis, Thesis, Synthesis, Christosophia, Signature of All Things (Signatura Rerum), Election of Grace (Gnadenwahl), Great Mystery (Mysterium Magnum), Testaments of Christ</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the final stages of Jakob Böhme's intellectual evolution, highlighting his move toward a dialectical system centered on the "Ungrund" (Groundless) and comparing his unreflective, intuitive method with the more rigid, dogmatic philosophy of Spinoza.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Spinoza, Ungrund, dialectics, Theosophy, Three Principles, negation, selfhood, Mysterium Magnum, philosophy of religion</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page concludes the comparison between Jakob Böhme’s mystical insights and the formal philosophical systems of Spinoza and Hegel, before transitioning into a critique of Böhme's later 17th-century followers and a list of printing corrections for the volume.</meta>

his intuition provided; Spinoza consciously derived his system from that which was posited. For this reason, too, they share a common ground, because the content of their teachings offers mediating points of view; the highest that Böhme offers is the doctrine of the three principles and the dialectical process; Spinoza offers the cause of itself <note>original Latin: "Causa sui"</note>, whose essence—comprising thought, concept, and being—involves existence <note>original Latin: "cujus essentia... involvit existenziam"</note>. Both are based on the identity of thinking and being within the Absolute. Böhme's view—excluding his various confusions and absurdities—satisfies the requirements of a pure doctrine of principles in its dialectical stage by resolving all contradictions in the Highest, and by synthesizing all being, thinking, and acting through the supreme Absolute. Spinoza, meanwhile, satisfies through the perfected sharpness and clarity of his method. Böhme pushes toward progress through the dissatisfaction caused by the highest contradiction; Spinoza through a lack of connection between God and His modes <note>In Spinozism, "modes" are the specific manifestations or individual things that exist as modifications of the one divine Substance</note>, which are seen only as human ways of perceiving.

The philosophers of the 19th century resolved these existing contradictions. Hegel consistently carried out the idealism grounded in the identity of thinking and being, as it had first appeared in Böhme and Spinoza. He was not wrong to draw attention to Böhme's theosophy <note>A philosophical system seeking direct knowledge of the divine</note>, whose dialectical period can be regarded without hesitation as the "dawn" of Hegel's own philosophy.

But those who identified as Böhme’s followers in the 17th century were mere caricatures—withered scraps without grandeur, depth, or a trace of genius. Frankenberg wasted away in a search for symbolic formulas and a piously playful fanaticism; Angelus Silesius, in the bosom of the Roman Catholic Church, became soft in emotional extravagance and thereby disorganized; Kuhlmann and Gichtel were overwrought fools, as were Ueberfeld and Rothe, the Dutch prophets. Only Spener <note>Philipp Jakob Spener, the "Father of Pietism"</note> is a worthy successor to Böhme's mild religious tolerance. The 18th century brought only the reverse side of the Enlightenment during the era of the classical upswing of all the nation's spiritual forces—an era in which the mysticism of Swedenborg or Hamann could not survive.

---

->Printed by Julius Köhler in Görlitz.<-
